## Preface / 序言
===Original===

Revolution breaks the social forms grown too narrow for man. It bursts the molds which constrict him the more solidified they become, and the more Life ever striving forward leaves them. In this dynamic process the Russian Revolution has gone further than any previous revolution.
The abolition of the established﻿—politically and economically, socially and ethically﻿—the attempt to replace it with something different, is the reflex of man’s changed needs, of the awakened consciousness of the people. Back of revolution are the millions of living humans who embody its inner spirit, who feel, think, and have their being in it. To them revolution is not a mere change of externals: it implies the complete dislocation of life, the shattering of dominant traditions, the annulment of accepted standards. The habitual, measured step of existence is interrupted, accustomed criterions become inoperative, former precedents are void. Existence is forced into uncharted channels; every action demands self-reliance; every detail calls for new, independent decision. The typical, the familiar, have disappeared; dissolved is the coherence and interrelation of the parts that formerly constituted one whole. New values are to be created.
This *inner* life of revolution, which is its sole meaning, has almost entirely been neglected by writers on the Russian Revolution. Many books have been published about that tremendous social upheaval, but seldom do they strike its true keynote. They treat of the fall and rise of institutions, of the new State and its structure, of constitutions and laws﻿—of the exclusively *external* manifestations, which nearly make one forget the living millions who continue to exist, to be, under all changing conditions.
Justly Taine said that in studying the French Revolution he found statistics and data, official documents and edicts least illuminative of the real character of the period. Its significant expression, its deeper sense, he discovered in the lives, thoughts, and feelings of the people, in their personal reactions as portrayed in the memoirs, journals, and letters of contemporaries.
The present work is compiled from the Diary which I kept during my two years’ stay in Russia. It is the chronicle of an intense experience, of impressions and observations noted down day by day, in different parts of the country, among various walks of life.

===Chinese===

革命打破了对人类而言已过于狭隘的社会形态。各种旧体制越是固化，奔涌向前的生命便越是将其抛在身后；革命正是冲决了束缚人类的这些桎梏。在这一充满张力的历史进程中，俄国革命比以往任何一场革命都走得更远。
在政治与经济、社会与道德伦理层面彻底废除既有秩序，并试图以全新的事物取而代之，这是人类不断演进的需求的投射，也是民众觉醒意识的体现。革命的背后，是数以百万计有血有肉、鲜活具体的人：正是他们构成了革命的内在精神，他们在革命中感知、思考、安身立命。对他们而言，革命绝非外在表象的简单更迭，它意味着整个生存状态的彻底颠覆，意味着统治性传统的轰然瓦解，意味着既定行事标准的彻底废止。按部就班、波澜不惊的日常生存节奏被打断，司空见惯的衡量尺度悉数失效，过往的先例皆化为乌有。生命被强行推入未知的暗流峡谷；每一个举动都需要自立自强；每一个微末细节都迫切要求做出崭新而独立的决断。曾经具有代表性的、为人熟稔的事物荡然无存；以往构成统一整体的各个有机部分之间的内在联系与逻辑，已彻底消解分散。全新的价值尺度亟待重建。
然而，这场革命真正且唯一的深层要义——革命的“内在”生命——却几乎被所有论及俄国革命的著述者抛诸脑后。关于这场惊天动地的社会剧变，市面上已出版了汗牛充栋的书籍，却极少有哪一部能够真正切中其核心基调。它们大多热衷于铺陈机构制度的兴衰沉浮、新国家的政治架构，或是宪章法度与法令规章——这些全都是浮于表面的“外在”表象；这些著作几乎让人淡忘了在一切动荡与变迁中艰难求生、苦苦挣扎的数百万鲜活生灵。
正如丹纳（Hippolyte Taine）在研读法国大革命时切中要害地指出：他发现统计数据、官方文件与法令告示，最不足以阐明那个时代真正的本质特征。大革命最意味深长的表达与最为深邃的内涵，深藏于底层民众的生活、思想与情感之中，深藏于同代人留下的回忆录、日记与书信所描摹的个体切身反应之中。
呈现在读者眼前的这部著作，正是我在俄国逗留的两年间所记录的日记汇编。它是一段刻骨铭心切身体验的编年记录，是作者日复一日深入这个国家的各个角落、穿梭于三教九流各色阶层之间所捕获的真实观感与现场记录。

===Original===

Most of the names are deleted, for the obvious reason of protecting the persons in question.
So far as I know it is the only journal kept in Russia during those momentous years (1920﻿–﻿1922). It was a rather difficult task, as those familiar with Russian conditions will understand. But long practice in such matters﻿—keeping memoranda even in prison﻿—enabled me to preserve my Diary through many vicissitudes and searches, and get it safely out of the country. Its Odyssey was adventurous and eventful. After having journeyed through Russia for two years, the Diary succeeded in crossing the border, only to be lost before it could join me. There followed an anxious hunt through several European lands, and when hope of locating my notebooks was almost given up, they were discovered in the attic of a very much frightened old lady in Germany. But that is another story.
Sufficient that the manuscript was finally found and can now be presented to the public in the present volume. If it will aid in visualizing the inner life of the Revolution during the period described, if it will bring the reader closer to the Russian people and their great martyrdom, the mission of my Diary will be accomplished and my efforts well repaid.
Alexander Berkman.

===Chinese===

出于保护相关当事人的显而易见的考量，文中的大多数人名已被隐去或略去。
据我所知，这是在那段动荡关键岁月（1920—1922年）里，在俄国境内留下的唯一一部私人日记。凡熟悉俄国当时真实国情的人都会明白，要完成并保全这一记录是一项何等凶险艰难的苦差。然而，凭借以往在类似处境下积累的长期经验——甚至在铁窗囹圄中我也曾坚持暗中记下备忘录——我终于让这部日记历经无数劫难与反复搜查后得以幸存，并最终安全带出了国境。这部手稿的“奥德赛之旅”可谓充满了惊涛骇浪与传奇波折：在俄国境内辗转漂流了两年之后，日记终于侥幸越过边境，却在尚未与我汇合前不幸下落不明。紧接着便是在欧洲数个国家展开的心急如焚的大搜寻；正当寻获笔记本的希望几乎彻底破灭之际，手稿终于在德国一位惊魂未定的老妇人家中的阁楼深处被奇迹般找回。不过，那完全是另一段曲折的故事了。
万幸的是手稿终于失而复得，如今得以在这卷著作中完整呈现在世人面前。倘若它有助于读者具象化感知所述时期革命的内在生命，倘若它能让读者更加贴近俄国民众及其所承受的伟大苦难与殉道历程，那么这部日记的使命便算圆满达成，我的心血与努力也未曾虚掷。
亚历山大·伯克曼（Alexander Berkman）

## I The Log of the Transport *Buford* / I “布福德号”运输船日志
===Original===

On Board the U.S.T. *Buford*.
*December 23, 1919.*﻿—We are somewhere near the Azores, already three days at sea. No one seems to know whither we are bound. The captain claims he is sailing under sealed orders. The men are nearly crazy with the uncertainty and worry over the women and children left behind. What if we are to be landed on Denikin territory?﻿ ﻿…
---
We were kidnapped, literally kidnapped out of bed in the dead of night.
It was late in the evening, December 20, when the prison keepers entered our cell at Ellis Island and ordered us to “get ready at once.” I was just undressing; the others were in their bunks, asleep. We were taken completely by surprise. Some of us expected to be deported, but we had been promised several days’ notice; while a number were to be released on bail, their cases not having been finally passed upon by the courts.
We were led into a large, bare room in the upper part of the building. Helter-skelter the men crowded in, dragging their things with them, badly packed in the haste and confusion. At four in the morning the order was given to start. In silence we filed into the prison yard, led by the guards and flanked on each side by city and Federal detectives. It was dark and cold; the night air chilled me to the bone. Scattered lights in the distance hinted of the huge city asleep.
Like shadows we passed through the yard toward the ferry, stumbling on the uneven ground. We did not speak; the prison keepers also were quiet. But the detectives laughed boisterously, and swore and sneered at the silent line. “Don’t like this country, damn you! Now you’ll get out, ye sons of b﻿—﻿—.”
At last we reached the steamer. I caught sight of three women, our fellow prisoners, being taken aboard. Stealthily, her sirens dumb, the vessel got under way. Within half an hour we boarded the *Buford*, awaiting us in the Bay.
At 6 a.m., Sunday, December 21, we started on our journey. Slowly the big city receded, wrapped in a milky veil. The tall skyscrapers, their outlines dimmed, looked like fairy castles lit by winking stars﻿—and then all was swallowed in the distance.
---
*December 24.*﻿—The *Buford* is an old boat built in 1885. She was used as a military transport during the Philippine War, and is not seaworthy any more. We ship sea constantly, and it pours through the hatches.

===Chinese===

在美军“布福德号”运输船（U.S.T. *Buford*）上。
*1919年12月23日。*——我们正航行在亚速尔群岛（Azores）附近的某处海域，出海已有三天了。似乎没有人知晓我们究竟驶向何方。船长声称自己是在执行密令航行。同伴们因前途未卜，加之极度牵挂被留下的妻儿老小，几乎快要发疯了。倘若我们被强行扔在邓尼金（Denikin）白军盘踞的领地，那该如何是好？……
---
我们是被绑架的——千真万确是在夜深人静之时从床上被生生绑架走的。
那是12月20日深夜，埃利斯岛（Ellis Island）拘留所的狱卒突然闯入我们的牢房，勒令我们“立刻收拾准备动身”。我当时正准备宽衣就寝；其他人则躺在各自的双层铺上熟睡。我们被打了个措手不及。我们当中固然有人料到会被驱逐出境，但当局此前曾亲口许诺会提前数日通知；更有相当一部分人本该获准保释，他们的案子法院甚至尚未做出终审判决。
我们被押解进大楼上层一间空旷简陋的大房间。男人们乱哄哄地涌了进来，拖曳着在慌乱仓促中胡乱塞紧的随身行囊。凌晨4点整，出发的号令下达了。在狱警押送下，队伍默默排成单列步入监狱大院，两侧夹道戒备着纽约市警与联邦密探。夜色漆黑寒冷，刺骨的夜风直钻骨髓。远方零星散落的微弱灯火，隐约昭示着这座沉睡中的庞大都市。
我们宛如幽灵般穿过大院奔向渡轮码头，在凹凸不平的泥地上跌跌撞撞。谁也没有开口言语；看守狱卒也保持着异样的沉默。然而那些密探却放肆地狂声浪笑，冲着沉默的队列大声咒骂嗤笑：“操你们的，不喜欢这个国家是吧！现在滚蛋吧，你们这帮狗娘养的杂种。”
终于登上了蒸汽渡轮。我瞥见三名同样身为囚徒的女性难友被押解上船。渡轮悄无声息地启航，警报汽笛紧闭哑默。不到半小时，我们便登上了早已在海湾深处静候的“布福德号”。
12月21日，星期日，清晨6点整，我们正式启程远航。庞大的都市裹挟在乳白色的薄雾之中，缓缓向后退去。高耸入云的摩天大楼轮廓逐渐模糊，宛如在闪烁繁星点缀下的童话城堡——紧接着，一切皆被吞噬在了茫茫地平线的远方。
---
*12月24日。*——“布福德号”是一艘建造于1885年的老爷船。美菲战争（Philippine War）期间它曾被用作运兵运输船，如今早已丧失了适航能力。海水一波接一波狠狠拍打上甲板，顺着舱口倾盆灌入舱底。

===Original===

Two inches of water cover the floor; our things are wet, and there is no steam heat.
Our three women companions occupy a separate cabin. The men are cooped up in crowded, ill-smelling steerage quarters. We sleep in bunks built three tiers high. The loose wire netting of the one above me bulges so low with the weight of its occupant, it scratches my face whenever the man moves.
We are prisoners. Armed sentinels on deck, in the gangways, and at every door. They are silent and sullen; strict orders not to talk to us. Yesterday I offered one of them an orange﻿—I thought he looked sick. But he refused it.
We caught a radio today about wholesale arrests of the radicals throughout the United States. Probably in connection with protests against our deportation.
There is much resentment among our men at the brutality that accompanied the deportation, and at the suddenness of the proceedings. They were given no time to get their money or clothing. Some of the boys were arrested at their workbenches, placed in jail, and deported without a chance to collect their pay checks. I am sure that the American people, if informed, would not stand for another boatload of deportees being set adrift in the Atlantic without enough clothes to keep them warm. I have faith in the American people, but American officialdom is ruthlessly bureaucratic.
Love of native soil, of home, is manifesting itself. I notice it especially among those who spent only a few years in America. More frequently the men of Southern Russia speak the Ukrainian language. All long to get to Russia quickly, to behold the land they had left in the clutches of Tsarism and which is now the freest on earth.
We have organized a committee to take a census. There are 246 of us, besides the three women. Various types and nationalities: Great Russians from New York and Baltimore; Ukrainian miners from Virginia; Letts, Lithuanians, and one Tartar. The majority are members of the Union of Russian Workers, an Anarchist organization with branches throughout the United States and Canada. About eleven belong to the Socialist Party in the United States, while some are nonpartisan. There are editors, lecturers, and manual workers of every kind among us. Some are bewhiskered, looking typically Russian; others smooth-shaven, American in appearance.

===Chinese===

积水足有2英寸深，漫过了船舱底板；我们的随身物件全被泡透，船舱内没有丝毫蒸汽供暖。
与我们同行的三位女性同伴被安置在独立的舱房。男人们则被死死禁锢在逼仄拥挤、恶臭熏天的统舱统舱下层。我们睡在三层高的简陋铁架吊铺上。我头顶上方铺位的粗铁丝网松垮下垂，在上方同伴的体重压迫下深陷得极低，对方每一次翻身挪动，铁丝网都会狠狠擦刮过我的面颊。
我们是名副其实的囚徒。全副武装的持枪哨兵把守在甲板、走廊通道以及每一道舱门口。他们面色阴郁、一言不发；军方下达了严令，严禁与我们交谈半句。昨天我曾递给其中一名哨兵一个橘子——我看他面如土色似有病容。但他冷冷拒绝了。
今天我们收到了一条无线电广播，通报全美各地正对激进分子展开大规模的全面大搜捕。这大概与抗议我们将我们驱逐出境的抗议风潮有关。
同伴们对这次驱逐出境过程中所充斥的野蛮行径，以及抓捕手段的极端猝然，满怀着强烈的悲愤与怨怼。他们根本没有得到片刻时间去取回自己的积蓄存款或过冬衣物。有些年轻人直接在工厂车间的工作台前被当场铐走、投进大牢，甚至连支取结算工资支票的机会都被彻底剥夺便被强行驱逐。我深信，如果美国人民获悉这一真相，绝不会容忍又一船被驱逐者在连御寒衣物都未备齐的绝境下，被孤零零遗弃在大西洋上随波逐流漂泊。我对美国人民依然抱有深切的信心，但美国官僚阶层却是冷酷无情、残忍至极的。
对故土家园的热爱与眷恋开始在人群中自然流露。在那些赴美仅寥寥数年的年轻同胞身上，这种乡土情怀尤为炽烈。来自俄国南方的同胞们越发频繁地操起乌克兰语促膝长谈。人人皆迫切渴望尽快踏上俄国的土地，去亲眼看一看他们当年在沙皇专制魔爪下仓皇告别、而如今已成为全天下最自由热土的伟大祖国。
我们自发组织了一个专门委员会来统计人口普查。除三名女性之外，我们全体男同胞共有246人。人员类型与民族成分极其多元：有来自纽约和巴尔的摩的大俄罗斯人；来自弗吉尼亚的乌克兰矿工；拉脱维亚人、立陶宛人，还有一名鞑靼人。绝大多数人皆是“俄国工人联盟”（Union of Russian Workers）的骨干成员，这是一个分支网络遍布全美与加拿大的无政府主义组织（Anarchist organization）。大约有11人隶属于美国社会党（Socialist Party），另有一些人则是无党派人士。在我们当中，编辑、巡回演说家以及各行各业的体力劳动者应有尽有。有的人胡子拉碴，长着典型的俄罗斯面孔；有的人则胡须刮得干干净净，一身地道的美式做派。

===Original===

Most of the men are of decided Slavic countenance, with broad face and high cheekbones.
“We’ll work like devils for the Revolution,” Big Samuel, the West Virginia miner, announces to the group gathered around him. He talks Russian.
“You bet we will,” comes from a corner bunk in English. It’s the mascot of our cabin, a red-cheeked youth, a six-footer, whom we have christened the “Baby.”
“Me for Baku,” an older man joins in. “I’m an oil driller. They’ll need *me* all right.”
I ponder over Russia, a country in revolution, a social revolution which has uprooted the very foundations, political, economical, ethical. There are the Allied invasion, the blockade, and internal counterrevolution. All forces must be bent, first of all, to secure the complete victory of the workers. Bourgeois resistance within must be crushed; interference from without defeated. Everything else will come later. To think that it was given to Russia, enslaved and tyrannized over for centuries, to usher in the New Day! It is almost beyond belief, past comprehension. Yesterday the most backward country; today in the vanguard. Nothing short of a miracle.
Unreservedly shall the remaining years of my life be consecrated to the service of the wonderful Russian people.
*December 25.*﻿—The military force of the *Buford* is in command of a Colonel of the United States Army, tall and severe-looking, about fifty. In his charge are a number of officers and a very considerable body of soldiers, most of them of the regular army. Direct supervision over the deportees is given to the representative of the Federal Government, Mr. Berkshire, who is here with a number of Secret Service men. The Captain of the *Buford* takes his orders from the Colonel, who is the supreme authority on board.
The deportees want exercise on deck and free association with our women comrades. As their chosen spokesman I submitted their demands to Berkshire, but he referred me to the Colonel. I refused to apply to the latter, on the ground that we are political, not military, prisoners. Later the Federal man informed me that “the higher authorities” had granted us exercise, but association with the women was refused. Permission, however, would be given me to convince myself that “the ladies are receiving humane treatment.”

===Chinese===

大多数同伴都长着鲜明典型的斯拉夫面庞，面颊宽阔，颧骨高耸。
“我们要为大革命像疯子一样拼命干活！”西弗吉尼亚矿工、人称“大萨穆埃尔”的壮汉操着俄语对围拢在身旁的伙伴们宣告道。
“那还用说，必须拼命！”从角落铺位里传来一句英文应和。那是我们舱房的吉祥物，一个面颊绯红、身高足有六英尺的年轻壮汉，我们戏称他为“大宝贝”。
“我是一定要去巴库（Baku）的，”一位年纪稍长的同伴插话道，“我是个熟练的石油钻井工。他们绝对少不了‘我’这样的人手。”
我陷入了对俄国的深沉思索：那是一个正处于大革命怒潮中的国度，一场社会大革命已经连根拔起了其政治、经济与伦理的旧基石。眼下正横亘着协约国武装干涉、严密封锁以及国内反革命叛乱。当务之急，必须倾尽全民族的一切力量，首先确保工人的彻底胜利。国内的资产阶级反抗必须被无情粉碎；来自外部的武装干涉必须被彻底击溃。除此以外的一切建设计划都必须退居其后。想一想吧：千百年来饱受奴役与残酷专制压迫的俄国，竟然承担起了开启人类崭新黎明的伟大天职！这简直令人难以置信，超出了寻常理性的理解范畴。昨日还是欧洲最落后愚昧的蛮荒之地；今日却昂首伫立在全人类的最前列。这无异于天崩地裂的伟大奇迹。
我将毫无保留地把自己残存的余生，全心全意奉献给服务这群不可思议的俄国人民！
*12月25日。*——“布福德号”上的军事武装押解部队由一名美利坚合众国陆军上校统领，他身形高大、神情冷酷严峻，年约50岁。他麾下统辖着多名军官以及一支规模相当庞大的武装卫队，其中绝大多数属于正规军。对全体被驱逐者的直接监管权，则被赋予了联邦政府代表伯克希尔先生（Mr. Berkshire），他率领着多名联邦特勤局便衣密探随船押解。“布福德号”的船长一律听命于这位上校，后者是整艘船上的最高独裁权威。
同伴们强烈要求获准在甲板上散步活动，并要求与我们的女性女同志自由走动交往。作为大家推选出的总发言人，我向伯克希尔当面递交了这些诉求，但他却推脱让我直接向陆军上校申请。我严词拒绝向上校卑躬屈膝，理由是我们是政治犯，而非战俘或军事囚徒。随后这位联邦特工通报我说：“最高上峰”已开恩批准我们每天在甲板散步，但与女性同伴接触的请求被断然驳回。不过，军方特批容许我前去探视，以便让我亲眼确信“女士们正受到人道公正的待遇”。

===Original===

Accompanied by Berkshire and one of his assistants, I was allowed to visit Emma Goldman, Dora Lipkin-Perkus, and Ethel Bernstein. I found them on the upper deck, Dora and Ethel bundled up and much the worse for seasickness, the motherly nurse ministering to them. They looked forlorn, those “dangerous enemies” of the United States. The powerful American Government never appeared to me in a more ridiculous light.
The women made no complaints: they are treated well and receive good food. But all three are penned in a small cabin intended for one person only; day and night armed sentinels guard their door.
No trace of Christ appeared anywhere on the ship this Christmas Day. The usual espionage and surveillance, the same discipline and severity. But in the general messroom, at dinner, there was an addition to the regular meal: currant bread and cranberries. More than half of the tables were vacant, however: most of the men are in their bunks, sick.
*December 26.*﻿—Rough sea, and more men “laid out.” The six-foot “Baby” is the sickest of them all. The hatches have been closed to keep the sea out, and it is suffocating below deck. There are forty-nine men in our compartment; the rest are in the two adjoining ones.
The ship physician has asked me to assist him on his daily rounds, as interpreter and nurse. The men suffer mostly from stomach and bowel complaints; but there are also cases of rheumatism, sciatica, and heart-disease. The Boris brothers are in a precarious condition; young John Birk is growing very weak; a number of others are in bad shape.
*December 27.*﻿—The Boston deportee, a former sailor, claims the course of the *Buford* was changed twice during the night. “Perhaps making for the Portugal Coast,” he said. It is rumored we may be turned over to Denikin. The men are much worried.
Human psychology everywhere has a basic kinship. Even in prison I found the deepest tragedies lit up by a touch of humor. In spite of the great anxiety regarding our destination, there is much laughter and joking in our cabin. Some wit among the boys has christened the *Buford* the “Mystery Ship.”
In the afternoon Berkshire informed me that the Colonel wished to see me. His cabin, not large, but light and dry, is quite different from our steerage quarters. The Colonel asked me what part of Russia we were “expecting to go to.” The Soviet part, of course, I said.

===Chinese===

在伯克希尔及其一名助手的押送陪同下，我获准探视了爱玛·戈尔德曼（Emma Goldman）、多拉·利普金-佩尔库斯（Dora Lipkin-Perkus）与埃塞尔·伯恩斯坦（Ethel Bernstein）。我在上层甲板见到了她们，多拉与埃塞尔全身包裹得严严实实，晕船折磨得她们面容憔悴，一位慈祥的女护士正悉心照料着她们。眼前这几位所谓美利坚合众国的“极度危险公敌”，看上去显得如此无助而凄凉。庞大强盛的美国政府在我眼中从未显得如此荒谬可笑。
女士们没有发一句怨言：她们受到了得体的对待，伙食也相当不错。然而她们三人却被死死囚禁在原本仅供单人居住的狭小船舱内；昼夜皆有荷枪实弹的武装哨兵寸步不离把守在她们门前。
在这个圣诞节里，整艘船上没有显露出一丝一毫基督仁爱的痕迹。依旧是司空见惯的密探盯梢与严密监视，依旧是冷酷森严的纪律与惩戒。不过在公共大食堂的晚餐盘中，倒是在日常配给之外添了一份节日点缀：葡萄干面包与蔓越莓果酱。然而半数以上的餐桌却空空荡荡：绝大多数男同胞都瘫倒在自己的铺位上，饱受疾病煎熬。
*12月26日。*——大风大浪，更多的人“倒下”了。那位六英尺高的“大宝贝”反倒成了全船病得最重的一个。为了防止巨浪倒灌，底舱所有的通风舱口全被死死封死，甲板下方憋闷得令人窒息。在我们所在的隔间里挤着49名同伴；其余的人则分散在紧邻的另外两个隔间内。
随船军医请求我协助他进行每日的巡诊查房，充当他的随行翻译兼临时护理。弟兄们所患的大多是肠胃溃烂与剧烈腹泻，但也有风湿病、坐骨神经痛以及突发心脏病的病例。鲍里斯兄弟（Boris brothers）的病情极其危殆；年轻的约翰·伯克（John Birk）身体正急剧衰竭；另有相当一批人陷入了极其恶劣的状态。
*12月27日。*——那位来自波士顿的被驱逐者——曾经当过水手——声称“布福德号”在夜间两度改变了航向。“八成是正驶向葡萄牙海岸，”他推测道。私下风传我们可能会被直接转交给出卖给邓尼金白军。弟兄们心头笼罩着浓重的阴云。
世间的人类心理在底层深处皆有着惊人的相通之处。即便是在铁窗大牢中，我也曾屡屡目睹最深沉的苦难悲剧被一抹幽默的火花所点亮。尽管大家对最终的流放目的地怀揣着巨大的焦虑，但我们的舱房里依然不时回荡着阵阵爽朗的笑声与诙谐的打趣。同伴中有些机灵鬼索性将“布福德号”戏称为“谜之幽灵船”。
下午时分，伯克希尔通报我说，陆军上校想要召见我。上校的私人客舱虽然不大，却明亮干燥、温暖宜人，与我们那潮湿肮脏的下等统舱形成了云泥之别。上校单刀直入询问我：我们究竟“指望被送往俄国的哪一部分土地”。苏维埃红军控制的地区，那是理所当然的，我干脆利落地答道。

===Original===

He began a discussion of the Bolsheviki. The Socialists, he insisted, wanted to “take away the hard-earned wealth of the rich, and divide it among the lazy and the shiftless.” Everyone willing to work could succeed in the world, he assured me; at least America﻿—the freest country on earth﻿—gives all an equal opportunity.
I had to explain to him the A.B.C. of social science, pointing out that no wealth can be created except by labor; and that by complex juggling﻿—legal, financial, economic﻿—the producer is robbed of his product. The Colonel admitted defects and imperfections in our system﻿—even in “the best system of the world, the American.” But they are human failings; we need improvement, not revolution, he thought. He listened with unconcealed impatience when I spoke of the crime of punishing men for their opinions and the folly of deporting ideas. He believes “the government must protect its people,” and that “these foreign agitators have no business in America, anyhow.”
I saw the futility of discussing with a person of such infantile mentality, and closed the argument by inquiring the exact point of our destination. “Sailing under sealed orders,” was all the information the Colonel would vouchsafe.
*New Year’s Day, 1920.*﻿—We are getting friendly with the soldiers. They are selling us their extra clothing, shoes, and everything else they can lay their hands on. Our boys are discussing war, government, and Anarchism with the sentinels. Some of the latter are much interested, and they are noting down addresses in New York where they can get our literature. One of the soldiers﻿—Long Sam, they call him﻿—is especially outspoken against his superiors. He is “sore as hell,” he says. He was to be married on Christmas, but he got orders to report for duty on the *Buford*. “I’m no damn tin soldier like them Nationals” (National Guard), he says; “I’m sev’n years a reg’lar, an’ them’s the thanks I get. ’Stead of bein’ with me goil I’m in this floatin’ dump, between Hell an’ nowhere.”
We have organized a committee to assess every “possessing” member of our group for the benefit of the deportees that lack warm clothing. The men from Pittsburgh, Erie, and Madison had been shipped to Ellis Island in their working clothes. Many others had also been given no time to take their trunks along.

===Chinese===

他随即同我争辩起布尔什维克来。他执拗地咬定：社会主义者无非是妄图“掠夺富人辛勤挣来的家当财富，分给那些好吃懒做、游手好闲的下层无赖”。他信誓旦旦向我保证说，凡是愿意勤勉劳作的人，在这世上无一例外都能出人头地；至少美利坚——这片举世最自由的乐土——为所有人提供了绝对平等的机遇。
我不得不从头向他启蒙社会科学的基础ABC常识，正告他：世间的一切财富皆由劳动创造；然而通过纷繁复杂的法律、金融与经济魔术巧取豪夺，真正的劳动生产者却被硬生生剥夺了属于自己的劳动果实。上校勉强承认我们的现行体制中存在某些缺陷与瑕疵——即便是在“全天下最完美的体制，即美式制度”之中。但他认为这不过是人性固有的弱点；我们所需要的只是改良与修补，而不是什么翻天覆地的大革命。当我慷慨陈词痛斥因思想言论而给人们定罪的暴行、以及妄图将思想驱逐出境的愚蠢至极时，他脸上露出了毫不掩饰的烦躁与不耐烦。他深信“政府必须保护自己的子民”，并且叫嚣“这帮外来的煽动分子无论如何都不该赖在美国”。
我深知同一个思维如此幼稚平庸之人辩论完全是对牛弹琴、徒劳无益，便果断掐断了争论，转而直截了当询问我们航程的确切终点。“奉密令航行，”这便是上校唯一愿意施舍透露的冷冰冰答复。
*1920年元旦。*——我们同押解的士兵们越发打得火热。他们正私下将自己多余的军服被服、皮鞋以及任何能够顺手牵羊搞到的物件偷偷变卖给我们。我们的弟兄们常常同门口的哨兵热烈讨论起战争、国家政权以及无政府主义（Anarchism）。有几个当兵的听得津津有味、极感兴趣，甚至悄悄记下了纽约的几个秘密联络地址，好在回国后索取我们的宣传书刊。其中有一名被称为“长腿萨姆”的士兵，对他的长官们骂得尤为露骨刻薄。他说自己简直“憋了一肚子邪火”。他原本定在圣诞节迎娶新娘完婚，却冷不丁接到紧急军令，被强行抓差押送到“布福德号”上服役。“老子可不是国民警卫队那帮中看不中用的铁皮玩具兵，”他咬牙切齿道，“老子在正规军里辛辛苦苦干了整整七年，得到的‘犒赏’就是这副操行！老子没能搂着心爱的姑娘入洞房，反倒被塞进了这个漂浮在人间地狱与虚无荒海之间的破烂垃圾堆里！”
我们自发组织了一个物资互助委员会，动员全体被驱逐者中尚有“存货”的伙伴们慷慨解囊，全力资助那些缺乏御寒冬衣的赤贫难友。来自匹兹堡（Pittsburgh）、伊利（Erie）和麦迪逊（Madison）的弟兄们，当年是直接穿着单薄工装就被强行押上渡轮送往埃利斯岛的；许多其他人更是连收拾随身行李箱的半点空当都未曾得到。

===Original===

A large pile of the collected apparel﻿—suits, hats, shoes, winter underwear, hosiery, etc.﻿—lies in the center of our cabin, and the committee is distributing the things. There is much shouting, laughing, and joking. It’s our first attempt at practical communism. The crowd surrounding the committee passes upon the claims of each applicant and immediately acts upon its verdict. A vital sense of social justice is manifested.
*January 2, 1920.*﻿—In Bisay Bay. Rolling badly. The sailors say last night’s storm threw us out of our course. Some ship, apparently Japanese, was signaling for help. We ourselves were in such a plight that we could not aid.
At noon the Captain sent for me. The *Buford* is not a modern ship﻿—he spoke guardedly﻿—and we are in difficult waters. Bad time of the year, too; storm season. No particular danger, but it is always well to be prepared. He would assign twelve lifeboats in my charge, and I should instruct the men what to do should the contingency arise.
I have divided the 246 male deportees into a number of groups, putting at the head of each one of the older comrades. (The three women are assigned to the sailors’ boat.) We are to have several trial alarms to teach the men how to handle the life belts, take their place in line, and get without confusion to their respective boats. The first test, this afternoon, was a bit lame. Another trial, by surprise, is to take place soon.
*January 3.*﻿—Rumors that we are bound for Danzig. It is certain now we are making for the English Channel and expect to reach it tomorrow. We feel greatly relieved.
*January 4.*﻿—No channel. No land. Very bad night. The old tub has been dancing up and down like a rubber shoe thrown into the ocean by vacationists at Coney Island. Been busy all night with the sick.
Everyone except Bianki and myself is keeping to his bunk. Some are seriously ill. Bianki’s nephew, the young schoolboy, has lost his hearing. John Birk is very low. Novikov, former editor of the New York Anarchist weekly, *Golos Truda*, hasn’t touched food for days. In Ellis Island he spent most of his time in the hospital. He refused to accept bail as long as the others arrested with him remained in prison. He consented only when almost at the point of death, and then he was dragged to the boat to be deported.

===Chinese===

堆积如山的募捐衣物——西装、帽子、皮鞋、冬季贴身内衣、厚袜等——整整齐齐码放在我们底舱的中央，委员会正在统一调配分发。现场充斥着欢呼、大笑与风趣的玩笑。这是我们践行共产主义实际分享的第一场成功尝试。围拢在委员会四周的人群现场公议每一位申请者的实际需求，并当场裁决兑现。一种鲜活具体、充满生命力的社会正义感在人群中油然而生。
*1920年1月2日。*——航行在比斯开湾（Bisay Bay，即 Biscay Bay）。船体剧烈颠簸摇晃。水手们说昨夜的狂风巨浪将我们吹偏了既定航线。有一艘轮船——似乎是日本货轮——正闪烁着信号灯呼救；但我们自己已泥菩萨过河自身难保，根本无力施以援手。
正午时分，船长差人紧急召见我。“布福德号”并不是一艘现代化的坚固轮船——他言辞谨慎晦涩——而我们此刻正航行在险象环生的暗礁水域。加之眼下一年的时令极差，正是风暴肆虐的凶险季风期。倒也谈不上什么灭顶之灾，但有备无患总归是明智之举。他决定将12艘救生艇全权托付给我掌管，由我负责训导弟兄们在突发险情降临之际该如何应急逃生。
我将246名男性被驱逐者编组为数个互助救援小组，指派几名德高望重的老同志出任各组组长。（三名女性被指派乘坐水手专属的救生艇。）我们商定将举行数次突发模拟演习，教导大家如何穿戴救生圈、排队就位，并在慌乱中井然有序地撤离至各自对应的救生艇。今天下午举行的第一次演习显得颇为笨拙混乱。不久之后还将举行一次毫无预警的突击拉动演练。
*1月3日。*——私下传言说我们即将驶往但泽（Danzig）。如今已确凿无疑的是，我们正全速驶向英吉利海峡（English Channel），预计明日即可抵达。大伙儿心头的大石头终于落了地，感到了一阵难得的如释重负。
*1月4日。*——不见海峡踪影。不见陆地痕迹。昨夜经历了一场噩梦般的恐怖黑夜。这艘破旧的老浴盆在惊涛骇浪中上下疯狂颠簸抛掷，活像康尼岛（Coney Island）上度假游客随意扔进大西洋里的破旧胶鞋。我整整忙碌了一整夜，穿梭在各个铺位照料病患。
除比亚恩基（Bianki）与我之外，全员皆瘫倒在铺位上动弹不得。好几个人病情极其沉重。比亚恩基的小侄子——一个稚气未脱的年轻学童——在剧烈呕吐颠簸中彻底丧失了听力。约翰·伯克（John Birk）气若游丝、命悬一线。纽约无政府主义周刊《劳动之声》（*Golos Truda*）前任编辑诺维科夫（Novikov）已有数日水米未进。在埃利斯岛关押期间，他的绝大部分时光都在拘留所医院里度过。只要与他一同被捕的战友仍深陷囹圄，他便誓死不肯接受保释；直到他病入膏肓、几近濒死的一刻，他才勉强点头答应保释——然而就在这一刹那，他却被强行从病床上拖拽上船，强行驱逐出境。

===Original===

It is hard to be torn out of the soil one has rooted in for over thirty years, and to leave the labors of a lifetime behind. Yet I am glad: I face the future, not the past. Already in 1917, at the outbreak of the Revolution, I longed to go to Russia. Shatov, my close friend and comrade, was about to leave, and I hoped to join him. But the Mooney case and the needs of the antiwar movement kept me in the United States. Then came my arrest for opposing the world slaughter, and my two years’ imprisonment in Atlanta.
But soon I shall be in Russia. What joy to behold the Revolution with my own eyes, to become part of it, to aid the great people that are transforming the world!
*January 5.*﻿—Pilot boat! Great rejoicing! Sent wire to our friends in New York to allay the anxiety they must feel because of our mysterious disappearance.
*January 7.*﻿—We’re in the North Sea. Clear, quiet, cool. In the afternoon a bit rolling.
The singing of the boys reaches me from the deck. I hear the strong baritone of Alyosha, the *zapevalo*, who begins every stanza, the whole crowd joining in the chorus. Old Russian folk songs with their mournful refrain, dripping quiet resignation and the suffering of centuries. Songs palpitating with the frank hatred of the *bourzhooi* and the militancy of impending struggle. Church hymns with their crescendo recitative, paraphrased by revolutionary words. The soldiers and sailors stand about wrapt in the weird, heart-gripping melodies. Yesterday I heard our guard absentmindedly humming “Stenka Razin.”
We’ve gotten so friendly with our guards now that we do as we please below deck. It has become the established rule for soldiers and deportees never to appeal to the officers in the case of dispute. All such matters are referred to me, and my judgment is respected. Berkshire has repeatedly hinted his displeasure at the influence I have gained. He feels himself entirely ignored.
The sameness of the food is disgusting. The bread is stale and doughy. We have made several protests, and at last the chief steward agreed to my proposition to put two men of our group in charge of the bakery.
*January 8.*﻿—At anchor in the Kiel Canal. Leaks in the boiler﻿—repairs begun. The men are chafing﻿—the accident may cause much delay. We’re sick of the journey. Eighteen days at sea already.
Most of the deportees left their money and effects in the United States.

===Chinese===

硬生生被连根拔起离开自己扎根了30余年的土地，抛下毕生的奋斗心血，这无疑是撕心裂肺的剧痛。然而我心中却充满了欢欣：我昂首面对的是未来，而不是过去。早在1917年大革命爆发之际，我便日夜渴望奔赴俄国。我的密友兼亲密战友沙托夫（Shatov）当时正准备动身启程，我原本指望能与他结伴同行。然而穆尼冤案（Mooney case）的严峻斗争以及反战和平运动的紧迫需求，将我硬生生留在了美国。紧接着便是我因坚决抗议帝国主义世界大战屠杀而遭到逮捕入狱，在亚特兰大（Atlanta）联邦监狱里服刑熬过了2年铁窗生涯。
然而用不了多久，我便将踏上俄国的土地！能亲眼见证这伟大的大革命，能投身其中成为其血肉的一部分，能竭尽绵薄之力去协助这群正在重塑整个人类世界的伟大人民，这是何等不可思议的至高幸福！
*1月5日。*——引水船现身了！全船欢声雷动！我们火速向纽约的战友们拍发了一份越洋电报，以抚平他们因我们此前神秘人间蒸发而必定承受的无尽焦虑。
*1月7日。*——我们正航行在北海（North Sea）碧波之上。晴空万里，海面宁静，海风清冽。午后稍有微弱的轻晃摇荡。
弟兄们嘹亮的歌声从甲板上方遥遥传来。我辨认出了领唱者（*zapevalo*）阿廖沙（Alyosha）那苍劲雄浑的男中音——他率先起调领唱每一段诗节，随后满船人潮齐声合唱响应。那是一曲曲古老的俄罗斯民间歌谣，带着哀怨凄楚的副歌尾调，浸透着千百年来的默默隐忍与沉重苦难；那是涌动着对资产阶级老爷们（*bourzhooi*）刻骨仇恨、澎湃着即将到来的决死抗争烈焰的战阵战歌；那是用革命词句重新填词、伴随着如潮诵念层层推进的教堂圣歌。押解的士兵与水手们痴痴地呆立在四周，完全沉醉沉浸在这摄人心魄、怪诞而动人的旋律之中。昨天我甚至听见我们舱门前的持枪哨兵正情不自禁、心不在焉地轻声哼唱着《斯捷潘·拉辛》（“Stenka Razin”）。
如今我们同押解的士兵们处得极其融洽友善，以至于我们在下层底舱几乎可以随心所欲自由行动。如今在士兵与被驱逐者之间甚至形成了一条心照不宣的不成文行规：凡发生任何纠纷争端，绝不上报军官裁决，所有事端一律呈交由我评判公断，我的仲裁裁决受到全员的一致敬重。伯克希尔曾屡屡话里带刺地对我日益增长的威望与影响力表达不满，他深感自己这个钦差特使已被全员彻底视若无物。
千篇一律的伙食令人作呕。配发的面包既发硬酸败又黏糊夹生。在我们发起了数次严正抗议之后，事务长终于点头接纳了我的提议：由我们小组选派两名同志全面接管把手船上的面包烘焙房。
*1月8日。*——停泊抛锚在基尔运河（Kiel Canal）。船体锅炉严重渗漏——抢修工程已经展开。弟兄们焦躁不安、怨声载道——这场机械事故恐怕会导致严重的漫长延误。大家对这场无休止的漂泊早已厌倦透顶。出海至今整整18天了。
绝大多数被驱逐者将自己的全部积蓄与家当家底遗落在了美国本土。

===Original===

Many have bank deposits which they could not draw because of the suddenness of their arrest and deportation. I have prepared a list of the funds and things owned by our group. The total amounts to over $45,000. I turned the list over to Berkshire today, who promised to “attend to the matter in Washington.” But few of the boys have any hope of ever receiving their clothes or money.
*January 9.*﻿—Much excitement. For two days we’ve had no fresh air. Orders are not to permit us on deck as long as we remain in German waters. They are afraid we might communicate with the outside or “jump overboard,” as Berkshire jocosely said. I told him the only place we want to jump off at is Soviet Russia.
I sent word to the Colonel that the men demand daily exercise. The atmosphere in the steerage is beastly: the hatches are shut, and we are almost suffocating. Berkshire resented the manner in which I addressed “the Chief.”
“The Colonel is the highest authority on the *Buford*,” he shouted.
The group of deportees about me grinned in his face. “Berkman is the only ‘Colonel’ we recognize,” they laughed.
I told Berkshire to repeat our message to the Colonel: we insist on fresh air; in case of refusal we will go on deck by force. The men are prepared to carry out their threat.
In the afternoon the hatches were opened, and we were permitted on deck. We noticed that the destroyer *Ballard*, U.S.S. 267, is alongside of us.
*January 10.*﻿—We are in the Bay, opposite the City of Kiel. On either side of us stretches of land with beautiful villas and clean-looking farmhouses, the stillness of death over all. Five years of carnage have left their indelible mark. The blood has been washed away, but the hand of destruction is still visible.
The German Quartermaster came on board. “You are surprised at the stillness?” he said. “We are being starved to death by the kindly powers that set out to make the world safe for democracy. We are not yet dead, but we are so faint we cannot cry out.”
*January 11.*﻿—We got in touch with the German sailors of the *Wasserversorger*, which brought us fresh water. Our bakers gave them food. Through the portholes we fired bread balls, oranges, and potatoes onto the boat. Her crew picked up the things, and read the notes hidden in them. One of the messages was a “Greeting of the American Political Deportees to the Proletariat of Germany.”
*Later.

===Chinese===

许多同伴在银行里存有巨额存款，却因搜捕与驱逐行动的极端猝然，根本来不及支取分文。我详尽统计并编撰了一份我们整个群体所拥有的资金财产清单。总金额折合超过45,000美元（4.5万美元）。今天我将这份财产清单正式移交给了伯克希尔，他信誓旦旦担保将在“华盛顿上报并妥善处置此事”。然而同伴中极少有人对能否追回自己的血汗钱款或衣物抱有任何奢望。
*1月9日。*——激烈的震荡与抗争。我们已经整整两天呼吸不到半口新鲜空气了。军方下达死命令：只要轮船仍滞留在德国水域内，便严禁我们踏上甲板半步。他们深恐我们会同外界通风报信，抑或是“跳海潜逃”——正如伯克希尔阴阳怪气调侃的那样。我当面正告他：我们唯一想要纵身跃入的土地，只有苏维埃俄国。
我向陆军上校带话通报：同胞们强烈要求每日的甲板放风散步。统舱底层的空气恶浊沉闷到了令人发指的地步：通风舱口被严丝合缝死死封闭，大家几乎要活活窒息而死。伯克希尔对我指涉“最高长官”的强硬措辞极其恼火暴怒。
“上校是‘布福德号’上的最高独裁统帅！”他声嘶力竭咆哮道。
簇拥在我身后的同伴们冲着他的嘴脸哄堂嗤笑。“伯克曼才是我们唯一承认的‘上校’！”大家哄笑着嘲讽道。
我勒令伯克希尔将我们的最后通牒原原本本转达给上校：我们誓死捍卫呼吸新鲜空气的人权；倘若遭到无理拒绝，我们将不惜动用武力强行冲上甲板！弟兄们已全面做好了付诸行动的流血准备。
到了下午，舱口终于被迫开启，我们获准踏上甲板放风。我们敏锐地瞥见：美军“巴拉德号”驱逐舰（USS Ballard, 舷号267）正全副武装死死并排贴行押送在我们身侧。
*1月10日。*——我们正锚泊在基尔港湾（City of Kiel）正对面的洋面上。在海湾两侧纵深展开着绵延秀丽的庄园别墅与粉刷齐整的农舍，然而漫山遍野却笼罩着一片死一般的死寂荒凉。长达5年的世界大战浩劫在欧洲大地上烙下了不可磨灭的焦黑疮痍。鲜血虽已冲刷殆尽，然而摧残破坏的毁灭魔爪却依然触目惊心。
一名德国军需官登船造访。“你们对这里的死寂感到诧异吗？”他满目凄凉地倾诉道，“那些宣称要‘让世界为了民主而安全’的所谓仁慈列强，正用严酷封锁将我们全民族活活饿死！我们如今尚未咽气，但我们早已饥饿虚弱得连喊叫的力气都没有了。”
*1月11日。*——我们同前来为我船补充淡水的“供水号”（*Wasserversorger*）上的德国水手们建立了秘密联络。我们的随船面包师将大批新鲜面包暗中塞给他们。透过船舷舷窗，我们将成团的面包球、橘子和土豆如雨点般精准抛向德国驳船甲板。对方水手心领神会拾起物资，并迫不及待阅读夹藏在其中的秘密纸条。其中一张纸条赫然写着：“美国政治被驱逐者致德国无产阶级的崇高革命敬礼！”
*稍后。

===Original===

*﻿—Most of the convoy and several officers are drunk. The sailors got schnapps from the Germans and have been selling it on board. “Long Sam” went “gunning” for his first lieutenant. Several soldiers called me for a secret confab and proposed that I take charge of the ship. They would arrest their officers, turn the boat over to me, and come with us to Russia. “Damn the United States Army, we’re with the Bolsheviks!” they shouted.
*January 12.*﻿—At noon Berkshire called me to the Colonel. Both looked nervous and worried. The Colonel regarded me with distrust and hatred. He had been informed that I was “inciting mutiny” among his men. “You’ve been fraternizing with the soldiers and weakening the discipline,” he said. He declared that guns, ammunition, and officers’ apparel were missing, and instructed Berkshire to have the effects of the deportees searched. I protested: the men would not submit to such an indignity.
Returning below deck I learned that several soldiers were under arrest for insubordination and drunkenness. The guards have been doubled at our door, and the convoy officers are much in evidence.
We passed the day in anxious suspense, but no attempt to search us was made.
*January 13.*﻿—We got under way again at 1:40 p.m. Making for the Baltic. I wonder how this leaky boat will navigate the North Sea and fight the ice there. The boys, including the soldiers, are very nervous: we are on a dangerous road, full of war mines.
Two of the ship’s crew are in the “cooler” for having overstayed their shore-leave. I withdrew our men from the bakery in protest against the arrest of the sailors and soldiers.
*January 15.*﻿—The 25th day at sea. We all feel worn out, tired of the long journey. In constant fear lest we strike some mine.
Our course has been changed again. Berkshire hinted this morning that conditions at Libau will not permit our going there. I gathered from his talk that the United States Government has so far failed to make arrangements for our landing in any country.
Sailors have overheard the Colonel, the Captain, and Berkshire discussing our going to Finland. The scheme is to send me, in company with Berkshire, with a white flag, 70 miles inland, to come to some understanding with the authorities about our landing. If we are successful, I am to remain there, while Berkshire is to return to our people.
The deportees are opposed to the plan.

===Chinese===

*——押解护航队的大多数士兵和几名军官都喝得酩酊大醉。水手们从德国人那里私自走私搞到了烈性施纳普斯酒（schnapps），并在船上大肆兜售。“长腿萨姆”端着枪满船追杀他的顶头上尉一等副官。几名当兵的甚至将我拉入底舱进行秘密密谋，正式提议由我全面接管整艘战船！他们声称愿意当场将所有军官逮捕收押，将整艘巨轮全权交由我指挥，并随同我们一道奔赴俄国。“去他妈的美国军队，我们要跟着布尔什维克干！”他们振臂怒吼道。
*1月12日。*——正午时分，伯克希尔将我紧急召见至上校面前。两人皆显得神色极其紧张仓皇。上校用充满了狐疑与仇视的目光死死打量着我。他获悉我正在他麾下的士兵中“煽动兵变”。“你一直在同当兵的称兄道弟、结伙联欢，公然瓦解军纪！”他声色俱厉地指控道。他宣称军方的枪支、弹药以及军官的私人物件大量失窃下落不明，并严令伯克希尔立刻对全体被驱逐者的随身行李展开翻箱倒柜的全面大搜查。我当场提出最严正的抗议：同胞们绝不会屈从顺受这种奇耻大辱。
折返回底舱后，我得知已有数名士兵因酗酒滋事与抗命不尊遭到拘捕关押。我们舱门前的戒备岗哨被加派了一倍，护航军官们频频在过道上拔枪戒备巡弋。
我们在焦灼难熬的紧绷悬念中熬过了漫长的一天，但军方终究未敢贸然强行搜查我们的铺位。
*1月13日。*——下午1点40分（1:40），巨轮再度起锚拔锚航行。全速驶向波罗的海（Baltic）。我实在无法想象这艘漏洞百出的破船将如何顶着惊涛骇浪在冰海中搏杀破冰。弟兄们——包括那些士兵——个个神经高度紧绷：我们正航行在危机四伏的凶险航线上，海面上布满了战争残留的水雷水障。
两名船员因擅自逾期未归、超假滞留岸上而被关进了底舱“禁闭室（cooler）”。我当即下令撤回在面包房帮工的全体同志，以严正抗议军方对水手与士兵的残暴拘禁。
*1月15日。*——出海漫游的第25天。我们所有人都感到筋疲力尽、精疲力竭，对这场漫无休止的长途漂泊厌倦到了极点。大伙儿时刻生活在触雷粉身碎骨的无尽梦魇中。
我们的航线再度被秘密更改。伯克希尔今早话里有话地暗示说：利鲍（Libau）当前的动荡局势绝不容许我们在彼处靠岸登陆。从他的闪烁言辞中我敏锐断定：美国政府迄今为止根本未能在任何欧洲国家为我们落实达成合法的靠岸登陆协议！
水手们无意中偷听到了上校、船长与伯克希尔在密室里商议密谋将我们送往芬兰（Finland）。他们的全盘算盘是：派我本人在伯克希尔的随同押解下，手持白旗深入芬兰内陆70英里，同当地芬兰军政当局秘密谈判以敲定我们的登陆条件。倘若谈判达成，我将作为“人质”留滞在彼处，而伯克希尔则折返船上接运全体同伴。
全体被驱逐者对此项阴谋坚决表示一致抵制与反对！

===Original===

Finland is dangerous for us﻿—the Mannheimer reaction is slaughtering the Finnish revolutionists. The men refuse to let me go. “We’ll all go together, or no one shall,” they declare.
*Evening.*﻿—This afternoon two American press correspondents boarded us, near Hango, and the Colonel gave them permission to interview me. American Consul from Helsingfors is also on board with his secretary. He is trying to get power of attorney from the deportees to collect their money in the United States. Many of the boys are transferring their bank accounts to relatives.
*January 16.*﻿—4:25 p.m. Reached Hango, Finland. Helsingfors inaccessible, they say.
*January 17.*﻿—Landed, 2 p.m. Sent radios to Chicherin (Moscow) and Shatov (Petrograd) notifying them of the arrival of the first group of political deportees from America.
We are to travel in sealed cars through Finland to the Russian border. The Captain of the *Buford* allowed us three days’ rations for the journey.
The leave-taking of the crew and soldiers touched me deeply. Many of them have become attached to us, and they have “treated us white,” to use their own expression. They made us promise to write them from Russia.
*January 18.*﻿—Crossing snow-clad country. Cars cold, unheated. The compartments are locked, with Finnish guards on every platform. Even within are the White soldiers, at every door. Silent, forbidding looking. They refuse to enter into conversation.
*2 p.m.*﻿—In Viborg. We are practically without food. The Finnish soldiers have stolen most of the products given us by the *Buford*.
Through our car windows we noticed a Finnish worker standing on the platform and surreptitiously signaling us with a miniature red flag. We waved recognition. Half an hour later the doors of our car were unlocked, and the workman entered to “fix the lights,” as he announced. “Fearful reaction here,” he whispered; “White terror against the workers. We need the help of revolutionary Russia.”
Wired again today to Chicherin and Shatov, urging haste in sending a committee to meet the deportees on the Russian border.
*January 19.*﻿—In Teryoki, near the border. No reply from Russia yet. The Finnish military authorities demand we should cross the frontier at once. We have refused because the Russian border guard, not informed of our identity, might regard us as invading Finns and shoot, thus giving Finland a pretext for war.

===Chinese===

芬兰对我们而言极其凶险——曼纳海姆（Mannerheim）反动白卫军正残酷血洗屠杀芬兰革命群众。同伴们坚决拒绝放我孤身涉险离船。“我们要去全伙一起去，否则谁也休想走！”大家异口同声誓死宣告。
*傍晚。*——今天下午，在靠近汉科（Hango）的海面上，两名美国战地记者登船采访，上校特批准许他们对我进行现场专访。驻赫尔辛福斯（Helsingfors）的美国领事亦偕同其秘书登船造访。他正企图从被驱逐者手中索取全权委托书，代为在美国本土索讨被扣押的私人财产。许多年轻同胞正将各自的银行存单权益紧急背书转让给留美的亲眷。
*1月16日。*——下午4点25分（4:25），巨轮终于抵达芬兰汉科。据说赫尔辛福斯港因冰封而无法通航靠泊。
*1月17日。*——下午2点整，全体被驱逐者正式踏上陆地登陆。我当即向莫斯科的契切林（Chicherin）以及彼得格勒的沙托夫（Shatov）拍发了加急无线电报，郑重通报自美利坚合众国驱逐出境的第一批政治流放者大队已平安抵境。
我们将换乘全封闭的密封列车横穿芬兰全境开赴俄国边境。“布福德号”船长慷慨特批配发给我们为期三天的行军口粮。
同船员与当兵的依依惜别之情深深触动了我的心扉。他们当中的许多人已同我们结下了深厚的战友情谊，用他们自己的大白话来说，大家“相处得极其正派体面（treated us white）”。在离别前夕，他们紧紧拉着我们的手，千叮万嘱要求我们抵达俄国后务必给他们写信报平安。
*1月18日。*——列车正风驰电掣穿行在白雪皑皑的荒原冻土之上。车厢内冰冷刺骨，毫无半分取暖供热设施。各个隔间全被铁锁反锁封死，每一个列车车厢连接处皆荷枪实弹站立着芬兰白卫军哨兵。甚至在车厢内部的每一道隔门前，都有面色冷峻严酷的白卫军士兵寸步不离持枪把守。他们面容阴森可怖、一言不发，严词拒绝同我们展开任何交谈。
*下午2点。*——抵达维堡（Viborg）。我们手头的口粮几乎已告罄断绝——芬兰白军士兵在途中暗中盗窃洗劫了“布福德号”此前馈赠给我们的大部分食品补给。
透过被严密钉死的车窗玻璃，我们敏锐瞥见一名芬兰工人正伫立在站台角落，用一面微型红旗鬼鬼祟祟地向我们连连打着秘密暗号。我们贴着玻璃挥手致意。半小时后，我们车厢的铁锁被悄然打开，那名工人以“检修车厢照明灯具”为掩护迅速钻入车厢。“这里的反动势力残暴到了极点，”他压低嗓门急促耳语道，“白军对工人们展开了令人发指的白色恐怖残杀。我们迫切渴望革命俄国的武装驰援！”
今天再度向契切林与沙托夫拍发加急电报，十万火急敦促他们火速派出官方接应代表团，赶赴俄国边境迎候全体流放同胞。
*1月19日。*——抵达靠近边境的泰里约基（Teryoki）。然而俄国方面迄今仍杳无音信、未予回电。芬兰军方当局气急败坏蛮横勒令我们必须立即徒步越过边境界河。我们断然拒绝盲目越境，因为在毫无事先通报身份的情况下，苏俄边防部队极可能将我们误认作发起越境突袭的芬兰白军入侵者而悍然开火射击，从而沦为芬兰当局蓄意挑起全面边境战争的借口把柄。

===Original===

A sort of armed truce exists now between the two countries, and feeling is very tense.
*Noon.*﻿—The Finns are worried about our continued presence. We refuse to leave the train.
Representatives of the Finnish Foreign Office agreed to permit a Committee of the Deportees to go to the Russian frontier to explain the situation to the Soviet outpost. Our party selected three persons, but the Finnish military would consent only to one.
In company with a Finnish Officer, soldier, and interpreter, and trailed by several correspondents (among them, needless to say, an American press man) I advanced to the border, walking in deep snow through the sparse forest west of the destroyed frontier railroad bridge. Not without trepidation did we trudge through those white woods, fearing possible attack from the one side or the other.
After a quarter of an hour we reached the border. Opposite us were drawn up the Bolshevik guards﻿—tall, strapping fellows in strange fur attire, with a black-bearded officer in charge.
“*Tovarish!*” I shouted in Russian across the frozen creek, “permit speech with you.”
The officer motioned me to step nearer, his soldiers standing back as I approached. In a few words I explained the situation to him and our predicament at Chicherin’s failure to reply to our repeated radios. He listened imperturbably, then said: “The Soviet Committee has just arrived.”
It was happy news. The Finnish authorities consented to permit the Russian Committee to come on Finnish soil as far as the train, to meet the deportees. Zorin and Feinberg, representing the Soviet Government, and Mme. Andreyeva, Gorky’s wife, who came with them unofficially, accompanied us to the railroad station.
“Kolchak has been arrested and his White Army broken up,” Zorin announced, and the deportees greeted the news with enthusiastic shouts and hurrahs. Presently arrangements were completed to transport the men and their luggage to the other side, and at last we crossed the border of revolutionary Russia.

===Chinese===

目前芬俄两国之间维持着一种极其脆弱的武装休战态势，空气中的紧张杀气几乎令人窒息。
*正午。*——芬兰当局对我们久久滞留境内感到了极度的恐慌与焦虑。而我们则抱定决心誓死拒绝跨下列车半步。
芬兰外交部代表最终点头妥协，同意容许被驱逐者代表委员会前往俄国边境前沿，向苏维埃前沿哨卡当面阐明危急实情。我们原本推选出了三名代表，然而芬兰军方却蛮横坚持只容许一人只身前往。
在一名芬兰军官、一名持枪士兵与一名随军翻译的押送下，身后尾随着数名战地随军记者（其中毫无疑问少不了一名美国大报记者），我大步迈向边境；我们在没膝的深雪中穿行跋涉，穿过被炸毁的边境铁路桥以西的稀疏松林。踩着厚厚的白雪穿行在银装素裹的林海之间，大家心头无不充满了心惊肉跳的惶恐与戒备，深恐从任何一侧突然射来致命的冷枪冷弹。
艰难跋涉了一刻钟（15分钟）后，我们终于抵达了边境线。在对岸的冰面上，整齐列阵伫立着一排布尔什维克边防红军卫士——个个身材魁梧高大，身披奇异厚重的毛皮大氅，由一名蓄着浓密黑胡须的指挥军官带队把守。
“同志（*Tovarish*）！”我跨过封冻的冰河，用流利的俄语向对岸放声高呼，“请允许我同您紧急交涉！”
军官庄严打了个手势示意我踏冰向前靠近，当兵的在我逼近之际端枪退后警戒。我言简意赅在短短数语内向他挑明了当前的危急局势，以及因契切林同志迟迟未能回复我方连环电报而导致的险恶僵局。军官神情自若、沉着冷静地倾听着，随后斩钉截铁正告道：“苏维埃官方接应代表团刚刚抵达到达前线！”
这真是一道喜从天降的特大喜讯！芬兰当局勉强点头应允苏维埃代表团踏上芬兰领土，一路乘车推进至列车驻地当面迎候全体被驱逐者。代表苏维埃工农政府全权前来的佐林（Zorin）与芬伯格（Feinberg），以及以非官方私人身份随同前来的高尔基夫人安德列耶娃（Mme. Andreyeva），陪同我们一路返回火车站台。
“高尔察克（Kolchak）已被工农红军彻底逮捕归案，其麾下的反动白卫大军已被悉数歼灭瓦解！”佐林当众高声宣布这一惊天捷报，站台上被驱逐的同胞们瞬间爆发出排山倒海般响彻云霄的狂热欢呼与“乌拉”声浪。不多时，人员与大批行李过境的驳运交接工作全盘安排停当；终于，在历史的这一刻，我们堂堂正正跨过了革命俄国的神圣边境大门！

## II On Soviet Soil / II 踏上苏维埃土地
===Original===

*January 20, 1920.*﻿—Late in the afternoon yesterday we touched the soil of Soviet Russia.
Driven out from the United States like criminals, we were received at Beloostrov with open arms. The revolutionary hymn, played by the military Red Band, greeted us as we crossed the frontier. The hurrahs of the red-capped soldiers, mixed with the cheers of the deportees, echoed through the woods, rolling into the distance like a challenge of joy and defiance. With bared head I stood in the presence of the visible symbols of the Revolution Triumphant.
A feeling of solemnity, of awe overwhelmed me. Thus my pious old forefathers must have felt on first entering the Holy of Holies. A strong desire was upon me to kneel down and kiss the ground﻿—the ground consecrated by the lifeblood of generations of suffering and martyrdom, consecrated anew by the revolutionists of my own day. Never before, not even at the first caress of freedom on that glorious May day, 1906﻿—after fourteen years in the Pennsylvania prison﻿—had I been stirred so profoundly. I longed to embrace humanity, to lay my heart at its feet, to give my life a thousand times to the service of the Social Revolution.
It was the most sublime day of my life.
---
At Beloostrov a mass meeting was held to welcome us. The large hall was filled with soldiers and peasants come to greet their comrades from America. They looked at us with large, wondering eyes, and asked many strange questions. “Are the workers starving in America?﻿—Is the revolution about to break out? How soon shall we get help for Russia?”
The crowded place was heavy with the human smell and the fumes of tobacco. There was much pushing and jostling, and loud shouting in rough border speech. Darkness had fallen, but the hall remained unlit. I felt a peculiar sensation in being swayed here and there by the noisy human billows, without being able to distinguish any faces. Then the voices and the motion ceased. My eyes turned toward the platform. It was lit by a few tallow candles, and in their dim light I could make out the figures of several women clad in black. They looked like nuns just out of the cloister, their countenances severe, forbidding. Then one of them stepped to the edge of the platform.
“*Tovarishi*,” she began, and the significant word vibrated through my whole being with the intensity of the speaker’s ardor.

===Chinese===

*1920年1月20日。*——昨天傍晚时分，我们的双脚终于真真切切踏上了苏维埃俄国的土地！
我们像罪犯一般被合众国暴力驱逐出境，而在别洛奥斯特罗夫（Beloostrov），我们却迎来了张开双臂的最热烈拥抱。当我们跨过边境线的那一刻，工农红军军乐队奏响的雄壮革命乐章迎面扑来。头戴红色军帽的苏维埃红军战士们呼啸的“乌拉”声浪，与被驱逐者们的狂热欢呼交织在一起，在莽莽林海雪原间激荡回响，宛如一道饱含着狂喜与蔑视旧世界的战书，滚滚涌向地平线的远方。我脱下帽子肃立在天地之间，仰望着眼前胜利革命的具象图腾。
一种难以言表的庄严与敬畏之情瞬间将我彻底淹没。我那虔诚的古老先祖们在平生头一遭迈入至圣所时，心中所感受到的想必正是这般神圣的战栗。一种无法遏制的炽烈渴望涌上心头，驱使着我真想当场双膝跪倒、亲吻脚下的热土——这片由世世代代殉道者的无尽苦难与生命鲜血所祝圣的神圣土地，如今又被我们这一代当代的革命先驱用热血再次祝圣。平生之中，哪怕是在1906年那个阳光明媚、光辉璀璨的五月天——在宾夕法尼亚大牢里熬过了整整14年残酷铁窗折磨后重获自由的最初抚慰——我也从未被这般深沉剧烈地震撼过。我渴望张开双臂拥抱全人类，渴望将自己的心赤诚奉献在人类的脚下，渴望将自己的生命千百次奉献给伟大的社会革命事业！
这是我平生所经历过的最崇高神圣的一天！
---
在别洛奥斯特罗夫，当地为欢迎我们的到来举行了一场盛大的群众集会。宽敞的大厅里挤满了从四面八方赶来迎候美洲阶级兄弟的红军战士与贫苦农夫。他们睁着大大的、写满惊奇探询的淳朴眼眸凝望着我们，迫不及待抛出了许多新奇的问题：“美洲的工人们也在挨饿受冻吗？——那里的革命马上就要爆发了吗？俄国什么时候才能迎来全世界的援手？”
水泄不通的集会大厅里弥漫着浓烈的体味汗酸与劣质烟草烟气。人群在粗犷的边境方言呼喊声中拥挤推搡、摩肩接踵。夜幕早已低垂，然而大厅里却并未点亮电灯。置身在嘈杂翻滚的人潮波浪中被推来搡去、却又在昏暗中看不清身旁任何一张面孔，这种体验带给我一种异样奇妙的震撼。随后，喧嚣的嘈杂与涌动的浪潮逐渐平息下来。我的视线投向了前方主席台。台上点着几根昏黄微弱的动物油蜡烛，借着微弱摇曳的烛光，我隐约辨认出几位身着一袭黑衣的妇女身影。她们看起来宛如刚刚步出修道院深处的修女，面容冷峻、庄严肃穆。紧接着，其中一位黑衣女性迈步走到了主席台边缘。
“同志们（*Tovarishi*），”她开口发表演说，这个承载着无尽重托的崇高字眼，伴随着演说者满腔炽热的烈焰，瞬间贯穿震颤了我的整个灵魂！

===Original===

She spoke passionately, vehemently, with a note of bitter defiance at the antagonistic world at large. She told of the high heroism of the revolutionary people, of their sacrifices and struggles, of the great work still to be done in Russia. She castigated the crimes of counterrevolutionists, the Allied invasion and murderous blockade. In fiery words she forecast the approach of the great world revolution, which is to destroy capitalism and the bourgeoisie throughout Europe and America, as Russia has done, and give the earth and the fullness thereof into the hands of the international proletariat.
Tumultuously the audience applauded. I felt the atmosphere charged with the spirit of revolutionary struggle, symbolic of the titanic war of two worlds﻿—the new breaking violent path for itself amid the confusion and chaos of conflicting passions. I was conscious of a world in the making, of the all-uprooting Social Revolution in action, and myself in the midst of it.
Zorin followed the woman in black, welcoming the arrivals in the name of Soviet Russia, and bespeaking their cooperation in the work of the Revolution. Then several of the deportees appeared on the rostrum. They were deeply moved by the wonderful reception, they said, and filled with admiration for the great Russian people, the first to throw off the yoke of capitalism and establish liberty and brotherhood upon the earth.
I was stirred to the depths of my being, too profoundly for words. Presently I became aware of people nudging me and whispering, “Speak, Berkman, speak! Answer him!” I had become absorbed in my emotion and did not listen to the man on the platform. I looked up. Bianki was speaking, the young Russian of Italian descent. I stood aghast as his words slowly carried comprehension to my mind. “We Anarchists,” he was saying, “are willing to work with the Bolsheviki if they will treat us right. But I warn you that we won’t stand for suppression. If you attempt it, it will mean war between us.”
I jumped on the platform. “Let not this great hour be debased by unworthy thoughts,” I cried. “From now on we are all one﻿—one in the sacred work of the Revolution, one in its defense, one in our common aim for the freedom and welfare of the people. Socialists or Anarchists﻿—our theoretical differences are left behind.

===Chinese===

她的演说慷慨激昂、铿锵有力，字里行间涌动着对外部敌对反动世界的决绝蔑视。她深情赞颂了革命人民惊天地泣鬼神的崇高英雄气概，倾诉了他们的壮烈牺牲与浴血苦斗，并阐明了在满目疮痍的俄国大地上尚待推进的宏伟事业。她以雷霆万钧之势痛斥了反革命分子的滔天罪行、协约国武装干涉者的强盗行径以及杀人不见血的灭绝性海上封锁。她用烈火般的词句预言了世界大革命的骤然降临——这场席卷全球的伟大革命必将像俄国人民所做的那样，在整个欧洲与美洲彻底埋葬资本主义与资产阶级，将整个大地及其所蕴藏的一切丰饶宝藏，完完全全交到国际无产阶级的铁拳之中！
台下的人群爆发出排山倒海般雷鸣不绝的狂热掌声。我深切感知到整座大厅的空气中都充斥着革命斗争的炽烈电荷，这正是新旧两个世界殊死对决的宏伟象征——在这场撕裂性的冲突与混沌风暴中，崭新的新世界正以不可阻挡之势为自己硬生生杀出一条铁血道路。我无比清醒地意识到：一个崭新的世界正在熔炉中诞生，一场摧枯拉朽、连根拔除一切旧势力的社会大革命正在波澜壮阔地展开，而我本人正屹立在这场风暴的正中央！
佐林（Zorin）紧随这位黑衣女战士登台演说，他代表苏维埃俄国向远道而来的同志们致以最崇高的兄弟敬礼，并深切恳请大家齐心协力投身于大革命的建设伟业之中。随后，数名被驱逐者代表相继登上了讲台。他们动情地表示：面对如此盛大隆重的迎候场面，他们深受感动、热泪盈眶；对率先推翻资本主义枷锁、在大地之上开创自由与博爱新纪元的伟大俄国人民，心中怀着无尽的由衷敬仰。
我整个人被震撼到了灵魂的最深处，万千感慨哽咽在喉，非言语所能形容。就在这时，我发觉身旁的同伴正急促推搡着我的臂膀，贴着我的耳朵焦急催促道：“快讲两句，伯克曼，快上去发言！快去驳倒他！”方才我完全沉浸在自己的澎湃心绪中，根本未曾留意台上究竟是何人在说话。我猛然抬头凝神看去：此时站在讲台上的竟然是比亚恩基（Bianki）——那位兼具俄罗斯与意大利血统的年轻战友。当我终于听真切他口中吐出的词句时，我顿时惊得目瞪口呆。“我们无政府主义者，”他正对着台下言辞激烈地高呼道，“只要布尔什维克能以公正平等的态度对待我们，我们非常乐意与他们携手共进。但我在此严正警告诸位：我们绝不容许任何压迫与剥夺！倘若你们胆敢动手弹压我们，那就意味着我们双方之间的彻底开战！”
我腾地一跃冲上了讲台！“绝不能让这神圣伟大的历史时刻，被狭隘卑劣的猜忌与杂念所玷污！”我向全场振臂高呼，“从此时时刻起，我们全员皆为一个不可分割的战斗整体——在革命的神圣伟业中万众一心，在保卫革命的钢铁阵线上一体同心，在为了人民的自由与福祉的共同宏愿下心心相印！无论是以往标榜社会党人还是无政府主义者——我们昔日的一切理论分歧早已被历史彻底甩在了身后！

===Original===

We are all revolutionists now, and shoulder to shoulder we’ll stand, together to fight and to work for the liberating Revolution. Comrades, heroes of the great revolutionary struggles of Russia, in the name of the American deportees I greet you. In their name I say to you: We’ve come to learn, not to teach. To learn and to help!”
The deportees applauded, other speeches followed, and soon the unpleasant Bianki incident was forgotten. Amid great enthusiasm the meeting closed late in the evening, the whole audience joining in the singing of “The Internationale.”
On the way to the station, where a train was waiting to take us to Petrograd, a large box of American crackers fell off the sleigh. The accompanying soldiers hungrily pounced upon it, but when told that the provisions were for the children of Petrograd, they immediately returned the box to us. “Quite right,” they said, “the little ones need it most.”
Another ovation awaited us in Petrograd, followed by a demonstration to the Tauride Palace and a large meeting. Then we marched to the Smolny, where the deportees were quartered for the night.

===Chinese===

如今我们全员都是誓死捍卫真理的革命战士，我们将肩并肩紧密挽臂伫立在一起，为了争取解放的社会大革命共同流血战斗、共同挥汗劳作！同志们，在俄国伟大的革命风暴中浴血搏杀的英雄们，我谨代表全体自美利坚被驱逐出境的革命流放者，向你们致以最崇高的阶级敬礼！我谨以他们的崇高名义向你们庄严宣告：我们回到祖国是来虚心求教学习的，绝不是来指手画脚当先生的；我们是来虚心学习、并倾尽全力施以援手的！”
被驱逐的同胞们爆发出雷鸣般的喝彩掌声，其他同志随后相继登台发言，方才比亚恩基引发的那场令人尴尬的不快风波很快便被全场的狂热所冲刷淡忘。在极其高涨澎湃的革命激情中，集会一直持续至深夜才宣告闭幕，全场数千名军民手挽着手，齐声高唱起气壮山河的《国际歌》（“The Internationale”）。
在步行奔赴火车站台——那里早已停靠好了一列准备接运我们前往彼得格勒的专列——的途中，一整大箱美制高档饼干不慎从雪橇滑落下来。身旁随行护送的红军战士们腹中饥肠辘辘，本能地扑上前去围住木箱；然而当他们获悉这批干粮是专程运往彼得格勒赈济当地饥饿儿童时，战士们毫不迟疑、立刻将整箱饼干原封不动抬还给了我们。“完全正确，”战士们平静而坚定地说道，“城里挨饿的孩子们比我们更需要它。”
在彼得格勒，另一场更为盛大壮烈的欢迎狂澜在等待着我们：队伍随即举行了浩浩荡荡开赴塔夫利宫（Tauride Palace）的盛大游行示威，并在彼处举行了万人集会。随后我们全副武装整队开进斯莫尔尼宫（Smolny），全体被驱逐者在彼处安顿下榻宿夜。

## III In Petrograd / III 在彼得格勒
===Original===

*January 21, 1920.*﻿—The bright winter sun shines upon the broad white bosom of the Neva. Stately buildings on either side of the river, with the Admiralty rearing its slender peak on high, foppishly graceful. Majestic edifices as far as the eye can reach, the Winter Palace towering in their midst in cold tranquillity. The brass rider on the trembling steed is poised on the rough Finnish rock,1 about to leap over the tall spire of the Petropavlovskaya guarding the city of his dream.
Familiar sight of my youth passed in the Tsar’s capital. But gone are the gilded glory of the past, the royal splendor, the gay banquets of nobles, and the iron columns of the slavish military marching to the thunder of drums. The hand of Revolution has turned the city of luxurious idleness into the home of labor. The spirit of revolt has changed even the names of the streets. The Nevsky, immortalized by Gogol, Pushkin, and Dostoyevsky, has become the Prospect of October 25th; the square in front of the Winter Palace is now named in honor of Uritsky; the Kamenovstrovsky is called the Red Dawn. At the Duma the heroic bust of Lassale faces the passersby as the symbol of the New Day; on the Konoguardeisky Boulevard stands the statue of Volodarsky, arm outstretched, addressing the people.
Almost every street reminds me of the past struggles. There, in front of the Winter Palace, stood the priest Gapon in the midst of the thousands that had come to beg the “Little Father” for mercy and bread. The square ran crimson with the blood of the workers on that fateful January day in 1905. Out of their graves, a year later, rose the first Revolution, and again the cries of the oppressed were drowned by the crack of artillery. A reign of terror followed, and many perished on the scaffold and in the prisons. But again and again rose the specter of revolt, and at last Tsarism gave way, powerless to defend itself, forsaken by all, regretted by none. Then came the great October Revolution and the triumph of the people﻿—and Petrograd ever in the first line of battle.
---
The city looks deserted. Its population, nearly 3,000,000 in 1917, is now reduced to 500,000. War and pestilence have almost decimated Petrograd. In the fights against Kaledin, Denikin, Kolchak, and other White forces, the workers of the Red City lost heavily. Its best proletarian element died for the Revolution.

===Chinese===

*1920年1月21日。*——明丽的冬日阳光洒在涅瓦河（Neva）宽阔洁白的冰雪胸膛上。两岸矗立着庄严肃穆的巍峨建筑，海军部大厦高耸起纤细入云的尖顶，显得格外俏丽优雅。极目所致皆是雄伟壮丽的殿宇，冬宫（Winter Palace）伫立在群楼之中，透出一股冷冽的静穆。那尊骑在战马上的青铜骑士雕像伫立在粗粝的芬兰巨石1之上，昂首欲跃过守卫着他梦想之城的彼得保罗要塞（Petropavlovskaya）的高耸尖塔。
这是我在沙皇旧都度过的青春岁月里无比熟悉的景象。然而，昔日金碧辉煌的荣耀、皇室的豪奢气派、贵族们欢歌笑语的盛宴，以及在雷鸣般的战鼓声中踢着正步前进的奴隶般森严的军队方阵，如今已荡然无存。革命的巨手已将这座沉溺于奢靡怠惰的都市，彻底塑造成劳动者的家园。起义的风暴甚至改变了这里的街名：因果戈理、普希金和陀思妥耶夫斯基的生花妙笔而流芳百世的涅瓦大街（Nevsky），如今更名为“10月25日大街”（Prospect of October 25th）；冬宫前的开阔广场，现在为纪念乌里茨基（Uritsky）而重新命名；卡缅诺奥斯特罗夫斯基大街则改称“红色黎明大街”。在昔日的杜马大厦前，拉萨尔（Lassale）英姿勃发的半身雕像凝视着过往行人，作为“新时代”的象征巍然耸立；在近卫骑兵林荫道上，沃洛达尔斯基（Volodarsky）的塑像挺拔而立，手臂前伸，仿佛仍在向人民慷慨陈词。
几乎每一条街道都在唤起我对昔日浴血抗争的记忆。当年就在冬宫门前，加邦神甫（Gapon）曾伫立在成千上万名前来祈求“沙皇小父亲”开恩施舍面包的民众中间。在1905年那个宿命般惨烈的1月日子里，这片广场曾被工人们滚烫的鲜血染得一片殷红。仅仅一年之后，第一场革命便从死难者的坟茔中浴火重生；然而，被压迫者的怒吼再次被隆隆炮火的轰鸣所吞没。随之而来的是白色恐怖的残酷统治，无数志士倒在断头台上或命丧铁窗之中。但反抗的幽灵一次又一次重新崛起，最终腐朽的沙皇专制政权土崩瓦解——它无力自保，众叛亲离，无人为之惋惜。紧接着，伟大的十月革命爆发，迎来了人民的最终胜利——而彼得格勒（Petrograd）始终伫立在战斗的最前沿！
---
整座城市显得空旷而冷清。1917年时全市人口近3,000,000人，如今已骤降至500,000人。战争与瘟疫几乎将彼得格勒摧残殆尽。在同卡列金（Kaledin）、邓尼金（Denikin）、高尔察克（Kolchak）及其他各路白卫军的殊死拼杀中，这座红色之城的工人们付出了惨重伤亡。无产阶级中最优秀的精华之士，皆已为革命献出了年轻而宝贵的生命。

===Original===

The streets are empty; the people are in the factories, at work. On the corner the young woman *militsioner*, rifle in hand, walks to and fro, stamping with her booted feet on the ground to keep warm. Now and then a solitary figure passes, all wrapped up and bent, dragging a heavy load on a sleigh.
The stores are closed, their shutters on. The signs still hang in their accustomed places﻿—painted fruit and vegetables advertising the wares no more to be found within. Doors and windows are locked and barred, and everything is silent about.
The famous Apraksin Dvor is no more. All the wealth of the country, bought or stolen, used to be paraded there to tempt the passerby. Highborn *barinya* and chambermaid, good-natured blond peasant and sullen Tartar, absentminded student and crafty thief, mingled here in the free democracy of the marketplace. All things were to be had in the Dvor; human bodies were bought and sold, and souls bartered for money.
It is all changed now. At the entrance of the Labor Temple flames the legend: “Who does not work shall not eat.”
In the public *stolovaya* (dining room) vegetable soup and kasha (gruel) are served. The diners bring with them their own bread, issued at the distributing points. The large room is unheated, and the people sit with their hats and coats on. They look cold and pale, pitifully emaciated. “If only the blockade were taken off,” my neighbor at the table says, “we might be saved.”
---
Some parts of the city bear evidence of the recent Yudenich campaign. Here and there are remnants of barricades, piles of sandbags, and artillery trained upon the railroad station. The story of that fight is still on everybody’s lips. “It was a superhuman effort,” little Vera enthusiastically related. “The enemy was five times our number and at our very gates﻿—on Krasnaya Gorka﻿—seven miles from the City. Men and women, even children, turned out to build barricades, carry munitions to the fighters, and prepare to defend our homes to the last hand-to-hand struggle.” Vera is only eighteen, fair and delicate as a lily, but she operated a machine gun.
“So sure were the Whites of their victory,” Vera continued, “they had already distributed the ministerial portfolios and appointed the military governor of Petrograd. Yudenich officials with their staffs were secretly in the city, waiting only for the triumphant entry of their Chief.

===Chinese===

街道上空空荡荡；人们都在工厂里埋头劳作。在街角，年轻的见习女民警（*militsioner*）手握步枪来回巡踱，脚上穿着厚皮靴不停跺着地面以驱赶刺骨的严寒。偶尔有孤零零的身影走过，浑身裹得严严实实，弓着腰在雪橇上拖着沉重的货物。
临街商铺全都关了门，门板紧闭。招牌依然挂在人们熟悉的老地方——上面画着水果与蔬菜，叫卖着店内早已无处寻觅的商品。门窗皆上了锁、插上了铁栅栏，四周一片死寂。
往昔闻名遐迩的阿普拉克辛市场（Apraksin Dvor）如今已复不存在。过去，全俄各地的奇珍异宝无论是买来的还是偷来的，都曾在此大肆罗列以诱惑过往行客。出身名门的大贵妇（*barinya*）与粗使侍女，生性敦厚的金发农夫与面容阴郁的鞑靼人，心不在焉的学生与老奸巨猾的窃贼，全都混杂在这片市集所特有的自由平等之中。在昔日的市场里，世间万物皆可交易：肉体被明码标价买卖，灵魂为金钱被随手典当。
如今这一切已彻底改观。在工会大楼（Labor Temple）的门厅入口处，赫然镌刻着一句烈火般醒目的标语：“不劳动者不得食。”
在公共食堂（*stolovaya*）里，人们分发着清淡的菜汤和麦糁稀粥（kasha）。就餐者各自携带着配给站领取的面包。宽敞的大厅里没有生火取暖，人们都戴着帽子、穿着厚重的大衣端坐着用餐。他们面容苍白冷峻，消瘦得令人心碎。“要是封锁能够解除就好了，”我同桌的邻座低声说道，“我们或许就能活命了。”
---
城中某些角落仍清晰留存着不久前反击尤登尼奇（Yudenich）战役的弹痕与遗迹。随处可见街垒的残骸、成堆的沙袋，以及瞄准火车站方向的重炮。那场惊心动魄的大战至今仍挂在每个人的嘴边。“那真是一场超人般的拼死搏击，”年轻的小维拉（Vera）热情洋溢地向我讲述道，“敌人的兵力是我们的5倍，而且直接逼到了我们的城门脚下——就在距离市区仅7英里的红山堡垒（Krasnaya Gorka）。全城的男人、女人甚至儿童全都动员起来修筑街垒，为前线战士运送弹药，誓死保卫我们的家园直到进行最后的白刃肉搏。”维拉年仅18岁，面容皎洁柔美如同一朵百合花，可她当时却亲自操纵一挺重机枪浴血杀敌。
“白匪军当时对胜利胜券在握，”维拉接着说道，“他们甚至提前瓜分了内阁部长的职位，任命了彼得格勒的军政总督。尤登尼奇的军官团及其参谋人员早已秘密潜伏进城内，单等他们的匪首大摇大摆举行入城凯旋仪式。

===Original===

We were in desperate straits; it seemed that all was lost. Our soldiers, reduced in numbers and exhausted, were disheartened. It was just then that Bill Shatov rushed to the scene. He gathered the little army about him, and addressed them in the name of the Revolution. His powerful voice reached the furthest lines; his passionate eloquence lit the embers of revolutionary zeal, inspiring new strength and faith.”
“Forward, boys! For the Revolution!” Shatov thundered, and like desperate furies the workers threw themselves upon the Yudenich army. The flower of the Petrograd proletariat perished in that struggle, but the Red City and the Revolution were saved.
With justified pride Shatov showed me the order of the Red Banner pinned on his breast. “For Krasnaya Gorka,” he said, with a happy smile.
He has remained the jovial good fellow I knew him in America, made riper and more earnest by his experience in the Revolution. He has held many important positions, and has won a reputation as an efficient worker and successful organizer. He has not joined the Communist Party; on many vital points, he says, he disagrees with the Bolsheviki. He has remained an Anarchist, believing in the ultimate abolition of political government as the only sure road to individual liberty and general well-being.
“Just now we are passing through the difficult stage of violent social revolution,” Shatov said. “Several fronts are to be defended, and we need a strong, well-disciplined army. There are counterrevolutionary plots to be guarded against and the Cheka must keep a watchful eye on the conspirators. Of course, the Bolsheviki have committed many errors; that’s because they are human. We live in the period of transition, of much confusion, constant danger, and anxiety. It is the hour of travail, and men are needed to help in the work of defense and reconstruction. We Anarchists should remain true to our ideals, but we should not criticize at this time. We must work and help to build.”
---
The *Buford* deportees are quartered in the Smolny. By Zorin’s invitation I am staying at the Hotel Astoria, now known as the First House of the Soviet. Zorin, who was employed in America as a millman, is now Secretary of the Petrograd Section of the Communist Party, and the editor of the *Krasnaya Gazetta*, the official daily of the Soviet.

===Chinese===

我们当时陷入了绝境；局势看起来已彻底无法挽回。我们的战士伤亡惨重、精疲力竭，士气低落到了极点。恰在千钧一发之际，比尔·沙托夫（Bill Shatov）火速赶到了前线。他将残存的部队集结在自己周围，以革命的名义向他们发表了慷慨悲壮的动员演说。他那洪亮如钟的嗓音传遍了最偏远的阵地；他充满激情的雄辩重新点燃了革命烈火的余烬，激发起全新的力量与信仰。”
‘冲啊，同志们！为了革命！’沙托夫振臂高呼，工人们犹如狂怒的复仇女神般奋不顾身地扑向尤登尼奇的敌军。彼得格勒无产阶级的精英骨干在那场血战中伤亡大半，但这座红色之城与革命本身终于转危为安。
沙托夫满怀欣慰与自豪地向我展示了佩戴在他胸前的红旗勋章（Order of the Red Banner）。“这是为了红山之战（Krasnaya Gorka）颁发的，”他绽开欢快的笑容说道。
他依然是我当年在美利坚所结识的那位性格爽朗的挚友，只是革命的淬炼使他变得更加沉稳与坚毅。他曾担任过多项重要领导职务，赢得了干练能干、组织有方的崇高声望。但他并没有加入共产党（Communist Party）；在许多根本原则问题上，他坦言自己同布尔什维克存在分歧。他始终坚守安那其主义信念，坚信唯有最终彻底废除政治政府，才是通向个体自由与普遍福祉的唯一切实之路。
“眼下我们正处于暴力社会革命的最艰难阵痛期，”沙托夫对我说，“前线数个战场需要誓死保卫，我们迫切需要一支强大且纪律严明的军队。严防反革命阴谋容不得丝毫松懈，契卡（Cheka）必须时刻警惕盯紧那些阴谋策划者。毫无疑问，布尔什维克犯下了许多错误；人非圣贤，孰能无过。我们生活在一个过渡时代，充满了混乱、持续的凶险与动荡不安。这是剧烈阵痛的严峻时刻，最迫切需要人手投身于防御与重建的浩繁工程中。我们安那其主义者理应坚守自己的理想初心，但在这个危急关头决不可袖手旁观、妄加指责。我们必须埋头实干，全力协助建设。”
---
“布福德号”（*Buford*）上的被驱逐者全员被安置在斯莫尔尼宫住宿。应佐林（Zorin）的盛情邀请，我下榻于阿斯托利亚酒店（Hotel Astoria）——该酒店如今已被命名为“苏维埃第一招待所”。佐林当年在美利坚曾是一名工厂轧钢工人，如今已荣任俄共（布）彼得格勒支部书记，兼任苏维埃官方机关报《红色报》（*Krasnaya Gazetta*）的总编辑。

===Original===

He impresses me as a most devoted Communist and indefatigable worker. His wife, Liza, also an American emigrant, is the typical I.W.W. Though very feminine in figure, she is rough and ready of speech, and an enthusiastic Bolshevik.
Together we visited the Smolny. Formerly the exclusive home of highborn young ladies, it is now the busy seat of the Petrograd Government. The quarters of the Third International are also located here, and the sanctum of Zinoviev, its secretary, a large chamber sumptuously furnished and decorated with potted flowers and plants. On his desk I noticed a leather portfolio of huge size, the gift of his co-workers.
In the Smolny dining room I met a number of prominent Communists and Soviet officials. Some were in military uniform, others in corduroys and black student shirts belted at the waist, the tails on the outside. All looked pale, with sunken eyes and high cheekbones, the result of systematic undernourishment, overwork, and worry.
The dinner was much superior to the meals served in the public *stolovaya*. “Only the ‘responsible workers,’ Communists holding important positions, dine here,” Zorin remarked. There are several gradations of *pyock* (rations), he explained. Soldiers and sailors receive one and a half pounds of bread per day; also sugar, salt, tobacco, and meat when possible. The factory workers get one pound, while the non-producers﻿—most of them intelligentsia﻿—receive half a pound and even less. There is no discrimination about this system, Zorin believes; it is just division, according to the value of one’s work.
I remember Vera’s remark. “Russia is very poor,” she said; “but whatever there is, all should share alike. That would be justice, and no one could complain.”
---
In the evening I attended the anniversary celebration of Alexander Herzen. For the first time I found myself within the walls of the Tsar’s Palace, whose very mention had filled me with awe in my childhood. Never had I dreamed then that the forbidden name of Herzen, the feared Nihilist and enemy of the Romanovs, would some day be glorified there.
Red flags and bunting decorated the plaform. With interest I read the inscriptions:

> “Socialism is the religion of Man;

A religion not of heaven but of the earth.”

> “The reign of the workers and peasants forever.”

===Chinese===

他给我的印象是一位极为忠诚的共产党员和不知疲倦的实干家。他的妻子丽莎（Liza）也是一名留美归国侨民，是典型的世界产业工人同盟（I.W.W.）骨干风格。她身材虽极富女性柔美，言谈举止却雷厉风行、直截了当，是一位热情饱满的布尔什维克。
我们一同前往斯莫尔尼宫参观。这里早年曾是贵族千金专属的贵族女子修道院学院，如今已成为彼得格勒苏维埃政府繁忙的中枢所在地。第三国际（共产国际）的总部亦设于此处，其总书记季诺维也夫（Zinoviev）的办公室便在其中——那是一间陈设极其奢华宽敞的套间，摆满了盆栽鲜花与绿植。在他的办公桌上，我注意到摆放着一只硕大无朋的精美皮质公文包，那是同事们赠送给他的礼物。
在斯莫尔尼宫的内部食堂里，我遇到了多位显赫的共产党员与苏维埃高官。他们有的身着军装，有的身穿灯芯绒便服和腰间束带、下摆垂在裤外的黑色青年学生衫。他们每个人面色都显得十分苍白，眼窝深陷，颧骨高耸，这显然是长期营养不良、过度操劳和日夜焦虑所留下的烙印。
这里的伙食水准显著优于外面的公共食堂（*stolovaya*）。“只有‘负责干部’，即担任重要要职的共产党员，才能在此就餐，”佐林向我解释道。他说明道，口粮配给制（*pyock*）被划分为若干个严格等级：前线红军战士和水兵每天可领到1.5磅面包，此外还尽可能供应糖、盐、烟草和肉食；工厂产业工人可分得1磅；而那些非生产人员——其中绝大多数是旧知识分子——每天只能领到区区半磅（0.5磅）甚至更少。在佐林看来，这种等级制度绝非阶级歧视，而是完全依据个人对革命所贡献的劳动价值进行合理公平的分配。
我不由得回想起小维拉说过的那番话。“俄国眼下十分穷困，”她说，“但无论剩下多少物资，大家都应当平分共享。只有那样才称得上公道，谁也不会心生怨言。”
---
傍晚时分，我参加了纪念亚历山大·赫尔岑（Alexander Herzen）的周年纪念大会。我平生第一次踏进了沙皇皇宫的大门，在我的童年时代，只要一提起这座宫殿的名字，便会令我心生无限畏惧。当年我何曾敢奢望，有朝一日，赫尔岑这个令罗曼诺夫王朝谈虎色变、深恶痛绝的虚无主义死敌的禁忌之名，竟能在这座皇宫深处获得至高无上的隆重颂扬！
讲台上挂满了红旗和彩带。我怀着浓厚的兴味读着横幅上的题词：

> “社会主义是人类的崇高信仰；

这并非来自虚无缥缈的天国，而是根植于现实大地的真理信仰。”

> “工农政权万世流芳。”

===Original===

A large crimson banner represented a bell (*Kolokol*), the name of the famous paper published by Herzen in exile. On its side was stamped: “1870﻿–﻿1920,” and beneath, the words:

> “Not in vain have you died;

What you have sown will grow.”

After the meeting the audience marched to the home of Herzen, still preserved on the Nevsky. The demonstration through the dark streets, lit only by the torches of the participants, the strains of revolutionary music and song, the enthusiasm of the men and women indifferent to the bitter cold﻿—all impressed me deeply. The moving silhouettes seemed the shades of the past come to life, the martyrs of Tsardom risen to avenge the injustice of the ages.
How true is the Herzen motto:

> “Not in vain have you died;

What you have sown will grow.”

===Chinese===

一面巨大的深红旗帜上绘制着一口大钟（*Kolokol*，即《钟声》报），这是赫尔岑流亡海外期间创办的著名革命刊物的名称。旗帜的一侧印有数字“1870—1920”，正下方写着几行大字：

> “你们未曾白白牺牲；

你们播下的火种终将破土成长。”

大会闭幕后，全场听众列队游行奔赴赫尔岑的故居——那栋坐落在涅瓦大街上的建筑至今仍完好保存着。在漆黑一团的街巷中举行的火炬游行，全凭与会者手中熊熊燃烧的火把照亮道路；激昂慷慨的革命乐曲与战歌在夜空中回荡，男女军民全然不顾刺骨的严寒所迸发出的满腔热忱——这一切无不深深震撼着我的心扉。黑夜中缓缓移动的队伍身影，宛若昔日沉睡的英灵重回人间，仿佛历代惨遭沙皇暴政荼毒的革命先烈们齐齐从坟墓中挺身而出，誓要洗雪千百年来受尽屈辱的奇冤宿怨。
赫尔岑当年的格言是何等的颠扑不破：

> “你们未曾白白牺牲；

你们播下的火种终将破土成长。”

===Original===

---
The assembly hall of the Tauride Palace was filled with Soviet deputies and invited guests. A special session had been called to consider the difficult situation created by the severe winter, and the growing scarcity of food and fuel.
Row above row stretched before me, occupied by men and women in grimy working clothes, their faces pale, their bodies emaciated. Here and there were men in peasant garb. They sat quietly, conversing little, as if exhausted by the day’s toil.
The military band struck up “The Internationale,” and the audience rose to their feet. Then Zinoviev ascended the platform. The winter had caused much suffering, he said; heavy snowfall impedes railroad traffic, and Petrograd is almost isolated. A further reduction of the *pyock* (ration) has unfortunately become necessary. He expressed confidence that the workers of Petrograd﻿—the most revolutionary, the advance-guard of Communism﻿—would understand that the Government is compelled to take this step, and would approve its action.
The measure is temporary, Zinoviev continued. The Revolution is achieving success on all fronts﻿—the glorious Red Army is winning great victories, the White forces will soon be entirely defeated, the country will get on its feet economically, and the workers will reap the fruit of their long martyrdom. The imperialists and capitalists of the whole world are against Russia, but the proletariat everywhere is with the Revolution. Soon the Social Revolution will break out in Europe and America﻿—it cannot be far off now, for capitalism is crumbling to earth everywhere. Then there will be an end to war and fratricidal bloodshed, and Russia will receive help from the workers of other countries.
Radek, recently returned from Germany where he was a prisoner, followed Zinoviev. He gave an interesting account of his experience, lashing the German “social patriots” with biting sarcasm. A psuedo Socalist Party, he said, now in power, but too cowardly to introduce Socialism; traitors to the Revolution they are, those Scheidemanns, Bernsteins, et al., bourgeois reformists, agents of Allied militarism and international capital. The only hope is in the Communist Party of Germany which is growing by leaps and bounds, and is supported by the proletariat of Germany.

===Chinese===

---
塔夫利宫的大礼堂里座无虚席，挤满了苏维埃代表与受邀特邀嘉宾。此次特别召开的紧急全体会议，旨在研究严酷寒冬所引发的极度困局，以及日益枯竭加剧的粮食与燃料危机。
一排排座椅在我眼前层层延伸，坐满了身穿污渍斑斑工装的男男女女，他们面容苍白憔悴，身形瘦骨嶙峋。人群中稀稀疏疏坐着几位身着朴素农服的农民代表。他们静静地端坐着，极少交头接耳，仿佛早已被白昼沉重的劳作榨干了最后一丝气力。
军乐队奏响了庄严激昂的《国际歌》，全场起立肃立。随后季诺维也夫步上主席台。他发言指出，酷寒给全市带来了无尽的磨难；暴风雪严重瘫痪了铁路运输，彼得格勒已近乎沦为一座孤岛。极其遗憾的是，政府不得不进一步削减口粮配给额度（*pyock*）。他表示坚信，彼得格勒最具革命觉悟、作为共产主义前锋近卫军的产业工人阶级，必将深刻体谅苏维埃政府迫于无奈采取这一举措的良苦用心，并对这一决定给予充分支持与谅解。
季诺维也夫接着强调，这项限缩措施纯属临时应急之计。当前革命已在全线各大战场取得辉煌战果——英勇的红军（Red Army）正节节胜利，白匪反动武装即将全线覆灭，国家经济很快便能重回正轨，广大工人阶级必将迎来漫长苦难殉道换来的胜利硕果。全世界的帝国主义与资本家虽然对苏维埃俄国虎视眈眈、四面围剿，但普天下的无产阶级大众却始终与苏维埃革命站在一起。用不了多久，社会大革命必将在欧洲与美洲轰轰烈烈爆发——这一刻绝不会遥远，因为资本主义在各地早已日薄西山、行将就木！届时，残酷的战争与同胞相残的流血杀戮将彻底终结，俄国亦将迎来世界各国工人兄弟倾囊相助的滚滚洪流。
刚刚从德国结束战俘监禁归国的拉狄克（Radek）紧随季诺维也夫之后登台演讲。他兴致盎然地讲述了自己在海外的惊险阅历，用辛辣刻薄的讽刺将德国“社会沙文主义爱国者”痛批得体无完肤。他指出，当前在德国执政的不过是一个打着社会主义旗号的伪政党，他们怯懦至极，根本不敢推行真正的社会主义制度；那些谢德曼（Scheidemann）、伯恩斯坦（Bernstein）之流纯属革命的无耻叛徒，是彻头彻尾的资产阶级改良派走狗，是协约国军国主义与国际垄断资本豢养的代理爪牙。唯一的希望完全寄托在德国共产党（Communist Party of Germany）身上——该党正以突飞猛进之势迅速壮大，并得到了德国全体无产阶级的坚决拥护！

===Original===

Soon that country will be swept by revolution﻿—not a make-believe Social Democratic one, but a Communist revolution, such as that of Russia, and then the workers of Germany will come to the aid of their brothers in Russia, and the world will learn what the revolutionary proletariat can accomplish.
Joffe was the next speaker. Of aristocratic appearance, well dressed, his beard neatly trimmed, he seemed strangely out of place in the assembly of ill-clad workers. As Chairman of the Peace Committee he reported on the conditions of the treaty just concluded with Latvia, receiving the applause of the assembly. The people are evidently eager for peace, whatever the conditions.
I had hoped to hear the deputies speak, and to learn the views and sentiments of the masses they represent. But the members of the Soviet took no active part in the proceedings. They listened quietly to the speakers, and voted mechanically on the resolutions presented by the Presidium. There was no discussion; the proceedings lacked vitality.
---
Some friction has developed among the *Buford* deportees. The Anarchists complain of discrimination in favor of the Communist members of the group, and I have been repeatedly called to the Smolny to smooth out difficulties.
The boys chafe at the delay in assigning them to work. I have prepared the *enquetes* of the group, classifying the deportees according to trade and ability, to aid in placing them to best advantage. But two weeks have passed, and the men are still haunting the Soviet departments, standing in line by the hour, seeking to be supplied with the necessary *propuski* and documents admitting them to work.
I have pointed out to Zorin what a valuable asset these deportees are to Russia: there are mechanics, miners, printers among them, needed in the present scarcity of skilled labor. Why waste their time and energy? I cited the matter of exchanging American currency. Most of the deportees brought some money with them. Their *pyock* is insufficient, but certain necessaries can be bought: bread, butter, and tobacco, even meat, are offered on the markets. At least a hundred of our boys have exchanged their American cash for Soviet money.

===Chinese===

用不了多久，那片土地就将被波澜壮阔的革命巨浪所席卷——那绝不是软弱欺瞒的社会民主党式改良，而是一场同苏俄完全一样的真正的共产主义大革命！到那时，德国的工人阶级必将大举赶来支援他们在俄国的阶级同胞兄弟，全世界也将亲眼见证觉醒的革命无产阶级究竟能够创造何等惊天动地的伟业！
越飞（Joffe）是下一位发言人。他外表颇具贵族绅士派头，衣着笔挺考究，修剪整齐的络腮胡须打理得一丝不苟，在这群衣衫褴褛的工人代表大会中显得格外格格不入。作为和平谈判委员会主席，他向大会汇报了刚刚与拉脱维亚签署的和平条约各项条款，赢得了全场雷鸣般的掌声。显而易见，无论条约附带何种苛刻条件，饱经磨难的人民对和平都怀着极其迫切的强烈渴望。
我本希望能亲耳聆听底层代表们的踊跃发言，倾听他们所代表的工农群众的真实心声与思想情感。然而，苏维埃的普通代表们自始至终未曾主动参与议程讨论。他们只是静静地端坐着聆听台上的领袖演讲，对主席团提交的各项决议机械化地举手表决通过。现场全无任何公开辩论；整场会议缺乏生机与活力。
---
在“布福德号”（*Buford*）的被遣返人员内部逐渐产生了一些矛盾摩擦。安那其主义者抱怨说，有关部门在安置待遇上明显优待偏袒同组的共产党员；为此，我已多次被紧急叫往斯莫尔尼宫居中调解斡旋。
小伙子们因迟迟得不到具体工作分配而焦躁难耐。我早已按行业专长与专业技能为全组人员编制了详尽的资格履历表（*enquetes*），以便协助将他们人尽其才地分配到最合适的岗位上去。然而整整2周过去了，大伙儿依然成天奔波徘徊于苏维埃各职能部门之间，动辄排上几个小时的长队，苦苦申领入职上岗所必需的通行许可证（*propuski*）与身份证明文件。
我直截了当地向佐林指出，这批被遣返者对百废待兴的苏俄而言是一笔何等宝贵的人才财富：他们之中有熟练机修工、采矿工、印刷排版工，皆是当前技术工人极度短缺的俄国最为急需的人才。为何要白白虚耗他们的宝贵精力与大好光阴呢？我还特意列举了兑换美钞这件棘手事。绝大多数被遣返者随身都带来了一些积蓄。官方发给他们的口粮配给（*pyock*）微薄得无法果腹，但自由市场上尚有各类生活必需品可供采买：面包、黄油、烟草乃至肉类，在黑市或自由集市上皆有兜售。我们这群人中至少有100名小伙子已经将自己兜里的美利坚现金兑换成了苏维埃卢布货币。

===Original===

Considering that each one had to find out for himself where the exchange could be made, often being directed wrongly, and the time each had to spend in the Soviet financial departments, it can be safely assumed that on the average each man required three hours for the transaction. If the deportees had a responsible committee, the whole matter could have been managed in less than a day. “Such a committee could attend to all their affairs, and save time,” I urged.
Zorin agreed with me. “It ought to be tried,” he said.
I proposed to go over to the Smolny, call the men together, explain my proposition to them, and have the committee elected. “It would be well to assign a little room as the Committee’s office, with a telephone to transact business,” I suggested.
“You are very American,” Zorin smiled. “You want it done on the spot. But that isn’t the way,” he added dryly. “I’ll submit your plan to the proper authorities, and then we’ll see.”
“At any rate,” I said, “I hope it can be done soon. And you may always call on me, for I am anxious to help.”
“By the way,” Zorin remarked, looking at me quizzically, “trading is forbidden. Buying and selling is speculation. Your people should not do such things.” He spoke severely.
“You cannot call buying a pound of bread speculation,” I replied. “Besides, the difference in the *pyock* encourages trade. The Government still issues money﻿—it is legally in circulation.”
“Y‑e‑s,” Zorin said, displeased. “But better tell your friends not to speculate any more. Only *shkurniki*, self-seeking skinners, do that.”
“You are unjust, Zorin. The *Buford* men have donated the greater part of their money, the provisions and medicines they brought, to the children of Petrograd. They have even deprived themselves of necessities, and the little cash they have kept the Government itself has turned into Soviet money for them.”
“Better warn the men,” Zorin repeated.

===Chinese===

考虑到每个人都必须独自摸索寻找兑换货币的办事地点，期间还屡遭错误指引，再加上在苏维埃财政部门排队办事所耗费的漫长时间，完全可以保守推算，每个人办理兑换平均都要耗费整整3小时。倘若这批被遣返者能成立一个负责任的自治协调委员会，整起兑换事宜不到一天时间即可统一妥善办结。“这样一个委员会能够通盘统筹照料他们的一切日常事务，大大节约宝贵的时间，”我恳切地敦促道。
佐林表示赞同我的提议。“确实值得一试，”他说道。
我提议立刻前往斯莫尔尼宫，将大伙儿召集起来，向他们详细阐述我的提议，并当场推选出委员会。“如果能拨出一间小房间作为委员会的固定办公室，并配备一部办公电话以便联络公事，那就再好不过了，”我建议道。
“你真是个典型的美利坚急性子，”佐林苦笑了一下说道，“总想着当场立竿见影办妥。但在我们这里可不能这么干，”他语气生硬地补充道，“我会把你的这项计划呈报给主管当局审批，随后再看情况吧。”
“无论如何，”我说道，“我真诚希望能尽快办成。只要有需要，你随时可以差遣我，我巴不得能多出几分力。”
“顺便提一句，”佐林意味深长地打量着我，目光审视地提醒道，“私下买卖交易是明令禁止的。一切私人买卖都是非法投机倒把。你们那帮人不该参与这种勾当。”他的口气十分严厉。
“花钱买一磅面包充饥，根本不能称之为投机倒把，”我据理力争道，“更何况，口粮配给额度（*pyock*）的悬殊差异客观上逼得人们不得不去交易。况且政府目前仍在印发纸币——国家货币在法律层面上依然是合法流通的。”
“是——啊，”佐林面带不悦地拉长了声音，“但你最好还是告诫你的朋友们，切莫再搞投机了。只有自私自利者/蛀虫（*shkurniki*），那些自私自利的剥皮鬼，才会干那种卑劣的营生。”
“你太不公道了，佐林。‘布福德号’上的同胞们早已把自己随身带来的绝大部分金钱、口粮和药品，毫无保留地捐赠给了彼得格勒的苦难儿童。他们甚至克扣剥夺了自己的最低生活必需品，而他们仅剩的那点微薄私房钱，也是由苏维埃政府官方亲自经手为他们兑换成卢布的。”
“你最好还是去警告他们一下，”佐林冷冷地重复道。

## IV Moscow / IV 莫斯科
===Original===

*February 10, 1920.*﻿—The opportunity to visit the capital came unexpectedly: Lansbury and Barry, of the London *Daily Herald*, were in Petrograd, and I was asked to accompany them to Moscow as interpreter. Though not entirely recovered from my recent illness, I accepted the rare chance, travel between Petrograd and Moscow being limited to absolute necessity.
The railroad conditions between the two capitals (both cities are so considered) are deplorable. The engines are old and weak, the road in need of repair. Several times we ran short of fuel, and our engineer left the train to go off into the woods for a fresh supply of wood. Some of the passengers accompanied the crew to help with the loading.
The cars were crowded with soldiers and Soviet officials. During the night many travelers boarded our train. There was much shouting and cursing, and the plaintive cries of children. Then sudden silence, and an imperious command, “Get off, you devils. You don’t belong here.”
“The railroad Cheka,” the *provodnik* (car porter) came into the coupé to warn us. “Get your papers ready, *tovarishi*.”
A dark, stocky man entered. My eye caught the gleam of a big Colt in his belt, without holster. Behind him stood two soldiers, with bayoneted rifles. “Your papers!” he demanded.
“English travelers,” I explained, showing our documents.
“Oh, pardon, *tovarishi*,”﻿—his manner changed instantly, as he caught sight of Lansbury, wrapped in his great fur coat, tall and side-whiskered, the typical British *bourzhooi*.
“Pardon,” the Chekist repeated, and without looking at our documents he stepped into the next coupé.
We were in the special coach reserved for high Bolshevik officials and foreign guests. It was lit by candles, had upholstered couches, and was comparatively clean. The rest of the train consisted of third-class cars, containing double tiers of wooden benches, and of some *teplushki* (freight cars) used for passenger traffic, without light or heat, incredibly crowded and filthy.
At every station we were besieged by crowds clamoring for admission. “*N’yet mesta, N’yet mesta!*” (No room!) the militiamen accompanying the train kept shouting, repeatedly drawing their guns. I called the attention of the officers to the vacant places in our compartment, but they waved me aside. “ ’Tis not for them,” they said.

===Chinese===

*1920年2月10日。*——前往首都的机会来得十分偶然：伦敦《每日先驱报》（*Daily Herald*）的兰斯伯里（Lansbury）和巴里（Barry）恰好来到彼得格勒，有关方面邀我作为随行翻译陪同他们前往莫斯科（Moscow）。虽然我前些日子的病情尚未彻底痊愈，但我依然欣然抓住了这次难得的良机，因为彼得格勒与莫斯科两地之间的交通往来受到极严格管制，非绝对必要概不放行。
连接这两座首都（两座城市皆被视作首都）之间的铁路路况令人堪忧。火车头年久失修、马力孱弱，铁路线路急需修缮。一路上火车数次耗尽了燃料，火车司机不得不临时停车钻进沿途树林去砍伐搜集新鲜的木柴。部分旅客还主动陪同乘务组一同下车帮忙搬运木柴装车。
车厢里挤满了红军官兵和苏维埃干部。入夜之后，又有大批旅客试图强行挤上我们的列车。四周充斥着嘈杂的呼喊、粗野的叫骂，以及孩子们凄厉的哭啼声。随后四周突然陷入一片死寂，只听得一声威严蛮横的断喝：“滚下去，你们这群捣蛋鬼！这里不是你们待的地方！”
“是铁路契卡（Cheka）的人，”列车员（*provodnik*）匆匆走进包厢向我们发出警示，“同志们（*tovarishi*），请把你们的证件提前准备好。”
一个面容黝黑、身材粗壮的男子迈步走了进来。我一眼瞥见他腰间别着一把擦得锃亮的大口径柯尔特手枪，甚至连枪套都没装。在他身后挺立着两名荷枪实弹、上了刺刀的红军战士。“出示证件！”他严厉命令道。
“我们是英国旅行代表团，”我一边解释，一边向他出示我们的通行文书。
“哦，万分抱歉，同志们（*tovarishi*），”——他的态度瞬间大转弯，因为他一眼瞧见了裹在奢华厚重皮草大衣里的兰斯伯里；兰斯伯里身材高大，留着络腮胡须，活脱脱一副典型的英国老爷/资产阶级（*bourzhooi*）派头。
“抱歉，”这位契卡人员连声赔罪，甚至都没多看一眼我们的证件，便识趣地转身踱进了隔壁包厢。
我们乘坐的是专门为布尔什维克高级首长和外宾专设的特快公务包厢车厢。车厢里点着蜡烛照明，配备有柔软的软垫沙发卧铺，环境相对洁净体面。而列车的其余部分全由普通三等车厢拼凑而成，里面设有双层木板硬座，外加几节临时改作载客用途的暖篷闷罐货车（*teplushki*）；里面既无照明也无暖气，拥挤肮脏到了极点。
列车每停靠一站，车厢四周立刻被蜂拥而上苦苦哀求登车的汹涌人潮团团围住。“没有空位！没有空位！（*N’yet mesta, N’yet mesta!*）”随车的武装民警不停大声喝斥，甚至三番五次拔出配枪威吓人群。我曾向随车军官示意我们包厢里尚有空座，但他却不耐烦地挥手将我挡开。“那不是留给他们坐的，”他们冷冰冰地回答道。

===Original===

Arriving at the Moscow depot we found platform and waiting room a dense mass, almost everyone with a heavy load on his back, pushing and shouting, those in front trying to get past the armed guards at the gates. The people looked worn and begrimed, most of them having spent several days at the station, sleeping at night on the floor, and waiting their turn to be let through.
With difficulty we made our way to the street.
