There scores of women and children fell upon our things, each trying to drag them to his little sleigh and assuring us he’ll carry our effects anywhere for a small price. “A bit of bread, little father,” the children begged; “just a little, for Christ’s sake.”
It was bitterly cold, deep snow on the ground. The children stood shivering, knocking one foot against the other for warmth. Their emaciated little faces were blue and pinched, some of the boys barefoot on the frozen steps.
“How starved they look, and how poorly clad,” I remarked.
“No worse than you see at the London stations,” Lansbury replied curtly. “You’re hypercritical, Berkman.”
In an automobile of the Foreign Office we were driven to a large house, with high iron fence and guard at the gate, the former residence of Y﻿—﻿—, the Sugar King of Russia, now occupied by Karakhan.
A palatial home, with costly carpets, rare tapestries, and paintings. The young man who met us and who introduced himself as Chicherin’s secretary, assigned Lansbury and Barry to the guest wing. “I regret we have no spare room for you,” he said to me; “we didn’t expect you. But I shall send you to the Kharitonensky.”
The latter proved to be a Soviet guest house, on the street of the same name. Formerly owned by a German merchant, it is now nationalized and serves to house delegates and visitors from other parts of the country.
In the Kharitonensky I was informed that the commandant of the house was absent, and that nothing could be done without his orders. I waited two hours, and when the commandant finally appeared he said that he had not been notified of my coming, had received no instructions to prepare a room for me, and that, moreover, no rooms were vacant.
Here was a dilemma. A stranger in a city without hotels or boarding houses, and no lodgings to be had except by order of one or the other of the Soviet institutions.

===Chinese===

抵达成莫斯科火车站后，我们发现站台与候车大厅里黑压压一片人山人海，几乎每个人背后都背着沉重不堪的行囊包裹，拼命推搡、呼喊叫嚷着；冲在前头的人正试图硬挤过大门处荷枪实弹的卫兵封锁线。人们面容憔悴灰暗、浑身污垢，其中大多数人已经在车站滞留熬过了好几天，夜晚就蜷缩在冰冷坚硬的地面上过夜，苦苦等待着轮到放行的那一刻。
我们历经千辛万苦才挤出车站来到大街上。刚一迈出门，数十名妇女和儿童便蜂拥而上扑抢我们的行李，每个人都争先恐后想把行囊拖到自己的小雪橇上，信誓旦旦保证只需付一点微薄小钱就能把行李运送到任何地方。“行行好施舍一小片面包吧，沙皇小父亲，”孩子们苦苦乞求着，“看在基督的份上，给一点点救救命吧。”
天寒地冻，积雪没膝。孩子们冻得浑身瑟瑟发抖，双脚不停互相踢打跺地以获取微弱的暖意。他们瘦骨嶙峋的稚嫩脸庞冻得发紫凹陷，几个小男孩甚至赤着双脚站在冰封的台阶上。
“他们看起来多么饥肠辘辘，穿得多么单薄破烂啊，”我忍不住感慨道。
“比起你在伦敦各大火车站看到的惨状也差不了多少，”兰斯伯里冷淡生硬地反驳道，“你太吹毛求疵了，伯克曼（Berkman）。”
乘坐外交人民委员部（Foreign Office）派来的小汽车，我们被送往一栋气派非凡的大宅第；院墙外筑着高耸的铁栅栏，大门口有武装卫兵站岗。这里原是全俄“糖业大王”Y某的私人官邸，如今由卡拉汉（Karakhan）入住办公。
这是一座宛若宫殿般的豪宅，铺设着名贵的波斯地毯，悬挂着罕见的精美挂毯与名家油画。前来迎候我们并自称是契切林（Chicherin）秘书的年轻人，将兰斯伯里和巴里安排入住客房侧翼。“非常遗憾，我们没有多余的空房招待您，”他对我说，“我们事前并不知道您会一同前来。但我会把您安置送往哈里托年斯基宾馆（Kharitonensky）。”
那座所谓的宾馆坐落在同名的街道上，原是一处苏维埃招待所。它早年属于一位德国大商贾，如今已被没收收归国有，专门用于接待来自全俄各地的苏维埃代表与外宾。
到了哈里托年斯基宾馆后，工作人员告诉我宾馆主管外出不在，没有他的亲自批示任何事情都办不成。我足足等待了整整2个小时；当那位主管终于现身时，他却声称自己事前从未接到关于我前来的任何通知，未曾收到为我预备房间的任何指示，更何况，馆内目前早已客满无房。
这下可陷入了进退维谷的绝境。在一个既无商业旅店也无任何私人旅社的陌生城市里，若没有某家苏维埃官方机构的正式批条，根本寸步难行、休想找到哪怕一处安身落脚之所。

===Original===

As I had not been invited or sent to Moscow by any of its government branches, I could not count upon them to secure a room for me. Moscow is fearfully overcrowded, and the multiplying Government departments constantly need new quarters. Visitors who cannot find a place often pass the night at the railroad station, the commandant suggested. I was about to take the hint, when we were approached by a man wearing a white fur cap with ear pieces reaching to his knees. A Siberian, I thought, from his dress.
“If the commandant does not object, perhaps you will share my room till another is vacant?” he said pleasantly, speaking good English.
The commandant, having examined my papers, consented, and presently I was installed in my friend’s large and pleasantly warm room.
He looked at me carefully, then asked:
“Are you from San Francisco?”
“Yes, I used to live there. Why do you ask?”
“Is your name Berkman?”
“Yes.”
“Alexander Berkman?” he persisted.
“Yes.”
He embraced me, kissing me thrice in Russian fashion. “Why,” he said, “I know you. I used to live in Frisco myself. Saw you many a time﻿—at meetings and lectures. Don’t you remember me? I’m Sergei. I lived on the Russian Hill. No, of course, you wouldn’t remember me,” he ran on. “Well, I returned to Russia at the outbreak of the February Revolution, by way of Japan. Been to Siberia, in Sakhalin and the East, and now I have brought our report to the Party.”
“Are you a Communist?” I inquired.
“A Bolshevik,” he smiled, “though not a Party member. I used to be a Left Social Revolutionist, but I’m close to the Communists now, and have been working with them since the Revolution.”
Again he embraced me.

===Chinese===

由于我此番既非受苏维埃任何政府分支机构的官方正式邀请，亦非受其官方差遣前来莫斯科，我自然无法指望他们出面为我解决住宿问题。莫斯科的住房拥挤状况达到了极其骇人的地步，而且急剧扩张繁衍的各级政府部门又在不断吞噬占用新的房舍。招待所主管暗示道，找不到住处的访客往往只能去火车站候车大厅对付过夜。正当我无可奈何打算接受这一建议时，一个头戴白毛皮帽、护耳长垂及膝的汉子朝我们走了过来。从这一身奇特的装束看，我想他大概是一位来自西伯利亚的人。
“要是主管同志不反对的话，在腾出新空房之前，您不妨先搬到我的房间里同我挤一挤？”他用一口流利的英语十分客气友善地说道。
主管审验了我的证件后点头应允，不多时我便搬进了这位新朋友宽敞而温暖舒适的房间。
他仔细打量着我，随后开口问道：
“您是从旧金山（San Francisco）来的吗？”
“是的，我早年曾在那里定居生活。您为何这么问？”
“您的名字是叫伯克曼吗？”
“是的。”
“是亚历山大·伯克曼（Alexander Berkman）同志吗？”他急切地追问道。
“正是我。”
他一把紧紧拥抱住我，依照俄国传统的最高礼仪连连亲吻了我3次。“哎呀！”他激动地喊道，“我认得您！我以前也曾在旧金山待过。在集会和公开演讲中我曾见过您好多次呢！您难道对我一点印象都没有了吗？我是谢尔盖（Sergei）啊，当年住在俄罗斯山（Russian Hill）。嗨，也是，您怎么可能记得住我呢，”他滔滔不绝地说了下去，“二月革命（February Revolution）爆发时，我绕道日本火速返回了俄国祖国。我跑遍了西伯利亚、库页岛（萨哈林）以及远东各地，这趟刚把我们地方的专题工作报告呈送给党中央。”
“您是一位共产党员吗？”我探询地问道。
“是一位布尔什维克，”他欣然微笑答道，“尽管我尚不是正式党员。过去我曾是左翼社会革命党人，但如今在政治上我与共产党休戚与共，自革命爆发以来便一直同他们并肩携手工作。”
说着，他又一次饱含深情地紧紧拥抱了我。

## V The Guest House / V 招待所
===Original===

*February 25.*﻿—Life in the Kharitonensky is interesting. It is an *ossobniak* (private house), large and roomy, and contains a number of delegates and guests. At meal time we gather in the common dining room, furnished in the bourgeois taste of the typical German merchant. The house has weathered the Revolution without any change. Nothing has been touched in it; even the oil painting of the former owner, life-size, flanked by those of his wife and children, still hangs in its accustomed place. One feels the atmosphere of respectability and correctness.
But at meals a different spirit prevails. The head of the table is occupied by V﻿—﻿—, a Red Army officer in military uniform of English cut. He is the chief of the Ukrainian delegation come for an important conference to “the center.” A tall, strapping fellow, not over thirty, of military bearing and commanding manner. He has been in many fights against Kaledin and Denikin, and was repeatedly wounded. When still an officer in the Tsar’s army he became a revolutionist. Later his party, the Left Social Revolutionists of the South, joined the Communists of the Ukraine.
Next to him sits K﻿—﻿—, black-haired and black-bearded, member of the Central Rada when it was broken up by Skoropadsky with the aid of German bayonets. To his right is another delegate from the Ukraine, a student with soft black beard, the only one who understands English. The editor of the Communist paper of Kiev and two young women are also in this party.
One of the foreign visitors is “Herman,” a middle-aged German grown gray and old in the revolutionary struggle. He was sent by the minority of the Spartacus Party to enlist the moral and financial support of the Bolsheviki; but Radek, he complains, refuses to recognize the rebellious minority. Near Herman sits young L﻿—﻿—, an American I.W.W., who hoboed his way to Russia without pass or money. There are also several correspondents from Sweden, Holland, and Italy, two Japanese, and a Korean Communist who was brought a prisoner from Siberia because of some peculiar misunderstanding.
The steaming samovar is on the table, and a buxom young woman is serving us. She is red-cheeked and country-like, but her demeanor is free and unforced, and she uses *tovarish* with an ease indicating a full-grown sense of equality.

===Chinese===

*2月25日。*——哈里托年斯基宾馆里的日常生活颇有意趣。这是一栋宽敞体面的私人宅邸/公馆（*ossobniak*），住着不少外地代表和各方宾客。每逢开饭时分，大家便齐聚在公共餐厅里，那里的陈设透着典型德国富商特有的资产阶级品味。整栋宅邸安然度过了革命风暴，几乎未遭丝毫改动。屋内的一切原样未动，甚至连前房主那幅等身大小的油画像，连同两侧悬挂的妻子与子女的肖像，依然端端正正地挂在原来的位置上。走在屋里，仍能隐隐感受到昔日那种体面体统与严谨规整的气氛。
然而，一到就餐时刻，席间弥漫的却是截然不同的精神面貌。餐桌的主座上坐着V某——一位身穿英式剪裁军装的红军（Red Army）军官。他是前来“莫斯科中央”参加某项极其重要会议的乌克兰（Ukraine）代表团团长。他身材魁梧壮实，年纪不超过30岁，举手投足透着军人特有的威严干练与发号施令的从容气度。他曾屡次同卡列金和邓尼金（Denikin）的白匪叛军浴血奋战，身上屡遭战伤。早在当年担任沙皇旧军队军官时，他就已秘密投身革命。后来他所在的党派——南方左翼社会革命党——集体加入了乌克兰共产党。
坐在他身旁的是K某，黑发黑髯，当年在德军刺刀扶植斯科罗帕茨基篡权、中央拉达被暴力解散前，他曾是中央拉达的核心成员。在K某右侧是另一位来自乌克兰的代表，那是一位留着柔软黑色胡须的大学生，他是全团唯一懂英语的人。基辅（Kiev）共产党机关报的主编以及2名年轻女性亦在这一代表团随行之列。
在外国访客中，有一位化名“赫尔曼”（Herman）的中年德国同志，在长期的革命斗争中已熬得两鬓斑白、面容沧桑。他是作为斯巴达克同盟（Spartacus Party）少数派的正式代表，专程前来寻求布尔什维克在道义与财政上的全力援助；但他愤愤不平地抱怨说，拉狄克（Radek）断然拒绝承认他们这支反叛的少数派。挨着赫尔曼坐着的是年轻的L某，一名美利坚世界产业工人同盟（I.W.W.）成员，他既无通行护照也无分文盘缠，全凭搭便车流浪一路颠簸辗转潜入了俄国。此外，餐桌上还有数名来自瑞典、荷兰和意大利的随军外国记者，2名日本人，以及一位因某种奇特的误会被当成战俘从西伯利亚押送至此的朝鲜共产党员。
餐桌上热气腾腾的俄式茶炊冒着白烟，一位体态丰腴健美的年轻姑娘正在为我们端茶倒水。她面色红润，带着乡下姑娘特有的质朴淳厚，但言谈举止却大方洒脱、毫无拘谨之态，她随口称呼大家为“同志”（*tovarish*），语调自然亲切，流露出一种骨子里已然成熟的完全平等意识。

===Original===

From snatches of her conversation with the diners I gather that she had been working in a shoe factory till she entered the service of the former owner of the house, before the Revolution, and has remained in the *ossobniak* after it was nationalized. She calls herself a Bolshevik, and speaks familiarly about the proceedings at the meetings of the women Communist circle, at which she often presides.
She seems to personify the great revolutionary upheaval: the master driven from the house, the servant become the equal of the guests, all *tovarishi* in a common cause.
*Surrogat* tea or coffee is served in the morning﻿—one cannot tell the difference. Breakfast consists of several small slices of black bread, a bit of butter and occasionally an attenuated layer of cheese. At dinner we receive a thin soup of fish or vegetables; sometimes there is also a piece of meat, cooked or fried. Supper is usually the same as breakfast. I always feel hungry after meals, but fortunately I still have some American crackers. Everyone watches anxiously if there is an unoccupied seat at the table. In their eyes I read the frank hope that the missing one may not come: there will be a little more soup left for the others.
The Ukrainians bring “private packages” to the table﻿—chunks of *salo* (fat) or pork sausage, wrapped in pieces of paper written on both sides. Yesterday I casually glanced at one of these wrappers. It was a circular letter of the Tsarist police, descriptive of a man charged with the murder of his brother. It was evidently torn out of an office file. Paper is scarce, and even old newspapers are too valuable to be used as wrappers.
The Ukrainians never offer their delicacies to their neighbors at table. Today at dinner I placed my can of condensed milk before the man at my side, but he needed urging before he dared use some in his coffee. I asked him to pass it around. In consternation he protested, “*Tovarish*, keep it for yourself, you’ll need it.” All the others declined at first, but their eyes burned with desire for the “American product.” The can was emptied quickly amid the general smacking of lips and words of admiration in Slavic superlatives. “Miraculous, worshipful,” they cried.
I spend considerable time with the Ukrainians, learning much about their country, its history, language, and its long revolutionary struggle.

===Chinese===

从她与就餐者的只言片语闲谈中，我了解到她在革命前原在一家制鞋厂当女工，后来才进入这座宅邸给前房主当仆役；而在宅邸/公馆（*ossobniak*）被收归国有后，她便作为工作人员留了下来。她自豪地称自己为布尔什维克，并如数家珍地向我们大谈妇女共产主义小组会议上的种种议题——在那类集会上，她经常担任主持人。
她仿佛就是这场惊天动地的革命大风暴的鲜活化身：往日骑在人头上的老爷已被彻底扫地出门，昔日的卑微女仆如今与座上宾客平起平坐，大家都是为了同一个崇高目标而并肩奋斗的同志（*tovarishi*）。
清晨供应的是代用品（*Surrogat*）茶或咖啡——说实话，谁也分辨不出二者究竟有何差别。早餐包括几片薄薄的黑面包、一小块黄油，偶尔还能分到一层薄如蝉翼的干酪片。午餐时我们能领到一碗清汤寡水的鱼汤或蔬菜汤；有时还会配上一小块煮肉或煎肉。晚餐通常与早餐相差无几。每顿饭后我都感到饥肠辘辘，万幸的是我随身还留有一些美制苏打饼干。每当餐桌上出现空位时，每个人都会投去焦虑而探询的目光。从他们的眼神中，我清晰地读出了一种赤裸裸的期盼：但愿缺席的那个人千万别来，这样其他人就能多分到小半勺清汤。
乌克兰代表们常把随身带来的“私人小包裹”带到餐桌上——那是一大块咸猪油膘（*salo*）或猪肉香肠，用写满双面字迹的废纸紧紧包裹着。昨天我无意中瞥了一眼其中一张包装纸，那竟是一份沙皇旧警察局通缉一名涉嫌弑兄凶犯的公文通报，显然是从旧公文档案卷宗里随手撕扯下来的。当下的纸张极度匮乏，甚至连旧报纸都珍贵得舍不得用来充当包装纸。
乌克兰同志从来不主动把自己的私房美味分给同桌的邻座。今天吃午饭时，我主动把自己的一罐炼乳推到了身旁那位同志的面前，但他再三推辞，直到我反复敦促，他才敢小心翼翼地舀了一点兑进自己的咖啡里。我请他顺手把炼乳罐传给其他人享用。他惊愕地连连阻拦道：“同志（*Tovarish*），快自己留着吧，你自己更需要它呢。”起初其他所有人也都纷纷推让，但他们炽热的目光中却分明闪烁着对这罐“美利坚稀罕货”的极度垂涎。在全场吧唧吧唧的咂嘴咀嚼声与斯拉夫语特有的夸张赞叹声中，这只炼乳罐瞬间被刮得干干净净。“简直是神迹，太令人顶礼膜拜了！”大家交口称赞道。
我花了大量时间同这批乌克兰同志交流长谈，从他们那里深入了解了关于乌克兰这个国度、其悠久历史、民族语言以及漫长悲壮的革命抗争史诗。

===Original===

Most of the delegates, though young in years, are old in the revolutionary movement. They worked “underground” under the Tsar, took part in numerous strikes and uprisings, and fought against the Provisional Government. Later, about the end of 1917, when the Rada turned reactionary and made common cause with Kaledin and Krasnov, the notorious White generals, these delegates helped the Bolsheviki to fight them. Then came the German invasion and Hetman Skoropadsky. Again these men fought the Directorium and Petlura, its dictator, after the latter had upset the Hetman. Finally they joined the Communist Party in waging war against Denikin and his counterrevolutionary forces.
A long and desperate struggle, full of suffering and misery. Most of them have lost near and dear ones at the hands of the Whites. The three brothers of the Rada member perished in the various fights. The young wife of the student was outraged and killed by a Denikin officer, while her husband was awaiting execution. Later he succeeded in escaping from prison. He showed me her picture, standing on the desk in his room. A beautiful, radiant creature. His eyes grew moist as he narrated the sad story.
Many visitors call on the Ukrainians. There is no *propusk* system in the Kharitonensky, and people come and go freely. I have made interesting acquaintances, and spent many hours listening to the Ukrainian delegates exchanging experiences with their Russian friends. Some days are like a kaleidoscope of the Revolution, every turn tossing up new facets of variegated hue and brilliancy: stirring incidents of struggle and strife, stories of martyrdom and heroic exploit. They visualize the darkness of the Tsarist dungeons suddenly lit up by the flames of the February Revolution, and the glorious enthusiasm of the liberation. Surpassing joy of freedom, and then the sadness of great hopes unfulfilled, and liberty remaining an empty sound. Again the rising waves of protest; the soldiers fraternizing with the enemy; and then the great October days that sweep capitalism and the bourgeoisie out of Russia, and usher in the new world and the new Humanity.
These men fill me with wonder and admiration. Common workers and soldiers, but yesterday mute slaves, they are today the masters of their fate, the rulers of Russia.

===Chinese===

这些代表虽然年纪尚轻，但在革命风暴中却早已是久经沙场的老兵。早在沙皇统治时期，他们便秘密投身地下工作，组织并参与过无数次罢工与起义暴动，随后又同资产阶级临时政府展开了殊死搏斗。后来，大约在1917年年底，当乌克兰拉达走向彻底反动、并与臭名昭著的白卫军反动将领卡列金及克拉斯诺夫同流合污之际，正是这批代表挺身而出，全力协助布尔什维克同他们浴血厮杀。紧接着便是德意志帝国主义的铁蹄入侵，扶植起傀儡盖特曼斯科罗帕茨基（Hetman Skoropadsky）。在彼得留拉（Petlura）推翻盖特曼、建立起督政府独裁统治之后，这群战士再度奋起反抗独裁者彼得留拉。最终，他们集体加入了共产党（Communist Party），同邓尼金（Denikin）及其麾下的反革命白卫军展开了长期的殊死鏖战。
这是一场充满苦难与血泪的漫长而惨烈的搏杀。他们之中的绝大多数人，都有至亲至爱的骨肉同胞惨遭白匪军的毒手杀害。那位前拉达成员的3位兄弟全都在各次战斗中壮烈牺牲。而那位大学生的年轻妻子，则在其丈夫身陷囹圄、等待执行死刑之际，惨遭一名邓尼金军官的兽性蹂躏并被残忍杀害。后来这位大学生侥幸越狱脱险。他从自己房里的书桌上取出一张妻子的遗照展示给我看：那是一个容貌出众、宛若天使般光彩照人的美丽女子。在讲述这段痛彻心扉的惨剧时，他的眼眶早已盈满了热泪。
前来拜访这批乌克兰同志的访客络绎不绝。在哈里托年斯基宾馆并没有实行严格的进出通行证（*propusk*）管制制度，人们可以自由出入走动。我结识了许多极有见地的新朋友，花费了大量时间静静倾听乌克兰代表们同他们的俄罗斯朋友促膝长谈、交流斗争阅历。某些日子宛若置身于一架展现革命风暴的万花筒中，每一次旋转都会折射出五光十色、绚丽夺目的全新画面：惊心动魄的浴血厮杀、英勇殉道的悲壮史诗与感人至深的英雄壮举。他们生动再现了沙皇幽暗地牢的漫漫长夜如何被二月革命（February Revolution）的熊熊烈焰骤然照亮，再现了解放降临那一刻工农群众所迸发出的无比狂喜。然而，在超越一切的自由狂喜之后，紧随而来的却是伟大宏愿化作泡影的悲凉幻灭，自由沦为一句空洞的辞藻。接着是抗议与反抗浪潮的再度怒卷，前线战士同敌军自发联欢停战；随后便是伟大的十月革命日子到来，将资本主义与资产阶级彻底横扫荡涤出国门，开创出一个崭新的世界与新的人类纪元！
这群人令我心中充满了由衷的赞叹与由衷的崇敬。他们不过是普普通通的工人和士兵，昨天还是任人宰割、沉默无声的卑微奴隶，今天却已昂首挺胸成为自身命运的主宰者，成为掌控全俄政权的主人！

===Original===

There is dignity in their bearing, self-reliance and determination﻿—the spirit of assurance that comes with struggle and the exercise of initiative. The fires of Revolution have forged new men, new personalities.

===Chinese===

他们的举止从容而威严，洋溢着自立自强与坚毅笃定——这种自信沉着的非凡气度，唯有在长期的生死搏斗与主动开创历史的伟大实践中方能淬炼而成。革命的熊熊烈火，已然锻造出了全新的时代新人与伟岸人格。

## VI Chicherin and Karakhan / VI 契切林与卡拉汉
===Original===

*February 24.*﻿—It was 3 a.m. In the Foreign Office correspondents were about and visitors come by appointment with Chicherin. The People’s Commissar for Foreign Affairs has turned night into day.
I found Chicherin at a desk in a large, cold office, an old shawl wrapped around his neck. Almost his first question was “how soon the revolution could be expected in the States.” When I replied that the American workers were still too much under the influence of the reactionary leaders, he called me pessimistic. In a revolutionary time like the present, he thought, even the Federation of Labor must quickly change to a more radical attitude. He was very hopeful of revolutionary developments in England and America in the near future.
We discussed the Industrial Workers of the World, Chicherin saying that he believed I exaggerated their importance as the only revolutionary proletarian movement in America. He considered the Communist Party in that country of far greater influence and significance. He had recently seen several American Communists, he explained, and they informed him on the labor and revolutionary situation in the States.
A clerk entered with a typed sheet. Chicherin scanned it carefully, and began making corrections. His neck shawl kept sliding down on the paper, and impatiently he would throw it back over his shoulder. He read the document again, made more corrections, and looked displeased. “Terribly confused,” he muttered irritably.
“I’ll have it retyped at once,” the clerk said, picking up the paper.
Chicherin impatiently snatched it out of his hand, and without another word his lean, bent figure disappeared through the door. I heard his short, nervous step in the corridor.
“We are used to his ways,” the clerk remarked apologetically.
“I met him on the stairs without hat or coat when I came up,” I said.
“He is all the time between the second and the fourth floor,” the clerk laughed. “He insists on taking every paper to the radio room himself.”
Chicherin returned all out of breath, and took up the conversation again. Messengers and telephone kept interrupting us, Chicherin personally answering every call. He looked worried and preoccupied, with difficulty picking up the thread of our talk.
“We must bend every effort toward recognition,” be said presently, “and especially to lift the blockade.”

===Chinese===

*2月24日。*——当时正是凌晨3点（3 a.m.）。外交人民委员部大楼里随处可见各国记者的身影，还有按约前来拜会契切林（Chicherin）的各方访客。这位外交人民委员完全把黑夜当成了白昼。
我在一间宽大而阴冷透骨的办公室里见到了契切林，他伏在一张办公桌前，脖颈上紧紧围着一条破旧的羊毛披肩。他开口向我提出的第一个问题几乎就是：“美利坚预计何时能爆发革命？”当我回答说美国工人阶级目前仍深受反动工运领袖的深重影响时，他直言我未免太过悲观。在他看来，在眼下这种急风暴雨般的革命时代，哪怕是美国劳工联合会（Federation of Labor）也必将迅速转向更为激进的立场。他对英美两国在不久的将来爆发革命的前景寄予了极大的厚望。
我们探讨了世界产业工人同盟（I.W.W.），契切林直言，他认为我把该组织视为美利坚唯一一支革命无产阶级力量，显然是夸大了其历史重要性。他认为美国共产党（Communist Party）在该国拥有大得多的影响力和历史意义。他解释说，自己最近刚刚接见过几位美共党人，他们向他全面汇报了美利坚当前的工人运动与革命态势。
一名办事员手拿一份打字机打印的文稿走了进来。契切林仔仔细细扫视着文稿，随即开始提笔修改。他脖子上的披肩三番五次滑落到稿纸上，他总是不耐烦地猛然一把将其甩回肩头。他将文件重读了一遍，又增补了数处修改，脸上的神色愈发阴沉不悦。“简直一团混乱！”他焦躁地喃喃自语道。
“我立刻让人重新打印一份，”办事员一边拿起文件一边说道。
契切林却不耐烦地一把从办事员手中夺过文稿，未再吐露半个字，便拖着瘦削而微偻的身躯快步冲出了大门。走廊里回荡起他短促而神经质的匆忙脚步声。
“我们早就习惯了他这种工作作风，”办事员略带歉意地向我解释道。
“我刚才上楼时，在楼梯上也撞见了他，连帽子和大衣都没穿，”我说道。
“他整天都在2楼到4楼（second and the fourth floor）之间来回飞奔，”办事员苦笑着说，“他坚持每一份电文都必须亲自送到无线电发报室去。”
不多时，契切林气喘吁吁地跑了回来，重新恢复了先前的交谈。然而通信员的通报与电话铃声接二连三打断我们的谈话，每一通电话契切林都坚持亲自接听。他神情焦虑、心事重重，好不容易才重新理清我们谈话的头绪。
“我们当前必须倾尽一切努力争取获得国际承认，”他顿了顿说道，“尤其是打破并解除帝国主义的经济封锁。”

===Original===

He hoped much in that direction from the friendly attitude of the workers abroad, and he was pleased to hear of the growing sentiment in the United States for the recall of American troops from Siberia.
“No one wants peace so much as Russia,” he said emphatically. “If the Allies would come to their senses, we would soon enter into commerce with them. We know that business in England and America is eager for such an opportunity.”
“The trouble with the Allies,” he continued, “is that they don’t want to realize that we have the country back of us. They still cling to the hope of some White general rallying the people to his banner. A vain and stupid hope, for Russia is solidly for the Soviet Government.”
I related to Chicherin the experience of the *Buford* deportees on the Finnish border, and repeated to him the request of a certain American correspondent I had met there to be admitted to Russia.
“He is from a bourgeois newspaper,” Chicherin remarked, recalling that the man had been refused a Soviet visa. “On what ground does he apply again?”
“He asked me to tell you that his newspaper was the first in America to take a friendly attitude to the Bolsheviki.”
Chicherin became interested, and promised to consider the application.
“I also need some ‘paper’ from you,” I remarked jestingly, explaining that I was probably the only person in Soviet Russia without “documents,” as I had left Petrograd before they were issued to the *Buford* deportees. He laughed at my being “unidentified,” and recalled the mass meeting of Kronstadt sailors and workers in the Tshinizelli Circus in Petrograd, in 1917, to protest against my being “identified” with the Mooney case and extradited to California.
He ordered the clerk to prepare a “little paper” for me, and he signed it, remarking that there was much work in the Foreign Office, and that he hoped I would help with translations.
When I looked at the document I saw that it referred in very favorable terms to the “well-known American revolutionist,” but that there was no mention of my being an Anarchist. Was that term avoided purposely, I wondered? What cause would there be for it in Soviet Russia? I felt as if a veil were stealthily drawn over my personality.
---
Later in the day I visited Karakhan. Tall, good-looking, and well-groomed, he sat leisurely in a sumptuous office, his feet resting on a fine tiger skin.

===Chinese===

他将打破封锁的莫大希望寄托于海外工人阶级对苏俄的友好声援态度；获悉美利坚国内要求从西伯利亚撤回美军部队的呼声日益高涨，他感到十分欣慰。
“全世界没有任何一个国家像苏维埃俄国这样渴望和平，”他语气极其笃定地说道，“只要协约国头脑清醒过来，我们很快就能同他们开展双边经贸往来。我们深知英美两国的工商界对这种通商良机早已望眼欲穿。”
“协约国的大毛病在于，”他接着说道，“他们始终不肯正视全俄广大人民是坚决拥护我们的这一铁的事实。他们依然死抱幻想，盼着哪个白匪头子能振臂一呼、聚拢民众归顺其反动旗帜。这纯粹是痴人说梦、愚不可及，因为全俄人民是铁板一块坚决拥护苏维埃政权的。”
我向契切林详述了“布福德号”（*Buford*）遣返同胞在芬兰边境的遭遇，并向他转达了我在边境结识的一位美国记者的入境申请。
“他是资产阶级报社派来的记者，”契切林忆及此人此前曾被苏维埃拒签，随即问道，“他凭什么再次提出申请呢？”
“他托我转告您，他的报社当年在全美是第一家对布尔什维克采取友善客观立场的报纸。”
契切林顿时来了兴致，当场应允重新慎重考虑这项申请。
“另外我也急需从您这里讨一份‘公文条子’，”我半开玩笑地说道，向他解释说我大概是整个苏维埃俄国唯一一个没有合法“身份文件”的人，因为在彼得格勒（Petrograd）向“布福德号”人员统一发证之前我就已经启程离开了。听闻我是个“身份未明者”，他不禁哑然失笑，并深情忆及1917年在彼得格勒奇尼泽利马戏院（Tshinizelli Circus），喀琅施塔得（Kronstadt）的水兵与工人们举行万人群众大会、严正抗议我被无端卷入穆尼冤案并险遭引渡去加利福尼亚的往事。
他吩咐办事员当即为我草拟了一份“小证明信”，并提笔签了字，随口说道外交人民委员部公文堆积如山，十分期盼我能协助分担一些英译汉或互译工作。
接过文件一看，我发现信中以极其赞许推崇的措辞称我为“美利坚著名革命家”，却只字未提我是一名安那其主义者。我不禁暗自思忖：他们是有意回避这个词吗？在堂堂苏维埃俄国，究竟有何必要避讳这一点呢？我心中隐隐感到，仿佛有一层无形的纱幔被悄无声息地笼罩在了我的人格本色之上。
---
当天晚些时候，我前往拜会了卡拉汉（Karakhan）。他身材颀长英挺，相貌堂堂，仪表修饰得无可挑剔；此时正神态悠闲地端坐在一间极尽奢华的宽大办公室里，双脚踏在一张精美的华贵虎皮地毯上。

===Original===

His appearance justified the humorous characterization I heard of him in the anteroom. “A Bolshevik who can wear white gloves gracefully,” someone had said.
Karakhan asked me to converse in Russian. “Nature has given me no talent for languages,” he remarked. We discussed the labor situation abroad, and he expressed himself confident of the speedy bankruptcy of international capitalism. He was enthusiastic about the “growing influence of the Communist Party in England and America,” and seemed much displeased when I pointed out that his optimism was entirely unjustified by the actual state of affairs. He listened with a smile of well-bred incredulity as I spoke of the reaction following the war and the persecution of radicalism, in the States. “But the workers of England and America, inspired by the Communists, will presently force their governments to lift the blockade,” he insisted. I sought to impress him that Russia must make up her mind to rely upon herself for the reconstruction of her economic life. “Of course, of course,” he assented, but there was no conviction in his tone.
“Our hope is in the lifting of the blockade,” he said again, “and then our industries will develop quickly. At present we are handicapped by the lack of machinery and skilled labor.”
Referring to the peasantry, Karakhan asserted that the farmer profited by the Revolution more than any other part of the population. “Why,” he exclaimed, “in the villages you will find upholstered furniture, French mirrors, graphophones, and pianos, all given to them by the city in exchange for food. The luxuries of the mansion have been transferred to the hovel,” he laughed, pleased with his bon mot and gracefully stroking his well-trimmed black beard. “We have declared ‘war to the palaces, peace to the huts,’ ” he continued, “and the *muzhik* lives like a *barin*2 now. But the Russian peasant is backward and deeply imbued with the petty bourgeois spirit of ownership. The *kulaki*3 often refuse to contribute of their surplus, but the Army and the city proletariat must be fed, of course. We have therefore been compelled to resort to the *razvyorstka*4﻿—an unpleasant system, forced upon us by the Allied blockade. The peasants must do their share to sustain the soldiers and the workers who are the vanguard of the Revolution, and on the whole they do so.

===Chinese===

他的这副翩翩风度，恰好印证了我在接待室里听到的那句关于他的诙谐打趣：“这是一位戴得起、也戴得惯白手套的布尔什维克。”
卡拉汉请我用俄语同他交谈。“老天爷没赏给我学外语的天赋，”他自嘲道。我们畅谈了海外的工人运动态势，他对国际资本主义行将迅速破产充满坚如磐石的信心。他对“共产党在英美两国与日俱增的巨大影响力”赞不绝口；而当我直言不讳指出，从客观现实状况来看他的这种乐观情绪完全站不住脚时，他显得十分不悦。当我向他陈述美利坚战后反动狂潮的疯狂反扑以及对激进派的残酷政治迫害时，他只是带着一种受过良好教养的怀疑微笑静静听着。“但是英美两国的产业工人受到共产党的感召，很快就会以强硬手段迫使其反动政府解除封锁，”他固执己见地强调道。我试图让他认清一个现实：在重建自身国民经济的道路上，苏维埃俄国必须下定决心走完全依靠自身力量的自力更生之路。“那是自然，那是自然，”他随口附和道，但语调中却感受不到半点坚定的信念。
“我们的首要厚望仍寄托在封锁的彻底解除上，”他又重申道，“届时我们的现代工业必将实现一日千里的飞跃。眼下我们受制于机器设备的极度匮乏和熟练技术工人的严重短缺。”
在谈及广大农民阶级时，卡拉汉断言，农民是这场伟大革命中获益最为丰厚的人群。“何止如此！”他兴致勃勃地高声说道，“在如今的乡村农舍里，你能见到高档沙发软垫家具、法式全身穿衣镜、留声机甚至昂贵的钢琴——这一切全是城市居民为了换取糊口粮食而奉送给乡下的。昔日深宅大院里的奢华珍玩，如今全搬进了庄稼汉的低矮茅舍！”他开怀大笑起来，显然对自己的这句妙语得意洋洋，一面优雅地抚弄着修剪整齐的黑色胡须。“我们庄严宣告了‘对宫殿开战，给茅屋和平！’”他接着说道，“如今的庄稼汉/农夫（*muzhik*）日子过得赛过旧社会的老爷2（*barin*）。然而俄国农民思想落后闭塞，骨子里深受小资产阶级私有观念的毒害。富农3（*kulaki*）经常死守余粮拒不缴纳，可是前线红军部队和城市的工人阶级总得张嘴吃饭啊！因此我们迫不得已，只能推行余粮征集制4（*razvyorstka*）——这是一项令人不悦的严苛制度，完全是被协约国反动派的无情封锁硬逼出来的。农民必须承担起自己的一份历史责任，以供养作为革命先锋主力军的工农子弟兵和产业工人；总的来看，他们大体上还是遵照执行的。”

===Original===

Occasionally the *muzhiki* resist requisition, and in such cases the military is called upon. Unfortunate occurrences, but not very frequent. They usually happen in the Ukraine, our richest wheat and corn region﻿—the peasants are mostly *kulaki* there.”
Karakhan lit a cigar and continued: “Of course, when requisition is made, the Government pays. That is, it gives the peasant its written obligation, as proof of its good faith. Those ‘papers’ will be honored as soon as civil war is over, and our economic life put in order.”
The conversation turned to the recent arrests in Moscow in connection with a counterrevolutionary conspiracy unearthed by the Cheka. “Oh, yes,” Karakhan smiled, “they are still plotting.” He grew thoughtful for a moment, then added: “We abolished capital punishment, but in certain cases exceptions have to be made.”
He leaned comfortably back in his armchair and continued:
“One mustn’t be sentimental. I remember how hard it was for me, way back in 1917, when I myself had to arrest my former college chums. Yes, with my own hands”﻿—he held out both hands, white and well-cared for﻿—“but what will you? The Revolution imposes stern duties upon us. We mustn’t be sentimental,” he repeated.
The subject changed to India, Karakhan remarking that a delegate had just arrived from that country. The movement there was revolutionary, though of nationalistic character, he thought, and could be exploited to keep England in check. Learning that while in California I was in touch with Hindu revolutionists and Anarchists of the Hindustan Gadar organization, he suggested the advisability of getting in communication with them. I promised to look after the matter.

===Chinese===

“偶尔也有个别庄稼汉（*muzhiki*）暴力抗拒征粮，遇到这种恶性事态，就不得不动用军队武力镇压了。这确实令人痛心，但好在并不算十分频繁。这种流血冲突通常多发于乌克兰（Ukraine），那里是我们最富饶的小麦与玉米粮仓——当地的农户绝大多数都是顽固的富农（*kulaki*）。”
卡拉汉点燃了一支雪茄，接着说道：“当然，在依法执行征粮时，政府是如数付账的。也就是说，政府会向农民开具具有法律效力的书面凭证，以此作为苏维埃政府守信的信誉担保。一旦国内革命战争宣告胜利结束、国家经济生活恢复正轨，这些‘借据凭单’定将全额兑现补偿。”
话题随后转到了莫斯科（Moscow）最近侦破的一起反革命叛乱阴谋所引发的系列大搜捕，该案系契卡（Cheka）一手破获。“哦，可不是嘛，”卡拉汉微微一笑，“那帮家伙直到现在依然在暗中密谋暴动。”他沉吟片刻，随即补充道：“我们虽然在法律上早已废除了死刑，但在某些极端特殊的大案要案面前，有时也不得不破例处置。”
他悠然自得地往扶手大皮椅深处靠了靠，继续侃侃而谈：
“干革命绝不能感情用事。我还清晰记得早在1917年那阵子，当我自己不得不亲手下令逮捕我当年的大学同窗老友时，内心是何等的痛苦纠结。是的，正是用这双手亲手抓捕的，”——他摊开双手，十指修长洁白、保养得极尽细腻——“然而，又能如何呢？伟大革命赋予我们铁一般的冷酷使命。我们决不能妇人之仁、感情用事，”他又刻意重复了一遍。
随后话题转向了印度问题，卡拉汉提及一位来自印度的代表刚刚抵京。在他看来，那里的反帝反殖运动虽然带有强烈的民族主义色彩，但本质上依然属于革命范畴，完全可以善加利用以牵制遏制大英帝国。获悉我在加利福尼亚期间曾同印度革命党人以及“印度起义党”（Gadar）的安那其主义者有过密切往来，他当即建议不妨同他们重新建立秘密联络。我答应他会亲自跟进这项事宜。

## VII The Market / VII 集市
===Original===

I like the feel of the hard snow singing under my feet. The streets are alive with people﻿—a striking contrast to Petrograd, which gave me the impression of a graveyard. The narrow sidewalks are crooked and slippery, and everybody walks in the middle of the street. Rarely does a streetcar pass, though an auto creaks by occasionally. The people are better dressed than in Petrograd and do not look so pale and exhausted. More soldiers are about and persons clad in leather. Cheka men, I am told. Almost everybody carries a bundle on his back or pulls a little sleigh loaded with a bag of potatoes dripping a blackish fluid. They walk with a preoccupied air and roughly push their way ahead.
Turning the corner into the Miasnitskaya Street, I noticed a large yellow poster on the wall. My eye caught the word *Prikaz* in big red letters. *Prikaz*﻿—order﻿—instinctively the expression associated itself in my mind with the old regime. The poster was couched in the familiar style, “I command,” “I order,” repeating themselves with the frequency usual in the old police proclamations. “I command the citizens of Moscow,” I read. Citizens? I sought the date. It was marked January 15, 1920, and was signed by the Commissar of Militia. The *Prikaz* vividly recalled the gendarmes and the Cossack order of things, and I resented it. The Revolution should find another language, I thought.
I passed the Red Square where the heroes of the Revolution are buried along the Kremlin wall. Thousands of others, as devoted and heroic, lie in unknown graves throughout the country and on the fronts. A new world is not born without pain. Much hunger and misery Russia is suffering still, the heritage of the past which the Revolution has come to abolish forever.
On the wall of the old Duma, near the Iverskaya Gate, I read the legend cut into the stone: “Religion is opium for the people.” But in the chapel nearby services were being held and the place was crowded. The cassocked priest, long hair down his back, was musically reciting the Greek-Catholic litany. The worshipers, mostly women, knelt on the cold floor, continuously crossing themselves. Several men, shabbily dressed and carrying portfolios, came in quietly, bowed low and crossed themselves reverently.
A little further I came upon a marketplace, the historic Okhotny Ryad, opposite the Hotel National.

===Chinese===

我喜欢脚下坚硬的积雪发出的咯吱咯吱清脆声响。大街上人头攒动、生机勃勃——这与给我留下宛若坟场般死寂印象的彼得格勒（Petrograd）形成了鲜明的反差。狭窄的人行道弯弯曲曲、泥泞湿滑，人人都习惯走在街道正当中。极少有有轨电车驶过，偶尔能见到一辆小汽车嘎吱嘎吱慢腾腾开过去。这里的人们衣着明显比彼得格勒体面考究，面容也不显得那般苍白枯槁。街头有更多的军人，还有许多身穿皮衣的人走动——旁人告诉我，那些人是契卡（Cheka）便衣人员。几乎每个人背后都背着包裹，或是拖着一架简易小雪橇，上面驮着一袋正滴淌着黑乎乎汁液的土豆。行人们个个面带焦虑之色，脚步匆匆地推搡着往前赶路。
拐过街角走上肉商大街（Miasnitskaya Street），我注意到墙壁上张贴着一张醒目的黄色大布告。我一眼捕捉到了上面用大红字印刷的醒目词汇——*Prikaz*（命令/法令）。*Prikaz*——命令——这个词在我的脑海中本能地与沙皇反动旧体制紧密勾连在一起。布告通篇充斥着令人熟悉的旧官僚腔调：“我下令”、“我严令”，频繁重复的频率与沙皇警察旧告示毫无二致。“我命令莫斯科（Moscow）市民们……”我读道。市民？我寻找着落款日期。上面赫然标注着1920年1月15日，由民警人民委员亲自签字下发。这份命令/法令（*Prikaz*）令我不由得栩栩如生地联想起了当年宪兵的严苛与哥萨克铁蹄统治下的旧秩序，我心头顿生抵触。我暗想：伟大的革命本应学会运用一种崭新的语言来同人民沟通！
我穿过了红场（Red Square），革命先烈们长眠在克里姆林宫红墙脚下。而在全俄各地以及各大前线，还有成千上万同样忠诚英勇的无名烈士长眠在未被记载的荒冢之中。一个崭新世界的诞生绝不可能毫无阵痛。如今苏维埃俄国依然在承受着极其沉重的饥荒与苦难，而这一切不过是旧时代遗留下来的沉疴余孽，革命终将将其彻底扫除荡涤。
在靠近伊维尔斯卡亚城门（Iverskaya Gate）的旧杜马大厦墙壁上，我读到了镌刻在坚硬石壁上的醒目标语：“宗教是麻醉人民的鸦片。”然而在近旁的一座东正教小礼拜堂内，宗教仪式正在照常进行，堂内挤得水泄不通。身穿黑袍法衣、长发披肩的神甫正用抑扬顿挫的吟诵调领念着希腊东正教连祷文。信众绝大多数是妇女，她们双膝跪在冰冷坚硬的石地面上，不断在胸前画着十字祈祷。几位衣衫褴褛、夹着公文包的中年男子悄无声息地溜了进来，深深鞠躬下拜，虔诚地在胸前划着十字。
再往前走几步，我便来到了国民宾馆（Hotel National）对面的猎人商行集市（Okhotny Ryad）——这是一处极富历史渊源的传统露天大市场。

===Original===

Rows of little stalls on one side, the more pretentious stores on the other, the sidewalk between them﻿—it has all remained as in the time past. Fish and butter were offered, bread and eggs, meat, candy, and cosmetics﻿—a living page from the life the Revolution has abolished. An old lady with finely chiseled features, in a threadbare coat, stood quietly holding a Japanese vase. Near her was another woman, younger and intellectual looking, with a basket containing crystal wine glasses of rare workmanship. On the corner little boys and girls were selling cigarettes and *lepyoshki*, a kind of potato pancake, and further I saw a crowd surrounding an old woman busily dishing out *tshtchi* (cabbage soup).
“A fiver, a fiver!” she cried in a hoarse, cracked voice. “Delicious *tshtchi*, only five kopeks!”
The steaming pot breathed an appetizing odor. “Give me a plate,” I said, handing the woman a rouble.
“God be with you, little uncle,” she eyed me suspiciously, “a fiver it costs, five kopeks.”
“Here’s a whole rouble,” I replied.
The crowd laughed good-humoredly. “She means five roubles,” someone explained, “a rouble is only a kopeck.”
“It ain’t worth that, either,” a little urchin chimed in.
The hot liquid sent a pleasing warmth through my body, but the taste of *voblia* (fish) was insufferable. I made a motion to return the dish.
“Please permit me,” a man at my elbow addressed me. He was of middle age, evidently of the intelligentsia, and spoke in accents of the cultured Russian. His shiny dark eyes lit up features of a sickly pallor. “Your permission,” he repeated, indicating the dish.
I handed him the plate. Avidly, like a starved man, he swallowed the hot *tshtchi*, gleaning the last shred of cabbage. Then he thanked me profusely.
I noticed a thick volume under his arm. “Bought it here?” I asked.
“Ah, no, how is it possible! I have been trying to sell it since morning. I’m a civil engineer, and this is one of my last,” he patted the book affectionately. “But excuse me, I must hurry to the store before it is too late. They haven’t given any bread out for two days. Extremely obliged to you.”
I felt a tug at my elbow. “Buy some cigarettes, little uncle,”﻿—a young girl, extremely emaciated, held her hand out to me. Her fingers, stiff with cold, were insecurely clutching the cigarettes lying loose in her palm.

===Chinese===

一侧是排成一线的简易小货摊，另一侧是门面略显气派的临街商铺，中间隔着一条人行道——这里的一切依然如昔日旧时光那般照旧运转。摊位上兜售着咸鱼和黄油、面包与鸡蛋、鲜肉、糖果乃至胭脂香水——活脱脱是一幅本已被革命彻底废黜的昔日旧生活的真实写照。一位面部轮廓端庄标致、身着破旧大衣的老妇人静静伫立着，双手小心捧着一只日本瓷瓶。在她身旁是另一位面容年轻、颇有知识分子气质的女子，臂弯里挎着一只竹篮，里面摆放着做工精巧罕见的水晶高脚酒杯。在街角，几个小男孩小姑娘正叫卖着香烟和土豆烙饼（*lepyoshki*），再往前走，我瞧见一群人正团团围住一位手脚麻利盛着卷心菜热汤（*tshtchi*）的老妪。
“五块，五块！”她用沙哑破锣般的嗓音高声叫卖着，“香喷喷的菜汤，只要5个戈比啦！”
热气腾腾的大铁锅散发出一股诱人的香气。“给我来一碗，”我一边说，一边递给老妇人1个卢布纸币。
“上帝保佑您啊，大叔，”她满脸狐疑地打量着我，“这汤卖5个戈比呢，可得要五块钱啊！”
“这可是整整1个卢布呢，”我答道。
周围的人群善意地哄堂大笑起来。“她说的其实是5个卢布，”有人向我解释道，“如今1个卢布贬值贬得连1个戈比都不如了。”
“连半个戈比都不值呢，”旁边一个小顽童随声附和道。
滚烫的热汤下肚，带来一阵令人舒畅的暖意，但里面掺杂的咸鱼干（*voblia*）那股刺鼻腥臭却令人实在难以下咽。我伸手打算把汤碗退还回去。
“劳驾，请赏给我吧，”挨着我身旁的一位男子凑上前来向我央求道。他步入中年，神态举止俨然是一位旧知识分子，操着一口字正腔圆的典雅俄语。他那双乌黑发亮的眼睛嵌在一张病态苍白的脸庞上。“请赏脸成全，”他指着菜汤碗再次恳求道。
我把汤碗递到了他手里。他犹如饿虎扑食般，贪婪地将滚烫的菜汤（*tshtchi*）一饮而尽，甚至连碗底最后一点卷心菜碎屑都舔得干干净净。随后他对我千恩万谢、感激涕零。
我注意到他腋下紧紧夹着一本厚重的精装大部头。“刚从这市场上买来的吗？”我探询地问道。
“唉，哪能呢，怎么可能买得起啊！我从大清早起就守在这儿打算把它卖掉换口粮呢。我是一名土木工程师，这可是我手里仅剩的最后一批专业藏书之一了，”他深情地摩挲拍打着书脊。“不过实在对不住，我得赶去配给商店碰碰运气了，再晚就来不及了。他们已经整整2天没发过一丁点面包了。真是太感激您了！”
这时我感到有人在轻轻拉扯我的衣肘。“大叔，买两包香烟吧，”——一位身形极其单薄瘦弱的小姑娘向我伸出了双手。她那冻得僵硬通红的手指，战战兢兢地紧紧拢着掌心里散落的几支香烟。

===Original===

She was without hat or coat, an old shawl wrapped tightly about her slender form.
“Buy, *barin*,” she pleaded in a thin voice.
“What *barin*,” a girl nearby resented. “No more *barin*,5 we’re all *tovarishi* now. Don’t you know,” she gently chided.
She was comely, not over seventeen, her red lips strongly contrasting with the paleness of her face. Her voice was soft and musical, her speech pleasing.
For a moment her eyes were full upon me, then she motioned me aside.
“Buy me a little white bread,” she said modestly, yet not in the least shamefaced; “for my sick mother.”
“You don’t work?” I asked.
“Don’t work!” she exclaimed, with a touch of resentment. “I’m typing in the *sovnarkhoz*, but we get only one-half pound of bread now, and little of anything else.”
“*Oblava!6 militsioneri*!” There were loud cries and shouts, and I heard the clanking of sabres. The market was surrounded by armed men.
The people were terror-stricken. Some sought to escape, but the military circle was complete; no one was permitted to leave without showing his papers. The soldiers were gruff and imperious, swearing coarse oaths and treating the crowd with roughness.
A *militsioner* had kicked over the *tshtchi* pot, and was dragging the old woman by the arm. “Let me get my pot, little father, my pot,” she pleaded.
“We’ll show you pots, you cursed speculator,” the man threatened, pulling her along.
“Don’t maltreat the woman,” I protested.
“Who are you? How dare you interfere!” a man in a leather cap shouted at me. “Your papers!”
I produced my identification document. The Chekist glanced at it, and his eye quickly caught the stamp of the Foreign Office and Chicherin’s signature. His manner changed. “Pardon me,” he said. “Pass the foreign *tovarish*,” he ordered the soldiers.
On the street the *militsioneri* were leading off their prisoners. Front and rear marched the soldiers with bayoneted rifles held horizontally, ready for action. On either flank were Cheka men, their revolvers pointed at the backs of the prisoners. I caught sight of the *tshtchi* woman and the tall engineer, the thick volume still under his arm; I saw the aristocratic old lady in the rear, the two girls I had spoken to, and several boys, some of them barefoot.
I turned toward the market.

===Chinese===

她既没有戴帽子也没穿棉大衣，仅用一条破旧的披巾紧紧裹着瘦削纤弱的身躯。
“买一点吧，老爷（*barin*），”她用微弱纤细的声音苦苦央求道。
“叫什么老爷！”身旁另一个姑娘气鼓鼓地反驳道。“哪还有什么老爷5（*barin*），如今大家全都是同志（*tovarishi*）了。你难道连这都不懂吗，”她温和地责备道。
这个插话的姑娘容貌秀美，年纪不超过17岁，红润的双唇与她那惨白无血色的脸颊形成了极强烈的反差。她的声音清脆甜美、富有韵律，吐字极为动听。
有一瞬间，她的双眼直勾勾地凝视着我，随后朝我示意走开两步。
“替我买一小块白面包吧，”她语气谦卑，神色间却丝毫不见羞赧窘迫，“是给我卧病在床的母亲买的。”
“你难道没有参加工作吗？”我关切地问道。
“怎么没工作！”她惊呼起来，语气中夹杂着几分委屈与忿懑，“我在最高国民经济委员会（*sovnarkhoz*）做打字员，可如今我们每人每天只能领到区区半磅（0.5磅）面包，其余物资几乎什么都领不到！”
“大搜捕！6（*Oblava!*）民警来了！”四周突然爆发出一阵凄厉尖锐的叫喊与惊呼，紧接着便传来了刺耳的马刀出鞘碰撞声。整个集市已被荷枪实弹的武装军警密密麻麻团团包围。
人群陷入了极度的惊恐恐慌之中。有人试图夺路逃跑，但军警构筑的封锁包围圈已水泄不通；任何人未经盘查证件一律严禁放行。军警们神情蛮横粗暴，嘴里狂飙着粗鄙下流的咒骂，极其野蛮粗暴地推搡押解着群众。
一名武装民警（*militsioner*）一脚踹翻了盛着卷心菜汤（*tshtchi*）的大铁锅，粗暴地反拽着那位卖汤老妪的胳膊往前拖。“让我捡起我的锅吧，沙皇小父亲，那是我的铁锅啊！”老妇人哀嚎乞求道。
“老子叫你锅！你这个该死的投机倒把奸商！”男子一边恶狠狠地恐吓咒骂，一边强行将她往前拖拽。
“不要虐待妇女！”我忍不住出声抗议道。
“你算老几？竟敢公然妨碍公务！”一个头戴皮帽的契卡便衣冲我怒吼道，“出示你的证件！”
我掏出了自己的身份证明文件。这位契卡人员扫了一眼，目光瞬间落在了外交人民委员部的印章以及契切林（Chicherin）亲笔签名的字迹上。他的态度骤然剧变。“多有得罪，请原谅，”他说道，转头向士兵们下达命令道：“放行这位外国同志（*tovarish*）。”
街道上，武装民警正押解着大批被捕人员离去。队伍前后两侧由荷枪实弹、横端刺刀的战士警戒护送，随时准备投入战斗；左右两翼则是契卡便衣人员，手枪黑洞洞的枪口直直顶在被捕人员的脊梁骨上。我一眼瞥见了卖热汤的老妪和那位身材高挑的工程师——那本厚重的大部头依然紧紧夹在他的腋下；我看到了落在队伍最后的贵妇老太太，看到了刚才同我搭话的那两个年轻姑娘，以及几个瘦弱的小男孩，其中有的孩子竟然在冰天雪地里光着脚丫。
我转过身，重新望向那片集市。

===Original===

Broken china and torn lace littered the ground; cigarettes and *lepyoshki* lay in the snow, stamped down by dirty boots, and dogs rapaciously fought for the bits of food. Children and women cowered in the doorways on the opposite side, their eyes following the soldiers left on guard at the market. The booty taken from the traders was being piled on a cart by Chekists.
I looked at the stores. They remained open; they had not been raided.
---
In the evening I dined at the Hotel National with several Communist friends who had known me in America. I used the occasion to call their attention to the scene I had witnessed on the marketplace. Instead of being indignant, as I expected, they chided me for my “sentimentality.” No mercy should be shown the speculators, they said. Trade must be rooted out: buying and selling cultivates petty middle-class psychology. It should be suppressed.
“Do you call those barefoot boys and old women speculators?” I protested.
“The worst kind,” replied R﻿—﻿—, formerly member of the Socialist Labor Party of America. “They live better than we do, eat white bread, and have money hidden away.”
“And the stores? Why are they permitted to continue?” I asked.
“We closed most of them,” put in K﻿—﻿—, Commissar of a Soviet House. “Soon there will not be any of them left open.”
“Listen, Berkman,” said D﻿—﻿—, an influential leader of the labor unions, in a leather coat, “you don’t know those ‘poor old men and women,’ as you call them. By day they sell *lepyoshki*, but at night they deal in diamonds and valuta. Every time their homes are searched we find valuables and money. Believe me, I know what I’m talking about. I have had charge of such searching parties myself.”
He looked severely at me, then continued: “I tell you, those, people are inveterate speculators, and there is no way of stopping them. The best thing is to put them to the wall, *razstrelyat*﻿—shoot them,” he raised his voice in growing irritation.
“Not seriously?” I protested.
“No? Eh?” he shouted in a rage. “We’re doing it every day.”
“But capital punishment is abolished.”
“It’s rarely resorted to now,” R﻿—﻿— tried to smooth matters, “and that only in the military zone.”
The labor Chekist eyed me with cold, inimical gaze, “Defending speculation is counterrevolutionary,” he said, leaving the table.

===Chinese===

碎裂的瓷器碎片和被撕碎的蕾丝散落一地；香烟和土豆烙饼（*lepyoshki*）在冰雪中被泥泞污秽的皮靴践踏得稀烂，几条野狗在雪地里贪婪凶猛地争夺着残存的面饼碎屑。妇女和儿童瑟瑟发抖地蜷缩在对面街巷的门廊深处，双眼惊恐万状地死盯着留守在市场上站岗的士兵。契卡便衣们正忙着将从商贩手中没收抄没来的大批“战利品”成堆装上一辆大马车。
我抬头望向那些临街正规商铺。它们居然全都大门敞开照常营业，根本没有遭到任何搜查或突袭。
---
傍晚时分，我同几位当年在美利坚与我相识的共产党老朋友在国民宾馆（Hotel National）共进晚餐。我借此机会，郑重向他们提及了今天白天在集市上亲眼目睹的骇人惨状。然而，他们非但没有如我预期的那样义愤填膺，反而严厉责备我太过“妇人之仁、感情用事”。他们声称，对投机倒把分子决不能心慈手软。私人买卖必须斩草除根：一切买卖交易都会滋生小资产阶级反动心理，必须以铁腕坚决取缔镇压！
“难道你们把那些赤脚的穷苦孩子和白发苍苍的老太太也打成投机倒把分子吗？”我义愤填膺地质问道。
“他们正是最狡诈恶毒的一类，”曾是美利坚社会主义劳工党成员的R某回答道，“他们的日子过得比我们滋润得多，顿顿吃白面包，暗地里不知私藏了多少金银细软！”
“那那些临街大商铺呢？为何允许他们大模大样继续开门营业？”我反问道。
“绝大多数商铺早就被我们勒令查封了，”一位苏维埃招待所特派员K某插话道，“要不了多久，一家商铺都不会留给他们开！”
“听着，伯克曼（Berkman），”一位身居工会要职、身穿皮大衣的重量级工运领袖D某开口说道，“你根本不了解你口中所谓的那些‘可怜的老头老太’。白天他们装模作样沿街叫卖土豆烙饼（*lepyoshki*），到了晚上背地里却在倒腾钻石珠宝和违禁外汇（valuta）！每次抄家搜查他们的住处，我们都能起获大批金银硬币和巨额钞票。相信我，我绝不是信口开河，我自己就曾亲自带队负责过好多次这种抄家搜查行动。”
他神色极其严厉地怒视着我，随后接着说道：“我明明白白告诉你，这帮人全都是不可救药的职业投机倒把惯犯，常规手段根本刹不住这股歪风邪气。唯一的根治办法就是把他们统统推到墙根底下，就地正法/枪毙（*razstrelyat*）——全给老子毙了！”随着怒气不断上涌，他的嗓门陡然拔高。
“你不是当真的吧？”我震惊得抗议道。
“当真？哈！”他勃然大怒地吼道，“老子们天天都在这么干！”
“但国家法律早已明令废除死刑了啊！”
“现在极少动用死刑了，”R某赶忙出面打圆场缓和气氛，“而且仅限在军事戒严战区内破例执行。”
这位兼管工会的契卡干部用极其冰冷、充满敌意的凌厉目光狠狠剜了我一眼：“替投机倒把辩护，本身就是反革命行为！”说完，他怒气冲冲地拂袖离席而去。

## VIII In the Moskkommune / VIII 在“莫斯科公社”
===Original===

The Commissar of our *ossobniak*, having to lay in provisions, invited me to accompany him to the Moskkommune. It is the great food supply center, a tremendous organization that feeds Moscow and its environs. Its trains have the right of way on all lines and carry food from parts as distant as Siberia and Turkestan. Not a pound of flour can be issued by any of the “stores”﻿—the distributing points scattered throughout the city﻿—without a written order signed and countersigned by the various bureaus of the Commune. From this center each “distributor” receives the amount necessary to supply the demands of the given district, according to the norm allowed on the bread and other cards.
The Moskkommune is the most popular and active institution; it is a beehive swarming with thousands of employees, busy determining the different categories of *pyock* and issuing “authorizations.” Besides the bread rations, sugar, tea, etc., given to the citizen by the “store” of his district, he also receives his ration in the institution that employs him. The *pyock* differs according to the “quality” of the citizen and the position he occupies. At present soldiers and sailors receive 2½ lbs. of bread per day; Soviet employees 3 lbs. every two days; those not working﻿—because of age, sickness or disability other than military﻿—receive ¾ lb. There are special categories of “preferred” *pyock*; the academical for old scientists and professors whose merits are recognized by the State, and also for old revolutionists not actively opposed to the Communists. There are “preferred” *pyocks* in important institutions, such as the Comintern (the Third International), the Narkominodel (Foreign Office), Narkomput (Commissariat of Railways), Sovnarkhoz (Soviet of Public Economy), and others. Members of the Communist Party have the opportunity of receiving extra rations through their Communist organizations, and preference is given them in the departments issuing clothing. There is also a Sovnarkom *pyock*, the best to be had, for important Communist officials, Commissars, their first assistants, and other high-placed functionaries. The Soviet Houses, where foreign visitors and influential delegates are quartered, such as Karakhan’s *ossobniak* and the Hotel Lux, receive special food supplies. These include fats and starches (butter, cheese, meat, sugar, candy, etc.)

===Chinese===

我们公馆（*ossobniak*）的特派员因要采购储备口粮给养，特邀我陪同他前往“莫斯科公社”旅馆/供应总署（Moskkommune）。这是全城庞大的粮食供应中枢，是一个负责供养莫斯科（Moscow）及其周边郊区的庞大官僚机构。该机构调配的货运列车在全国各条铁路线上皆享有绝对优先通行权，源源不断地从远至西伯利亚和突厥斯坦的边陲各地运来粮食。遍布全市各街区的所谓“配给站”或经销点，若无公社下属各科室联合签署盖章的书面正式批条，休想擅自分发调拨哪怕一磅面粉。正是从这一供应中枢，各街区的“分发站”才能依据各级居民粮票及其他配给副食卡上核准的严格定额，领取到满足本辖区基本需求的统配物资。
“莫斯科公社”是全城知名度最高、人流最密集的机构；它犹如一只喧闹嘈杂的巨大蜂巢，数以千计的公职人员在此忙得不可开交，忙于核定五花八门的口粮配给（*pyock*，即学术口粮及各类供应）等级，并签发盖印一张张“配给核准单”。除了普通市民凭户籍在所属街区“配给站”领取的面包、白糖、茶叶等基本定量口粮外，职工还可以在雇佣自己的工作单位领取一份单位配给。配给（*pyock*）额度完全依据公民的“阶级属性”及其所担任的职务高低而大相径庭。当前，前线红军（Red Army）战士和水兵每人每天可领到2.5磅（2½ lbs.）面包；苏维埃国家机关机关职员每2天（two days）可领到3磅（3 lbs.）；而那些因年迈、伤病或非战伤残疾而丧失劳动能力的人员，每天只能分到微薄的0.75磅（¾ lb.）。此外，还设有各种特殊级别的“特供优待口粮”：例如专为功勋卓著的老科学家、老教授设立的学术特供口粮（academical pyock），以及为那些未曾公开敌视反对共产党的辛亥老革命家特设的优待配给。在一些举足轻重的重要核心机构——如共产国际（第三国际）、外交人民委员部（Narkominodel）、铁道人民委员部（Narkomput）、最高国民经济委员会（Sovnarkhoz）等——也设有专属的“特供配给”。作为共产党（Communist Party）的党员，更有特权通过各自的党组织领取额外的特殊配给补给，并且在服装与被服配发部门享有优先挑选权。更有甚者，还专设有最高级别的“人民委员会特供口粮”（Sovnarkom pyock）——这是全俄所能搞到的最高档特供，专门供给共产党高级首长、人民委员及其第一助理等党政显赫高官享用。而安置外宾和重量级代表团下榻的苏维埃高级招待所——例如卡拉汉（Karakhan）所在的豪华公馆（*ossobniak*）以及卢克斯宾馆（Hotel Lux）——亦能享有特批的精美食品特供，其中包括高品质油脂与副食品（例如黄油、干酪、鲜肉、白糖、高档糖果等）

===Original===

, of which the average citizen receives very little.
I discussed the matter with our House Commissar, who is a devoted Party man. “The essence of Communism is equality,” I said; “there should be only one kind of *pyock*, so that all will share equally.”
“The Er-Kah-Peh7 decided the matter long ago, and it is right so,” he replied.
“But how can it be right?” I protested. “One person receives a generous *pyock*, more than enough to live on; another gets less than enough; a third almost nothing. You have endless categories.”
“Well,” he said, “the Red Army men at the front must get more than the city man; they do the hardest fighting. The soldier at home also must be encouraged, as well as the sailor; they are the backbone of the Revolution. Then the responsible officers deserve a little better food. Look how they work, sixteen hours a day and more, giving all their time and energy to the cause. The employees of such important institutions as Narkomput and Narkominodel must be shown some preference. Besides, a great deal depends on how well a certain institution is organized. Many of the big ones procure most of their supplies directly from the peasantry, through special representatives and the cooperatives.”
“If anyone is to receive preference, I think it should be the workers,” I replied. “But they get almost the worst *pyock*.”
“What can we do, *tovarish*! If it were not for the cursed Allies and the blockade, we’d have food enough for all,” he said sadly. “But it won’t last long now. Did you read in the *Izvestia* that a revolution is to break out soon in Germany and Italy? The proletariat of Europe will then come to our aid.”
“I doubt it, but let’s hope so. In the meantime we can’t be sitting and waiting for revolutions to happen somewhere. We must exert our own efforts to put the country on its feet.”
The Commissar’s turn in line came, and he was called into an inner office. We had been waiting several hours in the corridors of the various bureaus. It seemed that almost every door had to be entered before a sufficient number of *resolutsyi* (endorsements) were secured, and the final “order” for supplies obtained. There was a continuous movement of applicants and clerks from office to office, everyone scolding and pushing toward the head of the line. The waiting men watched closely that no one got ahead of his proper place.

===Chinese===

，而普通百姓对这些紧俏物资却几乎闻所未闻、微乎其微。
我同我们招待所的特派员探讨了这一严峻问题，他是一位极为忠诚的共产党员。“共产主义的真谛在于人人平等，”我说道，“全社会理应只有一种统一的口粮配给（*pyock*），唯有如此才能做到全员平分共享。”
“俄共（布）7早就开会通盘决议了这一事项，这种制度是完全正确的，”他胸有成竹地回答道。
“但这怎么可能称得上正确呢？”我据理力争道。“一个人能领到极其丰厚的口粮，多得根本吃不完；另一个人领到的口粮却连果腹都远远不够；第三个人几乎什么都领不到。你们设立了没完没了的等级条目！”
“哎，”他解释道，“在前线浴血奋战的红军战士自然理应比后方城里的老百姓多领一些；毕竟他们承担着最艰苦卓绝的流血战斗。后方留守的卫戍战士和水兵同样需要重点激励，他们是保卫伟大革命的坚强中流砥柱。此外，肩负重任的领导干部吃得稍微好一点也是理所应当的。你瞧瞧他们是怎么日夜操劳工作的——每天伏案工作16个小时（sixteen hours）以上，把全部身心与精力毫无保留地奉献给了崇高的革命事业。铁道部和外交部这样至关重要的核心职能部门的干部职工，自然应当给予适度照顾优待。更何况，这很大程度上还取决于某个具体单位自身的组织筹措能力。许多实力雄厚的大部委都是直接绕过中间环节，派出特派采购员通过各地的供销合作社，直接从农民手里采购调配绝大部分物资的。”
“如果真有什么人应当享有优先优待权的话，依我看首先应当是产业工人才对，”我针锋相对地反驳道。“然而现实中工人们领到的配给口粮却几乎是最糟糕低劣的。”
“我们又能有什么法子呢，同志（*tovarish*）！要不是那帮该死的协约国反动派发动封锁围剿，我们本该有充足的粮食供应全俄百姓，”他神情黯然地叹息道。“不过这种苦日子马上就要熬到头了。你难道没读最新一期的《消息报》（*Izvestia*）吗？上面披露德国和意大利很快就会全面爆发无产阶级大革命！到那时，全欧洲的工人阶级弟兄必将火速驰援我们！”
“对此我深表怀疑，但愿能如你所愿吧。然而在此期间，我们绝不能坐以待毙、空等别处爆发革命。我们必须自力更生，倾尽自身全力让饱经沧桑的国家重新站立起来。”
排了漫长的长队后，终于轮到了特派员，他被传唤进了里间办公室。在此之前，我们已经在各个处室门外阴冷潮湿的长廊里苦苦等待了数个小时。似乎每一道房门都必须硬着头皮挨个敲进去，好不容易凑齐足够数量的联合会签批条（*resolutsyi*），才能最终领到领粮的正式“调拨核准单”。走廊里满是神色慌乱地在各个科室之间来回奔波的申请人与办事员，每个人都在骂骂咧咧地争相往前挤。在队伍中焦急等待的人们警惕地死盯着前排，生怕有人投机钻营插队加塞。

===Original===

Frequently someone would march straight to the office door and try to enter, ignoring the queue.
“Into the line, into the line!” the cry would be raised at once. “The sly one! Here we’ve been standing for hours, and he’s just come and wants to enter already.”
“I’m *vne otcheredi*” (not to wait in line), the man would answer disdainfully.
“Show your authorization!”
One after another came these men and women *vne otcheredi*, with slips of paper securing immediate admission, while “the tail” was steadily growing longer. “I’m standing three hours already,” an old man complained; “in my bureau people are waiting for me on important business.”
“Learn patience, little father,” a workman replied good-humoredly. “Look at me, I’ve been in line all day yesterday since early morning, and all the time these *vne otcheredi* kept coming, and it was 2 p.m. when I got through the door. But the chief there, he looks at the clock and says to me, says he, ‘No more today; no orders issued after 2 p.m. Come tomorrow.’ ‘Have mercy, dear one,’ I plead. ‘I live seven versts away and I got up at five this morning to come here. Do me the favor, *golubtshik*, just a stroke of your pen and it’s done.’ ‘Go, go now,’ the cruel one says, ‘I haven’t time. Come tomorrow,’ and he pushed me out of the room.”
“True, true,” a woman back of him corroborated, “I was right behind you, and he wouldn’t let me in either, the hard-hearted one.”
The Commissar came out of the office. “Ready?” I asked.
“No, not yet,” he smiled wearily. “But you’d better go home, or you’ll lose your dinner.”
In the Kharitonensky Sergei was waiting for me.
“Berkman,” he said, as I entered, “will you let me share your room with you?”
“What do you mean?”
“I’ve been ordered to vacate. My time’s up, they say. But I have nowhere to go. I’ll look in the morning for another place, but meantime﻿—?”
“You’ll stay with me.”
“But if the House Commissar should object?”
“Are you to be driven into the street in this frost? Remain on my responsibility.”

===Chinese===

时不时会有人大模大样地无视排队长龙，径直冲向办公室大门打算强行闯入。
“排队去！排队去！”人群中顿时炸开了锅，愤怒的斥责声此起彼伏。“瞧瞧这个狡猾的家伙！大伙儿在这儿挨冻苦等了好几个小时，他刚来居然就想大摇大摆闯进去！”
“我有免排队特权（*vne otcheredi*，即享有特权不排队）！”那人满脸不屑、盛气凌人地反唇相讥。
“那就亮出你的特批证明来！”
然而，一个又一个手持免排队特批条子（*vne otcheredi*）的男女接踵而至，凭着一纸条文轻而易举直接被放行入内，而那条宛若长蛇般的“排队长尾”却在不断向后无休止地蔓延拉长。“我在这儿已经整整站了3个小时（three hours）了，”一位白发老者哀叹道，“我们局里还有一大帮人等着我回去签办紧急公务呢！”
“耐心点吧，老人家，”旁边一位工人模样的汉子苦中作乐地劝慰道。“你瞧瞧我，昨天从大清早起就守在这儿排了一整天，结果那些享有特权的家伙（*vne otcheredi*）源源不断地插队加塞，直到下午2点（2 p.m.）我才好不容易挤进了那扇大门。谁知里面的长官抬头看了一眼挂钟，冷冰冰地冲我嚷道：‘今天到此为止！过了下午两点（2 p.m.）一律不再签发调拨批单，明天再来吧！’‘求求您开开恩吧，青天大老爷，’我苦苦哀求道，‘我住的地方离这儿足足有7俄里（7韦尔斯塔）远，为了赶过来今天大清早5点（five this morning）就摸黑爬起来了。劳您大驾行行好，亲爱的同志（*golubtshik*），只需您抬手大笔一挥签个字就完事了。’‘走走走，赶紧出去！’那个铁石心肠的长官粗暴地呵斥道，‘我没闲工夫搭理你。明天再来！’说完一把将我推出了门外。”
“千真万确，一点不假，”站在他身后的一名中年妇女随声证实道，“我当时就紧挨在你屁股后头，那个狠心的家伙连门都没让我进！”
特派员终于神色疲倦地从办公室里走了出来。“办妥了吗？”我迎上前问道。
“还没呢，差得远呢，”他疲惫地苦笑了一下，“不过你还是先回招待所去吧，不然你连今晚的糊口晚饭都赶不上了。”
回到哈里托年斯基招待所，谢尔盖正在屋里焦急地等候着我。
“伯克曼（Berkman），”一见我推门进屋，他便迎上前来，“你能允许我继续跟你同住一个房间吗？”
“你这话是什么意思？”
“我接到了限期搬离腾房的驱逐通知。他们说我核准的留宿期限已经到期了。可我现在在城里根本无处安身。明早我会抓紧去另找住处，可今晚眼下这段时间——？”
“你就踏踏实实留在我屋里睡。”
“可要是招待所特派员出面干涉阻拦怎么办？”
“难道在这样天寒地冻的数九寒冬里，能任由你流落街头活活冻死吗？只管住下，天塌下来有我一人担着！”

## IX The Club on the Tverskaya / IX 特维尔大街上的俱乐部
===Original===

In the “Universalist” Club on Tverskaya Street I was surprised to meet a number of the *Buford* deportees. They had grown tired waiting to be assigned to work in Petrograd, they said, and had decided to come to Moscow. They are quartered in the Third Soviet House, where they receive less than a pound of bread and a plate of soup as their daily ration. Their American money is spent: the Petrograd authorities had paid them 18 roubles for the dollar, but in Moscow they learned that the rate is 500. “Robbed by the great revolutionary Government,” Alyosha, the ship *zapevalo*, commented bitterly.
“We are selling our last American things,” Vladimir remarked. “It’s lucky some markets are open yet.”
“Trading is forbidden,” I warned him.
“Forbidden!” he laughed scornfully. “Only to the peasant women and the kids peddling cigarettes. But look at the stores﻿—if they pay enough graft they can keep open all they want. You’ve never seen such corruption; America ain’t in it. Most of the Chekists are from the old police and gendarmery, and they graft to the limit. The militiamen are thieves and highwaymen that escaped being shot by joining the new police force. I had a few dollars when I came to Moscow; a Chekist changed them for me.”
People of every revolutionary tendency gather in the Club: moderate Left Social Revolutionists and the more extreme adherents of Spiridonova; Maximalists, Individualists, and Anarchists of various factions. There are old *katorzhane* among them who had passed years in prison and in Siberia under the old regime. Liberated by the February Revolution, they have since participated in all the great struggles. One of the most prominent is Barmash, who had been sentenced to death by the Tsar, somehow escaped execution, and later played a prominent role in the events of February and October, 1917. Askarev, for many years active in the Anarchist movement abroad, is now a member of the Moscow Soviet. B﻿—﻿— was a labor deputy in Petrograd during Kerensky’s time. Many others I have met at the “Universalist” headquarters, men and women grown gray and old in the revolutionary struggle.
There is great divergency of opinion in the Club about the character and role of the Bolsheviki. Some defend the Communist regime as an inevitable stage of the “transitional period.” Proletarian dictatorship is necessary to secure the complete triumph of the Revolution.

===Chinese===

在特维尔大街（Tverskaya）上的“普适主义者”（Universalist）俱乐部里，我十分意外地撞见了数名“布福德号”（*Buford*）上的遣返同胞。他们说，在彼得格勒（Petrograd）苦苦等待分配工作早已耗尽了他们的耐心，于是他们毅然决定赶来莫斯科（Moscow）。如今他们被集中安置在苏维埃第三招待所，每天领到的糊口口粮还不足一磅面包和一碗清汤。他们随身携带的美金现钞早已花光了：彼得格勒官方当初按一美元兑换18个卢布（18 roubles）的极低官价强行收兑，可到了莫斯科他们才获悉，黑市的真实汇率早已高达500卢布（500）！“我们被伟大的革命政府给彻底敲骨吸髓了，”轮船上的领唱者（*zapevalo*）阿廖沙（Alyosha）愤懑刻薄地嘲弄道。
“我们正在变卖随身带来的最后一点美制衣物，”弗拉基米尔（Vladimir）插话道，“万幸的是有些黑市集市至今还在私下开张。”
“私下买卖交易可是被明令禁止的啊，”我好心提醒他道。
“禁止？！”他轻蔑地嗤之以鼻冷笑道。“那不过是专挑软柿子捏，专门查禁那些卖香烟的乡下农妇和穷苦小孩罢了！你再去瞧瞧那些临街大店铺——只要行贿打点够了黑钱，他们想开多久就能开多久！你平生绝没见过如此骇人听闻的贪腐受贿；就连号称金钱至上的美利坚在它面前也得甘拜下风！绝大多数契卡（Cheka）便衣全是从沙皇旧警察和旧宪兵队伍里摇身一变转过来的，他们敲诈勒索贪污受贿到了毫无底线的地步。那些所谓的武装民警，不过是一群当年靠火速加入新警察队伍才侥幸逃脱死刑枪决的旧盗匪和小毛贼。我刚到莫斯科兜里还剩几个美金，就是一个契卡便衣私下帮我按黑市价兑换成卢布的。”
形形色色革命流派的人士齐聚在俱乐部里：温和派左翼社会革命党人、斯皮里多诺娃（Spiridonova）的激进极左追随者、最高纲领派、个人主义者，以及安那其主义各门各派的信徒。在他们中间，不乏在沙皇反动旧体制下坐穿牢底、在西伯利亚苦役营熬过漫长岁月的苦役劳教老囚犯（*katorzhane*）。在二月革命中重获自由之后，他们自始至终全身心投入了其后的每一场惊心动魄的大风暴。其中最声名显赫的当属巴尔马什（Barmash），他早年曾被沙皇法庭判处死刑，后来侥幸死里逃生越狱脱险，并在1917年二月与十月革命的急风暴雨中发挥了举足轻重的重要作用。在海外安那其主义运动中活跃多年的阿斯卡列夫（Askarev），如今已荣任莫斯科苏维埃的正式代表。B某在克伦斯基临时政府时期曾是彼得格勒的工人代表。在“普适主义者”俱乐部总部，我还结识了许许多多在漫长革命斗争中熬得青丝变白发、饱经风霜的各界志士。
俱乐部内部对于布尔什维克的历史性质及其扮演的政治角色存在着极其严重的分歧争议。一部分人竭力为共产党政权辩护，坚称这是人类迈向新社会不可逾越的“过渡时期”的历史必经阶段。在他们看来，为了夺取社会大革命的完全胜利，无产阶级专政是绝对必不可少的铁腕手段。

===Original===

The Bolsheviki were compelled to resort to the *razvyorstka* and confiscation, because the peasants refused to support the Red Army and the workers. The Cheka is needed to suppress speculation and counterrevolution. But for the constant danger of conspiracies and armed rebellion, incited by the Allies, the Communists would abolish the severe restrictions and permit greater liberty.
The more extreme elements condemn the Bolshevik State as the most unmitigated tyranny, as a dictatorship over the proletariat. Terrorism and the centralization of power in the exclusive hands of the Communist Party, they charge, have alienated the masses, limited revolutionary growth, and paralyzed constructive activity. They denounce the Cheka as counterrevolutionary, and call the *razvyorstka* downright robbery, responsible for the multiplying peasant insurrections.
Bolshevik policies and methods are the inexhaustible subjects of discussion at the Club. Little groups stand about in animated conversation, and K﻿—﻿—, the well-known former Schlüsselburgets,8 is haranguing some workers and soldiers in the corner. “The safety of the Revolution is in the masses being vitally interested in it,” he is saying. “There was no counterrevolution when we had free Soviets; every man stood on guard of the Revolution then, and we needed no Cheka. Its terrorism has cowed the workers, and driven the peasantry to revolt.”
“But if the peasants refuse to give us food, how are we going to live?” a soldier demands.
“The peasants never refused as long as their Soviets could deal directly with the soldiers and workers,” K﻿—﻿— replies. “But the Bolsheviki have taken the power away from the Soviets, and of course the peasants don’t want their food to go to the Commissars or to the markets where no worker can afford to buy it. ‘The Commissars are fat, but the workers starve,’ the peasants say.”
“The peasants are up in rebellion in our parts,” a tall man in fur cap puts in. “I’m from the Ural. The *razvyorstka* has taken everything from the farmers there. They haven’t even enough seed left for the next spring sowing. In one village they refused to give up and killed a Commissar, and then the punitive expedition came. They flogged the peasants, and many were shot.”
In the evening I attended the Anarchist Conference at the Club.

===Chinese===

布尔什维克之所以被迫推行余粮征集制（*razvyorstka*）与强制没收措施，纯粹是因为农民拒不支援保卫红军（Red Army）战士和城市工人阶级。契卡（Cheka）之所以不可或缺，是为了坚决取缔打击投机倒把和粉碎反革命复辟。倘若不是协约国反动派在暗中不断煽动阴谋政变和武装暴乱，共产党人早就彻底废除一切严苛禁令、给予人民更广泛的自由了。
然而，立场更为激进的极左分子则痛斥布尔什维克国家政权是彻头彻尾的专制暴政，是凌驾于无产阶级之上的残酷独裁。他们愤然指责道，恐怖清洗以及将全俄一切大权独揽于共产党一党之手的过度集权，已彻底割裂脱离了人民群众，扼杀了革命的发展生机，并彻底瘫痪了创造性的建设活力。他们痛斥契卡本身才是最大的反革命工具，将余粮征集制（*razvyorstka*）定性为赤裸裸的武装抢劫掠夺，直指正是这项暴政激起了各地层出不穷的农民起义烽火。
布尔什维克的路线方针与施政手段成了俱乐部里争论不休、永无止境的热门话题。三五成群的人们聚拢在一起热烈辩论；在角落里，著名的施吕塞尔堡要塞老囚犯8（Schlüsselburgets）K某正情绪激昂地向围拢上来的工人和士兵慷慨陈词。“保卫革命成果的唯一根本安全保障，在于让最广大的工农大众在革命中享有切身的根本利益！”他高声说道，“在当年我们拥有完全自由的苏维埃基层政权时，何曾有过什么反革命的立足之地！那时人人都是保卫革命的自觉近卫军，我们根本不需要什么契卡！正是契卡的恐怖手段吓退瓦解了工人阶级，将广大农民逼上了武装暴动的绝路！”
“可要是农民死活不肯卖给城里粮食，我们靠什么活命呢？”一名红军战士大声质问道。
“只要各地的工农苏维埃能够直接同战士和产业工人面对面协商互换物资，农民从来未曾拒绝供应过一粒粮食！”K某斩钉截铁地回答道。“然而布尔什维克却强行剥夺架空了苏维埃基层的全部自治大权；农民自然不甘心看到自己辛勤耕作的血汗口粮全被专横的人民委员统统征走，或是流向连工人自己都买不起的黑市！‘当官的个个脑满肠肥，下苦力的工人们却活活饿死！’——这正是广大农民发自肺腑的悲愤控诉！”
“在我们老家那块儿，农民早就全线揭竿而起暴动了，”一位头戴皮帽的身材高挑男子插话道。“我来自乌拉尔地区。余粮征集制（*razvyorstka*）把当地农户家里刮得底朝天，连开春播种仅剩的保命麦种都被强行搜刮一空！在某个村子里，农民拒不交出粮食并愤怒打死了一名人民委员，紧接着惩罚讨伐队就开进了村庄。他们用皮鞭毒打村民，许多庄稼汉当场被拉出去枪毙示众了。”
入夜时分，我参加了在俱乐部举办的安那其主义者代表大会。

===Original===

First *dokladi* were read, reports of activities of an educational and propagandistic character; then speeches were delivered by Anarchists of various schools, all critical of the existing regime. Some were very outspoken, in spite of the presence of several “suspicious ones,” Chekists, evidently. The Universalists, a new, distinctively Russian current, took a Center position, not so fully in accord with the Bolsheviki as the Anarchists of the moderate Golos Truda Group, but less antagonistic than the extreme wing. The most interesting talk was an impromptu speech by Rostchin, a popular university lecturer and old Anarchist. With biting irony he castigated the Left and Center for their lukewarm, almost antagonistic, attitude to the Bolsheviki. He eulogized the revolutionary role of the Communist Party, and called Lenin the greatest man of the age. He dwelt on the historic mission of the Bolsheviki, and asserted that they are directing the Revolution toward the Anarchist society, which will secure full individual liberty and social well-being. “It is the duty of every Anarchist to work wholeheartedly with the Communists, who are the advance guard of the Revolution,” he declared. “Leave your theories alone, and do practical work for the reconstruction of Russia. The need is great, and the Bolsheviki welcome you.”
“He’s a Sovietsky Anarchist,” came sarcastically from the audience.
Most of those present resented Rostchin’s attitude, but his appeal stirred me. I felt that he suggested the only way, under the circumstances, of aiding the Revolution and preparing the masses for libertarian, nongovernmental Communism.
The Conference proceeded with the main questions at issue﻿—the growing persecution of Left elements and the multiplying arrests of Anarchists. I learned that already in 1918 the Bolsheviki had practically declared war against all non-Communist revolutionary bodies. The Left Social Revolutionists, who had opposed the Brest-Litovsk peace and killed Mirbach in protest, were outlawed, and many of them executed or imprisoned. In April of that year Trotsky also ordered the suppression of the Moscow Anarchist Club, a powerful organization which had its own military units, known as the Black Guard. The Anarchist headquarters were attacked without warning by Bolshevik artillery and machine guns, and the Club dissolved.

===Chinese===

大会首先宣读了多篇工作报告（*dokladi*），通报了近期在文化启蒙与革命宣传阵线上的各项工作动态；随后各流派的安那其主义学者相继登台发言，无一例外对现行政权进行了深刻批判。尽管会场中明显坐着几名形迹可疑、显然是契卡（Cheka）便衣密探的人员，但部分代表的发言依然言辞犀利、毫不避讳。作为一股极具俄国本土特色的新兴派别，“普适主义者”采取了居中的中间立场：他们不像温和派“劳动之声”（Golos Truda）派安那其主义者那样对布尔什维克百依百顺、全盘附和，但也绝不像极左激进派那样水火不容、激烈对抗。席间最引人入胜的，当属著名大学学者、老资格安那其主义者罗斯钦（Rostchin）发表的即兴激情演说。他以辛辣刻薄的讽刺口吻，痛斥了左翼与中间派对待布尔什维克那种不冷不热、乃至近乎敌视的消极态度。他极力颂扬共产党在社会革命中的中流砥柱作用，盛赞列宁为当今时代最伟大的领袖伟人。他深入阐发了布尔什维克的历史使命，并断言他们正在引领这场伟大革命朝着保障个体充分自由与社会普遍福祉的无政府共产主义社会迈进。“每一位真正的安那其主义者神圣的时代天职，就是全心全意同作为革命先锋主力军的共产党人紧密并肩携手合作！”他振臂高呼道，“收起你们那些空洞脱离实际的教条理论吧，脚踏实地投入到重建饱经创伤的俄国的宏伟实践中去！祖国对人才的渴望是何等迫切，布尔什维克的大门时刻向你们敞开！”
“他不过是个摇尾乞怜的‘苏维埃御用安那其主义者’罢了！”台下观众席中有人冷嘲热讽地嘘道。
在场绝大多数代表对罗斯钦的投降立场深恶痛绝，然而他的慷慨陈词却深深触动了我。我隐隐感到，在当前残酷险恶的现实局势下，这或许正是保卫拯救革命、引导工农大众迈向崇高自由与非官僚共产主义的唯一可行之道。
大会随即转入探讨核心议程——面对左翼政治力量遭受日益严酷的政治清洗打压，以及安那其主义者惨遭大批搜捕入狱的危急现实。我获悉，早在1918年，布尔什维克实际上就已经全面打响了针对一切非共产党革命团体的清剿战役。当初因坚决反对签署《布列斯特和约》而刺杀德国公使米尔巴赫以示严正抗议的左翼社会革命党人，全员被宣布为非法叛党，其中大批骨干遭枪决或逮捕入狱。那年4月，托洛茨基（Trotsky）更亲自下达雷霆密令，以铁腕武装取缔了莫斯科安那其主义俱乐部——那是一支拥有自身专属武装部队“黑卫军”（Black Guard）的实力雄厚的革命组织。布尔什维克的重炮部队与机枪阵地在事先未予任何警告的情况下，悍然对安那其总指挥部实施猛烈炮轰围剿，强行解散取缔了该俱乐部。

===Original===

Since then persecution of the Left parties has continued intermittently, in spite of the fact that many of their members are at the front, while others are cooperating with the Communists in various Government institutions.
“We fought side by side with the Bolsheviki on the barricades,” the Schlüsselburgets declared; “thousands of our comrades died for the Revolution. Now most of our people are in prison, and we ourselves live in constant dread of the Cheka.”
“Rostchin says we ought to be thankful to the Bolsheviki,” someone sneered.
The Resolution passed by the Conference emphasized its devotion to the Revolution, but protested against the persecution of Left elements, and demanded the legalization of Anarchist cultural and educational work.
“It may seem strange to you that Anarchists should apply to the Government to be legalized,” the Universalist Askarev said to me. “As a matter of fact, we do not regard the Bolsheviki as an ordinary government. They are still revolutionary, and we recognize and give them credit for what they have accomplished. Some of us disagree with them fundamentally and disapprove of their methods and tactics, but we can speak to them as comrades.”
I consented to join the Committee chosen to present the Resolution of the Conference to Krestinsky, the secretary of the Central Committee of the Communist Party.
---
The anteroom of Krestinsky’s office was crowded with Communist delegates and committees from various parts of the country. Some of them had come from points as distant as Turkestan and Siberia, to report to “the center” or have some weighty problem decided by the Party. The delegates, with thick portfolios under their arms, looked conscious of the important missions entrusted to them. Almost everyone sought a personal interview with Lenin, or expected to make a verbal *doklad* to the full session of the Central Committee. But I understand that they seldom get further than the secretary’s office.
Almost two hours passed before we were admitted to Krestinsky, who received us in a businesslike, almost brusque, manner. The secretary of the all-powerful Communist Party is a man of middle age, short, and of dark complexion, in his whole appearance the typical Russian intellectual of the pre-Revolution days. He is very nearsighted and nervous, and speaks in a hasty, abrupt way.

===Chinese===

自那以后，针对各左翼党派的政治迫害便断断续续从未停歇，即便这些党派中有无数成员正在各大前线为保卫苏维埃流血搏杀，还有许多人正在苏维埃各级政府机构内部同共产党人精诚协作。
“当年在反抗沙皇与临时政府的街垒上，我们是同布尔什维克肩并肩共同浴血杀敌的同盟军！”那位施吕塞尔堡要塞老囚犯（Schlüsselburgets）义愤填膺地控诉道，“我们数以千计的同道先烈为这场革命献出了年轻生命！然而如今，我们的大多数骨干全被关进了暗无天日的大牢，而留存在外的人整天提心吊胆、生活在契卡（Cheka）便衣搜捕的无尽恐慌之中！”
“罗斯钦那家伙居然恬不知耻地叫嚣，说什么我们应当对布尔什维克感恩戴德呢！”人群中有人嘲弄冷笑道。
代表大会全票表决通过了一项决议案：决议再次重申了各流派对社会大革命的无限忠诚，但对当局残酷迫害清洗左翼阵营的行径提出了严正抗议，并强烈要求苏维埃政府正式批准承认安那其主义文化宣传与启蒙教育事业的合法地位。
“安那其主义者竟然会屈尊向政府当局申请合法地位，这在你看来或许显得匪夷所思，”普适主义代表阿斯卡列夫（Askarev）私下向我坦言道，“但说实话，在骨子里我们从未把布尔什维克视作寻常意义上的资产阶级旧政府。他们本质上依然是一支革命政权，我们充分承认并高度赞赏他们迄今所建立的丰功伟绩。虽然我们之中许多人在根本原则路线上同他们存在严重分歧，并坚决反对他们的施政手段与策略，但在人格与理想上，我们依然能够以同志的平等身份同他们展开对话。”
我欣然同意加入代表团联络委员会，负责将大会决议案亲手呈递给俄共（布）中央委员会书记克列斯廷斯基（Krestinsky）。
---
克列斯廷斯基办公室的接待室里挤满了来自全俄各地的共产党代表和请愿委员会。他们之中有许多人是从远至突厥斯坦和西伯利亚的千里之外日夜兼程赶来，专门向“莫斯科中央”当面汇报请示工作，或是恳求党中央出面裁决地方上的重大疑难决断。代表们腋下夹着厚重沉甸的公文皮包，脸上写满了肩负神圣重大使命的使命感。几乎每个人都极度渴望能获得列宁同志的亲自单独接见，或是期盼能向中央政治局或中央全会当面做一次口头工作汇报（*doklad*）。然而据我所知，绝大多数人终其一生也只能止步于中央书记的这间接待室门外。
足足熬过了近2个小时，我们才终于获准迈进克列斯廷斯基的办公室大门。他以一种极其公式化、近乎冷淡唐突的生硬态度接待了我们。这位执掌大权的核心政党总书记是一位步入中年的男子，身材短小精悍，肤色黝黑偏暗，浑身上下散发着革命前旧时代俄罗斯知识分子特有的典型气质。他重度近视，神情高度紧张神经质，说话语速极快、斩钉截铁而毫无转圜余地。

===Original===

Having explained the purpose of our call, we discussed the resolution of the Conference, and I expressed my surprise and sorrow at finding Anarchists and other Left elements imprisoned in the Soviet Republic. American radicals would not believe such a state of affairs in Russia, I remarked; a friendlier attitude on the part of the Communists, sympathy and understanding brought to bear on the situation, and the well-disposed Left element could be of the greatest service to our common cause. Some way should be found, I urged, to bridge the rupture and to bring all revolutionary elements into closer contact and cooperation.
“You think it possible?” Krestinsky asked dryly.
Askarev reminded him of the October days, when the Anarchists so effectively aided the Bolsheviki, and referred to the fact that most of them are still working together with the Communists in various fields of activity, in spite of the suppressive policies of the Government. Revolutionary ethics demand the liberation of the imprisoned Anarchists, he emphasized. They had been arrested without cause, and no charges have been brought against them.
“It’s solely a question of serving our purpose,” Krestinsky remarked. “Some of the prisoners may be dangerous. Perhaps the Cheka has something against them.”
“They have been in prison for months, yet not a single one of them has been tried or even received a hearing,” Askarev retorted.
“What guarantee have we that if released they will not continue their opposition to us?” Krestinsky demanded.
“We claim the right to carry on our educational work unhindered,” Askarev replied.
Krestinsky promised to submit the matter to the Central Committee of the Party, and the audience was over.

===Chinese===

在阐明我们的来意之后，我们同他逐条讨论了代表大会的决议案，我坦率表达了自己在苏维埃共和国境内亲眼目睹大批安那其主义者及其他左翼同志惨遭逮捕关押时的错愕与深切痛心。我指出，美利坚的激进劳工大众根本无法相信堂堂苏俄竟会出现如此令人痛心的局面；倘若共产党人能够拿出更为友好包容的博大胸襟，以阶级同情与相互理解来妥善处置当前态势，这支充满善意的左翼革命力量定能在我们共同的壮阔伟业中发挥无可替代的巨大助力。我恳切敦促道，当局理应想方设法弥合裂痕，推动全俄一切真诚的革命力量紧密团结、携手并进。
“你当真以为这具有可行性吗？”克列斯廷斯基冷冰冰地反问道。
阿斯卡列夫随即提醒他忆及十月革命风暴骤起之际，安那其主义武装力量是如何不惜牺牲一切全力驰援布尔什维克夺取政权的；他还特意强调了一个铁的事实：即便当前政府推行残酷的高压取缔政策，广大安那其同志依然在各条战线上勤勤恳恳同共产党人并肩并肩奋斗。他严肃指出，革命的道义与良知呼唤必须立即无条件释放所有被关押的安那其主义革命者；他们被捕完全是欲加之罪、无端构陷，官方迄今拿不出半条具有法律效力的正式指控罪名！
“这纯粹是出于服从我们根本大局考量的现实需要，”克列斯廷斯基轻描淡写地回应道。“被捕人员中有些人可能极具危险破坏性。也许契卡（Cheka）手里掌握着不利于他们的确凿铁证呢。”
“他们在大牢里已被关押折磨了数月之久，然而迄今为止甚至没有一个人上过法庭接受审判，甚至连一次正规的公开听证问讯都不曾给过！”阿斯卡列夫义正辞严地据理力争道。
“我们凭什么能够保证，一旦将他们开释出狱，他们不会转过头来变本加厉反对我们？”克列斯廷斯基冷酷地反唇相讥道。
“我们仅仅是严正主张合法享有不受任何公权力粗暴干涉、开展和平文化启蒙教育工作的正当权利！”阿斯卡列夫铿锵有力地回答道。
克列斯廷斯基随口答应会将该议案呈递给党中央委员会讨论裁决，接见便就此草草收场结束。

## X A Visit to Peter Kropotkin / X 拜访彼得·克鲁泡特金
===Original===

Kropotkin lives in Dmitrov, a small town sixty versts from Moscow. Owing to the deplorable railroad conditions, traveling from Petrograd to Dmitrov was not to be thought of. But recently I learned that the Government had made special arrangements to enable Lansbury to visit Kropotkin, and with two other friends I took advantage of the opportunity.
Since my arrival in Russia I have been hearing the most conflicting rumors about Old Peter. Some claim that he is favorable to the Bolsheviki; others, that he is opposed to them; it is reported that he is living in satisfactory material circumstances, and again that he is practically starving. I have been anxious to learn the truth of the matter and to meet my old teacher personally. In the years past I had had a sporadic correspondence with him, but we never met. I have admired Kropotkin since my early youth, when I had first heard his name and become acquainted with his writings. One incident, in particular, had left a deep impression on me.
It was about 1890, when the Anarchist movement was still in its infancy in America. We were just a handful then, young men and women fired by the enthusiasm of a sublime ideal, and passionately spreading the new faith among the population of the New York Ghetto. We held our gatherings in an obscure hall in Orchard Street, but we regarded our work as highly successful: every week greater numbers attended our meetings, much interest was manifested in the revolutionary teachings, and vital questions were discussed late into the night, with deep conviction and youthful vision. To most of us it then seemed that capitalism had almost reached the limit of its fiendish possibilities, and that the Social Revolution was not far off. But there were many difficult questions and knotty problems involved in the growing movement, which we ourselves could not solve satisfactorily. We longed to have our great teacher Kropotkin among us, if only for a short visit, to have him clear up many complex points and to give us the benefit of his intellectual aid and inspiration. And then, what a stimulus his presence would be for the movement!
We decided to reduce our living expenses to the minimum and devote our earnings, to defray the expense involved in our invitation to Kropotkin to visit America.

===Chinese===

克鲁泡特金（Kropotkin）定居在德米特罗夫（Dmitrov）——那是距离莫斯科（Moscow）约60俄里（sixty versts）的一座偏僻小镇。鉴于当前铁路运力近乎瘫痪的破败状况，从彼得格勒（Petrograd）辗转前往德米特罗夫在过去根本是不可想象的。然而最近我获悉，苏维埃政府做了特别协调安排，特批兰斯伯里（Lansbury）前往专程探望克鲁泡特金；我便携同另外2位（two）好友紧紧抓住了这次千载难逢的良机。
自我踏上俄国土地以来，关于老彼得（Old Peter）的种种传闻可谓众说纷纭、相互矛盾。有人声称他对布尔什维克颇有好感；另一些人则坚称他同当局势不两立；有人报告说他的物质生活条件相当充裕体面，但也有传闻说他实际上早已陷入了半饥半饱的垂死边缘。我一直极度渴望能弄清事实真相，并渴望能当面拜谒我这位久仰的导师。在往昔岁月里，我曾同他有过断断续续的零星通信，但我们彼此从未谋面。从我青少年时代起，当我第一次听闻他的名字并拜读到他的不朽著作时，我便对克鲁泡特金怀有无比崇敬之情。其中尤以一件往事，至今仍在我心头留下不可磨灭的深刻烙印。
那大约是在1890年前后，当时美利坚的安那其主义工人运动尚处于襁褓初创阶段。那时我们仅有一小撮人，一群满怀崇高理想与满腔热血的青年男女，在纽约犹太人贫民窟的下层劳苦大众中间狂热传播着革命新信仰。我们在果园街（Orchard Street）一处偏僻不起眼的小集会厅里定期聚会，但当时我们自认为工作开展得极其卓有成效：每周前来听讲的工友成倍激增，大家对革命学说展现出了极为浓厚的探索兴味；怀着坚如磐石的崇高信念与青年人特有的远大抱负，重大根本理论问题常常被热烈辩论直至深夜。在当时我们大多数人看来，资本主义展现其狰狞魔爪的罪恶潜能已近乎登峰造极，社会大革命的黎明早已指日可待。然而，在日益蓬勃生长的运动实践中，暴露出层出不穷的深层理论难题与错综复杂的棘手困境，仅凭我们自身的浅薄阅历根本无法给出令人信服的妥善解答。我们梦寐以求能将我们伟大的导师克鲁泡特金请到我们中间来，哪怕只是短暂讲学数日也好，请他出面为我们拨开重重迷雾、答疑解惑，给予我们宝贵而深邃的理论指导与精神鼓舞。更何况，他的亲自莅临，对整个工人运动又将是何等巨大而无可限量的磅礴推动啊！
于是我们下定决心，将各自的日常开销削减压榨至最低极限，将微薄的薪资收入倾囊汇聚起来，以承担全力邀请克鲁泡特金远涉重洋访问美利坚所需的一切旅费盘缠。

===Original===

Enthusiastically the matter was discussed in the group meetings of our most active and devoted comrades; all were unanimous in the great plan. A long letter was sent to our teacher, asking him to come for a lecture tour to America and emphasizing our need of him.
His negative reply gave us a shock: we were so sure of his acceptance, so convinced of the necessity of his coming. But the admiration we felt for him was even increased when we learned the motives of his refusal. He would very much like to come﻿—Kropotkin wrote﻿—and he deeply appreciated the spirit of our invitation. He hoped to visit the United States sometime in the future, and it would give him great joy to be among such good comrades. But just now he could not afford to come at his own expense, and he would not use the money of the movement even for such a purpose.
I pondered over his words. His viewpoint was just, I thought, but it could apply only under ordinary circumstances. His case, however, I considered exceptional, and I deeply regretted his decision not to come. But his motives epitomized to me the man and the grandeur of his nature. I visioned him as my ideal of revolutionist and Anarchist.
---
Meeting “celebrities” is generally disappointing: rarely does reality tally with the picture of our imagination. But it was not so in the case of Kropotkin; both physically and spiritually he corresponds almost exactly to the mental portrait I had made of him. He looks remarkably like his photographs, with his kindly eyes, sweet smile, and generous beard. Every time Kropotkin entered the room it seemed to light up by his presence. The stamp of the idealist is so strikingly upon him, the spirituality of his personality can almost be sensed. But I was shocked at the sight of his emaciation and feebleness.
Kropotkin receives the academic *pyock* which is considerably better than the ration issued to the ordinary citizen. But it is far from sufficient to support life, and it has been a struggle to keep the wolf from the door. The question of fuel and lighting is also a matter of constant worry. The winters are severe, and wood very scarce; kerosene is difficult to procure, and it is considered a luxury to burn more than one lamp at a time. This lack is particularly felt by Kropotkin; it greatly handicaps his literary labors.

===Chinese===

在我们最坚决、最忠诚的同道小组集会上，这项伟大的动议得到了极其热烈而亢奋的通盘讨论；大家全体一致通过了这项宏伟规划。一封情真意切的万言长信随即寄呈给了我们崇敬的导师，恳请他能赏光莅临美利坚展开巡回公开巡回演讲，并再三向他陈明我们对他的热切渴求。
然而，他委婉拒绝的回信却犹如晴天霹雳般令我们目瞪口呆：我们此前是那般笃定他一定会欣然应允，是那般坚信他的莅临乃是时代大势所趋！然而，当我们获悉他拒绝前来的深层动机时，我们对他油然而生的敬仰与崇拜之情反而愈发无以复加。克鲁泡特金在信中写道——他本人其实极度渴望能踏上美洲土地，他对我们满腔赤诚的盛情邀请致以最深沉的真挚谢意。他热切期盼未来某一天能够出访美利坚，能够与如此出色的优秀同志促膝相聚将带给他无上的莫大欣慰。然而在眼下，他自费承担不起昂贵跨洋旅费的重负，而他本人誓死绝不肯挪用属于革命工运事业的哪怕一分一厘公款来用于此项开销！
我一遍又一遍沉思琢磨着他的这番肺腑之言。我想，他的原则立场自然无可挑剔，但那充其量只适用于寻常普通情形。然而以他的崇高声望，理应完全破例对待；对于他不肯成行的最终决断，我内心深感惋惜与痛心。然而他展现出的崇高品格与动机，却在我心中生动勾勒出了这位伟人极其伟大恢弘的人格气度。在我眼中，他已然化作了我心目中最完美的革命家与安那其主义导师的至高典范。
---
拜会世俗眼中的“社会名流”通常总难免令人大失所望：残酷的现实极少能与我们美好的主观想象完全吻合。然而在克鲁泡特金身上却全然并非如此：无论从容貌体态还是精神气度来看，他几乎与我脑海深处多年构想的伟岸肖像完全别无二致。他与相片中的形象惊人神似，眉宇间流露着慈祥仁爱的目光，嘴角挂着和蔼可亲的温暖微笑，蓄着修剪茂密的大胡须。每一次克鲁泡特金迈步走进房间，整个屋宇仿佛瞬间因他的光临而熠熠生辉。理想主义的光环在他身上是如此鲜明夺目，他伟大人格所蕴含的崇高精神境界几乎令人触手可及。然而，目睹他当下那副形销骨立、衰弱不堪的病弱之躯，我心头顿遭重击、惊骇莫名。
克鲁泡特金享有专门核发的学术口粮（academic *pyock*），其标准固然明显优于普通市民领到的微薄配给。然而要靠这点微薄口粮维持生命却依然捉襟见肘，老两口终日都在同饥寒交迫的死亡威胁进行着艰苦卓绝的苦苦挣扎。燃料供应与夜间照明更是时刻困扰着他们的无休无止的揪心难题。这里的寒冬酷烈无情，而取暖木柴极度短缺；煤油千金难求，以至于在同一时间内点亮1盏以上的油灯都被视作难以企及的穷奢极欲！这种物质匮乏对克鲁泡特金的打击尤为致命，严重阻碍束缚着他宏伟学术著作的夜间著述。

===Original===

Several times the Kropotkin family had been dispossessed of their home in Moscow, their quarters being requisitioned for government purposes. Then they decided to move to Dmitrov. It is only about half a hundred versts from the capital, but it might as well be a thousand miles away, so completely is Kropotkin isolated. His friends can rarely visit him; news from the Western world, scientific works, or foreign publications are unattainable. Naturally Kropotkin feels deeply the lack of intellectual companionship and mental relaxation.
I was eager to learn his views on the situation in Russia, but I soon realized that Peter did not feel free to express himself in the presence of the English visitors. The conversation was therefore of a general character. But one of his remarks was very significant, and gave me the key to his attitude. “They have shown,” he said, referring to the Bolsheviki, “how the Revolution is *not* to be made.” I knew, of course, that as an Anarchist Kropotkin would not accept any Government position, but I wanted to learn why he is not participating in the economic upbuilding of Russia. Though old and physically weak, his advice and suggestions would be most valuable to the Revolution, and his influence of great advantage and encouragement to the Anarchist movement. Above all, I was interested to hear his positive ideas on the conduct of the Revolution. What I have heard so far from the revolutionary opposition is mostly critical, lacking helpful constructiveness.
The evening passed in desultory talk about the activities on the front, the crime of the Allied blockade in refusing even medicine to the sick, and the spread of disease resulting from lack of food and unhygienic conditions. Kropotkin looked tired, apparently exhausted by the mere presence of visitors. He is old and weak; I fear he may not live much longer under present conditions. He is evidently undernourished, though he said that the Anarchists of the Ukraine have been trying to make his life easier by supplying him with flour and other products. Makhno, also, when still friendly with the Bolsheviki, had sent him provisions.
We left early, spending the night in the train which did not start till morning for lack of an engine.

===Chinese===

克鲁泡特金一家此前在莫斯科（Moscow）的私宅曾数次遭到官方强行没收腾退，他们的寓所被各级政府部门强行征作公用。最终他们不得不下决心举家搬迁到了德米特罗夫。这里虽然距离首都仅有大约50俄里（半百俄里），但在现实中却犹如远隔1000英里之遥，克鲁泡特金几乎陷于彻头彻尾的完全隔绝状态之中。他的故旧同道极少能够成行前来探视；来自西方世界的学术消息、最新科研论著或国外出版物更是完全断绝。克鲁泡特金自然深切感受到了缺乏知识界同仁精神交流与智识慰藉的巨大孤独与苦闷。
我极度渴望能当面倾听他对当前全俄政局的真知灼见，但我很快便察觉到，当着几位英国来客的面，彼得并不愿畅所欲言、毫无保留地表达心声。因此整个谈话基本围绕着泛泛的宏观话题展开。然而他顺口说出的一句话却极具深意，为我窥探其内心真实态度提供了一把关键钥匙：“他们已经向世人充分证明了，”他在谈及布尔什维克时严肃地说道，“革命是绝对*不能*那样搞的！”我自然深知，作为一名坚定的安那其主义者，克鲁泡特金决不会在苏维埃政府内部谋取一官半职，但我迫切想弄清楚，他为何未曾投身参与俄国的经济建设大业。尽管他年事已高、体力羸弱，但他的真知灼见与指导意见对于保卫革命成果定当具有无与伦比的重大价值，而他的崇高威望对整个安那其主义运动亦将是莫大的强心剂。最重要的是，我极想聆听他对如何正确引领这场伟大革命所持有的建设性真知灼见。迄今为止，我在革命反对派口中所听到的论调大多偏重于消极指责批判，严重缺乏切实可行的宝贵建设性策略。
整个夜晚在断断续续的闲谈中悄然流逝，大家探讨了各大前线的战局动态、协约国反动封锁严禁向苏俄伤病员提供急救药品的反人类罪行，以及因粮食极度短缺和恶劣卫生环境导致的瘟疫肆虐。克鲁泡特金面带倦容，仅仅是接待应酬客人的到来似乎就已经耗尽了他仅剩的气力。他实在太年老体衰了；我极度担忧在眼下如此残酷的生活境况下，他恐怕很难再支撑太长时日。他显然长期严重营养不良，不过他宽慰我们说，乌克兰（Ukraine）的安那其主义同志们一直在设法周济支援他的晚年生活，不定期运来面粉和其他紧缺农产品。不仅如此，马赫诺（Makhno）在早年尚同布尔什维克维持友好统战关系之际，也曾专程向他派送过大批救命粮食。
我们很早就起身告辞了，夜晚在火车站的车厢里过夜；由于火车头极度短缺，列车直到次日大清早才终于缓缓开拔进发。

===Original===

Arriving in Moscow about noon, we found the station swarming with men and women loaded with bundles and waiting for an opportunity to get out of the hungry city. Scores of little children were about, clad in rags and begging bread.
“How pinched and frozen they look,” I remarked to my companions.
“Not so bad as the children of Austria,” Lansbury returned, drawing his big fur coat closer about him.

===Chinese===
