这时又有几位农夫悄无声息地推门溜了进来。他们默默注视着莫伊谢，莫伊谢向他们递了个安抚信任的眼神。从他们断断续续的闲谈中，我了解到他们正是负责向这位犹太商人供货的农户，莫伊谢充当着暗中倒手分销的掮客中介。莫伊谢提醒道，眼下做买卖绝不能麻痹大意跟陌生人随意搭腔；今天在市场上转悠的某些外来客形迹相当可疑。不过他拍胸脯保证可以向我私下供应各类农产品，而且开出的价码远低于莫斯科黑市的行情：在莫斯科大都市叫价高达1000个卢布（1,000 roubles）的大西洋腌鲱鱼，这里只要400卢布；一磅蚕豆或豌豆只要120卢布；掺杂一半小麦的面粉每磅只要250卢布；鸡蛋每只只要60卢布。
农夫们一致附和赞同莫伊谢的定论，痛陈“当下的日子过得比沙皇旧社会还要暗无天日！”在他们眼中，共产党人不过是一群明火执仗的强盗，当今世道根本找不到半点公理公道。他们对专横的人民委员的畏惧，远远超过了对昔日沙皇旧官僚（*tchinovniki*）的害怕。当我试探着询问他们是否更怀念君主专制旧体制时，他们立刻流露出强烈的抵触与反感。不！他们誓死不愿看到地主老爷（*pomeshtchiki*）卷土重来，更不愿沙皇死灰复燃，然而他们同样誓死不想要布尔什维克这一套！
“在旧社会，我们被人当成牛马牲畜任意宰割，”一位留着亚麻色浅发、生着一双蓝眼睛的农夫沉痛地说道，“那时是以‘沙皇小父亲’的神圣名义；而如今，他们口口声声是以‘党和无产阶级’的崇高旗号同我们发号施令，可到头来，我们依然像牛马牲口一样被人肆意践踏宰割，同往昔旧社会毫无二致！”
“列宁同志倒是一个大好人，”其中一位农夫插话道。
“对于列宁本人我们没什么好指摘非议的，”另一位庄稼汉附和道，“然而他手下派下来的那群人民委员，个个都是铁石心肠、凶残冷血的恶霸！”
“老天爷高高在上，而伊里奇16（Ilytch）又远在天边啊！”那位蓝眼睛农夫借用了一句俄罗斯传统民间谚语，意味深长地苦笑道。
“然而，正是布尔什维克把土地全部分给了你们啊，”我据理力争反驳道。
他缓缓伸手挠了挠后脑勺，狡黠幽默的光芒从他的双眸深处悄然闪过。

===Original===

“No, *golubtchik*,” he replied, “the land we took ourselves. Isn’t it so, little brothers?” he turned to the others.
“He speaks the truth,” they assented.
“Will it go on like this much longer?” they asked, as I was departing. “Maybe something will change?”
Returning to the station I met the members of our train crew straggling up the hill, weighted down with sacks of provisions. The young student of our medical staff carried a squealing hog. “How happy little old mother will be,” he said; “this porker will keep the family alive for a long time.”
“If they hide it well enough,” someone suggested.
A soldier drove by, and we asked for a ride to the station. Without answering he passed on. Presently another cart overtook us. We repeated our request. “Why not?” the young peasant exclaimed cheerfully, “jump in, all of you.” He was jolly and talkative, his “soul ajar,” as the student characterized him, and his conversation was entertaining. He liked the Bolsheviki, he said, but he had no use for the Communists. The Bolsheviki were good men, friends of the people: they had demanded the land for the farmer and all the power for the Soviets. But the Communists are bad: they rob and flog the peasants; they have put their own kind into the Soviets, and a non-Communist has no say there. The *kombed* is full of idle good-for-nothings; they are the bosses of the village, and the peasant who refuses to bow down before them is “in hard luck.” He had been on the Denikin front and there it was the same thing: the Communists and Commissars had everything their own way and lorded it over the drafted men. It was different when the soldiers could speak their minds and decide everything in their Company Committee: that was liberty and everyone felt himself a part of the Revolution. But now it is all changed. One is afraid to speak honestly﻿—there’s always a Communist about, and you are in danger of being denounced. That’s why he deserted; yes, deserted twice. He had heard that everything had been taken from his folks on the farm, and he decided to come home to see if it was true. Well, it was true; worse than what he had been told. Even his youngest brother, just past sixteen, had been drafted into the Army. No one remained at home but his mother and father, too old to work their piece of land without help, and all the cattle were gone.

===Chinese===

“才不是那么回事呢，亲爱的同志（*golubtchik*），”他朗声回答道，“土地分明是我们自己抄起家伙亲手夺回来的！乡亲兄弟们，你们说是不是这么个理儿？”他转过头大声征询其他农夫的意见。
“他说的全是大实话！”众农夫异口同声附和道。
“眼下这种苦日子究竟还得熬多久啊？”在我起身告辞之际，他们恋恋不舍地拉着我打听道，“老天爷保佑，往后世道保不齐能有什么转机吧？”
步行返回火车站的途中，我迎面撞见了我们列车上的几位乘务人员正气喘吁吁地爬上山坡，每个人肩头都沉甸甸地扛着几大麻袋搜罗来的口粮。我们医疗救护队的那位年轻医科学生甚至还怀抱着一头嗷嗷直叫的小乳猪。“这下我那老娘可要乐开花了，”他满脸自豪地炫耀道，“这头小猪崽足够我们全家老小顶过好长一段时日了！”
“前提是你们得有能耐把它藏得足够严实才行，”旁边有人意味深长地出言提醒道。
一名骑着大马的骑兵从身旁飞驰而过，我们开口央求他顺道捎我们一程去火车站。那骑兵甚至连头都没回，径直呼啸而去。不多时，另一辆农用大板车从后头赶了上来。我们再次提出了搭车请求。“那有何不可啊！”赶车的小年轻农夫爽朗地高声招呼道，“全跳上来吧，大伙儿坐稳啦！”他生性乐观健谈，正如那位医科学生所形容的那样，是一个“心胸坦荡如门户大开”的淳朴汉子，同他攀谈颇为风趣解闷。他坦言自己十分拥护布尔什维克，但对待共产党人却深恶痛绝。在他看来，布尔什维克全都是赤胆忠心的大好人，是劳苦大众的真正贴心知己：他们曾公开主张将一切土地毫无保留奉还给庄稼汉，主张“一切政权归工农苏维埃”。然而共产党人却全是一帮恶棍：他们无情搜刮并毒打庄稼人；他们把自己的党羽亲信安插进各级苏维埃基层政权，广大非党群众在里面连半点话语权都没有。贫农委员会（*kombed*）里塞满了整天游手好闲、好吃懒做的小流氓二流子；这帮地痞成了村里作威作福的霸王，哪个老实巴交的农夫胆敢不向他们低头哈腰跪拜，立马就得大祸临头“倒大霉”！他曾亲自在抵御邓尼金（Denikin）的前线浴血作战，那里的黑幕如出一辙：共产党人和专横的人民委员一手遮天、独断专行，骑在广大应征入伍的子弟兵头上作威作福。早年在前线，战士们尚能畅所欲言发表政见、连队委员会能够自主民主决断一切事务，那时才是真正崇高的真自由，人人都发自肺腑觉得自己是神圣革命的一份子！然而如今，这一切早已荡然无存。现在谁也不敢吐露半句肺腑真言——身旁时刻潜伏着共产党密探，你随时可能面临被暗中检举告密的灭顶之灾。正因如此，他才愤然当了逃兵——是的，他前前后后足足当了2次逃兵！他此前在军营里听说家里的农场遭到了官府抄家式洗劫，便下定决心偷跑回家瞧瞧究竟是不是真的。结果残酷的事实证明：现实比传闻还要凄惨百倍！甚至连他年仅刚满16岁的小弟弟也被强行征兵拉了壮丁。家里只剩下年迈体衰的双亲，无人帮衬他们根本无力耕作这片薄田，而家里所有的牲畜早就被官家没收充公一空！

===Original===

The Commissars had left almost no horses in his village and only one cow to each family of five persons, and if a peasant had only two little children his last cow was taken away. He decided to stay and help his folks﻿—it was spring, and planting had to be done. But he had a narrow escape. One day the whole village was surrounded by the Commissar and his men. He ran out of his hut and made for the woods. Bad luck, he was still in his soldier uniform, and they shot at him from all sides. He succeeded in reaching the nearest bushes, but he was exhausted and fell, rolling down the hill into a hollow. His pursuers must have thought him dead. Late in the night he stole back to the village, but he did not go to his people; a friendly neighbor hid him in his house. The next day he put on peasant clothes, and all spring and summer he helped his “old man” in the fields. Then he went back to the Army of his own accord: he wanted to serve the Revolution as long as the folks at home did not need him. But he was treated badly, food was scarce in his regiment, and he deserted again. “I would stay in the Army,” he concluded, “but I can’t see the old people starve to death.”
“Are you not afraid to talk so freely?” I warned him.
“Oh, who cares!” he laughed. “Let ’em shoot me. Am I a dog to wear a muzzle on my snout?”

===Chinese===

人民委员几乎把他们全村的耕马搜刮得一匹不剩，规定每5口人之家才准许留存1头奶牛；倘若某户农家只有2个年幼的娃娃，其家里仅剩的最后一头救命奶牛也会被强行夺走充公！他当即下决心留在家里帮衬爹娘——当时正值大好春耕时节，田里必须抓紧犁田下种。然而他却险些搭上了小命：有一天，人民委员率领大批荷枪实弹的武装军警突然将整个村庄围得水泄不通。他吓得夺门冲出茅屋直奔后山树林狂奔。倒霉的是他身上依然穿着惹眼的军装制服，四周密集的子弹如暴雨般朝他倾泻射击。他拼死冲进了最近的一处灌木丛，但终因体力透支一头栽倒，顺着山坡一路翻滚进了一处隐蔽的山洼深处。追击的追兵见状大概以为他早已中弹身亡。直到深夜他才偷偷潜回村庄，但他不敢贸然回家，一位仗义的好心邻居将他秘密藏匿在自己家中。次日一早他换上了粗布农服，整个春夏两季他都在田间地头协助“老父亲”辛勤耕作。随后，他又自觉自愿主动重返部队服役：只要家里老小暂时不需要他帮衬，他依然由衷渴望能够为保卫伟大革命流血效力。然而在部队里他却饱受残酷虐待，所在团队粮饷极度匮乏，于是他忍无可忍再度当了逃兵。“倘若情况允许，我本愿意继续在军队里战斗效忠，”他最后动情地总结道，“但我绝不能眼睁睁看着自己年迈的爹娘活活饿死啊！”
“你这般毫无顾忌地畅所欲言，难道就不怕惹来杀身之祸吗？”我好心出言提醒他道。
“嗨，爱谁谁，谁在乎呢！”他放声豁达大笑起来，“大不了由着他们把老子崩了就是！老子难道是一条生来就该被套上嘴套的看门狗吗？！”

### IV / IV
===Original===

Three days later Prelide notified me at Sebezh of the arrival of a new group of emigrants. Hoping they might be the long expected political deportees from America, I hastened to the border. To our great disappointment the men proved to be war prisoners returning from England. There were 108 in the group, captured the previous year in the Archangel district and still clad in their Red Guard uniforms. Among them were also five Russian workers, who had for years resided in England and who were now deported under the Alien Act. They were in civilian attire, and Prehde immediately decided that they were “suspicious,” and ordered them arrested as British spies. The deportees took the matter lightly, not realizing that it might mean a perfunctory field court-martial and immediate execution.
I had become friendly with Prehde, and grown to like his simplicity and sincerity. Entirely unsophisticated, he knows no consideration save his duty to the Revolution; his treatment of alleged counterrevolutionaries is no more severe than his personal asceticism. The taking of human life he considers a personal tragedy, a harsh ordeal his conscience is subjected to by revolutionary exigency. “It would be treachery to evade it,” he had said to me.
I decided to appeal to him in behalf of the arrested civilians. They should be informed of the suspicion against them, I urged, and be given an opportunity to clear themselves. Prehde consented to let me talk with the men and promised to be guided by my impressions.
“Just walk a bit with them and examine them,” he directed.
“Out here in the open?” I asked in surprise.
“Certainly. If they attempt to run, they are guilty. I’m a dead shot.”
Half an hour’s conversation with the “suspects” convinced me of their inoffensiveness. One of them, a half-witted young fellow, was deported from England as a public nuisance; another for refusing to pay his wife alimony; the third had been convicted of operating a gambling resort, and two were radical workingmen arrested at a Bolshevik meeting in Edinburgh. Prehde agreed to put them in my care till I returned to Petrograd, where they could be further examined and proper disposition made of them.
From the British officers accompanying the war prisoners I learned that no politicals had been deported from the United States since the *Buford* group.

===Chinese===

三天后，普列德（Prehde）在谢贝日（Sebezh）通知我，又有一批新移民抵达。我满心希望他们可能就是期盼已久的从美国遣返的政治流亡者，便匆匆赶往边境。然而令我们大失所望的是，这批人原来是从英国归国的战俘。整批人共有108名，是前一年在阿尔汉格尔斯克地区被俘的，身上仍穿着赤卫军制服。在他们当中，还有5名长年在英国定居、如今依据《外侨法》（Alien Act）被遣返的俄国工人。这5人身穿便服，普列德立刻认定他们“形迹可疑”，并下令以英国间谍罪名将他们逮捕。那些被遣返者对此不以为意，完全没有意识到这可能意味着走过场般的战地军事法庭审判和就地枪决。
我已与普列德结下了友谊，并渐渐喜欢上他的朴实与真诚。他为人纯朴至极，除了对革命的职责之外心中别无他念；他对待所谓反革命分子的严苛，丝毫不亚于他自身的苦修生活。夺取人的生命被他视为个人的悲剧，是他的良知在革命紧急情势下不得不承受的残酷考验。“逃避它便是背叛，”他曾对我这样说过。
我决定为了被捕的平民向他求情。我力陈应当告知他们所受到的怀疑，并给他们澄清自己的机会。普列德同意让我与这几个人谈话，并答应参考我的观感来作决定。
“你就陪他们走走，盘问他们一番，”他吩咐道。
“就在这片开阔地里？”我诧异地问。
“当然。如果他们企图逃跑，那就是有罪。我可是百发百中的神枪手。”
与这几名“嫌疑人”交谈了半个小时后，我确信他们毫无危害。其中一个是个半痴半呆的年轻小伙，因在公共场所滋事而被英国遣返；另一个是因为拒绝给妻子支付赡养费；第三个曾因经营赌场被定罪；还有两个人是在爱丁堡的一次布尔什维克集会上被捕的激进工人。普列德同意将他们交由我监护，直到我返回彼得格勒，届时再对他们作进一步审查并妥善处置。
从护送战俘的英国军官口中我得知，自从“布福德号”（Buford）那一批人之后，美国便再也没有驱逐过任何政治犯。

===Original===

The Major in charge of the convoy is American born; his assistant, a lieutenant, a Russian Jew from Petrograd. Both asserted that Europe is tired of war, and they spoke sympathetically of the Soviet Republic. “It ought to be given a fair chance,” the Major said.
I wired to Chicherin about the arrival of the second group and the certainty that no American deportees are en route. At the same time I informed him that I would use Sanitary Train 81, the only one remaining on the border, to take the men to Petrograd.
By long distance telephone and by telegram came Chicherin’s order to “wait till the Foreign Office learns the date of arrival of the American emigrants.” We had already spent over a week on the border, and our provisions were running low, Petrograd having supplied us with only three days’ rations. What was to be done with over a hundred men, some of them ill? Feeling certain that Chicherin was misinformed about the “American emigrants,” I decided to ignore directions from “the center” and return to Petrograd.
But the local officials resented such defiance of authority and refused to act, and we were compelled to remain. Two more days passed, the famished war prisoners grew threatening, and at last the authorities consented to permit our train to depart.
Returning with Karus and Ethel that evening from the village to make final preparations for leaving, we were surprised not to find our train at the station. For hours we searched in every direction till a passing soldier informed us that heavy firing had been heard on the border, and as a precaution our white-painted train was moved out of range.
The night was pitch black. Leaving Ethel on the station platform I walked along the railroad track till I stumbled against a wall of cars. Someone hailed me and I recognized the voice of Karus. He lit his portable lamp and we tried to enter a car, but the doors were locked and sealed. Suddenly we felt the air hissing, and bullets began to pelt about us. “They are shooting at my light,” Karus cried, throwing his lamp down. We slowly followed the tracks till we came to a car emitting sounds of snoring, and we entered.
The smell of unclean human bodies hung heavily in the heated air, assailing us with suffocating force. We felt our way in the darkness along the aisle between double rows of booted feet when a gruff voice shouted:
“*Dezhurney*,17 who’s there?”

===Chinese===

负责护送车队的少校是美国土生土长的人；他的助手是一名中尉，来自彼得格勒的俄籍犹太人。两人都声称欧洲对战争已厌倦至极，并且同情地谈起了苏维埃共和国。“应当给它一个公平的机会，”少校说。
我给契切林发了电报，告知第二批人的抵达，并确认没有美国被遣返者在途。同时我通知他，我将使用留在边境的唯一一列列车——81号卫生列车，把这批人送往彼得格勒。
通过长途电话和电报，契切林传来了命令：“等外交人民委员部获悉美国移民抵俄的具体日期再行动。”我们已在边境待了一个多星期，给养渐渐耗尽，彼得格勒只给我们配发了3天的口粮。面对这一百多号人，其中有些还患有疾病，该怎么办？我确信契切林关于“美国移民”的消息有误，于是决定无视来自“中心”的指示，直接返回彼得格勒。
然而地方官员对这种蔑视上级权威的行径大为不满，拒绝配合行动，我们被迫滞留。又过了两天，饥肠辘辘的战俘情绪愈发激愤，地方当局最终才同意放行我们的列车。
那天傍晚，我与卡鲁斯和埃塞尔从村里回来准备启程前的最终事宜，却惊愕地发现我们的列车不在车站。我们在各个方向搜寻了数小时，直到一名路过的士兵告诉我们，边境传来了激烈的交火声，出于防范考量，我们那列漆成白色的列车已被调到了射程之外。
黑夜漆黑一片。我把埃塞尔留在车站站台，自己沿着铁轨摸索前行，直到撞上了一堵车厢壁。有人冲我喊话，我听出是卡鲁斯的声音。他点亮了手提灯，我们试图钻进一节车厢，但车门都被锁死并贴上了封条。突然间，我们感到空气中传来尖锐的呼啸声，子弹开始在我们周围倾泻。“他们在朝我的灯光射击！”卡鲁斯大喊一声，扔掉了手提灯。我们沿着铁轨缓缓摸索，直到来到一节传出鼾声的车厢前，便钻了进去。
污秽的人体气味在闷热的空气中浓重弥漫，带着令人窒息的恶臭扑面而来。我们在黑暗中沿着两排穿着皮靴的脚之间的过道摸索前行，突然一个粗哑的嗓门吼道：
“执勤员17，谁在那儿？”

===Original===

From one of the benches a soldier rose, fully clad and with gun in hand.
“Who goes there?” he challenged sleepily.
“How dare you let anyone into this car, you scoundrel, you!” another shouted.
“They just came in, *tovarish*.”
“You’re a liar, you’ve been sleeping on duty.” A string of curses poured forth upon the soldier, involving his mother and her alleged lovers in the picturesque vocabulary of the Russian oath.
The cursing voice sounded near. I saw a huge red star, five-pointed, with hammer and sickle in the center, pinned on the man’s breast.
“Get out of here, you devils,” the man shouted, “or I’ll fill you full of lead.”
“Easy, *tovarish*,” Karus warned him, “and be a bit more polite.”
“Get out!” the Commissar roared. “You don’t know to whom you’re talking. We’re the *boyevaia*18 Cheka.”
“There may be others such,” Karus replied significantly. “We can’t find our car and we’d like to pass the night here.”
“But you can’t remain here,” the man remonstrated in a quieter tone, “we may be called for action any moment.”
“My *tovarish* is from the Petro-Soviet,” Karus declared, indicating me; “we can’t remain in the open.”
“Well, stay then.” The Commissar yawned and stretched himself on the bench.
I called Ethel into the car. She looked cold and tired, and hardly able to stand. In the dark I felt for a vacant place, but everywhere my hands touched human bodies. The men snored to various tunes, some cursing in their sleep.
I heard Karus climb up to the second tier and a woman’s angry voice, “Quit your pushing, devil.” “Make room, you heifer,” came from Karus, “fine fighting men these, with a car full of whores.”
In a corner we found a bench piled with rifles, dishes, and old clothes. No sooner did we sit down than we became conscious of vermin crawling upon us. “I hope we don’t catch typhus,” Ethel whispered fearfully. In the distance guns were being fired; now and then shots sounded nearby. Outside on the tracks two men were quarreling.
“You leave my woman be,” a drunken voice threatened.
“Your woman!” the other sneered. “Why not mine?”
“I’ll show you, you bastard son of your mother’s lovers!” There came a dull thud, and all was quiet again.
Ethel shuddered. “If it were only daylight,” she murmured. Her head fell heavily on my shoulder and she slept.
---
*March 27.*﻿—Arrived in Petrograd today.

===Chinese===

一名士兵从长椅上爬了起来，衣冠整齐，手握枪支。
“谁在那儿？”他睡眼惺忪地盘问道。
“你竟敢放人进这节车厢，你这个混账东西！”另一个人吼道。
“他们刚进来，同志。”
“你撒谎，你刚才值班时睡着了。”一连串不堪入耳的咒骂向那名士兵劈头盖脸砸去，用俄语骂人话中最绘声绘色的词汇，问候了他的母亲及其所谓的情人们。
咒骂声听上去就在近旁。我看到那人胸前别着一枚巨大的五角红星，中央带有锤子和镰刀图案。
“滚出去，你们这群魔鬼，”那人叫嚷道，“不然我拿子弹把你们喂饱。”
“悠着点，同志，”卡鲁斯警告他，“客气一点。”
“滚出去！”政委咆哮道，“你根本不知道在跟谁说话。我们可是战斗18契卡（boyevaia Cheka）！”
“这样的部门可不止你们一家，”卡鲁斯意味深长地回答，“我们找不到自己的车厢，想在这里过夜。”
“但你们不能待在这儿，”那人放低了声音抗议道，“我们随时可能奉命出动。”
“我这位同志是彼得格勒苏维埃的，”卡鲁斯指着我声明道，“我们不能露宿在外头。”
“行吧，那就待着吧。”政委打了个哈欠，在长椅上伸展身子躺下了。
我把埃塞尔叫进了车厢。她浑身发冷，疲惫不堪，几乎站立不稳。我在黑暗中摸索空位，但双手摸到的到处都是人的身躯。男人们打着各式各样的呼噜，有的还在梦呓中咒骂。
我听见卡鲁斯爬上了第二层铺位，跟着传来一个女人愤怒的尖叫：“别推了，活见鬼！”“挪个地方，你这头小母牛，”卡鲁斯回敬道，“好一群战斗勇士，车厢里竟塞满了妓女。”
我们在角落里找到一张堆满步枪、碗碟和旧衣服的长椅。刚一坐下，我们便察觉到跳蚤虱子在身上爬动。“但愿我们别染上斑疹伤寒，”埃塞尔心惊胆战地低语。远处炮声隆隆，近处不时传来枪声。车厢外的铁轨上，两个男人正在争吵。
“离我的女人远点，”一个醉醺醺的嗓门威胁道。
“你的女人！”另一个人冷笑，“怎么就不能是我的？”
“我让你尝尝厉害，你这个野种杂碎！”随即传来一声沉闷的重击，四周又重归死寂。
埃塞尔打了个寒战。“要是天亮了该多好，”她喃喃自语。她的头沉沉地垂在我的肩头，睡了过去。
---
3月27日。——今日抵达彼得格勒。

===Original===

To my consternation I found the returned war prisoners still at the railroad station. No steps had been taken to quarter and feed them because they “were not expected” and no “orders” had yet come from Moscow.

===Chinese===

令我惊愕的是，我发现归国的战俘依然滞留在火车站。根本没有任何安置食宿的举措，因为他们“不在预期计划之中”，而且莫斯科方面也尚未下达任何“指示”。

## XV Back in Petrograd / XV 重返彼得格勒
===Original===

*April 2, 1920.*﻿—I found Zinoviev very ill; his condition﻿—it is rumored﻿—is due to mistreatment at the hands of workers. The story goes that several factories had passed resolutions criticizing the administration for corruption and inefficiency, and that subsequently some of the men were arrested. When Zinoviev later visited the mill, he was assaulted.
Nothing of such matters is to be found in the *Pravda* or *Krasnaya Gazetta*, the official dailies. They contain little news of any kind, being almost exclusively devoted to agitation and to appeals to the people to stand by the Government and the Communist Party in saving the country from counterrevolution and economic ruin.
Bill Shatov is expected back from Siberia. His wife Nunya is in the hospital, at the point of death, it is feared, and Bill has been wired for. With surprise I have learned that Shatov failed to reply to our radios or to meet the *Buford* group at the border because he was forbidden to do so by “higher authorities.” It also explains why Zorin pretended that Shatov had gone East when as a matter of fact he was still in Petrograd.
It appears that Bill, in spite of his great services to the Revolution, had fallen into disfavor; grave charges were made against him, and he was even in danger of his life. Lenin saved Shatov because he was a good organizer and “could still be useful.” Bill was virtually exiled to Siberia, and it is believed that he will not be permitted to return to Petrograd to see his dying wife.
Most of the *Buford* deportees are still idle. The data I prepared for Zorin, and the plans I worked out for the employment of the men, have not been acted upon. The former enthusiasm of the boys has turned to discouragement. “Bolshevik red tape,” S﻿—﻿— remarked to me, “is wasting our time and energies. I’ve worn out my last pair of shoes running about trying to get work. They discriminate against the non-Communists. The Bolsheviki claim they need good workers, but if you are not a Communist they don’t want you. We’ve been called counterrevolutionists, and the Chief of the Cheka has even threatened to send us to prison.”
---
At the home of my friend M﻿—﻿—, on the Vassilevsky Ostrov, I met several men and women, sitting in their overcoats around the *bourzhuika*, the little iron stove which they kept feeding with old newspapers and magazines.

===Chinese===

1920年4月2日。——我发现季诺维也夫（Zinoviev）病得很重；据传他的病情是因为遭到了工人们的殴打。传闻称，有几家工厂通过了决议，指责行政管理部门贪腐无能，随后便有几名工人被捕。季诺维也夫后来前去视察工厂时，便遭到了围攻。
在官方日报《真理报》（Pravda）或《红色报》（Krasnaya Gazetta）上，根本看不到任何有关此类事件的报道。这些报纸几乎没有任何新闻内容，通篇充斥着鼓动宣传以及呼吁人民支持政府和共产党以挽救国家免于反革命和经济崩溃的口号。
比尔·沙托夫（Bill Shatov）预计将从西伯利亚返回。他的妻子努尼娅（Nunya）正在住院，恐怕已濒临死亡，大家已发电报催促比尔赶回。令我诧异的是，我得知沙托夫当初之所以既未回复我们的无线电报，也未在边境迎接“布福德号”一行，是因为受到了“上级当局”的严令禁止。这也解释了佐林当时为何谎称沙托夫去了远东，而实际上他那时人还在彼得格勒。
看来比尔尽管为革命立下了汗马功劳，却已失宠；甚至有人对他提出了严重的指控，他甚至一度面临生命危险。列宁救下了沙托夫，因为他是个出色的组织人才，“依然大有用处”。比尔实际上是被流放到了西伯利亚，人们认为当局甚至不会允许他回到彼得格勒见病危的妻子最后一面。
“布福德号”上的大多数被遣返者依然处于无所事事的状态。我为佐林准备的数据材料，以及我为这些人的就业安置制订的详尽方案，全都没有得到落实。小伙子们最初的热情如今已化作了心灰意冷。“布尔什维克的官僚主义红榜公文，”S——对我抱怨道，“正在白白虚耗我们的时间和精力。为了找份工作到处奔波，我连最后一双鞋都磨破了。他们歧视非共产党员。布尔什维克嘴上说需要优秀的劳动者，但如果你不是共产党员，他们就根本不要你。我们甚至被扣上了反革命分子的帽子，契卡（Cheka）的头目甚至扬言要把我们关进监狱。”
---
在瓦西里岛（Vassilevsky Ostrov）我的朋友M——家中，我见到了几位男女。他们穿着大衣围坐在“资产阶级小铁炉”（bourzhuika）旁，不停地往炉子里塞进旧报纸和旧杂志取暖。

===Original===

“Doesn’t it seem incredible,” the host was saying, “that Petrograd, with great forests in its vicinity, should freeze for lack of fuel? We’d get the wood if they’d only let us. You remember those barges on the Neva? They had been neglected, and they were falling to pieces. The workers of the N﻿—﻿— factory wanted to take them apart and use the lumber for fuel. But the Government refused. ‘We’ll attend to it ourselves,’ they said. Well, what happened? Nothing was done, of course, and the tide didn’t wait for official routine. The barges were swept out to sea and lost.”
“The Communists won’t stand for independent initiative,” one of the women remarked; “it’s dangerous for their regime.”
“No, my friends, it’s no use deluding yourselves,” a tall, bearded man retorted. “Russia is not ripe for Communism. Social revolution is possible only in a country with the highest industrial development. It was the greatest crime of the Bolsheviki that they forcibly suspended the Constituent Assembly. They usurped governmental power, but the whole country is against them. What can you expect under such circumstances? They have to resort to terror to force the people to do their bidding, and of course everything goes to ruin.”
“That’s a good Marxist talking,” a Left Socialist Revolutionist rejoined, good humoredly; “but you forget that Russia is an agrarian, not an industrial country, and will always remain such. You Social Democrats don’t understand the peasant; the Bolsheviki distrust him and discriminate against him. Their proletarian dictatorship is an insult and an injury to the peasantry. Dictatorship must be that of Toil, to be exercised by the peasants and the workers together. Without the cooperation of the peasantry the country is doomed.”
“As long as you’ll have dictatorship, you’ll have present conditions,” the Anarchist host replied. “The centralized State, that is the great evil. It does not permit the creative impulses of the people to express themselves. Give the people a chance, let them exercise their initiative and constructive energies﻿—only that will save the Revolution.”
“You fellows don’t realize the great role the Bolsheviki have played,” a slender, nervous man spoke up. “They have made mistakes, of course, but they were not those of timidity or cowardice. They dispersed the Constituent Assembly? The more power to them!

===Chinese===

“难道这不可笑、不可思议吗，”主人说道，“彼得格勒周围明明有大片茂密的森林，却因为缺少燃料而挨冻？只要他们肯允许，我们自己就能把木柴搞来。你们还记得涅瓦河上的那些驳船吗？那些驳船无人照看，正在逐渐腐烂散架。N——工厂的工人们想把它们拆掉，将木料作为燃料。但政府拒绝了。‘我们自己会处理，’他们说。可结果呢？当然是什么都没做，而潮水可不会等待官方的公文流程。驳船被水流卷入大海，彻底损失了。”
“共产党人容不下任何独立的自主倡议，”一位妇女评论道，“这对他们的政权构成了威胁。”
“不，朋友们，自欺欺人是毫无用处的，”一位身材高大、留着胡须的男子反驳道，“俄国根本还没有成熟到可以实行共产主义。社会革命唯有在工业最发达的国家才有可能实现。布尔什维克最严重的罪行，就是强行解散了立宪会议。他们篡夺了国家政权，但全国人民都反对他们。在这种情况下你们还能指望什么呢？他们不得不诉诸恐怖手段来强迫人民听从差遣，而结果自然就是百业凋敝、一切走向毁灭。”
“这番高论真像是个十足的马克思主义者在说话，”一位左翼社会革命党人诙谐地插话道，“但你忘了，俄国是一个农业国，而不是工业国，而且永远都将如此。你们社会民主党人根本不懂农民；布尔什维克不信任农民，并且歧视农民。他们的无产阶级专政对农民而言既是一种侮辱，也是一种伤害。专政必须是劳动的专政，必须由农民和工人共同行使。没有农民的通力合作，这个国家注定走向灭亡。”
“只要专政继续存在，目前的局面就永远不会改变，”无政府主义者主人回答道，“中央集权国家才是万恶之源。它根本不允许人民的创造性冲动得以舒展表达。给人民一个机会，让他们发挥自己的主动性与建设性能量——唯有如此才能挽救革命。”
“你们这些人根本没有意识到布尔什维克所扮演的伟大角色，”一位瘦弱神经质的男子开口道，“他们固然犯过错误，但绝非出于怯懦或软弱。他们驱散了立宪会议？那正说明他们干得漂亮！”

===Original===

They did no more than Cromwell did to the Long Parliament: they sent the idle talkers away. And, incidentally, it was an Anarchist, Anton Zhelezniakov, on duty that night with his sailors at the palace, who ordered the Assembly to go home. You talk of violence and terror﻿—do you imagine a Revolution is a drawing-room affair? The Revolution must be sustained at all costs; the more drastic the measures, the more humanitarian in the long run. The Bolsheviki are Statists, extreme governmentalists, and their ruthless centralization holds danger. But a revolutionary period, such as we are going through, is not possible without dictatorship. It is a necessary evil that will be outlived only with the full victory of the Revolution. If the left political opponents would join hands with the Bolsheviki and help in the great work, the evils of the present regime would be mitigated and constructive effort hastened.”
“You’re a Sovietski Anarchist,” the others teased him.
---
Almost every *otvetstvenny* (responsible) Communist is gone to Moscow to attend the Ninth Congress of the Party. Grave questions are at issue, and Lenin and Trotsky have sounded the keynote﻿—militarization of labor. The papers are filled with the discussion of the proposed introduction of *yedinolitchiye* (one-man industrial management) to take the place of the present collegiate form. “We must learn from the bourgeoisie,” Lenin says, “and use them for our purposes.”
Among the labor elements there is strong opposition to the new plan, but Trotsky contends that the unions have failed in the management of industry: the proposed system is to organize production more efficiently. The labor men, on the contrary, say that the workers had not been given the opportunity, extreme State centralization having taken over the functions of the unions. *Yedinolitchiye*, they claim, means complete charge of factory and shop by one man, the so-called *spets* (specialists), to the exclusion of the workers from management.
“Step by step we are losing everything we’ve gained by the Revolution,” a shop committeeman said to me. “The new plan means the return of the former master. The *spets* is the old *bourzhooi*, and now he is coming back to whip us to work again. But last year Lenin himself denounced the plan as counterrevolutionary, when the Mensheviki advocated it. They are still in prison for it.”

===Chinese===

“他们所做的无非就像克伦威尔对待长期议会那样：把那些空谈客统统打发回家。顺便提一句，正是无政府主义者安东·热列兹尼亚科夫（Anton Zhelezniakov），当晚带领水兵在宫殿值勤时，下令让立宪会议解散回家的。你们口口声声谈论暴力与恐怖——难道你们以为革命是客厅里的闲情雅致吗？革命必须不惜一切代价维系下去；手段越果断彻底，从长远来看就越具有人道主义意义。布尔什维克固然是国家主义者、极端的政府管治论者，其无情的中央集权确实潜藏着危险。然而像我们如今所经历的革命时期，没有专政是绝对无法度过的。这是一种必要的恶，只有随着革命的彻底胜利才会被历史淘汰。倘若左翼反对派能够同布尔什维克携起手来，在伟大的事业中助一臂之力，现行政权的弊端就会减轻，建设性的努力也会大大加快。”
“你简直是个‘苏维埃无政府主义者’，”其他人取笑他道。
---
几乎所有负有责任的共产党人（otvetstvenny）都前往莫斯科出席党的第九次代表大会（Ninth Congress）。大会面临着一系列重大议题，列宁和托洛茨基已经定下了基调——劳动军事化。各家报纸充斥着关于提议推行“一长制”（yedinolitchiye，即个人负责制管理工业）以取代现行委员会集体制的讨论。“我们必须向资产阶级学习，”列宁说道，“并利用他们来达到我们的目的。”
在劳工阶层中，对这项新方案存在着强烈的反对意见，但托洛茨基坚称工会在工业管理方面已经失败：拟议的制度是为了更高效地组织生产。相反，工会人士则表示，工人们根本没有获得施展的机会，极端的国家集权已经剥夺了工会的各项职能。他们声称，“一长制”意味着工厂和车间由一人独揽大权，即所谓的“旧专家”（spets，专家），把工人们彻底排除在管理之外。
“我们正一步步丧失通过革命所获得的一切成果，”一名车间委员会成员对我说道，“这个新方案意味着昔日骑在我们头上的东家卷土重来。‘专家’就是过去的‘资产阶级老爷’（bourzhooi），如今他又回来挥舞皮鞭逼我们干活了。可就在去年，当孟什维克提议推行这种方案时，列宁亲自谴责它是反革命行径。他们至今还因此被关在牢里呢。”

===Original===

Others are less outspoken. This morning I met N﻿—﻿—, of the *Buford* group, a man of intellectual attainment and much political acumen. “What do you think of it?” I asked, anxious to know his view of the proposed changes.
“I can’t afford the luxury of expressing an opinion,” he replied with a sad smile. “I have been promised a place on a commission to be sent to Europe. It’s my only chance of joining my wife and children.”
---
*April 4.*﻿—A beautiful, bright Sunday. In the morning I attended the burial of Semyon Voskov, a prominent Communist agitator killed on the front by typhus. I had met him in the States, and he impressed me as a fine type of revolutionist and enthusiastic devotee of the Bolsheviki. Now his body lay in state in the Uritsky Palace, and high tribute was paid the dead as an heroic victim of the Revolution.
Along the Nevsky the funeral procession wended its way to the Field of Mars, marching to the strains of music and the singing of a choir from Archangel. Thousands of workers followed the hearse, line after line of men and women from shop and factory, tired toilers, in a spiritless, mechanical way. Military salutes were fired at the grave, and eulogies pronounced by several speakers﻿—rather official, I thought; all too partisan, lacking the warm personal note.
The huge demonstration, arranged by the Petrograd Soviet of labor unions within twenty-four hours, as I was informed, seemed a striking proof of organization. I congratulated the chief Committee man on the quick and efficient work.
“Done without my leaving the office,” he said proudly. “The Soviet decision was wired to every mill and factory, ordering each to send a certain contingent of its employees to the demonstration. And the thing was ready.”
“It was not left to the choice of the men?” I asked in surprise.
“Well,” he smiled, “we leave nothing to chance.”
Returning from the Voskov funeral I met another procession. Two men and a woman walked behind a pushcart on which stood a rough, unpainted pine coffin, holding the dead body of their brother. A young girl, leading a little child by the hand, was wearily following the remains to its last resting place. Three men on the sidewalk stopped to watch the tragic sight. The mourners passed in silence, the picture of misery and friendlessness﻿—black cameos sharply etched on the bright day.

===Chinese===

其他人则不那么直言不讳。今天上午，我碰见了“布福德号”一行中的N——，他是一位颇有学识且极富政治敏锐度的人。“你对这事怎么看？”我急切地想了解他对拟议变革的看法，便问道。
“我可享受不起发表意见的奢侈，”他凄然一笑回答道，“有人许诺给我一个派往欧洲的代表团名额。这是我与妻子和孩子们团聚的唯一机会。”
---
4月4日。——一个明媚晴朗的星期天。上午我参加了谢苗·沃斯科夫（Semyon Voskov）的葬礼，他是一位著名的共产党鼓动家，在战线前沿染上斑疹伤寒身亡。我曾在美国与他相识，他给我的印象是一位优秀的革命者和布尔什维克的狂热拥趸。如今他的灵柩停放在乌里茨基宫接受瞻仰，人们向这位革命的英雄牺牲者致以崇高的敬意。
送葬队伍沿着涅瓦大街缓缓前行，在哀乐声与来自阿尔汉格尔斯克的合唱团的歌声中走向战神广场。成千上万名工人紧跟在灵车之后，车间和工厂的男女工人们排成一队又一队，那些疲惫的劳苦大众神情麻木、机械地迈着步子。墓地前鸣放了军礼枪，几位发言人宣读了悼词——在我看来官僚味颇浓；言辞全然带有强烈的党派色彩，缺乏温润的人情味。
据我得知，这场由彼得格勒工会苏维埃在24小时之内筹办起来的盛大游行集会，似乎成了强大组织能力的惊人证明。我就这番迅速而高效的筹备工作向委员会主要负责人表示祝贺。
“我连办公室都没出就办妥了，”他得意地说道，“苏维埃的决议通过电报拍发到了各大工厂，命令每家工厂抽调一定比例的雇员参加示威游行。事情就这样办成了。”
“这并非出于工人们的自愿选择？”我惊讶地问。
“哎，”他微笑道，“我们绝不把任何事情留给侥幸。”
从沃斯科夫的葬礼归来途中，我又遇到了另一支出殡队伍。两名男子和一名女子推着一辆手推车前行，车上放着一口粗糙、未上漆的松木薄棺，里面盛放着他们兄弟的遗体。一名年轻姑娘手里牵着一个幼童，步履沉重疲惫地跟在遗体后面，送他前往最后的安息之所。人行道上有3名男子停下脚步注视着这悲惨的一幕。送葬者们默默无语地走过，满面尽是苦难与无助的苍凉神色——宛如在明媚的白昼中被深刻雕凿出的一枚枚漆黑浮雕。

===Original===

In the distance crashed the martial music of the Bolshevik funeral and long lines of soldiers in parade dress, their bayoneted guns glistening in the sun, marched to the Field of Mars to pay honor to Voskov, Communist martyr.
---
*Easter Week.*﻿—No newspapers have appeared for several days. There have been rumors of possible excesses by the religious element, but the city is quiet.
At midnight (April 10) I attended mass at St. Isaac’s Cathedral. The huge edifice was cold and vault-like; the deep bass of the priest sounded like a requiem of his faith. The flock, mostly men and women of the former middle class, looked depressed, as if by thoughts of a glory past forever.
After services the worshipers formed in procession on the street, thrice circling the Cathedral. They walked slowly, in silence, no joy in the traditional greeting, “Christ is risen!” “Indeed he is risen,” came spiritlessly in reply. Scattered shots were heard in the distance. Two women embraced on the steps of the church and sobbed aloud.
In the Kazan Cathedral the attendance was predominately proletarian. I felt the same suppressed atmosphere, as if some vague fear possessed the people. The procession in the dark streets was mournful, funereal. The little wax candles resembled will-o’-the-wisps blown about by the breeze, their unsteady flicker faintly suggesting the icons and banners fluttering above the heads of the worshipers. Faith is still alive, but the power of the Church is broken.
---
Bieland arrived from America bringing the first direct news I have had from the States. Reaction is rampant, he relates; 100% Americanism is celebrating its bloody victory. The wartime laws passed as measures of temporary necessity remain in operation and are applied with greater severity than before. The prisons are filled with politicals; most of the active I.W.W. are in jail, and draft evaders and conscientious objectors are still being arrested. Radicalism is outlawed; independent opinion a crime. The militaristic humanitarianism of Wilson has become a war against progress. The heritage of “war against war” is more deadly than the slaughter itself.
Bill Haywood, released on bail, has again been arrested. Rose Pastor Stokes was extradited to Illinois for a speech that displeased some officials; Larkin is about to be put on trial, and Gitlow was condemned to fifteen years.

===Chinese===

远处传来了布尔什维克国葬宏亮铿锵的军乐声，身穿阅兵礼服的士兵排成长队，刺刀在阳光下闪闪发亮，正迈步向战神广场行进，去缅怀共产主义烈士沃斯科夫。
---
复活节周。——一连好几天都没有报纸发行。曾有传闻称宗教信徒可能会有极端举动，但全城依然平静。
4月10日午夜，我前往圣伊萨克大教堂参加弥撒。这座巨大的建筑阴冷得犹如地窖；神父低沉的男低音回荡其间，听上去宛如其信仰的安魂曲。前来的教众大多是昔日中产阶级的男女，面带愁容，仿佛正沉湎于对一去不复返的往日荣耀的哀思之中。
仪式结束后，信徒们在街头排成游行队伍，绕大教堂转了三次。他们默默地缓步前行，传统的节日问候“基督复活了！”中没有丝毫喜悦。“他确实复活了，”回应的声音有气无力。远处传来了稀落的枪声。两名妇女在教堂的台阶上相拥而泣。
在喀山大教堂，出席的信众则以无产者为主。我感受到了同样压抑的气氛，仿佛某种莫名的恐惧攫住了人们的心灵。黑暗街道上的游行队伍凄凉得如同出殡。微弱的蜡烛光宛如被微风摇曳的磷火，摇曳不定的烛光依稀映出信徒头顶上飘动的圣像与旗帜。信仰虽仍残存，但教会的权威已被彻底摧毁。
---
比兰（Bieland）从美国赶来，带来了自我离开后得到的第一手来自美国的直接消息。据他讲述，反动势力猖獗泛滥；“100%美国主义”正在庆祝其血腥的胜利。作为战时临时应急措施而通过的各项法令依然在执行，且执行力度比以往更加严苛。监狱里关满了政治犯；世界产业工人联合会（I.W.W.）的活跃分子大部分身陷囹圄，逃避兵役者和出于良知拒服兵役者仍在不断遭到逮捕。激进主义被定为非法；独立意见沦为罪行。威尔逊军事化的人道主义已演变成一场针对进步力量的战争。“反对战争的战争”所留下的遗产，比屠杀本身更为致命。
保释出狱的比尔·海伍德（Bill Haywood）再次被捕。罗丝·帕斯托尔·斯托克斯（Rose Pastor Stokes）因发表令某些官员不悦的演说而被引渡至伊利诺伊州；拉金（Larkin）即将受审，而吉特洛（Gitlow）则被判处了15年徒刑。

===Original===

A similar spirit of reaction is manifest all through Europe. White Terror is in the saddle. Jack Reed has been arrested in Finland en route to America.
“Only here we can breathe freely,” Bieland remarked fervently. I did not gainsay it. Notwithstanding all the faults and shortcomings of the Bolsheviki, I feel that Russia is still the hearth of the Revolution. It is the torch whose light is visible throughout the world, and proletarian hearts in every land are warmed by its glow.
---
*April 12.*﻿—A gloomy day; cloudy, with slight rain﻿—very oppressive after the springlike weather we have been having. It remains light now till 10 p.m.﻿—the clocks had been turned back two hours and recently again another hour.
Liza Zorin was taken to the hospital today, suffering much pain: her child is expected in a few days. Liza refused a private room, even objecting to being treated by a doctor instead of a midwife, like any other proletarian mother. Of delicate physique and suffering from a weak heart, she is strong in spirit: a true Communist who refuses to accept special privileges. She has nothing for her baby, but “other mothers have no more, and why should I?” she says.
Moscow has refused Bill Shatov permission to leave Siberia to visit his sick wife. Though Commissar of Railroads in the Far Eastern Republic, Bill is virtually in exile.
---
Disclosures in the *Pravda* about the Petrograd reformatories for children have stirred the city. A Committee of the Communist Youth had been investigating the institutions, and now its report has uncovered a most deplorable state of affairs. The “reformatories” are charged with being veritable prisons in which the young inmates are regarded as criminals. Defective children are subject to severe punishment, and boyish pranks are treated as serious offenses. The general management has been found permeated with bureaucracy and corruption. A favorite mode of punishment is to deprive the children of their meals, and the food thus saved is appropriated by the managers of the institution. By corrupt methods the Commissars procure supplies on padded lists for purposes of speculation. Nepotism prevails, the number of employees often equaling that of the children.
I had been considering for some time taking up educational work, and I used the opportunity to discuss the matter with Zorin.

===Chinese===

类似的反动气焰在整个欧洲随处可见。白色恐怖肆虐猖獗。约翰·里德（Jack Reed）在取道芬兰前往美国途中被捕。
“唯有在这里我们才能自由呼吸，”比兰动情地感慨道。我并未反驳他的话。抛开布尔什维克的一切过失与不足不谈，我依然感到俄国仍是革命的圣火源地。它是一支照亮全世界的火炬，各国无产者的心灵都在它的光芒中感受到了温暖。
---
4月12日。——阴郁的一天；阴云密布，伴着微雨——在连日的融融春意之后，令人倍感压抑。如今直到晚上10点天色依然明亮——时钟此前已被拨慢了两小时，最近又拨慢了一小时。
丽莎·佐林（Liza Zorin）今天被送往医院，腹痛剧烈：几天后孩子就要出生了。丽莎拒绝住进单人病房，甚至反对由医生而非助产士接生，坚持要像任何其他无产阶级母亲一样对待。她体质羸弱，患有心脏病，但精神上却无比刚毅：她是一位坚决拒绝享有任何特权的真正共产党员。她没有给婴儿准备任何多余的物品，但她常说：“其他母亲也没有更多的东西，凭什么我要搞特殊？”
莫斯科已驳回了比尔·沙托夫离开西伯利亚探望病妻的请求。尽管身为远东共和国的铁道人民委员，比尔实际上仍处于流放状态。
---
《真理报》上关于彼得格勒儿童感化院的内幕揭露在全城引起了轩然大波。共青团的一个委员会一直在对这些机构进行调查，如今其报告揭露出了极其令人痛心的现状。“感化院”被指控沦为了名副其实的监狱，被收容的青少年被视同罪犯。有缺陷的儿童遭到严厉惩罚，男孩子的顽皮打闹也被当作严重罪过严惩。机构的整体管理被发现彻底充斥着官僚主义与贪腐堕落。最常见的惩罚手段之一便是剥夺儿童的饮食，由此省下的口粮则被机构的管理人员据为己有。政委们通过伪造虚报名单的卑劣手段套取给养物资，用于倒买倒卖投机牟利。任人唯亲的裙带风气盛行，工作人员的数量往往与收容儿童的总数旗鼓相当。
一段时间以来我一直在考虑投身教育工作，便借此机会同佐林探讨了此事。

===Original===

He was greatly displeased at the revelations and inclined to consider the school situation exaggerated by the youthful investigators. He insisted that existing evils are due chiefly to the lack of Bolshevik teachers. Only Communists can be trusted in responsible positions, he asserted. Where nonpartisans hold high office, it has become necessary to put a *politkom* (political commissar) at the head of the institution to guard against sabotage. This system, though uneconomical, is imperative in view of the scarcity of Communist organizers and workers. Evils and abuses in Soviet institutions are almost wholly due to this situation, Zorin claims. The average man is a Philistine, whose sole thought is to exploit every opportunity to secure greater advantages for himself, his family, and friends. It is bourgeois human nature, *nitcheve ne podelayesh*. It is true, of course, that most Soviet employees steal and speculate. But the Government is fighting these evils with merciless hand. Such men are often shot as guilty of crime against the Revolution. But the hunger is so great that even communists, those not sufficiently grounded in the ideas and discipline of the Party, often fall victims to temptation. Such receive even less shrift than others. To them the Government is ruthless and justly so: Communists are the advance guard of the Revolution﻿—they should show an example of devotion, honesty, and self-sacrifice.
We discussed means of eradicating the evils in the children’s institutions, and Zorin welcomed my practical suggestions based on educational experience in America. I offered to devote myself to the work, but I felt compelled to make the condition that I be relieved of *politkoms* and be given opportunity to carry out my ideas in the treatment of backward and so-called morally defective children. Zorin referred me to Lilina, Zinoviev’s wife, who is at the head of the educational institutions of Petrograd, and playfully warned me not to repeat the faux pas I had made when I first met the lady.
On that occasion, when I called at Zinoviev’s rooms in the Astoria, a comely young woman answered the bell. “Are you Mme. Zinoviev?”

===Chinese===

他对这些揭露感到十分不快，并倾向于认为年轻的调查员夸大了学校的实际问题。他坚称，现存的弊端主要是由于缺少布尔什维克教师所致。他断言，只有共产党员才能在关键责任岗位上受到信任。在非党人士担任要职的地方，就必须在机构首脑位置派驻一名政治委员（politkom），以防范怠工破坏。鉴于布尔什维克组织者和工作人员极度匮乏，这种制度尽管不够经济，却是势在必行。佐林声称，苏维埃机构内部的种种弊端与滥权几乎完全是这种局面造成的。普通人不过是庸俗市侩，满脑子想的都是利用一切机会为自己、家人和朋友捞取好处。这就是资产阶级的人性弱点，毫无办法（nitcheve ne podelayesh）。固然，大多数苏维埃雇员偷盗贪污、倒买倒卖是事实，但政府正以铁腕无情地打击这些恶行。这类人往往因犯下危害革命罪而被枪决。然而饥荒如此严重，甚至连某些共产党员——那些党性观念与纪律修养不够扎实的人——也往往经受不住诱惑而堕落。这种人受到的处置甚至比普通人更为严厉。政府对待他们冷酷无情，这完全是理所应当的：共产党员是革命的先锋队——他们必须树立奉献、诚实与自我牺牲的榜样。
我们探讨了根除儿童机构弊端的手段，佐林对我基于在美教育经验提出的务实建议表示赞许。我提出愿意全心投入这项工作，但我感到必须附带一个前提条件：即免除对我派遣政治委员，并允许我贯彻自己在对待落后及所谓道德缺陷儿童方面的教育理念。佐林让我去找彼得格勒教育机构负责人、季诺维也夫的妻子利莉娜（Lilina），并开玩笑地提醒我，切莫重蹈初次与这位夫人会面时的失礼覆辙。
那一次，我去阿斯托里亚酒店（Astoria）拜访季诺维也夫的房间，开门的是一位容貌姣好的年轻女子。“请问您是季诺维也夫夫人吗？”

===Original===

I inquired, unconscious of the fact act that I was committing an unpardonable breach of Bolshevik etiquette; in fact, a double breach in employing the bourgeois expression “Madame” and in failing to address her by her own name, which I could not remember at the moment.
“I’ll call *tovarish* Lilina,” she said censoriously, and the next instant I faced an irate, middle-aged woman with the face of a disgruntled spinster. She had evidently heard my question, and her reception was ungracious.
“*Tovarish* Zinoviev does not receive here. Go to the Smolny,” she said, without permitting me to enter.
“I should like to use the direct wire to the Foreign Office, on business with Chicherin,” I explained.
“You can’t do it, and I don’t know who you are,” she replied curtly, closing the door.
On the present occasion Lilina was more gracious. We spoke of the conditions in the reformatories and she admitted that certain evils existed there, but protested that the published report was grossly exaggerated. We discussed modern methods of education and I explained the system followed by the Ferrer School in New York. She was inclined to agree in theory, “but we must fit our youth,” she remarked, “to continue the work of our Revolution.” “Surely,” I assented, “but is that to be done by the conventional methods which stultify and cripple the young mind by imposing upon it predigested views and dogmas?” I emphasized that the true aim of education is to aid the harmonious development of the child’s physical and mental qualities, to encourage independence of thought and inspire creative effort.
Lilina thought my views too Anarchistic.

===Chinese===

我询问道，浑然不知自己犯下了布尔什维克礼节中不可饶恕的大忌；实际上是双重逾矩：既使用了资产阶级的称谓“夫人”（Madame），又未能直呼其本名——而我当时偏偏没能记起她的名字。
“我去叫利莉娜同志（tovarish Lilina），”她带着责备的口吻说道。下一秒，我面对的便是一位怒气冲冲的中年妇女，脸上带着一副心怀不满的老处女神情。她显然听到了我的问话，接待我的态度极不客气。
“季诺维也夫同志不在这里会客。去斯莫尔尼宫（Smolny），”她冷冷地说道，甚至不许我踏进门槛一步。
“我想借用与外交人民委员部的专线电话，有要事同契切林商谈，”我解释道。
“不行，而且我根本不认识你，”她生硬地回绝，随即当面关上了大门。
而这一次，利莉娜的态度则显得和气了许多。我们谈到了感化院的现状，她承认那里确实存在某些弊端，但坚称发表的调查报告言过其实。我们探讨了现代教育方法，我向她阐述了纽约费雷尔学校（Ferrer School）所采用的教学体系。在理论上她颇为赞同，“但我们必须培养我们的青年，”她指出，“去接续我们的革命事业。”“当然，”我赞同道，“但难道要靠那些陈旧守旧的说教方法，把现成咀嚼过的观点和教条强加给年轻人，从而窒息并摧残他们幼小的心灵来实现吗？”我着重强调，教育的真正宗旨应当是促进儿童身心素质的和谐发展，鼓励独立思考，并激发创造性潜能。
利莉娜认为我的见解未免太具无政府主义色彩了。

## XVI Rest Homes for Workers / XVI 工人疗养院
===Original===

For months Zorin had been thinking of a project to afford the toilers of Petrograd an opportunity to recuperate during the summer. The workers are systematically undernourished and exhausted﻿—a few weeks’ rest and an improved *pyock* would give them new strength, and would at the same time be a demonstration of the interest the Communist Party is taking in their welfare.
After protracted discussion Zorin’s idea was approved by the Executive Committee of the Petrograd Soviet, and he received authority to put his cherished dream in operation. The former villas of the Russian nobility in the environs of the city were to be turned into proletarian “rest homes” and rebuilt to hold fifty thousand workers, who will spend two weeks there in groups of five thousand.
Zorin enlisted my cooperation, and I have enthusiastically accepted. We have paid several visits to Kameny Island, where the most beautiful villas and palaces are situated, and I have worked out a detailed plan for transforming them into homes for small families of workers, providing also for dining-rooms, libraries, and recreation places. Zorin has appointed me general manager and requested that the work be rushed “in hurry-up American style,” as he expressed himself, in order that everything be completed by the 1st of May, which is to be celebrated on a large scale as a revolutionary holiday.
The island has been neglected since the Revolution; most of the villas need thorough renovating and even the roads are badly out of repair. We mean to create an artistic summer resort, with modern improvements and comforts for the benefit of the proletarians. Surely no government has ever undertaken such work before.
Architects and civil engineers are on hand, but we find great difficulty in procuring building material and efficient labor. The Petrograd warehouses are stocked with the things needed, but it is almost impossible to learn just what is on hand and where it is to be found. When private property was nationalized, the stores and warehouses were sealed, and no one apparently knows what they contain. Our architects, engineers, and workers fly about the city, wasting their time in a vain effort to secure the required material.

===Chinese===

几个月来，佐林一直在酝酿一个设想：让彼得格勒的劳苦大众在夏季能有机会休养生息。工人们长期营养不良、精疲力竭——几周的休养和改善的口粮（pyock）将给他们带来新的体力，同时也能够展现出共产党对他们福祉的深切关怀。
经过漫长的讨论，佐林的构想获得了彼得格勒苏维埃执行委员会的批准，他得到了放手实施这一夙愿的授权。位于该市郊外的昔日俄国贵族别墅将被改造成无产阶级的“疗养院”，经翻建后可容纳5万名工人，他们将分批前往，每批5000人，在岛上度过为期两周的疗养。
佐林邀请我一同合作，我欣然热情应允。我们多次走访了坐落着最美丽别墅与庄园的卡缅内岛（Kameny Island），我拟订了一套详尽的改造方案，计划将这些庄园改造成适合工人小家庭居住的住所，并配备餐厅、图书馆和娱乐休闲场所。佐林任命我为总负责人，并要求按照他所说的“风风火火的美国速度”加速推进工程，以便赶在5月1日之前完工，届时好作为盛大的革命节日隆重庆祝。
自革命爆发以来，该岛一直疏于打理；大多数别墅都需要彻底翻修，就连道路也严重破损失修。我们打算打造一处充满艺术气息的避暑胜地，配备现代化设施与舒适条件，造福无产者。确信此前从未有任何政府开展过如此宏大的工程。
建筑师和土木工程师均已就位，但我们在获取建筑建材和高效劳动力方面却遇到了巨大的困难。彼得格勒的各个仓库里明明堆满了所需物资，但外界几乎无法弄清究竟存有什么物资以及具体存放在何处。当年私有财产被收归国有后，各个店铺和仓库都被贴上了封条，显然无人知晓其内藏何物。我们的建筑师、工程师和工人们在全城四处奔波，为了筹措所需物资徒劳地虚耗时间。

===Original===

For days they crowd the various bureaus to procure “authorized orders” for a few spades or lengths of water pipe, and when these are finally secured, we are balked by the general ignorance as to where the object sought can be found. In this situation the only economic and efficient mode of procedure would be to have our own committee overhaul the warehouses and take an inventory of the stock on hand. But my proposition to this effect has impugned upon the thick wall of the prevailing bureaucratic system. The Commissars of the various departments﻿—all Communists﻿—are inclined to take offense at such apparent ignoring of their authority: established modes of procedure have to be followed. Moreover, the stores and warehouses had been sealed by the Cheka; without its permission in each particular case the locks cannot be touched. The Cheka frowns upon my suggestion, coming from a nonmember of the Party, at that. *Nitcheve ne podelayesh*, Zorin says.
I find the new Soviet bureaucracy, its inefficiency and indifference, the greatest handicap in the work. It involves a continuous struggle against official red tape, precedence, and petty jealousy. Time is passing, and almost no progress is being made. The situation is disheartening.
I consider it vital that the men employed in the work of preparing a recreation place for the proletariat should themselves feel an interest in the matter, for only thus can effective cooperation be secured and results achieved. I have, therefore, suggested the formation of a committee to visit the shops and factories, to explain our plan to the workers, and enlist their interest and voluntary aid. I pointed out also the moral value of such a proceeding, and offered to organize the committee from the *Buford* deportees, most of whom are still looking for employment. Zorin favors the idea, but objections have been raised in various quarters. I wonder whether it is official distrust of the *Buford* men or disinclination to permit such a committee to get in direct contact with the workers. At any rate, the carrying out of my suggestion has become involved in endless applications to various Commissars and has apparently been lost in the intricate network of the Soviet machinery.

===Chinese===

一连几天，他们挤满了各个机关的办事处，只为了求批几把铁锹或几截水管的“批准调令”；而当调令终于到手时，大家又因为普遍不知道去哪儿找这些物件而碰壁。在这种情况下，唯一经济高效的处置办法就是让我们自己的委员会去盘查各个仓库，清点盘查现有库存。然而，我关于这一设想的提议却撞上了现行官僚体制的厚重铁壁。各个部门的人民委员——清一色都是共产党员——认为这种做法明显是对其职权的藐视，因而大为不悦：必须按既定的繁琐程序办事。此外，这些店铺和仓库都是由契卡（Cheka）查封的；未经契卡对每一个具体案由特批，任何人不得动其锁头。面对这一提议，尤其是出自一个非党人士之口，契卡更是不以为然。佐林无奈地说：“毫无办法（nitcheve ne podelayesh）。”
我发现，苏维埃新生的官僚体制及其低效与冷漠，成了这项工作最大的绊脚石。它意味着不得不持续与官方的繁文缛节、按资排辈和微不足道的嫉妒心作斗争。时间一天天流逝，工程几乎毫无进展。局面令人灰心丧气。
我认为至关重要的是，让那些受雇为无产阶级修建休养胜地的工人们自己对这项事业产生由衷的兴趣，因为唯有如此才能形成有效的协作并取得成效。因此，我建议成立一个委员会，深入各大车间和工厂，向工人们阐明我们的规划，争取他们的兴趣与自愿援助。我还指出了这一举措的精神感召价值，并主动提出由“布福德号”上的被遣返者组成这个委员会，他们中的大多数人仍在四处寻找工作。佐林赞同这个想法，但在各个方面却遭到了反对。我不知道这究竟是因为官方对“布福德号”上的人心存猜忌，还是不愿意让这样一个委员会与基层工人产生直接接触。无论如何，我的建议在提交给各路政委的无休止审批流转中被彻底搁置，显然已淹没在了苏维埃官僚机器那错综复杂的网络深渊之中。

===Original===

Instead, soldiers and prisoners from the forced labor camps of the city have been commandeered for road repairing, cleaning neglected gardens and renovating the houses. But they have no interest in the work; their thoughts and time are entirely occupied with the question of the *pyock*. A most vital matter: for not being employed at their regular tasks, they risk losing the rations due them, and no adequate provision has been made to feed them on the island. A general mess hall has been opened, but such favoritism prevails there that the prisoners and soldiers without influence often remain without meals, preference being given to the numerous friends and appointees of the Commissars and Communists. The common laborers at work are growing dissatisfied. “The actual worker,” a soldier said to me, “will not get into the summer resort. It will be only for Commissars and Communists.”
Some buildings in the area of the planned rest homes are occupied as children’s homes and schools; others, by the families of the intelligentsia. All of them have been ordered to vacate. But while arrangements have been made to secure quarters for the schools in the city, the private dwellers are considered *bourzhooi* and as such not worthy of any consideration: they are to be evicted. Yet hidden influences are at work: a number of the *bourzhooi* have received “protection,” while those without friends in high places are vainly begging for mercy. Zorin has asked me to execute the order for eviction, but eager as I am to establish rest homes for the workers, I had to refuse to cooperate in what seems to me gross injustice and needless brutality. Zorin is displeased at my “sentimentality,” and I am being eliminated from the work.

===Chinese===

相反，当局从该市的强制劳动营征调了士兵和囚犯来修整道路、清理荒芜的花园并修缮房屋。然而他们对这项工作毫无兴趣；他们的心思和时间全被口粮（pyock）问题所占据。这是一件关乎生计的大事：由于没有从事原本固定的工作，他们冒着丧失应得配给的风险，而在岛上又没有为他们的伙食做好充分的保障。虽然开设了一间公共食堂，但那里优亲厚友的风气极盛，没有门路的囚犯和士兵往往吃不上饭，有限的饭菜全被优先供应给各路政委和共产党员的大批亲朋好友及亲信。干苦力的底层劳工们日益心怀不满。一名士兵对我说道：“真正的工人根本住不进这处避暑胜地。这里纯粹是为政委和共产党员准备的。”
拟建疗养院所在区域的部分建筑目前被用作儿童福利院和学校；另一些则由知识分子家庭居住。所有人均已被责令腾退搬迁。然而，虽然有关方面已在城里为学校安排了安置用房，但那些私人住户却被划定为“资产阶级老爷们”（bourzhooi），因而不配享有任何体恤考量：他们必须被无情驱逐。然而私下里却存在着暗箱操作：若干“资产阶级”得到了“上头的庇护”，而那些在权贵阶层中没有靠山的人则只能徒劳地哀求宽恕。佐林要求我去执行这项驱逐命令，尽管我极度渴望为工人建立疗养院，但我不得不拒绝参与这种在我看来赤裸裸的不公与毫无必要的暴行。佐林对我的“伤感主义”颇为不悦，而我也正被逐渐排挤出这项工程之外。

## XVII The First of May / XVII 五一劳动节
===Original===

Awakened early in the morning by strains of music and song, I went out into the street. The city was in gala attire: flags and banners fluttered in the air; red carpets and curtains hung from windows and doors, the variety of shade and design producing a warm, Oriental effect.
On the Nevsky a large automobile passed me, stopping a few paces ahead. A curly, black head rose from the depths of the machine, and someone hailed me: “Hello, Berkman, come and join us.” I recognized Zinoviev.
Detachments of military filed by, singing revolutionary songs, and groups of boys and girls marched to the strains of “The Internationale.” “*Subotniki*,”19 Zinoviev remarked, “going to Marsove Pole to plant trees on the graves of our heroic dead.” Our car moved slowly between phalanxes of revolutionary youths and Red Army men, and my mind reverted to a previous May Day demonstration. It was my first experience of the kind, in New York, in the latter part of the 80’s. Radicals of every camp had cooperated to make the event successful, and a huge demonstration was expected on the historic Union Square. But the majority of the American workers of the city remained deaf to our proclamation, and only a few thousands attended, mostly of the foreign element.
The meeting had just begun when suddenly the blue-coated giants appeared, and the gathering was attacked with riot clubs and dispersed into the side streets. Some of us had foreseen such a possibility, and a little group of the younger element had prepared to resist the police. But on the eve of the demonstration, in our last committee conference, H﻿—﻿—, the leader of the older members, had warned us against “being provoked into violence,” and well I remember how passionately I resented the “arguments” of the pusillanimous Social Democrat. “We are the teachers of the people,” he had said, “and we must lead them to greater class consciousness. But we are few and it were folly to sacrifice ourselves unnecessarily. We must save ourselves for more important work.”
I scoffed at the cowardly warning and called it the spiritual acme of our Christian civilization which has turned the bold eagle, man, into a fox. But H﻿—﻿—’s speech paled the enthusiasm of our group, and there was no resistance to the police brutality. I went home discouraged by the ignominious failure of our 1st of May demonstration.

===Chinese===

清晨被阵阵乐声与歌声唤醒，我走上了街头。全城沉浸在节日的盛装之中：旗帜与横幅在空中迎风招展；门窗上挂着红地毯与红色帷幔，各种深浅不一的色调与图案交相辉映，营造出一种温暖而富有东方情调的效果。
在涅瓦大街上，一辆大轿车从我身旁驶过，停在前方数步之遥。一个留着黑色鬈发的脑袋从车厢深处探了出来，有人冲我喊道：“喂，伯克曼，快上车跟我们一道！”我认出那是季诺维也夫。
一队队军人迈步走过，高唱着革命歌曲，成群的少男少女伴着《国际歌》的旋律昂首行进。“这是义务星期六劳动者19（Subotniki），”季诺维也夫说道，“正前往战神广场（Marsove Pole），去为我们英勇牺牲的烈士墓植树。”我们的汽车在革命青年与红军战士组成的方阵之间缓缓前行，我的思绪不由得回到了以往的一场五一游行示威。那是我初次经历此类场面，是在19世纪80年代后期的纽约。当时各派激进分子通力合作力促集会成功，预料在历史悠久的联合广场将举行一场声势浩大的示威游行。然而该城大多数美国本土工人却对我们的宣言充耳不闻，只有数千人前来参加，且主要都是外来移民。
集会刚刚开始，一群身穿蓝制服的彪形大汉（警察）便突然现身，手持警棍对集会人群展开袭击，并将众人驱散到了旁侧的小巷里。我们当中有些人早已预料到这种可能，一小批年轻分子曾准备奋起抵抗警察。然而在示威前夕举行的最后一次委员会会议上，年长成员的领袖H——曾告诫我们切莫“受到挑衅而诉诸暴力”，我至今仍清楚记得自己当时是何等强烈地痛恨这位胆怯畏缩的社会民主党人的所谓“论据”。“我们是人民的导师，”他当时说，“我们必须引导他们提高阶级觉悟。然而我们人数稀少，作无谓的牺牲纯属愚蠢。我们必须保存力量，以从事更为重要的工作。”
我对这种怯懦的警告嗤之以鼻，斥之为将人类这只雄鹰蜕化为狐狸的基督教文明的精神巅峰。然而，H——的发言冷却了我们这群人的热情，面对警察的暴行我们没有组织起任何抵抗。五一示威集会蒙受如此奇耻大辱的溃败，我满怀沮丧地回到了家中。

===Original===

The metallic thunder of “The Internationale,” struck up by several bands at once, recalled me to the present. Here, indeed, was the First of May of my youthful dreams. Here was the Revolution itself!
At the Uritsky Square we alighted. Affectionately I looked at the workers and soldiers that joined our group. Here were the builders of the Revolution who, in the face of insurmountable difficulties, are leading it to victory. I glanced at Zinoviev﻿—he looked weary, overworked, heavy rings under his eyes﻿—the “Communist look” I had become familiar with.
The procession formed. Zinoviev put his arm through mine, and someone pushed us into the front rank. Holding hands, the lines marched toward the Field of Mars, Zorin carrying the huge red banner. His slender figure staggered beneath its weight, and willing hands stretched out to relieve him. But Zorin would not be deprived of the precious burden.
The Field of Mars was dotted with bending figures busily at work﻿—the *subotniki* decorating the graves of the revolutionary martyrs. They labored joyfully, snatches of their song reaching us between the pauses of the brass bands in our rear.
I stood with Zinoviev on the reviewing stand, interpreting his answers to the American correspondent whom Chicherin finally admitted into Russia. As far as the eye could reach, soldiers and workers filled the huge square and adjoining streets. Proletarians from the factories marched by, each group with its crimson banner inscribed with revolutionary mottoes. Red Army nurses, women employees of shops and Soviet institutions, regiments of the Communist Youth, the *vsevobutch* of the armed workers, and long lines of children, boys and girls, filed by with the flags of their organizations.
It was the most imposing demonstration of revolutionary consciousness I had ever seen, and I felt inspired by it. But the appearance of the marchers was depressing; they were undernourished, exhausted, poorly clad, and I noticed many children walking barefoot. It was probably due to their physical weakness, I thought, that the paraders showed so little enthusiasm﻿—they barely responded to the greetings of the Communists on the reviewing stand, and the frequent “Hurrah, Hurrah, *Tovarishi*!” shouted by Lashevitch and Antselovitch, Zinoviev’s lieutenants, found but a weak, spiritless echo in the ranks of the passing demonstrators.

===Chinese===

数支军乐队齐声奏响的《国际歌》的金石轰鸣，将我的思绪拉回到了眼前。这里，确确实实正是我青年时期梦寐以求的五一劳动节！这里展现的正是革命本身！
我们在乌里茨基广场下了车。我满怀深情地注视着汇入我们队伍的工人和士兵。正是这些革命的缔造者，面对无法克服的重重艰难，正引领着革命走向胜利。我瞥了季诺维也夫一眼——他神色疲惫，操劳过度，眼圈黑重——这正是我早已熟悉的“共产党人面容”。
队伍排好了。季诺维也夫挽起我的手臂，有人把我们推到了队伍的最前列。队伍手挽着手向战神广场行进，佐林扛着一面巨大的红旗。在他瘦削的身躯下，红旗的重压使他步伐有些踉跄，有人热心伸出手想替他分担，但佐林绝不肯放弃这份珍贵的重负。
战神广场上到处都是躬身忙碌的身影——那是义务星期六劳动者们正在装点革命烈士的陵墓。他们欢快地劳动着，歌声从我们身后军乐队演奏的间隙阵阵飘来。
我同季诺维也夫站在检阅台上，为契切林最终批准入境的美国记者翻译季诺维也夫的回答。极目所望，士兵与工人挤满了巨大的广场和毗邻的街道。各大工厂的无产者列队走过，每个方阵都高举着绣有革命口号的绯红旗帜。红军护士、商店与苏维埃机构的女雇员、共青团各团队、武装工人的普及全民军训队（vsevobutch），以及长长的少男少女儿童队伍，手持各自组织的旗帜依次走过。
这是我所见过的最壮观的革命觉悟大检阅，我深受鼓舞。然而游行者的面貌却令人心酸：他们营养不良、精疲力竭、衣衫褴褛，我注意到许多孩子甚至赤脚行进。我想，大概正是因为身体极度虚弱，游行队伍才表现得如此缺乏热情——他们对检阅台上共产党人的致意几乎毫无回应，而季诺维也夫的得力助手拉舍维奇（Lashevitch）与安采洛维奇频频高呼的“乌拉，乌拉，同志们！”，在走过的示威者方阵中只换来了微弱无力的回响。

===Original===

The festivities closed in the evening with an open air mass spectacle, illustrating the triumph of the Revolution. It was a powerful portrayal of the age-long slavery of the people, of their suffering and misery, and the underground revolutionary activities of the pioneers of liberty. The best artists of the city participated in the portrayal of the great Russian drama and gave an intense and moving presentation. I was spellbound by the horrors of the Tsars’ tyranny; the clanking of the slaves’ chains echoed in my consciousness, and I heard the muttering of approaching storm from the depths. Then sudden thunder of cannon, groans of the wounded and dying in the world slaughter, followed by the lightning of rebellion and the Triumph of the Revolution.
I lived through the whole gamut of the great struggle within the two hours of the performance, and I was profoundly stirred. But the huge audience remained silent﻿—not a sign of approval was manifested. Was it the apathy of the northern temperament, I wondered, when I heard a young workman nearby saying: “What’s the use of it all! I’d like to know what we have gained.”

===Chinese===

庆祝活动在傍晚以一场露天大型群戏演出收尾，生动展现了革命的胜利。该剧强有力地刻画了人民世代遭受的奴役、苦难与悲惨境遇，以及自由先驱们的地下革命斗争活动。该市最优秀的艺术家们参与了这部伟大俄国史诗戏剧的演绎，呈现出极具震撼力与感染力的演出。我完全被沙皇专制暴政的恐怖所震慑；奴隶脚镣的铿锵声在我脑海中回荡，我听到了来自社会底层逼近的风暴呼啸声。紧接着是大炮的骤然轰鸣，世界大屠杀中伤员与垂死者的呻吟，随后是起义的耀眼闪电以及革命的伟大胜利。
在短短两小时（2小时）的演出中，我身临其境般经历了一整部波澜壮阔的斗争史诗，内心深受震撼。然而庞大的观众群体却保持着一片沉寂——没有表现出丝毫赞许的迹象。我不禁疑惑这是否是由于北方人性格中的冷漠，就在这时我听到身旁一位年轻工人说道：“搞这一切究竟有什么用！我倒真想知道我们到底得到了什么。”

## XVIII The British Labor Mission / XVIII 英国工党代表团
===Original===

*May, 1920.*﻿—New life has come to Petrograd with the arrival of the British Mission; many meetings, banquets, and festivities are taking place in its honor. I believe the Communists are inclined to exaggerate the importance of the visit and its probable results. Some even think the coming of the Englishmen augurs the political recognition of Russia in the near future. Soviet newspapers and Communist speeches have created the impression that the Mission represents the sentiment of the whole British proletariat, and that the latter is about to come to the aid of Russia.
I heard the subject discussed by a group of workers and soldiers at the meeting in the Labor Temple. I had been asked to render into English the resolutions to be presented, and a small table was assigned to me. People crowded about me to get a better look at the delegates on the platform. The full glare of the electric lights shone upon Ben Turner, the Chairman of the Mission, short, stocky, and well-fed.
“There, look at him!” a worker behind me exclaimed, “you can tell he’s from abroad. Our people are not so fat.”
“What wonder!” a soldier replied, “it isn’t Russia, England isn’t, and people don’t go hungry there.”
“The workers starve everywhere,” a hoarse voice said.
“These are not workers,” the first man corrected. “They are delegates.”
“Of course, delegates, but proletarian delegates,” the hoarse voice insisted. “The English working class sent them to see what help we need.”
“You think they will help?” the soldier asked hopefully.
“That’s what they are here for. They’ll go back home and tell the proletariat there how we suffer, and they’ll have the blockade taken off.”
“God be willing, God be willing,” the worker sighed fervently.
A man passed by, energetically pushing his way through the crowd, and ascended the platform. His prosperous appearance, well-fitting clothes, and ruddy face were in striking contrast with the people about.
“Look at that fat delegate! They ain’t starving in England,” the soldier whispered to his neighbor.
Something familiar about the stout “delegate” fastened my attention. His eye fell upon me and he smiled recognition. It was Melnitchansky, the Chairman of the Moscow Soviet of Labor Unions.
---
Considerable disappointment is felt in Communist circles regarding the Mission.

===Chinese===

1920年5月。——随着英国代表团的抵达，彼得格勒焕发出了新的生机；为向他们致敬，举行了许多集会、宴会和庆祝活动。我认为共产党人倾向于夸大这次访问的重要性及其可能产生的成果。有些人甚至认为英国人的到来预示着俄国在不久的将来会获得政治上的承认。苏维埃各家报纸和共产党人的演说制造了这样一种印象：代表团代表着全体英国无产阶级的心声，而后者正准备前来援助俄国。
在劳动大厦（Labor Temple）的集会上，我听到一群工人和士兵在议论这个话题。当时我受托将拟提交的各项决议翻译成英文，会场给我安排了一张小桌子。人们挤在我周围，以便更清楚地打量主席台上的代表们。耀眼的电灯光芒洒在代表团团长本·特纳（Ben Turner）身上，他身材矮壮，营养充足。
“瞧啊，看看他！”我身后的一名工人惊叹道，“一看就是打国外来的。咱们这儿的人可没这么胖。”
“这有什么好奇怪的！”一名士兵搭腔道，“英国又不是俄国，那儿的人可挨不着饿。”
“全世界的工人都在挨饿，”一个沙哑的嗓门说道。
“这些人可不是普通工人，”最初说话的人纠正道，“他们是代表。”
“那当然是代表，不过是无产阶级的代表，”那个沙哑的嗓门坚持道，“英国工人阶级派他们来，是看看我们需要什么帮助。”
“你觉得他们真能帮上忙？”士兵满怀希冀地问道。
“人家大老远来就是为了这个。他们回国后会告诉那里的无产阶级我们受了多少苦，他们会让封锁彻底解除。”
“但愿老天保佑，但愿老天保佑，”工人动情地叹了口气。
一名男子快步走过，有力地挤过人群，登上了主席台。他体态富态、衣着合身、面色红润，与周围的人群形成了极其鲜明的对比。
“瞧那个胖代表！英国人可真没挨饿，”士兵向身旁的伙伴窃窃私语。
这位体态发福的“代表”身上某种熟悉的气质吸引了我的目光。他的视线落在我身上，会心地微笑着认出了我。原来是莫斯科工会苏维埃主席梅尔尼昌斯基（Melnitchansky）。
---
在共产党内部，对于这个代表团却弥漫着相当大的失望情绪。

===Original===

The military displays have failed to impress them, the visits to mills and factories have produced no enthusiasm among the “cold-blooded Britishers.” They seem purposely to avoid a definite expression of opinion regarding aid that might be expected from their country or the nature of their report to the workers of England. Certain remarks by individual delegates have caused uneasiness. Some Communists think it poor taste to honor a labor mission with military demonstrations, some of whose members are outspoken pacifists. A revolutionary country like Russia, they say, should lay more stress on the proletarian consciousness of the people as the true symbol of its character and the best guarantee of its peaceful intentions. The visits to the industrial establishments, it is claimed, could impress only with their lack of productive results and the fact that the factories and mills had been “primed” for the delegates. It is even whispered about that the Britishers sense in the official atmosphere with which they are surrounded a sort of surveillance very irksome to them.
The men sent from Moscow to welcome the Mission﻿—Radek, Melnitchansky, and Petrovsky﻿—believe that every effort must be made to create a good impression on the delegates, in the hope of securing their favorable report in England and correspondent action there in behalf of Russia. Radek and Petrovsky are strong defenders of “diplomacy”; Petrovsky, especially, who apparently enjoys considerable influence in the councils of the Party, though his allegiance to Bolshevism is of very recent origin. I knew him in America as Dr. Goldfarb, labor editor of the *New York Jewish Forward*, and a very fanatical Social Democrat﻿—a Menshevik, in the Russian terminology. His conversion to Bolshevism was rather sudden, and I am surprised to learn that he holds the important position of Commissar of military education.
Angelica Balabanova, an old revolutionist and very lovable personality, who is on the Reception Committee, agrees with me that the best policy is to enable the Mission to learn the whole truth concerning Russia, and to enlist their friendship and cooperation in the work of upbuilding the country by their adequate understanding of its needs, rather than by the lack of it. But the other members of the Committee of Welcome hold a different view.

===Chinese===

阅兵式未能打动他们，对工矿企业的参观也未能在这些“冷若冰霜的英国人”当中激起任何热情。关于英国可能提供的援助，或者他们向英国工人汇报的性质，他们似乎刻意回避给出任何明确的表态。个别代表的某些言论甚至引发了不安。一些共产党人认为，用军事示威来招待一个劳工代表团未免流于低俗，尤其是代表团中某些成员还是直言不讳的和平主义者。他们认为，像俄国这样的革命国家，应当更加着重展现人民的无产阶级觉悟，以此作为国家品格的真实象征和爱好和平意图的最佳保证。有人指出，对工业企业的走访所留下的印象，无非是生产成果的匮乏，以及各家工厂为迎接代表团而提前进行的“刻意粉饰”。甚至有人私下议论，英国人在笼罩着他们的官方氛围中察觉到了某种令人生厌的监视。
从莫斯科派来迎接代表团的人员——拉狄克（Radek）、梅尔尼昌斯基和彼得罗夫斯基（Petrovsky）——认为必须竭尽全力给代表们留下良好的印象，以期争取他们在英国作出有利的报告，并在当地采取声援俄国的相应行动。拉狄克和彼得罗夫斯基是“外交手腕”的坚定拥护者；尤其是彼得罗夫斯基，尽管他归顺布尔什维克的时间很短，却显然在党内高层决策中享有相当大的影响力。当年在美洲时我就认识他，那时他叫戈德法布博士（Dr. Goldfarb），是《纽约犹太前进报》（New York Jewish Forward）的劳工编辑，也是一名狂热的社会民主党人——按俄国的术语来说就是孟什维克。他倒向布尔什维克的过程相当突然，得知他如今竟担任着军事教育人民委员的要职，令我颇感意外。
接待委员会成员、老革命家安杰利卡·巴拉巴诺娃（Angelica Balabanova）为人十分和蔼可亲，她赞同我的看法，认为最好的方针是让代表团了解关于俄国的全部真相，通过让他们充分了解这个国家的需求而非隐瞒真相，来争取他们的友谊与合作，共同投入到国家的建设事业中。然而，欢迎委员会的其他成员却持有截然相反的见解。

===Original===

Overzealous and anxious, they exaggerate the truth and minimize or entirely deny the weak points. At demonstrations and meetings this policy has been followed, but it is evident that some of the delegates saw through the mask of pretense. At the final banquet given in honor of the Britishers before their departure for Moscow, almost every speaker emphasized the fact that only the truth had been told the Mission, unconscious of the smile of incredulity in the polite attention of the delegates. Antselovitch, Chairman of the Petrograd Soviet of Labor Unions, rose even to the height of asserting that full individual liberty is established in Russia﻿—at least for the workers, he added, as if suddenly become aware of the recklessness of his statement.
Perhaps I did Antselovitch an injustice by omitting that falsehood in my translation of his speech. But I could not stand up before the delegates and repeat what I knew, as well as they, to be a deliberate lie, as stupid as it was unnecessary. The delegates are aware that dictatorship is the reverse of liberty. They know there is no freedom of speech or press for anyone in Soviet Russia, not even for Communists, and that sanctity of home or person is unknown. The exigencies of the revolutionary struggle make such a condition of affairs imperative, Lenin frankly admits. It is an insult to the intelligence of the Mission to pretend otherwise.
At our visits to the mills and factories Antselovitch and his aides danced attendance upon the delegates in a manner clearly displeasing to them. One of the Britishers hinted to his colleagues that the places had received previous notice and were “prepared” for the distinguished guests. The information about conditions and output given by managers, foremen, and Communist employees varied so obviously as to elicit surprised remarks. Some members of the Mission were aware of the attendance of Chekists and conscious of the timidity of the workers in their presence.
---
A train de luxe, with Pullman sleepers and diner, was waiting at the Nikolayevsky Station to take the British Mission to Moscow. At every car the delegates were saluted by the guard of honor, young Mussulmen *kursanti*20 in their picturesque Cherkess uniforms. The place presented an unusually serene appearance. The customary crowds with their heavy loads, shouting and pushing, were absent.

===Chinese===

他们急功近利、患得患失，极力夸大事实，缩小乃至全盘否认存在的弱点。在各项集会和会议中一直奉行着这种方针，但显然某些代表已经识破了这副伪善的面具。在代表团启程前往莫斯科之前为英国人举行的告别宴会上，几乎每位发言人都信誓旦旦地强调他们向代表团介绍的句句属实，却浑然不觉代表们在礼貌聆听背后流露出的怀疑微笑。彼得格勒工会苏维埃主席安采洛维奇甚至信口开河地宣称俄国已建立了充分的个人自由——至少对工人而言是如此，他仿佛突然意识到自己言辞的鲁莽，连忙补充了一句。
也许我在翻译他的演说时省略了这一谎言是对安采洛维奇的不公。然而我实在无法当着代表们的面，复述一句我和他们心知肚明的蓄意谎言，那既愚蠢又毫无必要。代表们深知，专政乃是自由的对立面。他们清楚在苏维埃俄国，任何人都没有言论或出版自由，甚至连共产党员也没有，更无所谓住宅或人身不可侵犯的权利。列宁坦率承认，革命斗争的严酷现实使得这种局面势在必行。若在此问题上弄虚作假，无异于对代表团智商的侮辱。
在走访工厂期间，安采洛维奇及其随从对代表们极尽谄媚奉迎之能事，令代表们大感不快。一名英国代表向同僚暗示，这些参观点早就接到了事先通知，为尊贵的客人进行了“精心布置”。经理、工长以及共产党员雇员所提供的有关生产状况和产量的数据前后矛盾、大相径庭，以至于引来了客人们惊讶的追问。代表团的部分成员察觉到了现场契卡（Cheka）人员的存在，并注意到了工人们在契卡人员面前那种战战兢兢的胆怯神情。
---
一列配备普尔曼卧铺车厢和餐车的豪华专列，已在尼古拉耶夫斯基车站静候，准备接载英国代表团前往莫斯科。在每一节车厢门前，身着别致切尔克斯制服的年轻穆斯林军校学员20（kursanti）组成的仪仗队向代表们庄严敬礼。车站呈现出不同寻常的宁静祥和景象。以往那些肩扛沉重行囊、大呼小叫、拥挤推搡的平民人潮全都不见了踪影。

===Original===

Not a bedraggled workman or filthy beggar was in sight. Station and platform were the picture of cleanliness and well-regulated order.
At the stroke of 11 p.m. on Sunday, May 16, the Mission started for Moscow. The delegates were accompanied by a large coterie of prominent Communists, including Radek, Kollontai, Losovsky, his daughter, who acts as his secretary, Balabanova, Zorin, and some lesser lights. By request I went with the Mission as unofficial interpreter, sharing my coupé with Ichov, head of the Government publications in Petrograd.
On the way Russia and Russian conditions were discussed, the Communists striving to “draw out” the delegates, while most of the latter were careful to express no definite opinion. In general terms Ben Turner, the Chairman of the Mission, spoke of the need of a more humane attitude to Russia, while Messrs. Skinner and Purcell nodded their approval﻿—more of the generality of the Chairman’s remarks, it seemed to me, than of their meaning. Williams was outspoken in his admiration of the good order which prevails in Petrograd, while Wallhead, of the Independent Labor Party, agreed with Allen﻿—the only Communist among the Englishmen﻿—in roundly denouncing the Allied crime of the blockade which is starving millions of innocent women and little children. Mrs. Snowden preserved the well-bred dignity of high society, participating in the conversation to the extent of a patronizing smile that said very plainly, “I am with you, but not of you.” Once she voiced her pleasant surprise at not finding the streets of Petrograd infested with highwaymen who robbed the people unhindered by daylight, as “some folks in England believed.”
Of all the delegates, the most sympathetic to me were Allen, with his thoughtful, ascetic face, and Bertrand Russell, who accompanied the Mission in a private capacity, I believe. Unlike each other in temperament and viewpoint, both impressed me as men of deep insight and social sincerity.
In Moscow a great ovation had been prepared for the Mission. The railway platform was lined with Red Army men in dress parade uniforms and shining accoutrements, military bands played “The Internationale,” and Communist orators gave a “triumphant welcome” to the British guests.

===Chinese===

视野里看不到任何衣衫褴褛的工人和肮脏不堪的乞丐。车站与站台宛如一幅整洁规范、井然有序的画卷。
5月16日星期日晚上11点整，代表团启程前往莫斯科。陪同代表团同行的有一大批著名共产党人，包括拉狄克、柯伦泰（Kollontai）、洛佐夫斯基（Losovsky）及其担任秘书的女儿、巴拉巴诺娃、佐林以及若干稍次要的人物。应有关方面邀请，我作为非官方翻译随代表团同行，与彼得格勒政府出版部门负责人伊乔夫（Ichov）同住一个包厢。
一路上大家讨论着俄国及其国情，共产党人极力试图探明代表们的真实想法，而大部分英国代表则格外谨慎，避免发表任何明确的见解。代表团团长本·特纳用泛泛的套话谈到了对俄国采取更加人道态度的必要性，斯金纳（Skinner）先生与珀塞尔（Purcell）先生则频频点头赞同——在我看来，与其说是赞同其含义，不如说是赞同团长发言的含蓄宽泛。威廉斯（Williams）直言不讳地盛赞彼得格勒维持的良好秩序，而独立工党的沃尔黑德（Wallhead）则同艾伦（Allen）——英国代表中唯一的共产党员——一致强烈谴责协约国封锁导致数百万无辜妇孺挨饿的罪行。斯诺登夫人（Mrs. Snowden）保持着上流社会教养良好的尊贵风范，仅以居高临下的微笑参与交谈，那神态极其分明地传递出：“我和你们在一起，但我绝不是你们的一员。”有一次，她流露出欣慰的诧异，因为她并未在彼得格勒街头看到像“英国某些人所相信的那样”在大白天肆无忌惮抢劫行人的绿林强盗。
在所有代表当中，最令我产生共鸣的是面容沉思而清瘦的艾伦，以及据我所知以私人身份随行的伯特兰·罗素（Bertrand Russell）。两人在气质和观点上截然不同，但都给我留下了具有深刻洞察力与社会诚意的深刻印象。
在莫斯科，人们为代表团准备了隆重的盛大欢迎仪式。车站站台上列队站满了身穿阅兵礼服、佩戴锃亮军械的红军战士，军乐队演奏着《国际歌》，共产党演说家们向英国来宾致以“凯旋般的崇高敬礼”。

===Original===

Kamenev greeted them on behalf of the Central Government, and Tomsky, President of the All-Russian Soviet of Labor Unions, in a long speech addressed the representatives of the British workers in the name of their Russian brothers. All the speakers characterized the happy occasion as the symbol of the common cause of the toilers of the two countries and voiced the conviction that the English proletariat is about to come to the aid of the Revolution.
For almost two hours the delegates were kept standing on the platform listening to speeches in a language unintelligible to them. But at last the ceremony was over, and the visitors were seated in automobiles and driven to the Soviet Hotel, assigned as their quarters. In the great crowding the Englishmen became separated, some of them almost submerged by the surging waves of humanity. Gradually the soldiers filed out, the crowd thinned, and at last I was able to make my way to the street. The Government machines had already left, and I looked about for an *isvoshtchik* (cab), when I noticed Bertrand Russell struggling out of the station. He stood bewildered on the steps, not knowing where to turn, forgotten amidst the excited people shouting a strange jargon. An auto drove up at that moment, and I recognized Karakhan.
“I am a little late,” he said; “are all the delegates gone?”
“Bertrand Russell is here yet,” I replied.
“Russell? Who is he?”
I explained.
“Never heard of him,” Karakhan said naively. “But let him come in; there’s room for both of you.”
---
Delovoi Dvor, the Soviet Hotel assigned to the British guests, has been entirely renovated, and looks clean and fresh. The large dining room is tastefully decorated with crimson banners and mottoes of welcome. Socialist legends of the solidarity of the workers of the world and the triumph of the Revolution through the dictatorship of the proletariat speak from the walls in various tongues. Potted plants lend the spacious room warmth and color.
Covers were set for a large number, including the delegates, the official representatives of the Soviet Government, some members of the Third International, and the invited spokesmen of labor. Russian caviar, soup, white bread, two kinds of meat and a variety of vegetables were on the menu. When fried chicken was served, I saw some of the Britishers exchange wondering glances.

===Chinese===

加米涅夫代表中央政府向他们致辞欢迎，全俄工会苏维埃主席托姆斯基（Tomsky）发表了长篇演说，以俄国弟兄的名义向英国工人的代表表示欢迎。所有发言人都将这一喜庆时刻定性为两国劳苦大众共同事业的象征，并坚信英国无产阶级即将前来援助革命。
代表们在站台上足足站了将近两小时（2小时），聆听着他们一句也听不懂的外语演讲。仪式终于告一段落，来宾们被安排坐上轿车，送往指派给他们的住所苏维埃宾馆。在巨大的拥挤混乱中，英国代表们被冲散了，有些人几乎被汹涌的人潮吞没。渐渐地，士兵们列队撤出，人群散开，我终于挤到了街上。政府的车辆已经开走，我正四处张望寻找一辆敞篷马车（isvoshtchik），突然注意到伯特兰·罗素正艰难地从车站挤出来。他困惑地站在台阶上，不知该往哪儿走，在叫嚷着陌生异国语言的狂热人群中被人遗忘了。恰在此刻，一辆轿车开了过来，我认出那是卡拉汉。
“我来晚了一步，”他说，“代表们都走了吗？”
“伯特兰·罗素还在这里，”我回答。
“罗素？他是谁？”
我解释了一番。
“从没听说过，”卡拉汉天真地说道，“不过让他上车吧，车里坐得下你们两个人。”
---
指定接待英国来宾的苏维埃宾馆“商务庭院”（Delovoi Dvor）经过了全面翻新，显得格外整洁明快。宽敞的餐厅高雅地装饰着绯红色的横幅和欢迎标语。墙上用各种文字书写着全世界工人团结一致、通过无产阶级专政赢得革命胜利的社会主义颂词。盆栽植物为宽敞的大厅增添了温情与色彩。
宴席为众多宾客而设，包括代表团成员、苏维埃政府的官方代表、第三国际的部分成员以及受邀的劳工代言人。菜单上摆满了俄国鱼子酱、热汤、白面包、两种（2种）肉类以及各式各样的蔬菜。当端上炸鸡时，我看到几位英国代表面面相觑，交换着诧异的目光。

===Original===

“A jolly good meal for starving Russia,” a delegate at my side remarked to his neighbor in the lull of clattering dishes and laughter.
“Rather. Natty wench,” the other replied with a suggestive wink at the winsome young waitress serving him. “Thought the Bolsheviks had done away with servants.”
Angelica Balabanova, sitting opposite me, looked perturbed.
---
On May 18th, the day following its arrival in Moscow, the Mission was honored with a great demonstration. It was a splendid military display, all branches of the Red Army participating. No workers marched in the parade.
The continuous round of festivities, special theatrical performances, and visits to factories, are apparently palling upon the delegates. A feeling of dissatisfaction is noticeable among them, a sense of resentment at the apparent surveillance to which they feel themselves subjected. Several have complained of inability to see their callers, the *propusk* system introduced in the Delovoy Dvor since the arrival of the Mission practically excluding visitors considered persona non grata by the Cheka agent at the clerk’s desk. The delegates are becoming aware of the subtle curtailment of their liberty, conscious of their every step and word being spied upon. They resent the “prison atmosphere,” as a member of the Mission characterized the environment. “We are friendly disposed,” he said to me, “and there is no sense in such tactics.” He was not content with seeing only things officially shown to the Mission, he said. He was anxious to look deeper, and he complained of being compelled to resort to stratagems in order to come in contact with persons whose views he wanted to learn.
“The Russian Revolution is the greatest event in all history,” one of the delegates remarked to me, “petty consideration should have no place in it. A new world is in the making; to minimize the terrific travail of such a birth is worse than folly. The Bolsheviki, in the vanguard of the revolutionary masses, are playing a part in the process whose significance history will not fail to estimate. That they have made mistakes is inevitable, is human; but in spite of errors, they are founding a new civilization. History does not forgive failure: it will immortalize the Bolsheviki because of their success in the face of almost insuperable difficulties. They may justly be proud of their achievements.”

===Chinese===

“对于正在挨饿的俄国来说，这顿大餐可真够体面的，”我身旁的一位代表在餐具碰撞声与笑声的间隙对他的同伴说道。
“可不是嘛。挺标致的俏妞，”另一人回答道，暧昧地冲为他服务的那位妩媚年轻女招待眨了眨眼。“我还以为布尔什维克早就废除了仆人呢。”
坐在我对面的安杰利卡·巴拉巴诺娃显得局促不安。
---
在抵达莫斯科的次日——5月18日，为向代表团表示敬意，举行了盛大的游行检阅。这是一场极其壮观的军事盛典，红军各兵种悉数受阅。游行队伍中没有任何工人参加。
一连串接连不断的庆祝活动、专场戏剧演出和工厂走访，显然让代表们感到厌倦。在他们当中可以明显觉察到一种不满情绪，对所受到的明显监视产生了一种抵触感。有几位代表抱怨说无法会见来访者，自代表团抵达以来在“商务庭院”推行的通行证制度（propusk），实际上将总台契卡（Cheka）特派人员认为不受欢迎的访客统统拒之门外。代表们日益意识到自己的自由受到了微妙的剥夺，清楚自己的一举一动、一言一行都受到了暗中窥探。他们反感这种“监狱般的气氛”——代表团的一位成员正是这样形容周围环境的。“我们是带着友好善意来的，”他对我说，“采取这种手段毫无意义。”他说，他绝不满足于只看官方安排给代表团展示的东西。他急切地想看得更深，并抱怨自己不得不耍些手腕，才能与自己想了解其见解的人士取得接触。
“俄国革命是整个人类历史上最伟大的事件，”一位代表对我说道，“微不足道的末节考量根本不应置于其中。一个新世界正在诞生；若去贬低如此分娩所承受的剧烈阵痛，简直比愚蠢还要糟糕。布尔什维克作为革命群众的先锋队，在这个过程中所扮演的角色，历史必将作出公正的评价。他们犯下错误是在所难免的，是人性的弱点；但尽管存在失误，他们正在奠定一种崭新的文明。历史绝不会宽恕失败：历史必将使布尔什维克流芳百世，因为他们在面对几乎无法克服的困难时取得了成功。他们完全有理由为自己的成就感到自豪。”

===Original===

He paused, then continued thoughtfully: “Let the delegates and the world look the situation full in the face. We must learn what revolution actually *is*. The Russian Revolution is not a matter of mere political recognition; it is a world-changing event. Of course we’ll find wrongs and abuses in it. A period of such storm and strife is unthinkable without them. Evils discovered need only be cured, and well-intentioned criticism is of utmost value. Nor is it a secret that Russia is suffering from starvation, and it is criminal to pretend well-being by grand banquets and dinners. On the contrary, let the delegates behold the terrible effects of the blockade, let them see the frightful disease and mortality resulting from it. No outsider can have even an approximate conception of the full extent of the Allied crime against Russia. The closer the delegates come in contact with the actuality, the more convincing will be their appeal to the British proletariat, and the more effectively will they be able to fight the blockade and Entente intervention.”

===Chinese===

他停顿了片刻，随即若有所思地继续说道：“让代表们和全世界都正视这一局面吧。我们必须弄清革命究竟‘是什么’。俄国革命绝非仅仅关乎政治承认；它是一个改变世界的历史事件。我们当然会在其中发现诸多错误与弊端。在这样一个风暴与激荡的年代，没有这些弊端是不可想象的。发现恶疾只需加以医治，善意的批评具有无与伦比的价值。俄国正在忍饥挨饿也绝非秘密，用奢华的宴席来粉饰太平无异于犯罪。相反，应当让代表们目睹封锁所造成的惨痛恶果，让他们亲眼看看封锁导致的骇人疾病与死亡率。任何局外人对协约国针对俄国所犯下的滔天罪行，都甚至无法形成哪怕大致准确的概念。代表们与真实现实接触得越紧密，他们向英国无产阶级发出的呼吁就越有说服力，他们抗击封锁和协约国武装干涉的斗争也就越坚决有效。”

## XIX The Spirit of Fanaticism / XIX 狂热的精神
===Original===

The Universalist Club on the Tverskaya was in great commotion. Anarchists, Left Social Revolutionists, and Maximalists, with a considerable sprinkling of factory workers and soldiers, filled the lecture room and were excitedly discussing something. As I entered, a tall, well-built young man in a naval blouse separated himself from the crowd and approached me. It was my friend G﻿—﻿—, the Anarchist sailor.
“What do you say *now*, Berkman?” he demanded, his strong face expressive of deep indignation. “Do you still think the Bolsheviki revolutionary?”
I learned that forty-five Anarchists in the Butirki prison (Moscow) had been subjected to such unbearable conditions of existence that they at last resorted to the desperate protest of a hunger strike. All of them have been in prison for many months, ever since the Leontievsky affair,21 without charges being preferred against them. They are kept under a most rigid regime, deprived of exercise and visitors, and the food served them is so insufficient and unwholesome that almost all of the prisoners are ill with scurvy. The hunger strikers demand to be tried or released, and their action is considered so justifiable by the other prisoners that the entire Butirki population of over 1,500 have joined the strikers. They have sent a collective protest to the Central Executive of the Communist Party, copies of which have also been forwarded to Lenin, the Moscow Soviet, the Labor Unions, and other official bodies. In view of the urgency of the situation the Universalists have elected a Committee to call on the Secretary of the Communist Party, and it has been suggested that I also join the Committee.
“Will you help?” my sailor friend asked, “or have you entirely deserted us?”
“Perhaps you’ll soon be in the Party,” another remarked bitterly, “you’re a Bolshevik now, a Sovietsky Anarchist.”
In the hope that a reapproachment may still be established between the Communists and the Left elements, I consented.
