Returning home that evening, I reflected on the failure of my previous efforts to bring about a better understanding between the warring revolutionary factions. I recalled my visits to Lenin and Krestinsky, my talks with Zinoviev, Chicherin, and other leading Bolsheviki.

===Chinese===

特维尔大街（Tverskaya）上的世界主义者俱乐部里一片骚动。无政府主义者、左翼社会革命党人和最高纲领派，夹杂着相当多的工厂工人和士兵，挤满了演讲大厅，正激动地讨论着什么。我一走进去，一名身穿水兵套头衫、身材高大魁梧的年轻人便从人群中挤出来走向我。那正是我的朋友G——，一位无政府主义水兵。
“你‘现在’怎么说，伯克曼？”他质问道，坚毅的面庞上流露出极度的愤慨。“你现在还认为布尔什维克具有革命性吗？”
我获悉，关押在莫斯科布蒂尔卡监狱（Butirki prison）的45名无政府主义者，由于遭受了不堪忍受的生存环境，终于采取了绝食抗议的绝望举动。自从列昂季耶夫事件21（Leontievsky affair）以来，他们所有人都已被囚禁了数月之久，却未受到任何正式指控。他们处于极其严苛的管训之下，被剥夺了放风散步和亲友探视的权利，所提供的伙食严重不足且极其劣质，以至于几乎所有囚犯都患上了坏血病。绝食抗议者要求接受审判或予以释放，他们的举动得到了其他犯人的广泛支持与同情，以至于布蒂尔卡监狱全狱1500多名在押人员全体加入了声援绝食的行列。他们向共产党中央执行委员会递交了集体抗议书，并向列宁、莫斯科苏维埃、各大工会及其他官方机构抄送了副本。鉴于事态紧急，世界主义者推选出了一个委员会去拜会共产党书记，并提议我也加入该委员会。
“你愿意帮忙吗？”水兵朋友问道，“还是说你已经彻底抛弃了我们？”
“说不定你很快就要入党了，”另一个人酸溜溜地讽刺道，“你现在是个布尔什维克了，是个‘苏维埃无政府主义者’。”
抱着或许仍能在共产党与左翼各派之间促成和解的一线希望，我答应了。
那天傍晚回到住处，我反思起自己此前试图在交战的革命派系之间促成相互理解而付出的种种失败努力。我回忆起自己对列宁和克列斯廷斯基（Krestinsky）的拜访，以及同季诺维也夫、契切林等布尔什维克领导人的长谈。

===Original===

Lenin had promised to have the Central Committee consider the matter, but his reply﻿—in the form of a resolution of the Party﻿—merely repeated that “*ideini* Anarchists22 are not persecuted,” but emphasized that “agitation against the Soviet Government cannot be tolerated.” The question of legalizing Anarchist educational work, which I discussed with Krestinsky several weeks ago, has not been acted upon and has evidently been ignored. Persecution of the Left elements continues, and the prisons are filled with revolutionists. Many of them have been outlawed and compelled to “go underground.” Maria Spiridonova23 has for a long time been imprisoned in the Kremlin, and her friends are being hunted as in the days of the Tsar.
A sense of discouragement comes over me as I witness the bitter animosity of the Communists toward the other revolutionary elements. They are even more ruthless in suppressing the Left opposition than that of the Right. Lenin, Chicherin, and Zinoviev assured me that Spiridonova and her circle are dangerous enemies of the Revolution. The Government pretended to consider Maria insane and had placed her in a sanitarium, from which she recently escaped. But I had an opportunity to visit the young woman, who is again hiding as in the Romanov times. I found her perfectly well-balanced, a most sincere idealist passionately devoted to the peasantry and the best interests of the Revolution. The other members of her circle﻿—Kamkov, Trutovsky, Izmailovitch﻿—are persons of high intelligence and integrity. The Bolsheviki, they believe, have betrayed the Revolution; but they do not advocate armed resistance to the Soviet Government, demanding only freedom of expression. They consider the Brest peace as the most fatal Communist step, the beginning of their reactionary policies and of the persecution of the Left elements. In protest against it and against the presence of the representative of German imperialism in Soviet Russia, they caused the death of Count Mirbach in 1918.
The Communists have grown Jesuitical in their attitude to other viewpoints. Yet most of them I find sincere and hardworking men, devoted to their cause and serving it to the point of self-abnegation. Very illuminating was my experience with Bakaiev, the head of the Petrograd Cheka, with whom I interceded in behalf of three Anarchists arrested recently.

===Chinese===

列宁曾允诺让中央委员会考虑此事，但他随后以党的决议形式给出的答复，却仅仅重复了“思想上22的无政府主义者（ideini Anarchists）不受迫害”，同时着重强调“反对苏维埃政权的煽动活动绝不能容忍”。几周前我同克列斯廷斯基探讨的关于将无政府主义教育启蒙工作合法化的问题，至今毫无下文，显然已被置之不理。对左翼势力的迫害仍在继续，监狱里关满了革命者。他们当中的许多人被定为非法，被迫转入“地下”。玛丽亚·斯皮里多诺娃23（Maria Spiridonova）长期被关押在克里姆林宫，她的朋友们则像在沙皇时代一样四处遭到缉捕。
亲眼目睹共产党对其他革命阵营的那种刻骨敌意，一股沮丧感油然而生。他们在镇压左翼反对派时甚至比对待右翼更加冷酷无情。列宁、契切林和季诺维也夫向我保证，斯皮里多诺娃及其圈子是革命的危险敌人。政府假称玛丽亚精神失常，将她送进了一家疗养院，她最近刚从那里逃脱。但我曾有机会探访这位年轻女性，她此时又像罗曼诺夫王朝时代一样隐蔽藏身。我发现她心智完全健全清醒，是一位极其赤诚的理想主义者，满腔热忱地献身于农民利益和革命的最高福祉。她圈子里的其他成员——卡姆科夫（Kamkov）、特鲁托夫斯基（Trutovsky）、伊兹马伊洛维奇（Izmailovitch）——都是极富才智与操守的人士。他们认为布尔什维克背叛了革命；但他们并不主张对苏维埃政权进行武装抵抗，仅仅要求言论表达的自由。他们认为《布列斯特和约》是共产党最致命的错招，是其走向反动政策以及迫害左翼势力的开端。为了抗议这项和约以及抗议德国帝国主义代表在苏维埃俄国的存在，他们在1918年策划击毙了米尔巴赫伯爵（Count Mirbach）。
共产党人在对待其他观点的态度上变得犹如耶稣会士般阴沉偏执。然而我发现他们当中的大多数人依然是赤诚而勤勉的人，忠于自己的事业，甚至达到了忘我奉献的境地。我同彼得格勒契卡（Cheka）负责人巴卡耶夫（Bakaiev）的一次接触极具启发性，我曾为了最近被捕的3名无政府主义者向他求情。

===Original===

A simple and unassuming man, I found him in a small unpretentious room in the Astoria, at dinner with his brother. They sat before a meager meal of thin soup and rice dessert; there was no meat and only a few slices of black bread. I could not help noticing that both men remained hungry.
Introduced by a personal note from Zinoviev, I appealed to Bakaiev for the prisoners, informing him that I knew them personally and considered their arrest unjustifiable.
“They are true revolutionists,” I urged. “Why do you keep them in prison?”
“In the room of Tch﻿—﻿—,” Bakaiev replied, “we found certain apparatus.”
“Tch﻿—﻿— is a chemist,” I explained.
“We know it,” he retorted; “but anti-Soviet handbills had been found in the factories, and my men thought they might have some connection with Tch﻿—﻿—’s laboratory. But he stubbornly refused to answer questions.”
“Well, that’s an old practice of arrested revolutionists,” I reminded him.
Bakaiev grew indignant. “That is why I’m holding him,” he declared. “Such tactics were justified against the bourgeois regime, but it is an insult to treat us so. Tch﻿—﻿— acts as if we were gendarmes.”
“Do you think it matters by whom one is kept in jail?”
“Well, don’t let us discuss it, Berkman,” he said. “You don’t know for whom you are interceding.”
“And the other two men?”
“They were found with Tch﻿—﻿—,” he replied. “We are not persecuting Anarchists, believe me; but these men are not safe at liberty.”
I appealed to Ravitch, the Commissar of Internal Affairs for the Petrograd District, a young woman with the impress of tragic revolutionary experience on her comely face. She regretted that she could do nothing, the Cheka having exclusive authority in such matters, and referred me to Zinoviev. The latter had not been informed of the arrests, but he assured me I need not be anxious about my friends.
“You know, Berkman, we do not arrest *ideini* Anarchists,” he said; “but those people are not your kind. Anyhow, rest easy; Bakaiev knows what he is about.”
He slapped me cheerfully on the shoulder and invited me to join him in the Imperial loge at the ballet that evening.
Later I learned that Bakaiev was suspended and exiled to the Caucasus for his too zealous use of summary execution.
*May 25.*﻿—This morning, on the fifth day of the Burtiki hunger strike, I called at the offices of the Central Committee of the Party, on Mokhovaia Street.

===Chinese===

他是一个朴素谦和的人，我在阿斯托里亚酒店一间简朴狭小的小房间里见到了他，他当时正同兄弟一起吃晚餐。他们面前摆着一顿寒酸的饭食，只有清汤和米饭甜点；不见半点肉腥，只有几片黑面包。我不由得注意到两个人都没吃饱。
凭着季诺维也夫的亲笔便条引荐，我向巴卡耶夫为那些囚犯求情，告知他我与他们熟识，认为对他们的逮捕是毫无道理的。
“他们是真正的革命者，”我力陈道，“你们为何要把他们关在牢里？”
“在Tch——的房间里，”巴卡耶夫回答道，“我们搜出了一定数量的仪器装置。”
“Tch——是一位化学家，”我解释道。
“我们清楚这点，”他反驳道，“但工厂里搜出了反苏传单，我手下的人怀疑这可能同Tch——的实验室有关。然而他执拗地拒绝回答任何问题。”
“哎，这是革命者被捕时的老传统了，”我提醒他。
巴卡耶夫勃然大怒。“这正是我关押他的原因！”他宣称道，“对付资产阶级政权，采取这种策略无可厚非；但这样对待我们简直是一种侮辱。Tch——的做派就仿佛我们是沙皇宪兵一样！”
“难道你认为被谁关进监狱有什么实质区别吗？”
“好了，咱们别争论这个了，伯克曼，”他说，“你根本不知道你在替谁求情。”
“那另外两个人呢？”
“他们是在Tch——那里被当场发现的，”他回答道，“请相信我，我们绝没有迫害无政府主义者；但这些人一旦获得自由便具有危险性。”
我又去向彼得格勒内务人民委员拉维奇求助，这是一位容貌姣好、脸上带着革命残酷悲剧印记的年轻女子。她遗憾地表示自己无能为力，契卡在这类事务上拥有独家专断大权，并建议我去找季诺维也夫。后者对逮捕一事并不知情，但他向我保证不必为朋友们担忧。
“你知道的，伯克曼，我们绝不逮捕思想上的无政府主义者，”他说，“但那些人可不是你这种人。总之，放宽心吧；巴卡耶夫办事心里有数。”
他爽朗地拍了拍我的肩膀，并邀请我当晚同他一道去皇室包厢观看芭蕾舞剧。
后来我获悉，巴卡耶夫因滥用就地处决的大权过于残酷狂热而被停职，并被流放到了高加索。
5月25日。——今天上午，在布蒂尔卡监狱绝食抗议进入第5天之际，我前往位于莫霍瓦亚大街（Mokhovaia Street）的党中央委员会办公处。

===Original===

As on my previous visit the anterooms were crowded with callers; numerous clerks, mostly young girls in abbreviated skirts and high-heeled lacquered shoes, flitted about with arms full of documents; others sat at desks writing and sorting large piles of reports and *dokladi*. I felt in the whirl of a huge machine, its wheels unceasingly revolving above the beehive on the street and grinding out slips of paper, endless paper for the guidance of the millions of Russia.
Preobrazhensky, formerly Commissar of Finance and now in Krestinsky’s place, received me somewhat coldly. He had read the protest of the hunger strikers, he said, but what of it? “What is it you come for?” he demanded.
I stated my mission. The politicals have been kept in prison for nine months, some of them even for two years, without trial or charges, and now they demand some action in their cases.
“They are within their rights,” Preobrazhensky replied, “but if your friends think they can influence us by a hunger strike, they are mistaken. They may starve as long as they want.” He paused and a hard expression came into his eyes. “If they die,” he added thoughtfully, “perhaps it would be best.”
“I have come to you as a comrade,” I said indignantly, “but if you take such an attitude﻿—”
“I have no time to discuss it,” he interrupted. “The matter will be considered this evening by the Central Committee.”
Later in the day I learned that ten of the imprisoned Anarchists, including Gordin﻿—the founder of the Universalist group﻿—were released by order of the Cheka, in the hope of breaking the hunger strike. This development was independent of any action of the Central Committee. It also became known that some of the Butirki politicals were condemned to five years’ prison, without having received hearing or trial, while others were sentenced to concentration camps “till the end of civil war.”
---
I was in a room in the Hotel National translating for the British Labor Mission various resolutions, articles, and Losovsky’s brochure on the history of Russian unionism, when I received a message from Radek asking me to call on a matter of great urgency. Wondering, I entered the automobile he had sent for me and was driven at a fast clip through the city till we reached the former quarters of the German Legation, now occupied by the Third International.

===Chinese===

正如我上次造访时一样，接待室里挤满了来访者；大批办事员——大都是穿着短裙、踩着漆皮高跟鞋的年轻姑娘——怀抱着厚厚的公文穿梭奔忙；另一些人坐在办公桌前批写并分拣着大堆大堆的报告和文件（dokladi）。我感到自己仿佛置身于一台轰鸣巨兽的漩涡之中，它的齿轮在街头熙攘的蜂窝上空永不停歇地旋转，吐出一张又一张纸片，无休无止的纸质公文，用以指引全俄数以千万计的民众。
前财政人民委员、如今接替克列斯廷斯基职位的普列奥布拉任斯基（Preobrazhensky）接待了我，神情颇为冷淡。他说自己已经看过了绝食者的抗议书，但那又如何？“你来到底是为了什么？”他质问道。
我陈述了来意。这些政治犯在未受审讯、未被起诉的情况下已被囚禁了9个月，有的甚至长达两年（2年），如今他们要求对自己的案情作出明确结论。
“这是他们的权利，”普列奥布拉任斯基回答道，“但如果你的朋友们以为通过绝食抗议就能左右我们，那他们就打错算盘了。他们想挨饿多久就挨饿多久好了。”他停顿了一下，眼神中露出一抹冷酷的神色。“如果他们死了，”他若有所思地补充道，“也许反倒是最好的解脱。”
“我是作为同志来找你的，”我愤怒地说道，“但如果你抱有这种态度——”
“我没时间探讨这个，”他打断我，“今晚中央委员会将审议此事。”
当天晚些时候我获悉，被囚禁的无政府主义者中有10名——包括世界主义者团体的创始人戈尔金（Gordin）——已遵照契卡的命令获释，以此希望打破绝食抗议的僵局。这一举措完全独立于中央委员会的任何决议。同时外界获悉，布蒂尔卡监狱的部分政治犯在未获任何听证或审讯的情况下被判处了5年监禁，另一些人则被判处关进集中营，“直到内战结束”。
---
当时我正在国民宾馆（Hotel National）的一个房间里，为英国工党代表团翻译各项决议、文章以及洛佐夫斯基关于俄国工会运动历史的小册子，突然接到拉狄克的急信，请我就紧急要事前去面谈。带着疑惑，我坐上了他派来的汽车，汽车在市内飞驰，直到抵达昔日德国公使馆旧址——如今这里是第三国际的驻地。

===Original===

The elegant reception hall was filled with callers and foreign delegates, some of whom were curiously examining the bullet marks in the mosaic floor and walls﻿—reminders of the violent death Mirbach had met in this room at the hands of Left Social Revolutionists opposed to the Brest peace.
I was conscious of the disapproving looks directed at me when, out of my turn, I was requested to follow the attendant to the private office of the Secretary of the Communist International. Radek received me very cordially, inquired about my health, and thanked me for so promptly responding to his call. Then, handing me a thick manuscript, he said:
“Ilyitch24 has just finished this work and he is anxious to have you render it into English for the British Mission. You will do us a great service.”
It was the manuscript of *The Infantile Sickness of Leftism*. I had already heard about the forthcoming work and knew it to be an attack on the Left revolutionary tendencies critical of Leninism. I turned over some pages, with their profusely underscored lines corrected in Lenin’s small but legible handwriting. “Petty bourgeois ideology of Anarchism,” I read; “the infantile stupidity of Leftism,” “the ultrarevolutionists suffocating in the fervor of their childish enthusiasm.” The pale faces of the Butirki hunger strikers rose before me. I saw their burning eyes peering accusingly at me through the iron bars. “Have you forsaken us?” I heard them whisper.
“We are in a great hurry about this translation.” Radek was saying, and I felt impatience in his voice. “We want it done within three days.”
“It will require at least a week,” I replied. “Besides, I have other work on hand, already promised.”
“I know, Losovsky’s,” he remarked with a disparaging tilt of the head; “that’s all right. Lenin’s takes precedence. You can drop everything else, on my responsibility.”
“I will undertake it if I may add a preface.”
“This is no joking matter, Berkman.” Radek was frankly displeased.
“I speak seriously. This pamphlet misrepresents and besmirches all my ideals. I cannot agree to translate it without adding a few words in defense.”
“Otherwise you decline?”
“I do.”
Radek’s manner lacked warmth as I took my departure.
---
A subtle change has taken place in the attitude of the Communists toward me. I notice coldness in their greeting, a touch of resentment even.

===Chinese===

典雅的接待大厅里挤满了来访者和外国代表，其中有些人正好奇地打量着马赛克地面和墙壁上的弹孔——正是反对《布列斯特和约》的左翼社会革命党人在这个房间里将米尔巴赫击毙时留下的痕迹。
当我被侍从破例带向共产国际书记的私人办公室时，我察觉到了投向我的不满目光。拉狄克十分热情地接待了我，询问了我的身体状况，并感谢我如此迅速地应召赶来。随后，他递给我一份厚厚的手稿，说道：
“伊里奇24（Ilyitch）刚完成这部著作，他急切地希望你能把它译成英文提供给英国代表团。你这将是帮了我们一个大忙。”
这正是《“左派”幼稚病》（The Infantile Sickness of Leftism）的手稿。我此前已听说过这部即将问世的著作，知道这是对批评列宁主义的左翼革命倾向的一场抨击。我翻看了几页，上面密集地划着下划线，留有列宁虽小却清晰工整的笔迹校改。“无政府主义的小资产阶级意识形态，”我读道；“左派的幼稚愚昧”，“在狂热的孩童般热情中窒息的极左革命者”。布蒂尔卡监狱绝食者苍白的面孔浮现在我的眼前。我看到他们那灼灼如炬的双眼，隔着铁窗带着谴责的神色凝视着我。“你抛弃我们了吗？”我听见他们在喃喃低语。
“这篇译文我们要得很急，”拉狄克说着，我听出了他语气中的不耐烦，“我们需要在3天之内译完。”
“这至少需要一周（1周）时间，”我回答道，“而且，我手头还有答应过的其他工作。”
“我知道，洛佐夫斯基的那份嘛，”他轻蔑地歪了歪头说道，“那没关系。列宁的著作享有绝对优先权。由我负责，你可以把其他事情统统搁下。”
“如果允许我加一篇译者序言，我便承接。”
“这可不是开玩笑的事，伯克曼，”拉狄克显然大为不悦。
“我是认真的。这本小册子歪曲并抹黑了我所有的理想。如果不让我加几句辩白之词，我绝不能同意翻译它。”
“否则你就拒绝？”
“是的，拒绝。”
当我告辞时，拉狄克的态度已毫无温情可言。
---
共产党人对待我的态度发生了一种微妙的变化。我注意到他们问候时的冷淡，甚至带着一丝怨怼。

===Original===

My refusal to translate Lenin’s brochure has become known, and I am made to feel guilty of lèse majesté.
I have been accompanying the British Mission on its visits to mills, theaters, and schools, and everywhere I was aware of the scrutinizing gaze of the Chekamen attending the delegates as guides and interpreters. In the Delovoi Dvor the clerk has suddenly begun to demand my *propusk* and to ask my “business,” though he knows that I live there and am helping the delegates with translations.
I have decided to give up my room in the Dvor and to accept the hospitality of a friend in the National. It is contrary to the rules of the Soviet Houses, no visitor being permitted to remain after midnight. At that hour the day’s *propuski*, with the names of the callers and the persons visited, are turned over to the Cheka. Not being an official guest of the Hotel, I am not entitled to meals and am compelled to commit another breach of Communist order by resorting to the markets, officially abolished but practically in operation. The situation is growing intolerable, and I am preparing to leave for Petrograd.
“You have become persona non grata,” Augustine Souchy, the delegate of the German Syndicalist Union, remarked as we were sitting in the Delovoi translating the resolutions submitted by Losovsky to the labor representatives of Sweden, Norway, and Germany.
“In both camps,” I laughed. “My friends of the Left call me a Bolshevik, while the Communists look askance at me.”
“Many of us are in the same boat,” Souchy replied.
Bertrand Russell passed by and called me aside. “I think nothing will come of our proposed visit to Peter Kropotkin,” he said. “For five days they have been promising a machine. It’s always ‘in a moment it will be here,’ and the days pass in vain waiting.”
A curly-headed little Communist, one of the English-speaking guides assigned to the Mission, sauntered by, as if inadvertently.
“Is the machine ready?” Russell asked. “It was to be here at ten this morning; it’s 2 p.m. now.”
“The Commissar just told me that the machine unfortunately got out of order,” the guide replied.
Russell smiled. “They are sabotaging our visit,” he said; “we’ll have to drop it.” Then he added sadly: “I feel like a prisoner, every step watched. Already in Petrograd I became aware of this annoying surveillance. It’s rather stupid of them.”

===Chinese===

我拒绝翻译列宁小册子的事已传了开来，大家让我觉得自己犯下了大不敬罪。
我一直陪同英国代表团参观工厂、剧场和学校，无论走到哪里，我都敏锐地察觉到作为向导和翻译随行的契卡（Cheka）人员那审视挑剔的目光。在“商务庭院”，前台办事员突然开始查验我的通行证（propusk），并盘问我的“事由”，尽管他明知我就住在那里并且协助代表们翻译。
我决定放弃在商务庭院的房间，转去国民宾馆接受一位朋友的留宿招待。这违反了苏维埃客舍的规章制度，因为午夜之后严禁任何访客滞留。在那个时刻，一整天的通行证底根——连同来访者姓名及被访者名单——都必须上缴给契卡。由于我不是宾馆的正式官方住客，我无权享有配给餐食，不得不诉诸官方虽已明令取缔却实际上照常运转的自由市场，从而犯下了对共产主义秩序的又一次逾矩。处境日益令人难以忍受，我正准备动身返回彼得格勒。
“你已经成了不受欢迎的人（persona non grata），”德国工团主义同盟代表奥古斯丁·苏希（Augustine Souchy）说道，当时我们正坐在商务庭院，翻译洛佐夫斯基提交给瑞典、挪威和德国工会代表的决议。
“在两个阵营里都是如此，”我自嘲地笑道，“左翼的朋友骂我是布尔什维克，而共产党人又对我侧目而视。”
“我们当中的许多人都同病相怜，”苏希回答。
伯特兰·罗素从一旁走过，把我叫到一边。“我想我们拜访彼得·克鲁泡特金（Peter Kropotkin）的计划恐怕要泡汤了，”他说，“5天来他们一直许诺安排一辆汽车。每次都说‘马上就到’，可日子全在徒劳的等待中溜走了。”
一名留着鬈发的年轻共产党人——分配给代表团的英语向导之一——仿佛无意般漫步走过。
“车准备好了吗？”罗素问道，“原本说好今天上午10点到位，现在都下午2点了。”
“政委刚才告诉我，车子不幸出了故障，”向导回答道。
罗素微笑了。“他们这是在暗中破坏我们的走访，”他说，“我们不得不取消这次拜访了。”接着他黯然补充道：“我感觉自己像个囚徒，每走一步都有人监视。早在彼得格勒我就察觉到了这种令人厌烦的监视。他们这样做未免太愚蠢了。”

===Original===

I listened to some of the British delegates discussing the printers’ meeting from which they had just returned. Melnitchansky and other Bolsheviki had addressed the gathering, eulogizing the Soviet regime and the Communist dictatorship. Suddenly a man wearing a long black beard appeared on the platform. Before anyone realized his identity, he launched an attack on the Bolsheviki. He branded them as the corrupters of the Revolution and denounced their tyranny as worse than the Tsar’s. His fiery oratory kept the audience spellbound. Then someone shouted: “Who are you? Your name!”
“I am Chernov, Victor Chernov,” the man replied in bold, defiant voice.
The Bolsheviki on the platform jumped to their feet in rage.
“Hurrah! Long live Chernov, brave Chernov!” the audience shouted, and a wild ovation was tendered the Social Revolutionary leader and former President of the Constituent Assembly.
“Arrest him! Hold the traitor!” came from the Communists. There was a rush to the platform, but Chernov had disappeared.
Some of the Britishers expressed admiration for the daring of the man whom the Cheka has been so assiduously searching for a long time. “It was rather exciting,” someone remarked.
“I shudder to think what will happen to him if he’s caught,” said another.
“Deucedly clever, his escape.”
“The printers will pay for it.”
“I hear the leaders of the Third Soviet bakery are under arrest and the men locked out for demanding more bread.”
“It’s different at home,” a delegate sighed. “But I believe we all agree that the blockade must be raised.”

===Chinese===

我倾听着几位刚刚回来的英国代表谈论印刷工人的集会。梅尔尼昌斯基和其他布尔什维克在集会上发表了演说，盛赞苏维埃政权和共产党专政。突然，一名留着黑色长须的男子出现在主席台上。还没等任何人弄清他的身份，他便对布尔什维克发起了猛烈的抨击。他斥责他们是革命的败坏者，指责他们的暴政比沙皇还要残暴。他那慷慨激昂的雄辩震撼了全场。这时有人大喊：“你是谁？报上名来！”
“我是切尔诺夫，维克多·切尔诺夫（Victor Chernov）！”那人以大胆无畏的声音回答道。
主席台上的布尔什维克愤怒地跳了起来。
“乌拉！切尔诺夫万岁，勇敢的切尔诺夫万岁！”台下的听众狂呼起来，全场向这位社会革命党领袖、前立宪会议主席报以狂热的欢呼。
“逮捕他！抓住这个叛徒！”共产党人喊叫道。众人冲向主席台，但切尔诺夫已经无影无踪了。
几位英国代表对这名契卡苦苦搜捕多时的通缉犯的胆魄表示钦佩。“那场面真是惊心动魄，”有人评价道。
“一想到他万一被抓住会是什么下场，我就不寒而栗，”另一人说。
“逃得可真够狡黠利落的。”
“印刷工人们可要为此遭殃了。”
“我听说第三苏维埃面包厂（Third Soviet bakery）的负责人们被捕了，工人们也因为要求增加面包配给而被关在厂门外停工。”
“国内的情况可完全不同，”一名代表叹息道，“但我相信大家一致同意，封锁必须立即解除。”

## XX Other People / XX 其他人
===Original===

*June.*﻿—Winter has released its icy grip, and the sun shines brightly. In the parks the benches are filling with people.
Our *Buford* mascot, the “Baby,” passed me and I hailed him. The color has faded from his face, and he looks yellow and weary.
“No, most of our boys are not working yet,” he said, “and we’re sick of the red tape. They always tell you they need workers, but nobody really wants us. Of course, the Communists of our group have all gotten good berths. Have you heard about Bianki? You remember how he roasted them at that meeting in Beloostrov? How he joined the Party and got a responsible job? The Boston sailor, remember him? Well, I met him walking on the street the other day, all dressed up in a leather suit, with a gun as big as your arm. In the Cheka. His old business. Did you know he was a detective in Boston?”
“I thought he was a sailor.”
“Years back. Later he served in a private sleuth agency.
“Several of our boys worked for a while in the Petrotop,”25 the “Baby” continued. “The Cheka thought there were too many Anarchists there and they kicked us out. Dzerzhinsky26 says the Petrotop is an Anarchist nest; but everyone knows the city would have frozen to death last winter if it wasn’t for Kolobushkin. He is an Anarchist and the whole brains of that place, but they talk of arresting him. An old Schlüsselburg man at that; spent ten years in the dungeons there.”
---
With primitive unconcern of those about her, an old peasant woman has bared the back of a young girl at her side and is closely scrutinizing her garments. With deliberate movement her thumb and forefinger come together, she withdraws her hand, straightens herself, and releases her captive on the ground. Her neighbor draws nervously aside. “Be careful, good woman,” he chides her, “I have enough of my own.”
“Tell me, my dear,” the old woman inquires, “is it true what people are saying about new wars?”
“Yes.”
“With whom, then?”
“With the Poles.”
“Oh, God be merciful! And why must they always be fighting, Little Uncle?”
The man is silent. The girl lifts her face from the woman’s lap. “It’s chilly, aunt. Are you done now?”
“You’re full of ’em, child.”
---
On the corner two militiamen are directing a group of street cleaners, oldish men and boys from the concentration camp, and women arrested without documents on trains.

===Chinese===

6月。——严冬终于松开了它冰冷的掌控，阳光明媚。公园里的长椅上坐满了人。
我们“布福德号”上的吉祥物“娃娃”（Baby）从我身旁走过，我向他打招呼。他脸上的红润已经褪去，显得面色蜡黄、神情疲惫。
“唉，我们那帮小伙子大部分还没找着活干呢，”他说，“繁文缛节真把我们恶心透了。他们总说需要劳动力，可根本没人真心要我们。当然，我们那批人里的共产党员全都在好单位安插了肥缺。你听说比安基（Bianki）的事了吗？你还记得他在别洛奥斯特罗夫（Beloostrov）的集会上怎么把他们痛骂一顿的吧？后来他入了党，捞到了一个体面的负责职位！还有那个波士顿水兵，还记得他吧？嘿，前几天我在街上碰见他，一身皮衣皮裤，腰里别着一把足有你胳膊那么粗的大枪。进了契卡（Cheka）。干他的老本行。你知道他以前在波士顿干过私家侦探吗？”
“我还以为他是个水手呢。”
“那是好几年前的事了。后来他在一家私人侦探所干活。”
“我们有几个小伙子在彼得格勒燃料委员会25（Petrotop）干了一阵子，”‘娃娃’继续说道，“契卡觉得那里无政府主义者太多，把我们统统轰了出来。捷尔任斯基26（Dzerzhinsky）说彼得格勒燃料委员会是个无政府主义分子的贼窝；可谁都知道，去年冬天要不是靠科洛布什金（Kolobushkin），全城的人早就冻死了。他是个无政府主义者，也是那个部门的真正主心骨，可他们竟然传言要逮捕他。而且他还是个在施吕塞尔堡（Schlüsselburg）要塞蹲过10年地牢的老资历革命者呢！”
---
一位老农妇完全不在乎周围的人，正掀开身边一个小姑娘的后背衣衫，聚精会神地仔细搜寻着她的衣物。只见她的大拇指和食指从容地捏合在一起，收回手，挺起身子，将捕获的猎物掐死丢在地上。身旁的邻座神色紧张地往旁挪了挪。“当心点，好大娘，”他埋怨道，“我自己身上的已经够多了。”
“告诉我，老弟，”老妇人打听道，“人们传闻又要打新仗了，是真的吗？”
“是真的。”
“跟谁打呀？”
“跟波兰人。”
“哎呀，老天慈悲！大叔，他们为什么总有打不完的仗呢？”
那男子沉默不语。小姑娘从老妇人的膝头抬起脸：“大娘，好冷啊。你掐完了吗？”
“孩子，你身上全都是虱子。”
---
街角处，两名民警正监督着一群清扫街道的人，有从集中营押来的上了年纪的男人和半大小子，还有在火车上因未带证件而被捕的妇女。

===Original===

Some have high felt boots on, the loose soles flapping noisily in the liquid dung. Others are barefoot. They work apathetically, carrying the filth from the yards to the street and loading it upon carts. The stench is nauseating.
A husky *militsioner* leisurely saunters up to one of the women. She is young and good-looking, though extremely pale and gaunt.
“What’s your dreaming! Work, you wench,” he says, playfully poking her in the ribs.
“Have a heart,” she pleads. “I’m so weak; just out of the hospital when they nabbed me.”
“Serves you right for riding without a pass.”
“Couldn’t help it, little pigeon,” she says good humoredly. “They told me my husband is in Peter,27 back from the front, and he away from me five years. So I goes to the office; three days in line and then they refuse me a pass. I thought I’d come some way, but they took me off the train, and I’m so weak and sick and they give me no *pyock*. How am I to find my man now?”
“Get yourself another,” the militiaman laughs. “You won’t see him again.”
“Why won’t I?” she demands angrily.
“Cause he’s likely been sent against the Poles.”
“Oh, my misfortune!” the woman wails. “Is there to be no end to war?”
“You’re a woman and naturally stupid. Can’t expect you to understand such things!”
---
In the Dom Outchonikh (Home of the Learned) I met literary men, scientists, and intellectuals of various political camps, all looking the mere shadows of humans. They sat about listlessly, some nibbling pieces of black bread.
In a corner a group was discussing the rumors of war.
“It is a great blow to the hope of industrial revival,” B﻿—﻿—, the well-known political economist, said. “And we had begun to dream of more freedom to breathe.”
“The worst of it is,” Z﻿—﻿—, the ethnologist, remarked: “we shall not be able to receive the book donations promised us from abroad.”
“I’m so out of touch with scientific progress, I feel downright ignorant,” said Prof. L﻿—﻿—, the bacteriologist.
“Poland is on the eve of Revolution,” F﻿—﻿—, the Communist asserted. “The Red Army will go straight to Warsaw and we’ll help the Polish proletariat drive out the masters and establish a Soviet Republic.”
“Like our own,” B﻿—﻿— retorted ironically. “They are to be congratulated.”
---
In the evening I visited my friend Pyotr, a nonpartisan worker in the Trubotchny mill. “We have received war orders in the shop,” he was saying to his wife.

===Chinese===

有些人脚上蹬着高筒毡靴，松脱的靴底在稀烂的污泥粪水里拍打作响。另一些人则光着脚。他们麻木迟钝地干着活，把院落里的污秽垃圾运到街上装上板车。臭气熏天，令人作呕。
一个体格壮硕的民警悠哉游哉地踱到一名妇女跟前。她年纪尚轻，容貌秀丽，但面容极其苍白憔悴。
“发什么呆！干活去，你个小娘们，”他说着，戏谑地用手指捅了捅她的肋骨。
“行行好吧，”她哀求道，“我浑身没力气；他们抓我的时候，我刚出院呢。”
“谁叫你无证乘车，活该。”
“我也是没办法呀，长官哥，”她好声好气地说，“人家告诉我，我丈夫调回彼得27（Peter）了，刚从前线回来，他整整离开我5年了。我就去机关申请；排了整整3天队，他们硬是不给我开通行证。我寻思着总能设法赶过来，可他们把我从火车上拽了下来，我又虚弱又生病，他们还不给我口粮（pyock）。如今叫我怎么找我的男人啊？”
“再找一个呗，”民警嘲笑道，“你反正再也见不着他了。”
“凭什么见不着？”她急得质问道。
“因为他八成已经被派去打波兰人了。”
“哎呀，我这苦命的人啊！”妇女哀嚎起来，“这仗难道就没完没了吗？”
“你们头发长见识短，天生就是蠢。还能指望你懂这种军国大事！”
---
在“学者之家”（Dom Outchonikh），我见到了来自各个政治阵营的文人、科学家和知识分子，所有人都消瘦得只剩人形。他们无精打采地坐着，有些人嘴里啃着小块黑面包。
角落里有一群人正在讨论有关战争的传闻。
“这对工业复苏的希望是一个沉重的打击，”著名政治经济学家B——说道，“而我们原本才刚开始憧憬能有更多喘息的自由。”
“最糟糕的是，”人种学家Z——指出，“国外许诺捐赠给我们的学术书籍，我们将无法收到了。”
“我完全同外部科学进展脱节了，觉得自己简直成了一个彻底的文盲，”细菌学教授L——叹息道。
“波兰正处于革命的前夜，”共产党员F——断言道，“红军将直捣华沙，我们将协助波兰无产阶级驱逐那帮老爷，建立苏维埃共和国。”
“建立一个像我们这样的共和国，”B——讽刺地反驳道，“那可真得好好祝贺他们一番了。”
---
傍晚，我拜访了我的朋友彼得（Pyotr），他是特鲁博奇内工厂的一名无党派工人。“车间里接到了军品订单，”他正对妻子说道。

===Original===

“How are we to conquer the *razrukha*, our terrible economic ruin, when everything works for war again?”
A middle-aged man, stout and coarse looking, came in.
“Well, Pyotr Vassilitch,” he addressed the host cheerily, “it’s war with Poland and we’ll teach those *pani* a lesson.”
“It’s easy for you to talk, Ivan Nikolaievitch,” Pyotr replied; “you don’t have to live on your *pyock*. He supplies lumber to the government,” he explained, turning to me, “and he don’t starve, *he* don’t.”
“We must defend our country against the Poles,” the contractor replied sententiously.
“Will they take Vanya?” the housewife asked tearfully; “he is not seventeen yet.”
“I don’t mind going to the front,” came from the boy lying on the stove. “They get a good *pyock* in the Army, and I may advance to *Kommandir* like cousin Vaska did.”
He rose, drew a herring and a hunk of bread from his *polushubka*, and began to eat. His father watched him hungrily. “Give mother a bite,” he urged after a while; “she’s had nothing since yesterday.”
“I’m not hungry,” the mother said apologetically.
“Yes, my friends,” the contractor spoke again as if remembering an unfinished thought, “the Poles must be taught a lesson, and we must all defend the Revolution.”
“What are we to defend?” Pyotr demanded bitterly. “The fat Commissars and the Cheka with its shooting, that’s what we defend. We haven’t got anything else.”
“You talk like a counterrevolutionist,” Vanya shouted, jumping off the stove.
“We haven’t even our children,” his father continued. “That boy has become a hoodlum since he joined the Komsomol.28 He learns there to hate his parents.”
Vanya pushed his fur cap over his ears and stepped toward the door. “Take care I don’t tell on you,” he said, slamming it behind him.
---
The Italian Socialist Mission, headed by Seratti, is in the city, and the occasion is celebrated with the usual military parades, demonstrations, and meetings. But the show has lost interest for me. I have looked back of the curtain. The performances lack sincerity; political intrigue is the mainspring of the spectacles. The workers have no part in them except for mechanical obedience to orders; hypocrisy conducts the delegates through the factories; false information deceives them regarding the actual state of affairs; surveillance prevents their getting in touch with the people and learning the truth of the situation.

===Chinese===

“既然万事又要重新为战争服务，我们又该如何战胜经济崩溃（razrukha）——这场可怕的经济浩劫呢？”
一位身材发福、相貌粗鲁的中年男子走了进来。
“喂，彼得·瓦西里奇，”他兴冲冲地向主人打招呼，“要同波兰开战了，咱们得给那帮波兰地主老爷（pani）一点颜色瞧瞧。”
“你当然说得轻巧，伊万·尼古拉耶维奇，”彼得回答道，“你又用不着靠那点口粮（pyock）过活。他是给政府供应木材的包工头，”他转过头向我解释道，“他可挨不着饿，‘他’绝挨不着。”
“我们必须保卫祖国抵抗波兰人，”承包商一本正经地回答。
“他们会把万尼亚抓走吗？”主妇泪眼婆娑地问道，“他还不满17岁呢。”
“去前线我倒无所谓，”躺在火炕炉子上方的男孩子接茬道，“部队里能领到优厚的口粮，说不定我还能像表哥瓦斯卡那样升任指挥官（Kommandir）呢。”
他坐起身，从短皮袄（polushubka）里掏出一条鲱鱼和一大块面包吃了起来。他的父亲眼巴巴地咽着口水看着他。“给你娘咬一口，”过了一会儿他催促道，“她从昨天起就粒米未进。”
“我不饿，”母亲怯生生、带着歉意地说道。
“是啊，朋友们，”承包商又开了口，仿佛想起了一句没说完的话，“必须给波兰人一个教训，我们大家都得保卫革命。”
“我们到底保卫什么？”彼得愤恨地质问道，“保卫那些脑满肠肥的政委，保卫动不动就开枪枪毙人的契卡（Cheka），这就是我们保卫的东西！除了这些，我们还有什么？！”
“你说话简直像个反革命分子！”万尼亚大喊一声，从火炕炉子上跳了下来。
“我们甚至连自己的骨肉都没能保住，”父亲继续说道，“这孩子自从加入了共青团28（Komsomol），就彻底变成了一个二流子流氓。他在那里学会了仇恨自己的亲生父母。”
万尼亚把皮帽往下按压遮住耳朵，快步走向大门。“留神我向上头告发你！”他说完便狠狠摔门而去。
---
由塞拉蒂（Seratti）率领的意大利社会党代表团正在本市访问，官方照例举行了阅兵式、示威游行和大会集会以示庆祝。然而这出把戏对我而言已失去了吸引力。我已经看透了帷幕背后的真相。这些演出缺乏真诚；政治图谋才是这一幕幕盛景的真正驱动机轴。工人们除了机械地服从命令外，根本没有任何实质参与；虚伪做作引导着代表们走访工厂；虚假数据隐瞒了真实客观的实情；密不透风的严密监视阻止了他们接触底层民众并了解客观真相。

===Original===

The delegates are dined, fêted, and influenced to bring their organizations into the fold of the Third International, under the leadership of Moscow.
How far it all is from my conception of revolutionary probity and purpose!
The Communist leaders have become involved in schemes of political recognition and are wasting the energies of the Revolution to create an appearance of military strength and industrial health. They have lost sight of the real values underlying the great change. The people sense the false tendencies of the new regime and helplessly see it return to old practices. The proletariat is growing disillusioned; it sees its revolutionary conquests sacrificed one by one, the former champions of liberty become hard rulers, defenders of the existing regime, and the revolutionary slogans and hopes turn to dying embers.
An atmosphere of embittered helplessness pervades the circles of the intelligentsia, a paralyzing sense of their lack of cohesion and energizing purpose. They are exhausted by years of starvation; their mental bases are weakened, the spiritual bonds with the people severed.
The revolutionists of the Left are disorganized, broken by persecution and internal disunion. The stress and storm period has shattered old moorings and set accepted values adrift. Little of constructive character is manifest in the general confusion. The ruthless hand of life in the making, more than Bolshevik fiat, has destroyed old forms, creating a chaos of things physical and spiritual. Institutions and ideas, thrown into a common heap, rage in primitive passion and wildly seek to disentangle themselves, desperately clutching at each other in the attempt to rise to the surface. And above the shouts and din of the struggling mass, drowning all other cries, sounds the desperate, ceaseless plea: Bread! Bread!
Moscow is eaten with bureaucracy, Petrograd is a dying city. Not here is the Revolution. Out in the country, among the common people, one must see new Russia and live its life in the making.
I have been requested to join the expedition planned by the Museum of the Revolution. Its purpose is to collect historic material of the revolutionary movement since its inception, almost a hundred years ago. I had hoped to participate in more constructive labors, but circumstances and the growing coldness of the Communist attitude exclude me from more vital work.

===Chinese===

代表们受到山珍海味的款待与盛情欢迎，并被施加影响，以促使其背后的组织归入以莫斯科为首的第三国际旗下。
这一切同我对革命操守与崇高宗旨的理解相距是何等遥远！
共产党领导人深陷于谋求政治承认的算计之中，正在虚掷革命的元气去营造一种军事强盛和工业繁荣的虚假表象。他们已经忽视了这场伟大剧变背后蕴含的真正核心价值。人民察觉到了新政权的危险偏向，眼睁睁无可奈何地看着它重蹈昔日的陈规旧习。无产阶级正日益陷入幻灭；他们看到自己的革命成果被一点点蚕食断送，昔日的自由捍卫者蜕变成了铁石心肠的统治者和既得体制的卫道士，而革命口号与希望正化作即将熄灭的余烬。
在知识分子圈子里弥漫着一种愤懑而无助的气氛，一种因缺乏凝聚力与振作目标而带来的瘫痪感。多年的饥馑已经耗尽了他们的精力；他们的精神根基遭到削弱，与普通民众之间的精神纽带已被割裂。
左翼革命者组织涣散，在迫害与内部倾轧中支离破碎。狂风暴雨的激荡岁月粉碎了旧日的定盘星，让公认的价值体系随波逐流。在普遍的混乱困顿之中，几乎看不到任何建设性的生机。正在形成中的现实生活的残酷铁掌，比起布尔什维克的强权法令，更有力地摧毁了旧体制形式，造成了物质与精神层面的双重混乱。各种制度与思想被混杂堆砌在一起，在原始的激情中激烈冲撞，狂乱地试图摆脱纠缠，并在挣扎浮出水面的过程中拼命相互拉扯。而在苦苦挣扎的人群的呼号与喧嚣之上，淹没一切其他哭喊的，是那声嘶力竭、永无休止的绝望哀求：面包！面包！
莫斯科已被官僚主义蚕食殆尽，彼得格勒是一座奄奄一息的垂死之城。革命不在这里。必须深入广袤的乡间，置身于普通平民之中，才能目睹正在诞生的新俄国并体验其孕育中的新生。
有关方面邀请我参加革命博物馆策划的历史考察队。其宗旨是搜集自近一百年前（100年前）发端以来的革命运动历史文献与实物。我原本期盼能参与更为切实的建设性工作，然而眼下的处境以及共产党人日趋冷淡的态度，已将我彻底排斥在更为核心的关键工作之外。

===Original===

The mission of the expedition is nonpolitical, and I have decided to accept the offer.

===Chinese===

考察队的使命是非政治性的，我决定接受这项邀请。

## XXI En Route to the Ukraine / XXI 途中：开赴乌克兰
===Original===

*July, 1920.*﻿—Turbulent mobs besiege our train at every station. Soldiers and workers, peasants, women, and children, loaded with heavy bags, frantically fight for admission. Yelling and cursing, they force their way toward the cars. They climb through the broken windows, board the bumpers, and crowd upon the steps, recklessly clinging to door handles and clutching at each other for support. Like maddened ants they cover every inch of space, in momentary danger of limb and life. It is a dense, surging human sea moved by the one passion of securing a foothold on the already moving train. Even the roofs are crowded, the women and children lying flat, the men kneeling or standing up. Frequently at night, the train passing under a bridge or trestle, scores of them are swept to their death.
At the stations the railroad militia await us. They surround a car, drive the passengers off roof and steps, and proceed to another coach. But the next instant there is a rush and struggle, and the cleared car is again covered with the human swarm. Often the *militsioneri* resort to arms, firing salvos over the train. But the people are desperate: they had spent days, even weeks, in procuring “traveling papers”﻿—they are in search of food or returning with filled bags to their hungry families. Death from a bullet is no more terrible to them than starvation.
With sickening regularity these scenes repeat themselves at every stopping place. It is becoming a torture to travel in comparative comfort in our conspicuous-looking car, recently renovated and painted a bright red, and bearing the inscription, “Extraordinary Commission of the Museum of the Revolution.”
The Expedition consists of six persons, comprising the secretary, Miss A. Shakol; the treasurer, Emma Goldman; the historical “expert” Yakovlev, and his wife; a young Communist, a student of the Petrograd University; and myself as chairman. Our party also includes the official *provodnik* (porter) and Henry Alsberg, the American correspondent, whose friendly attitude to Russia had secured for him Zinoviev’s permission to accompany us. Our coach is divided into several coupés, an office, dining room, and a kitchen furnished with the linen and silverware of the Winter Palace, now the headquarters of the Museum.

===Chinese===

1920年7月。——在每一个车站，汹涌骚乱的人群都围攻着我们的列车。士兵、工人、农民、妇女和儿童，背负着沉重的大包小裹，发狂般地争抢上车。他们叫喊着、咒骂着，拼命向车厢挤去。他们爬过破碎的车窗，跨上车厢缓冲器，挤满车梯踏板，不顾死活地抓着门把手，彼此死死拉扯以求支撑。他们宛如发狂的蚁群般覆盖了每一寸空间，每时每刻都面临着肢体残损乃至丧命的危险。这是一片密密麻麻、汹涌澎湃的人海，全被在已经启动的列车上争抢一席之地的狂热本能所驱使。就连车顶也挤满了人，妇孺趴伏着，男人们则跪着或直立着。在夜间，当列车穿过桥梁或栈桥时，屡屡有数十人被直接刮落扫下车顶丧命。
在各个车站，铁路民警严阵以待。他们围住一节车厢，把乘客从车顶和踏板上驱赶下来，然后走向下一节车厢。然而下一秒，人群便又是一阵疯狂的冲锋争抢，被清理过的车厢又重新被密密麻麻的人群所覆盖。民警（militsioneri）往往诉诸武器，朝列车上方鸣枪射击。然而百姓早已陷入绝境：他们花了几天乃至几周时间才弄到“通行公文”——他们要么是四处寻找粮食，要么正带着装满粮食的布袋赶回去拯救饥肠辘辘的家人。对他们而言，饮弹身亡并不比活活饿死更加可怕。
这些令人心悸的场景在每一个停靠站以令人作呕的规律性反复重演。乘坐着我们那节引人注目的车厢在相对舒适的环境中旅行，竟变成了一种煎熬——这节车厢最近经过翻新并漆成了鲜艳的红色，上面赫然写着“革命博物馆特派委员会”的字样。
考察队由6人组成，包括秘书A. 沙科尔小姐（Miss A. Shakol）、司库爱玛·戈尔德曼、历史“专家”雅科夫列夫（Yakovlev）及其妻子、一名彼得格勒大学学生的青年共产党员，以及由我担任主席。我们一行还包括官方列车员（provodnik），以及美国记者亨利·阿尔斯伯格（Henry Alsberg），正是鉴于他对俄国的友好态度，季诺维也夫才特批他随我们同行。我们的客车车厢被隔成几个包厢、一间办公室、一间餐厅，以及一间配有冬宫——如今革命博物馆总部所在地——亚麻织品与银制餐具的厨房。

===Original===

In the daytime the people remain at a respectful distance, the inscription on our car evidently creating the impression that it is occupied by the Cheka, the most dreaded institution in Russia. But at night, the stations in semidarkness, we are besieged by throngs clamoring for accommodation. It is contrary to our instructions to admit anyone, because of the danger of having our material stolen, as well as for fear of disease. The people are vermin-infected; almost everyone traveling in the Ukraine is afflicted with *sipnyak*, a form of typhus that often results fatally. Our historian lives in mortal dread of it, and vehemently protests against receiving outsiders. We compromise by permitting several old women and cripples to ride on the platform, and stealthily we feed them from the supplies of our “commune.”
The population of the districts we are passing through is in a state of disquiet and alarm. At every station we are warned not to proceed further: the Whites, robber bands, Makhno, and Wrangel are within gunshot, we are assured. The atmosphere thickens with fear﻿—inspiring rumors as we advance southward.
A cauldron of seething emotions, life in the South constrasts strikingly with that of the North. By comparison Moscow and Petrograd appear quiet and orderly. Here all is unformed, grotesque, chaotic. Frequent changes of government, with their accompaniment of civil war and destruction, have produced a mental and physical condition unknown in other parts of the country. They have created an atmosphere of uncertainty, of life lacking roots, of constant anxiety. Some parts of the Ukraine have experienced fourteen different regimes within the period of 1917﻿–﻿1920, each involving violent disturbance of normal existence, disorganizing and tearing life from its foundations.
The whole gamut of revolutionary and counterrevolutionary passions has been played on this territory. Here the nationalistic Rada had fought the local organs of the Kerensky government till the Brest Treaty opened Southern Russia to German occupation. Prussian bayonets dissolved the Rada, and Hetman Skoropadsky, by grace of the Kaiser, lorded it over the country in the name of an “independent and self-determining” people. Disaster on the Western Front and revolution in their own country compelled the Germans to withdraw, the new state of affairs giving Petlura victory over the Hetman.

===Chinese===

白天，人们同车厢保持着敬而远之的距离，我们车厢上的字样显然让人误以为里面坐的是契卡（Cheka）——全俄最令人闻风丧胆的机关。然而一到夜间，车站在半昏暗之中，我们便被大群吵闹着要求上车的人群团团围困。接纳任何人上车都违反了我们的指示纪律，这不仅是因为存在档案资料被盗的风险，更是出于对瘟疫传染的恐惧。百姓身上遍布虱子；几乎所有在乌克兰境内旅行的人都染上了“斑疹伤寒”（sipnyak），这是一种往往导致致命后果的伤寒。我们的历史学者对此怕得要命，强烈反对接纳任何外人。我们作出了折中：允许几名老妇人和残疾人坐在车厢外踏板上，并且暗中用我们“公社”的给养接济他们充饥。
我们途经各地区的百姓均处于惊惶不安的惶恐状态。在每一个车站，都有人警告我们切莫继续前行：人们言之凿凿地向我们保证，白军、强盗武装、马赫诺（Makhno）和弗兰格尔的军队就在咫尺一枪之遥。随着我们不断向南挺进，空气中弥漫着越来越浓重的令人胆寒的谣言。
南方宛如一锅沸腾的情绪大熔炉，其现实生活同北方形成了极其强烈的反差。相比之下，莫斯科和彼得格勒显得宁静而井然有序。而这里的一切都尚未定型，怪诞离奇，混乱不堪。政权的频繁更迭伴随着内战与破坏，造成了一种在全国其他地方闻所未闻的身心状态。这种动荡营造出一种充满不确定性、宛如无根浮萍、终日惶惶不可终日的气氛。在1917—1920年期间，乌克兰的部分地区经历了多达14种不同的政权更迭，每一次都对正常的社会生活造成剧烈冲击，把日常生活从其根本基石上彻底连根拔起并彻底瓦解。
整部革命与反革命的狂乱激情都在这片土地上上演得淋漓尽致。在这里，民族主义的“拉达”（Rada，议会）曾同克伦斯基政府的地方机关浴血厮杀，直到《布列斯特和约》为德军占领俄国南部敞开了大门。普鲁士的刺刀解散了拉达，盖特曼斯科罗帕茨基仰承德皇的鼻息，假借“独立自主”人民的名义在此称王称霸。西线战场的惨败以及德国本土爆发的革命迫使德军撤离，由此带来的新局势使得彼得留拉赢得了击溃盖特曼的胜利。

===Original===

Kaleidoscopically changed the governments. Dictator Petlura and his Directorium were driven out by the rebel peasantry and the Red Army, the latter in turn giving way to Denikin. Subsequently the Bolsheviki became the masters of the Ukraine, soon to be forced back by the Poles, and then again the Communists took possession.
The long-continued military and civil struggles have deranged the entire life of the South. Social classes have been destroyed, old customs and traditions abolished, cultural barriers broken down, without the people having been able to adjust themselves to the new conditions, which are in constant flux. There has been neither time nor opportunity to reconstruct one’s mental and physical mode of life, to orient oneself within the constantly changing environment.
The instincts of hunger and fear have become the sole leitmotif of thought, feeling, and action. Uncertainty is all-pervading and persistent: it is the only definite, actual reality. The question of bread, the danger of attack, are the exclusive topics of interest. You hear stories of armed forces sacking the environs of the city, and fanciful speculation about the character of the marauders, whom some claim as Whites, others as Greens,29 or pogrom bandits. The legendary figures of Makhno, Marusya, and Stchooss loom large in the atmosphere of panic created by the horrors lived through and the still more fearful apprehension of the unknown.
Alarm and dread punctuate the life and thought of the people. They permeate the entire consciousness of being. Characteristic of it, as of the general chaos of the situation, is the reply one receives on inquiring the time of day. It is indicative of the degree of the informant’s Bolshevik or opposition sentiments when one is told: “three o’clock by the old,” “five by the new,” or “six by the latest,” the Communists having recently ordered, for the third time, the “saving” of another hour of daylight.
The whole country resembles a military camp living in constant expectation of invasion, civil war, and sudden change of government, bringing with it renewed slaughter and oppression, confiscation, and famine. Industrial activity is paralyzed, the financial situation hopeless. Every regime has issued its own money, interdicting all previous forms of exchange.

===Chinese===

各届政权如同万花筒般眼花缭乱地轮番登场。独裁者彼得留拉及其督政府（Directorium）被起义农民和红军驱逐，而红军随后又败退让位于邓尼金。紧接着，布尔什维克重新成为乌克兰的主人，但不久又被波兰军队逼退，随后共产党人又卷土重来夺回了控制权。
长期的军事征伐与内部分崩离析彻底打乱了南方的整个社会生活。社会阶级被摧毁，旧的风俗传统被废除，文化壁垒被冲垮，而人民却根本来不及适应这种瞬息万变的动荡新环境。既没有时间，也没有机会去重建自己的身心生活方式，去在不断变换的环境中确立自己的人生坐标。
饥饿与恐惧的本能成了思想、情感与行动的唯一主旋律。不确定性无所不在、经久不散：这是唯一确定而无可争议的现实。面包问题、遭受袭击的危险，成了人们唯有关切的核心话题。你随时能听到武装势力洗劫城市郊外的传闻，以及关于劫掠者身份的各种离奇猜测——有人说他们是白军，有人说是绿军29（Greens），还有人说是犹太屠杀暴徒。在经历了种种骇人惨剧以及对未知危险更为深重的恐惧所构成的恐慌氛围中，马赫诺、玛鲁霞（Marusya）和休斯（Stchooss）等传奇人物的阴影显得格外庞大森严。
恐慌与惊惧主宰着百姓的生死与所思所想。它们渗透到了生命意识的每一个角落。关于这一现状以及普遍混乱局面的一个典型体现，便是向人打听时间时所得到的答复。当对方告诉你“按老时间是3点，按新时间是5点，按最新时间是6点”时，便足以表明其亲布尔什维克或持反对派立场的政治倾向程度——因为共产党人最近刚第三次下令将日照时间再拨快一小时。
整个国家宛如一座兵营，整日生活在对敌军入侵、爆发内战和政权骤变的永恒提心吊胆之中，而政权的更迭又总会带来新一轮的屠杀、压迫、没收物资和饥荒。工业活动陷于瘫痪，财政状况濒临绝境。每一个政权都发行了自己的货币，严禁使用此前的一切交易手段。

===Original===

But among the people the various “papers” are circulating, including Kerensky, Tsarist, Ukrainian, and Soviet money. Every “rouble” has its own, constantly varying value, so that the market women have to become professors of mathematics﻿—as the people jestingly say﻿—to find their way in this financial labyrinth.
Beneath the surface of the daily life man’s primitive passions, unleashed, hold almost free sway. Ethical values are dissolved, the gloss of civilization rubbed off. There remains only the unadorned instinct of self-preservation and the ever-present dread of tomorrow. The victory of the Whites or the investing of a city by them involves savage reprisals, pogroms against Jews, death for Communists, prison and torture for those suspected of sympathizing with the latter. The advent of the Bolsheviki signifies indiscriminate Red terror. Either is disastrous; it has happened many times, and the people live in perpetual fear of its repetition. Internecine strife has marched through the Ukraine like a veritable man-eater, devouring, devastating, and leaving ruin, despair, and horror in its wake. Stories of White and Red atrocities are on everybody’s lips, accounts of personal experiences harrowing in their recital of fiendish murder and rapine, of inhuman cruelty and unspeakable outrages.

===Chinese===

然而在百姓之间，各种各样的“纸币”依然在私下流通，包括克伦斯基纸币、沙皇卢布、乌克兰货币以及苏维埃纸币。每一种“卢布”都有其自身不断浮动的价值，以至于集市上的大娘大婶们不得不变成数学教授——正如人们戏谑的那样——才能在这个金融迷宫中辨清方向。
在日常生活表象之下，人类被释放出的原始兽性冲动几乎占据了绝对主导。道德准则荡然无存，文明的外衣被剥除殆尽。所剩下的唯有赤裸裸的自我求生本能以及对明日未知命运的永恒恐惧。白军的胜利或对某座城市的占领，往往意味着野蛮的报复、对犹太人的残暴屠杀、对共产党人的处决，以及对被怀疑同情后者的人施以牢狱与酷刑。而布尔什维克的到来，则意味着不分青红皂白的红色恐怖。无论哪一方胜出都是一场浩劫；这种浩劫已经上演过无数次，人们整日生活在重蹈覆辙的无休止恐惧之中。自相残杀的内战犹如一只名副其实的食人巨兽横扫乌克兰，一路吞噬、蹂躏，在其身后留下了废墟、绝望与恐怖。白军与红军的暴行传闻在每个人的嘴边流传，那些关于亲身经历的讲述充斥着令人毛骨悚然的残暴屠杀与劫掠，充斥着灭绝人性的残酷与难以言表的暴虐。

## XXII First Days in Kharkov / XXII 初到哈尔科夫
===Original===

The work of collecting material is divided among the members of our Expedition according to fitness and inclination. By general consent, and to his own great satisfaction, the only Communist among us, a very intelligent and idealistic youth, is assigned to visit Party headquarters. Besides my general duties as Chairman, my domain includes labor unions, revolutionary organizations, and semi-legal or “underground” bodies.
In the Soviet institutions, as among the people at large, an intensely nationalistic, even chauvinistic spirit is felt. To the natives the Ukraine is the only true and real Russia; its culture, language, and customs superior to those of the North. They dislike the “Russian” and resent the domination of Moscow. Antagonism to the Bolsheviki is general, the hatred of the Cheka universal. Even the Communists are incensed over the arbitrary methods of the Center, and demand greater independence and self-determination. But the policy of the Kremlin is to put its own men at the head of Ukrainian institutions, and frequently a whole trainload of Moscow Bolsheviki, including clerks and typists, are dispatched to the South to take charge of a certain department or bureau. The imported officials, unfamiliar with the conditions and psychology of the country, often even ignorant of its language, apply Moscow methods and force Moscow views upon the population with the result of alienating even the friendly disposed elements.
---
A July day, with the Southern sun steadily pouring down heat, and the stone pavement seeming to melt beneath my feet. The streets are crowded with people in variegated attire, the play of color pleasing to the eye. The Ukrainians are better clad and nourished than the people in Petrograd or Moscow. The women are strikingly beautiful, with expressive dark eyes and oval faces, olive skinned. The men are less prepossessing, often low-browed and coarse-featured, the trace of the Mongol evident. Most of the girls, comely and buxom, are short-skirted and bare-legged; others, well shod but stockingless, present an incongruous sight. Some wear *lapti*, a rough wooden sandal that clatters noisily on the pavement. Almost everyone is chewing the popular *semetchki*, dried sunflower seeds, deftly ejecting the shell, and covering the filthy sidewalks with a sheet of whitish gray.

===Chinese===

搜集史料的工作根据我们考察队成员的特长和志愿进行了分工。经大家一致同意，并令他本人大感满意的是，我们当中唯一的共产党员——一位极其聪颖且富有理想主义的年轻人——受命负责走访党的各级总部。除了作为主席的总体统筹职责外，我的工作范围包括工会、各革命组织以及半合法或“地下”团体。
在各苏维埃机构内部，正如在广大普通民众当中一样，可以深切感受到一种强烈的民族主义甚至沙文主义情绪。对于当地人来说，乌克兰才是唯一纯正而真实的俄国；其文化、语言和风俗均凌驾于北方之上。他们反感“大俄罗斯人”，并对莫斯科的统治深恶痛绝。对布尔什维克的对立情绪相当普遍，对契卡（Cheka）的仇恨更是深入人心。甚至连本地的共产党人也对“中心”（莫斯科）专断跋扈的做法大为光火，要求享有更大的自主权与自决权。然而克里姆林宫的既定方针，则是安插自己的人马执掌乌克兰各机构的领导权，往往整整一列火车的莫斯科布尔什维克——包括普通办事员和打字员——被浩浩荡荡派往南方，去接管某个部门或局处。这些空降而来的官僚不熟悉当地的实际国情与民情心理，甚至往往连乌克兰语都不懂，却生搬硬套莫斯科的一套方法，将莫斯科的观点强加给当地百姓，结果连那些原本抱有善意的阶层也彻底被推向了对立面。
---
7月的一天，南方的炎阳无情地倾泻着滚滚热浪，石板路面仿佛要在脚下融化一般。街头挤满了身着各式服装的人群，绚丽多彩的服饰颇为赏心悦目。乌克兰人的穿着和营养状况明显优于彼得格勒或莫斯科的居民。妇女们容貌惊艳动人，深色的眼眸含情脉脉，鹅蛋脸庞，肤色呈健康的小麦橄榄色。男人们的相貌则稍逊一筹，往往前额狭窄、面容粗犷，带有明显的蒙古人种特征。大部分姑娘体态丰满俊俏，穿着短裙，露出光洁的双腿；另一些人虽然脚上蹬着整齐的皮鞋，却没有穿袜子，呈现出一幅颇不协调的景象。有些人脚上穿着树皮鞋（lapti）——一种粗糙的树皮木凉鞋，走在石板路上发出哒哒的脆响。几乎所有人都在嗑着风靡当地的葵花籽瓜子（semetchki），熟练地吐出瓜子壳，把肮脏的人行道铺上了一层灰白色的碎壳。

===Original===

On the corner two boys in worn student uniforms vociferously call the attention of the passersby to hot *pirozhki*, the Russian doughy cake filled with meat or cabbage. A bevy of young girls, almost children, faces powdered, lips crimson, approach the venders.
“What costs the pleasure?” inquires one in a thin, high-pitched voice.
“Fifty roubles.”
“Oh, you little speculator,” the girl teases. “Make it cheaper for me, won’t you, dear one?” she coaxes, pressing closer to the boy.
Three sailors approach, whistling the popular Stenka Razin tune. “What beauties!” one comments, unceremoniously embracing the girl nearest him.
“Hey, lasses, come with us,” another commands. “Don’t be hanging around those speculators.”
With ribald laughter the girls join them. Arm in arm they march down the street.
“Damn those Sovietsky cavaliers,” one of the students rages. “Hot *pirozhki*, hot! Buying, who’s buying, *tovarishi*!”
With considerable difficulty I find the home of Nadya, the Left Social Revolutionist, for whom I have a message from her friends in Moscow. My knock is answered by an old lady with kindly face and snow-white hair. “My daughter is at work,” she says, suspiciously scrutinizing me. “May I know what you wish?”
Reassured by my explanation, she bids me enter, but her manner continues guarded. It requires some time before she is convinced of my good intentions, and then she begins to unburden her heart. She owned the house in which she now occupies one room together with her daughter, the rest having been requisitioned by the Soviet Housing Committee. “It is enough for our modest needs,” the old lady says resignedly, her glance passing over the small chamber containing a single bed, a kitchen table, and several wooden chairs. “I have only Nadya now,” she adds, a tremor in her voice.
“I thank God I have her,” she continues after a while. “Oh, the terrible times we have lived through. You will surely not believe it﻿—I’m not fifty yet.” She passes her delicate, thin hand over her white hair. “I don’t know how it is where you come from, but here life is a *koshmar*.30 I have grown used to hunger and cold, but the constant fear for the safety of my child makes life a torture. But it is a sin to complain,” she crosses herself devoutly. “Blessed be the Lord, for he has left me my daughter.”

===Chinese===

在街角处，两名身穿磨破的学生制服的男孩正扯着嗓子大声招揽过路人购买热馅饼（pirozhki）——一种包着肉馅或卷心菜的面点。一群年纪尚小、脸上搽着白粉、嘴唇涂得鲜红的年轻姑娘走近小贩。
“买个乐子得花多少钱呀？”其中一个姑娘用尖细的嗓门打听道。
“50卢布。”
“哟，你个小倒爷，”姑娘打趣道，“便宜点卖给我吧，好不好嘛，小帅哥？”她说着，身子往那男孩身边贴了过去。
3名水兵吹着时下流行的《斯捷潘·拉辛》（Stenka Razin）曲调晃荡过来。“好标致的妞儿！”其中一个嚷道，毫不客气地揽住了离他最近的那个姑娘。
“嘿，姑娘们，跟哥几个走，”另一个人发号施令道，“别在这帮投机倒把分子跟前转悠。”
伴着放肆的浪笑，姑娘们跟着他们走了。几个人挽着胳膊昂首阔步走过街头。
“真他妈该死，这帮苏维埃恶少军阀，”其中一名学生愤愤不平地咒骂道，“热馅饼，热腾腾的！谁买呀，同志们，谁买馅饼！”
费了九牛二虎之力，我才找到了左翼社会革命党人娜佳（Nadya）的家，我受莫斯科朋友之托给她带来了一封口信。应门的是一位面容和蔼、满头白发的老太太。“我女儿上班去了，”她狐疑地上下打量着我说道，“请问您有什么事？”
听完我的解释后，她打消了疑虑，请我进屋，但举止依然保持着戒备。过了好一阵子，她才完全相信我的善意，随后便开始向我倾诉心声。这座房子原本全属于她，如今她和女儿只占有一间屋子，其余房间全被苏维埃房管委员会征用了。“这对我们微不足道的起居来说已经足够了，”老太太无可奈何地说道，目光扫过这间狭小的屋子，里面只放着一张单人床、一张餐桌和几把木椅。“如今我只有娜佳相依为命了，”她补充道，声音微微颤抖。
“感谢上帝，我还拥有她，”过了一会儿她继续说道，“唉，我们经历的日子太可怕了。你肯定不会相信——其实我今年还不到50岁（50岁）呢。”她用纤细消瘦的手抚摸着满头白发。“我不知道你们那边是什么光景，可在这里，生活简直就是一场噩梦30（koshmar）。饥饿和寒冷我都已经习惯了，但终日为我孩子的安全提心吊胆，真把生活变成了一种残酷的煎熬。然而怨天尤人总归是罪过，”她虔诚地在胸前画了个十字，“赞美仁慈的主，他终究把女儿留给了我。”

===Original===

In the course of the conversation I learn that her eldest son was killed by Denikin men; the youngest, Volodya, a boy of twenty, was shot by the Bolsheviki. She could never find out the reason. “The terrible Cheka,” she sighs, with tears in her eyes. “But the *predsedatel*31 was a kind man,” she continues presently; “it was he who saved my little Nadya. She had also been doomed to die. Once they took her to the cellar, stark naked﻿—may, God forgive them! They forced her to the floor, face downward. Then a shot was fired over her head. Oh, the horror of it! She was told to confess and her life would be saved. But what could the poor child confess? She had nothing to tell. Indeed, she wouldn’t if she could, for Nadenka is like steel. Then she was sent back to her cell, and every night she expected to be taken out and shot, and when she heard a footstep, she would think they were coming for her. What torture the child lived through! But it was always someone else they took, and those never returned. Then one day the *predsedatel* sent for her and told her he did not want her shot, and that she was free to go home. Before that the Cheka had assured me that my daughter had been sent to Moscow for trial. And there she stood before me﻿—ah, so pale and wan, more like a ghost of herself. Glory to the Lord for His goodness,” she sobs quietly.
The door opens and a girl steps in, carrying a bag slung across her shoulders. She is young and attractive, not over twenty, with her face lit up by black, shining eyes.
She stops affrighted as her glance falls upon me. “A friend,” I hasten to reassure her, delivering the message entrusted to me in Moscow. She brightens at once, puts the bag on the table, and kisses her mother. “We’ll celebrate today, *mamenka*,” she announces; “I got my *pyock*.” She begins sorting the things, calling out cheerily, “Herrings, two pounds; half a pound of soap; one pound of vegetable butter; a quarter of a pound of tobacco. That’s from the Sobezh” (Department of Social Care), she explains, turning to me. “I am employed there, but the main ‘social care’ is given to the ration,” she says jestingly. “It’s better in quality and quantity than I receive at the other two places. You know, some of us have to hold three or even four, positions to make ends meet.

===Chinese===

在交谈中我得知，她的大儿子死于邓尼金部下之手；小儿子沃洛佳（Volodya），一个年仅20岁的小伙子，则被布尔什维克枪决了。她始终未能打听到原因。“那可怕的契卡（Cheka），”她眼中噙着泪水叹息道。“但那位主席31（predsedatel）却是个好心人，”她随后继续说道，“正是他救下了我的小娜佳。她原本也难逃一死。有一次他们把她押进地下室，剥得一丝不挂——愿上帝宽恕他们的罪过！他们强按她趴在地上，脸朝下。随后一枪从她头顶擦过。噢，那是何等可怕的恐怖经历啊！他们逼她招供，说只要招认就能保住性命。可怜的孩子能招认什么呢？她根本无话可说。而且哪怕她知道什么也绝不会招供，因为纳坚卡（Nadenka）坚强得就像钢铁一样。随后她被押回牢房，每个夜晚她都提心吊胆随时会被押出去枪毙，每当听见走廊里的脚步声，她就以为是来抓她的。这孩子承受了何等非人的折磨啊！然而被带走的总是别人，而且那些人再也没有回来过。直到有一天，主席派人传唤她，告诉她并不想枪决她，她可以自由回家了。而在此之前，契卡一直向我谎称我女儿已被解送莫斯科受审。可她就这样突然站在了我的面前——啊，面容苍白憔悴，简直就像她自己的幽灵一样。赞美仁慈的主恩赐福，”她轻声啜泣道。
房门打开了，一个姑娘跨步走进来，肩上斜挎着一个大布袋。她年轻貌美，年纪不超过20岁，一双乌黑明亮的眼睛顾盼生辉。
当目光落在我身上时，她惊恐地停住了脚步。“是一位朋友，”我连忙出言宽慰她，并转达了莫斯科朋友托付给我的口信。她的神色立刻明朗起来，把布袋搁在桌上，亲吻了母亲。“今天我们可以好好庆祝一下了，妈妈，”她宣布道，“我领到了口粮（pyock）。”她开始分拣布袋里的东西，欢快地念叨着：“鲱鱼2磅；肥皂半磅（0.5磅）；植物黄油1磅；烟草四分之一磅（0.25磅）。这是社会保障部（Sobezh）发的，”她转过头向我解释道，“我在那里工作，不过那里的‘社会保障’主要体现在这份口粮配给上，”她戏谑地笑道，“无论是质量还是分量，都比我在另外两处（2处）地方领到的好得多。您知道的，我们当中有些人为了勉强糊口，不得不兼职打三份甚至四份（3或4份）工。”

===Original===

Mother and I together receive one and three-quarter pounds of bread per day, and with this monthly *pyock* and what I get from my other positions, we manage to live. Isn’t it so, *mamenka*?” and she again embraces her mother affectionately.
“It would be sinful to complain, my child,” the old lady replies; “other people are worse off.”
Nadya has preserved her sense of humor, and her silvery laugh frequently punctuates the conversation. She is much concerned about the fate of her friends in the North, and is overjoyed to get direct news of Marusya, as she affectionately calls Maria Spiridonova. Eagerly she listens to the story of my repeated visits to the famous leader of the Left Social Revolutionists, who is now in hiding in Moscow. “I love and worship her,” she declares impetuously; “she has been the heroine of my life. And to think how the Bolsheviki hound her! Here in the South,” she continues more calmly, “our Party has been almost entirely liquidated. The persecution has forced the weaker ones to make peace with the Communists; some have even joined them. Those of us who have remained true keep ‘underground.’ The Red terror is such that activity now is out of the question. With paper, presses, and everything else nationalized, we cannot even print a handbill, as we used to do in the time of the Tsar. Besides, the workers are so cowed, their need so great, they will listen to you only if you can offer them bread. Moreover, their minds have been poisoned against the intelligentsia. The latter are actually dying of starvation. Here in Kharkov, for instance, they receive six to seven thousand roubles per month, while a pound of bread costs two to three thousand. Some wit figured out that the Soviet salary of twenty of the most noted Russian professors equals﻿—according to the present purchasing power of the rouble﻿—the amount allowed by the old regime budget for the support of the watchdog at the government institutions.”
By the aid of Nadya I am enabled to get in touch with several “irreconcilables” of the Left Social Revolutionists. The most interesting personality among them is N﻿—﻿—, a former *katorzhanin*32 and later instructor in literature in the People’s University of Kharkov. Recently he has been discharged because the political commissar, a Communist youth, considered his lectures of an anti-Marxian tendency.

===Chinese===

“我和母亲每天合领一又四分之三磅（1.75磅）面包，加上这笔每月口粮（pyock）以及我从其他兼职领到的微薄报酬，我们勉强能熬过去。是不是这样呀，妈妈？”说着她再次深情地搂住母亲。
“要是再抱怨可就有罪了，我的孩子，”老太太回答道，“别人的处境比咱们还要艰难呢。”
娜佳依然保持着幽默感，她那银铃般的笑声不时打破交谈的沉重。她十分关切北方朋友们的安危，当听到关于玛鲁霞——她对玛丽亚·斯皮里多诺娃的亲昵称呼——的直接消息时，她欣喜若狂。她如饥似渴地倾听着我多次拜访这位如今正在莫斯科隐蔽藏身的左翼社会革命党著名领袖的经过。“我热爱她，崇拜她，”她按捺不住激动地宣称道，“她是我一生的巾帼楷模。可一想到布尔什维克是如何对她赶尽杀绝的！在南方这里，”她平复了语气继续说道，“我们党几乎已经被彻底肃清取缔了。残酷的迫害迫使那些意志薄弱的人与共产党媾和；有些人甚至直接倒戈加入了他们。我们这些坚持忠诚的人则转入了‘地下’。红色恐怖已经到了如此骇人的地步，任何公开活动如今都是天方夜谭。纸张、印刷厂以及一切设备统统被收归国有，我们甚至无法像沙皇时代那样印发哪怕一张传单。此外，工人们被吓破了胆，生计又如此困顿，唯有你能给他们提供面包时，他们才肯听你讲话。而且，工人们的心智已被严重毒化，对知识分子充满了敌意。而知识分子阶层实际上正被活活饿死。就拿哈尔科夫来说吧，他们每月只能领到6000到7000卢布（6,000-7,000卢布），而一磅面包的价格就高达2000到3000卢布（2,000-3,000卢布）。有个机智之士曾算过一笔账：按照卢布眼下的实际购买力，20位全俄最著名的顶尖教授领到的苏维埃工资总和，仅仅相当于旧政权财政预算中拨给政府机关喂养看门狗的开销！”
在娜佳的引荐下，我得以同左翼社会革命党中的几位“不妥协分子”取得联系。其中最引人瞩目的人物是N——，他曾是一名苦役犯32（katorzhanin），后来在哈尔科夫人民大学担任文学讲师。最近他被解职了，起因是一名年轻共产党员政治委员认定他的讲座带有反马克思主义倾向。

===Original===

“The Bolsheviki complain that they lack teachers and educators,” N﻿—﻿— said, “but in reality they permit no one to work with them unless he be a Communist or ingratiate himself with the Communist ‘cell.’ It is the latter, the Party unit in every institution, that decides on the ‘reliability’ and fitness﻿—even of professors and teachers.”
“The Bolsheviki have failed,” he remarked to me on another occasion, “chiefly because of their total intellectual barbarism. Social life, no less than individual, is impossible without certain ethical and human values. The Bolsheviki have abolished them, and in their place we have only the arbitrary will of the Soviet bureaucracy and irresponsible terror.”
N﻿—﻿— voices the sentiments of the Left Social Revolutionary group, his views fully shared by his comrades. The rule of a minority, they agree, is necessarily a despotism based on oppression and violence. Thus 10,000 Spartans governed 300,000 Helots, while in the French Revolution 300,000 Jacobins sought to control the 7,000,000 citizens of France. Now 500,000 Communists have by the same methods enslaved the whole of Russia with its population of more than 100,000,000. Such a regime must become the negation of its original source. Though born of the Revolution, the offspring of the movement for liberation, it must deny and pervert the very ideals and aims that gave it birth. In consequence there is crying inequality of the new social groups, instead of the proclaimed equality; the stifling of every popular opinion instead of the promised freedom; violence and terror instead of the expected reign of brotherhood and love.
The present situation, N﻿—﻿— believes, is the inevitable result of Bolshevik dictatorship. The Communists have discredited the ideas and slogans of the Revolution. They have started among the people a counterrevolutionary wave which is bound to destroy the conquests of 1917. The strength of the Bolsheviki is in reality insignificant. They remain in power only because of the weakness of their political opponents and the exhaustion of the masses. “But their Ninth Thermidor33 must soon come,” N﻿—﻿— concluded with conviction, “and no one will rise to their defense.”
---
Returning late in the evening to the room assigned me in the home of G﻿—﻿—, a former bourgeois, and finding the bell out of order, I knocked long and persistently without receiving a reply.

===Chinese===

“布尔什维克整天抱怨缺少教师和教育人才，”N——说道，“可事实上，除非你是共产党员，或者讨好巴结共产党‘党支部’，否则他们根本不准任何人参与教学。正是各个机构里的基层党支部，在决定着包括教授和学者在内的所有人是否‘可靠’与胜任。”
“布尔什维克之所以归于失败，”他在另一次长谈中对我指出，“主要是由于他们在智识上的彻底野蛮行径。无论是社会生活还是个体生存，缺乏特定的伦理与人道价值都是无法维系的。布尔什维克废黜了这些价值，取而代之的只有苏维埃官僚体制的专断意志和不负责任的白色与红色恐怖。”
N——吐露了左翼社会革命党人群体的心声，他的观点得到了同仁们的广泛认同。他们一致认为，少数人的统治必然是建立在压迫与暴力基础之上的专制暴政。正因如此，当年1万名（10,000名）斯巴达人统治着30万名（300,000名）黑劳士，而在法国大革命中，30万名（300,000名）雅各宾派企图控制法国的700万名（7,000,000名）公民。如今，50万名（500,000名）共产党人正采用相同的手段，奴役着人口超过1亿（100,000,000人）的整个俄国。这样的政权必然会演变成对其最初起源的彻底背叛。它虽脱胎于革命，是解放运动的产儿，却不得不否定并曲解赋予其生命的理想与崇高宗旨。结果便是，宣告平等的诺言变成了新特权阶层赤裸裸的不平等；许诺的自由变成了对一切民间舆论的残酷窒息；期盼的博爱与仁慈变成了野蛮的暴力与恐怖。
N——坚信，眼下的局面是布尔什维克专政的必然恶果。共产党人已经玷污败坏了革命的思想与口号。他们在民间激起了一股反革命狂潮，这股暗潮势必会摧毁1917年所赢得的一切果实。布尔什维克的实际力量其实微不足道。他们之所以能维持统治，完全是由于政治对手的软弱无能以及人民大众的精疲力竭。“但他们的‘热月九日’33（Ninth Thermidor）必然很快降临，”N——笃定地下结论道，“届时绝不会有任何人挺身而出保卫他们。”
---
深夜回到分配给我的住所——一位昔日资产阶级人士G——的家中，发现门铃坏了，我执着地敲了很久的门，却始终无人应门。

===Original===

I almost despaired of gaining admittance, when there resounded the clanking of chains, a heavy bar was lifted, someone fumbled with the keys, and at last the door opened before me. I could see no one about, and a feeling of uneasiness possessed me when suddenly a tall, slender figure stepped before me, and I recognized the owner of the apartment.
“I did not see you,” I exclaimed in surprise.
“A simple precaution,” he replied, pointing to the niche between the double doors where he had evidently been hiding.
“One can’t tell these days,” he remarked nervously: “ ‘they’ have the habit of visiting us unexpectedly. I can slip through,” he added significantly.
I invited him to my room, and we talked until early morning. G﻿—﻿—’s story proved a most interesting page from the recent life of Russia. He formerly lived in Petrograd, where he was employed as a mechanical engineer in the Putilov Mills, his brother-in-law serving as his assistant. Neither of them participated in politics, all their time being devoted to their work. One morning Petrograd was stirred by the killing of Uritsky, the head of the Cheka. G﻿—﻿— and his brother-in-law had never before heard of Kannegisser, who committed the deed, yet both were arrested together with several hundred other bourgeois. His brother-in-law was shot﻿—by mistake, as the Cheka later admitted, his name resembling that of a distant relative, a former officer in the Tsar’s army. The wife of the executed, G﻿—﻿—’s sister, learning of the fate of her husband, committed suicide. G﻿—﻿— himself was released, then rearrested, and sent to forced labor in Vologda as a *bourzhooi*.
“It happened so unexpectedly,” he related, “they did not even give us time to take a few things along. It was a windy, cold day, in October, 1918. I was crossing the Nevsky on my way home from work, when all at once I realized that the whole district was surrounded by the military and Chekists. Everyone was detained. Those who could not produce a Communist membership card or a document proving themselves Soviet employees were arrested. The women also, though they were released in the morning. Unfortunately I had left my portfolio at my office, with all my papers in it. They would not listen to explanations or give me a chance to communicate with anyone. Within forty-eight hours, all the men were transported to Vologda.

===Chinese===

正当我几乎对进门绝望之际，屋内传来了铁链的铿锵声，一根沉重的门闩被抬起，有人在摸索钥匙，门终于在我面前打开。我环顾四周却看不见一个人，正感到一阵心惊胆战时，一个高挑瘦削的身影突然从门后闪出站在我面前，我认出正是这间公寓的房东。
“我刚才都没看见您，”我惊讶地叫道。
“不过是小小的戒备罢了，”他指着双层门之间的凹陷门洞回答道，他刚才显然就躲藏在里面。
“现如今谁也说不准，”他神色紧张地说道，“‘他们’总有半夜突击登门搜查的习惯。我可以从这里溜掉，”他意味深长地补充了一句。
我邀请他来到我的房间，我们一直长谈到拂晓。G——的经历堪称现代俄国生活的一页引人入胜的缩影。他此前住在彼得格勒，在普梯洛夫工厂担任机械工程师，他的内弟担任他的助手。两人均不参与任何政治，全部心力都扑在工作上。某个早晨，契卡（Cheka）负责人乌里茨基遇刺身亡的消息震动了彼得格勒。G——及其内弟此前从未听说过行刺者坎内吉瑟（Kannegisser）的大名，但两人却与其他数百名资产阶级分子一同遭到了逮捕。他的内弟被枪决了——契卡后来承认是“抓错了”，因为他的名字恰巧与沙皇军队中一名旧军官远亲相似。死者的妻子、即G——的妹妹获悉丈夫的噩耗后自杀身亡。G——本人获释后又被再次逮捕，并作为“资产阶级”（bourzhooi）被发配到沃洛格达（Vologda）服强制劳役。
“事情发生得太突兀了，”他回忆道，“他们甚至没给我们留出收拾几件随身物品的时间。那是1918年10月一个寒风凛冽的日子。我下班回家正穿过涅瓦大街，猛然间发现整个街区已被军队和契卡人员重重包围。所有过往行人都被扣押。凡是出示不了共产党员证或证明自己是苏维埃雇员公文的人，统统被当场逮捕。妇女也未能幸免，不过次日清晨她们便被释放了。倒霉的是，我把公文包忘在了办公室里，所有的证件都在里面。他们根本不听任何辩解，也不给我任何同外界联络的机会。在48小时（48小时）之内，所有的成年男子便被全数押送运往沃洛格达。”

===Original===

My family﻿—my dear wife and three children﻿—remained in complete ignorance of my fate.” G﻿—﻿— paused. “Shall we have some tea?” he asked, trying to hide his emotion.
As he continued, I learned that together with several hundred other men, almost all alleged bourgeois, G﻿—﻿— was kept in the Vologda prison for several weeks, being treated as dangerous criminals and finally ordered to the front. There they were divided into working parties of ten, on the principle of collective responsibility: should one member of the party escape, the other nine would forfeit their lives.
The prisoners had to dig trenches, build barracks for the soldiers, and lay roads. Often they were forced to expose themselves to the fire of the English, to save machine guns deserted by the Red Army during the fight. They could be kept, according to Soviet decree, only three months at the front, yet they were forced to remain till the end of the campaign. Exposed to danger, cold, and hunger, without warm clothing in the raw winter of the North, the ranks of the men thinned daily, to be filled by new parties of forced labor collected in a similar manner.
After a few months G﻿—﻿— fell ill. By the aid of a military surgeon, a drafted medical student whom he had known before, he succeeded in being returned home. But when he reached Petrograd, he failed to locate his family. All the bourgeois tenants of his house had been ejected, to make place for workers; he could find no trace of his wife and children. Laid low by fever acquired on the front, G﻿—﻿— was sent to a hospital. The physicians held out little hope of recovery, but the determination to find his family rekindled the dying embers of life, and after four weeks G﻿—﻿— left his sick bed.
He had just started his search again when he received an order mobilizing him, as an engineer, to a machine factory on the Ural. His efforts to secure delay proved fruitless. Friends promised to continue looking for his loved ones, and he departed for the East. There he applied himself conscientiously to the work, making the necessary repairs, so that the factory could presently begin operations. After a while he asked permission to return home, but he was informed that he would go as a prisoner, the political commissar having denounced him for “unfriendly disposition” toward the Bolsheviki. G﻿—﻿— was arrested and sent to Moscow.

===Chinese===

“我的家人——我亲爱的妻子和3个孩子——对我的下落一无所知。”G——停顿了一下。“咱们喝点茶好吗？”他问道，极力掩饰内心的波澜。
听他继续讲下去，我得知G——与其他数百名几乎全被指为资产阶级的人一道，被关押在沃洛格达监狱数周，被视作危险罪犯严加看管，最后被强令押往前线。在那里，他们按照连坐责任制的原则被分成10人一组的苦役队：只要队伍中有一人逃跑，其余9人便要人头落地。
这批苦役囚徒被迫挖掘战壕、为红军士兵建造营房并铺设道路。他们往往被迫顶着英国军队的密集炮火，去抢救红军在溃退战斗中遗弃的机枪。依照苏维埃法令，他们在前线服役最多只能留驻3个月，但他们却被迫一直留到战事彻底结束。在北方严酷的寒冬里，面对枪林弹雨、酷寒与饥馑，身上又缺乏御寒冬衣，队伍中的人数一天天锐减，随后又被以类似手段抓来的新一批强制苦役队伍所补充。
几个月后，G——病倒了。在一位军医——一位他过去熟识的被征入伍的医学院学生——的鼎力相助下，他终于侥幸获准遣送返乡。然而当他抵达彼得格勒时，却怎么也找不到家人的踪影。他那栋公寓楼里所有的资产阶级住户均已被驱逐腾房，以便为工人们腾出住处；关于他的妻子和孩子，他打听不到半点音讯。由于在前线染上的高烧恶疾发作，G——被送进了医院。医生们对他能否痊愈几乎不抱希望，但寻获家人的坚定信念重新点燃了他奄奄一息的生命火种，4周之后，G——便挣扎着离开了病榻。
他刚重新展开寻找，便接到了作为工程师动员前往乌拉尔一家机械厂的调令。他试图申请延期，但全属徒劳。朋友们答应替他继续寻找妻儿，他便动身踏上了前往东方的旅程。在乌拉尔，他恪尽职守投入工作，完成了必要的检修，使工厂得以迅速投入运转。不久之后他申请获准返乡，却被告知他必须作为犯人被押送回去——因为政治委员告发他对布尔什维克怀有“敌对情绪”。G——被当场逮捕并押往莫斯科。

===Original===

When he reached the capital, he found a charge of sabotage against him. He succeeded in proving the falsity of the accusation, and after four months of imprisonment he was released. But the experience so affected him that he suffered two successive attacks of “returning” typhus, from which he emerged entirely unfit for work. He secured permission to visit his relatives in Kharkov where he hoped to recuperate. There, to his great joy, quite unexpectedly, he found his family. They had long thought him dead, their inquiries and numerous letters having remained unanswered. Reunited with his wife and children, G﻿—﻿— remained in the city, having received a position in a local institution. He finds life in Kharkov much more bearable, though the Communist campaign against the intellectuals constantly rouses the people against them.
“The Bolsheviki have turned the intelligentsia into a class of hunted animals,” G﻿—﻿— said. “We are looked upon as even worse than the bourgeoisie. As a matter of fact, we are much worse off than the latter, for they usually have ‘connections’ in influential places, and most of them still possess some of the wealth they had hidden. They can speculate; yes, even grow rich, while we of the professional class have nothing. We are doomed to slow starvation.”
Snatches of song and music reached us from across the street, coming apparently from the house opposite, its windows flooded with light. “One of the Cheka commissars,” my host answered my questioning look. “By the way, a curious incident happened to me,” he continued, smiling sadly. “The other day I met that Chekist. Something about him attracted my attention﻿—a peculiar sense of the familiar that I could not account for. Suddenly it dawned on me﻿—that new dark-brown suit he wore, why, it was mine! They took it from me in the last house raid, two weeks ago. ‘For the proletariat,’ they said.”

===Chinese===

当他抵达首都时，发现自己被扣上了怠工破坏罪的罪名。他历经周折终于证明了指控的子虚乌有，在被关押了4个月之后获释出狱。然而这一连串的折磨严重摧毁了他的健康，他接连经历了两次（2次）复发性斑疹伤寒的打击，病愈后已完全无法从事体力或脑力工作。他获准前往哈尔科夫投奔亲戚并在那里疗养。在哈尔科夫，令他喜出望外的是，他竟出乎意料地与家人重逢了！家人们早以为他已不在人世，因为他们四处打探的信件均石沉大海。与妻子和孩子团聚后，G——留在了这座城市，在当地一家机构谋得了一份差事。他觉得在哈尔科夫的生活比起北方要容易忍受得多，尽管共产党发起的针对知识分子的批判运动依然不断在挑动民众与知识分子对立。
“布尔什维克已经把知识分子变成了人人喊打的丧家之犬，”G——感叹道，“在我们身上投来的目光甚至比对待资产阶级还要恶毒。事实上，我们的处境比真正的资产阶级还要凄惨得多，因为他们往往在要害权势部门拥有‘门路靠山’，而且他们大多数人家中还藏有未被抄缴的隐秘钱财。他们能够投机倒把，甚至大发横财；而我们这些专业技术人员却一无所有。我们注定只能慢慢饿死。”
街道对面隐隐飘来了歌声与琴声，显然出自对街那栋灯火通明的宅邸。“住着一位契卡（Cheka）特派员，”房东迎着我疑惑的目光苦笑解释道。“顺便提一句，我前几天经历了一件离奇的怪事，”他凄然微笑着继续说道，“那天我在街上碰见了那位契卡特派员。他身上有样东西吸引了我的注意——一种我百思不得其解的强烈熟悉感。猛然间我恍然大悟——他身上穿的那套崭新的深褐色西装，天哪，那分明就是我的西装！那是两周前（2周前）他们在上一次抄家突击检查中从我家里抄走的。当时他们说：‘为了无产阶级。’”

## XXIII In Soviet Institutions / XXIII 在苏维埃机构中
===Original===

Petrovsky, Chairman of the All-Ukrainian Central Executive Committee, the supreme government body of the South, sat at his desk busy over a pile of documents. A middle-aged man of medium stature, his typical Ukrainian face is framed in a black beard, lit up by intelligent eyes and a winning smile. A peasant-Communist appointed by Moscow to high office, he has remained democratic and simple in manner.
Learning the mission of our Expedition, Petrovsky evinced the greatest interest. “I am heartily in sympathy with it,” he said; “it’s splendid, this idea of collecting the material of our great Revolution for the information of the present and future generations. I’ll help you all I can. Here, in the Ukraine, you will find a wealth of documents, covering all the political changes we have had since 1917. Of course,” he continued, “we have not reached the well-organized and ordered condition of Russia. The development of our country has been quite different, and since 1918 we have been living in constant turmoil. It’s only two months ago that we have driven the Poles out of Kiev﻿—but we have driven them out for good,” he laughed heartily.
“Yes, we drove them out for good,” he repeated after a while. “But we must do more; we must teach the cursed Poles a lesson﻿—the Polish *pani*,34 I mean,” he corrected himself. “Our good Red Army is almost at the gates of Warsaw now. The Polish proletariat are ready to throw off the yoke of their oppressors; they are only waiting for us to give them a helping hand. We expect the Revolution to break out there any day,” he concluded in a confidential way, “and then Soviet Poland will combine federatively with Soviet Russia, as Ukraine has done.”
“Don’t you think such an aggressive policy may produce a harmful effect?” I asked. “Threatened invasion may serve to arouse patriotic ardor.”
“Pooh-pooh!” the Chairman laughed. “You evidently don’t know the revolutionary temper of the Polish workers. The whole country is on fire. The Red Army will be received ‘with bread and salt,’ as our saying is﻿—be given a hearty welcome.”
The conversation turned to the situation in the South. “The work of organizing Soviet conditions,” Petrovsky said, “is progressing very satisfactorily in the districts evacuated by the Poles.

===Chinese===

全乌克兰中央执行委员会——南方最高政权机关——主席彼得罗夫斯基正坐在办公桌前，埋头于一大堆公文之中。他是一位中等身材的中年男子，典型的乌克兰人面庞上蓄着黑色络腮胡，一双聪慧的眼睛炯炯有神，带着富有感染力的迷人微笑。作为一名被莫斯科任命为高级官员的农民出身的共产党员，他依然保持着民主平易与朴实无华的作风。
获悉我们考察队的使命后，彼得罗夫斯基表现出了极大的兴趣。“我由衷赞同这项工作，”他说，“为当下和后代搜集我们这场伟大革命的史料文献，这个设想太棒了。我一定竭尽全力提供协助。在乌克兰这里，你们将发现浩瀚丰富的文献档案，涵盖了自1917年以来我们所经历的所有政权变迁。当然，”他继续说道，“我们尚未达到俄罗斯本土那种组织严密、井然有序的状态。我们这里的发展历程截然不同，自1918年以来，我们一直生活在持续不断的动荡之中。就在两个月前（2个月前），我们才把波兰人赶出了基辅——而且是永远把他们赶跑了，”他爽朗地大笑起来。
“是的，我们永远把他们赶跑了，”过了一会儿他重复道。“但我们必须做得更多；我们必须给该死的波兰人一点教训——我是指波兰的地主老爷34（pani），”他纠正自己的措辞道。“如今我们英勇的红军几乎已经打到了华沙城下。波兰无产阶级正准备挣脱压迫者的枷锁；他们正急切等待着我们伸出援助之手。我们预计那里随时可能爆发革命，”他带着亲近信任的口吻总结道，“届时苏维埃波兰将像乌克兰一样，同苏维埃俄罗斯组成联邦联盟。”
“您难道不认为这种进攻性的政策可能会产生适得其反的负面影响吗？”我问道，“面临入侵的威胁，或许会激发起波兰人的爱国狂热。”
“呸，哪里的话！”主席大笑道，“你显然不了解波兰工人的革命性情。整个国家早已烈火燎原。正如我们俗话所说的那样，红军将受到‘面包与盐’的热烈礼遇——受到最由衷的盛大欢迎。”
话题转到了南方的局势。“苏维埃政权秩序的建设工作，”彼得罗夫斯基说，“在波兰军队撤出的各个地区进展十分顺利。”

===Original===

As to the economic situation, the Ukraine used to be the great bread-giver of Russia, but the farmers have suffered much from confiscation and robbery by the White forces. However, the peasants have learned that only under the Communists they are secure in the enjoyment of their land. It is true, many of them are *kulaki*; that is, rich farmers who resent sharing their surplus with the Red Army and the workers. They and the numerous counterrevolutionary bands make the work of the Soviet Government very difficult. Makhno, in particular, is a source of much trouble. But the Greens and other bandits are being gradually liquidated, and before long Makhno also will be eliminated. The Government has decreed merciless war against these Soviet enemies, and the peasantry is aiding in its efforts.”
“You must have surely heard in Russia about Makhno,” Petrovsky remarked, giving me a searching look. “Many legends have grown around his name, and to some he appears almost a heroic figure. But here in the Ukraine you will learn the truth about him. Just a robber ataman, that’s all he is. Under the mask of Anarchism he conducts raids upon villages and towns, destroys railroad communications, and takes a fiendish delight in murdering commissars and Communists. But before long we shall terminate his activities.”
Girl clerks kept coming in, bringing documents, and answering telephone calls. Most of them were barefoot, while some wore new, high-heeled shoes without hosiery. From time to time the Chairman interrupted the conversation to glance at the papers, putting his signature to some and referring others to the secretary. But he seemed eager to continue our talk, dwelling on the difficult problems presented by the Ukraine, the steps taken to assure greater production of coal, the reorganization of the railways, and the clearing of the labor unions of anti-Soviet influences.
He spoke unaffectedly, in the language of the workingman whose native intelligence has been sharpened by experience in the school of life. His conception of Communism is a simple matter of a strong government and determination to execute its will. It is not a question of experimentation or idealistic possibilities. His picture of a Bolshevik society has no shadows. A powerful central authority, consistently carrying out its policies, would solve all problems, he believes.

===Chinese===

“至于经济状况，乌克兰历来是全俄最主要的粮仓，但农民们饱受白军没收与劫掠之苦。然而，广大农民已经认识到，唯有在共产党人的领导下，他们才能安稳享有自己的土地。确实，他们当中许多人是富农（kulaki）；也就是说，那些不愿将多余粮食分给红军和工人的富裕农民。他们同大批反革命武装匪帮串通一气，给苏维埃政府的工作造成了极大困难。尤其是马赫诺，更是惹出了不少麻烦。但绿军以及其他匪帮正被逐步肃清，要不了多久马赫诺也难逃被消灭的下场。政府已下令对这些苏维埃的死敌展开殊死打击，农民们也在协助政府的剿匪努力。”
“你在俄罗斯本土肯定也听说过不少关于马赫诺的传闻吧，”彼得罗夫斯基若有所思地打量着我说道，“围绕着他的名字产生了许多离奇传说，在某些人眼里他甚至俨然成了一个英雄式的人物。但在这里，在乌克兰，你将认清他的真实嘴脸。他不过是个强盗匪帮阿塔曼（ataman），仅此而已。他打着无政府主义的幌子，四处打劫村庄与城镇，破坏铁路交通，并以残暴虐杀政委和共产党员为乐。但要不了多久，我们就会彻底终结他的活动。”
女办事员们进进出出，递送公文并接听电话。她们大多赤着双脚，少数几人虽然脚蹬崭新的高跟鞋，却没有穿袜子。主席不时停下交谈翻阅公文，在部分文件上签字，将其余文件批转给秘书。但他似乎很乐意继续同我们长谈，详尽探讨了乌克兰所面临的棘手难题、为确保提高煤炭产量而采取的各项举措、铁路的重组改造，以及工会内部清除反苏倾向影响的工作。
他谈吐自然平易，用的是普通劳动大众的朴素语言，其天生的聪慧经过了社会大熔炉的历练而愈发干练。在他心目中，共产主义被理解为纯粹建立一个强有力政权并坚决贯彻其意志的务实事业。这根本不是什么理论实验或理想主义探讨。在他勾勒的布尔什维克社会画卷中没有任何阴暗面。他坚信，只要有一个强有力的中央政权坚定不移地贯彻其方针，所有问题都将迎刃而解。

===Original===

Opposition must be eliminated; disturbing elements and inciters of the peasantry against the Soviet regime, such as Makhno, crushed. At the same time the work of the *polit-prosvet* (political education) should be broadened; the youth, especially, must be trained to regard the Bolsheviki as the revolutionary advance guard of humanity. On the whole, Communism is a problem of right bookkeeping, as Lenin had truly said; of taking an invoice of the country’s wealth, actual and potential, and arranging for its equalized distribution.
The subject of peasant dissatisfaction kept returning in our conversation. The *povstantsi* (armed rebel peasantry), Petrovsky admitted, had played a vital part in the Revolution. They repeatedly saved the Ukraine, and even Russia, at most critical moments. By guerrilla warfare they disorganized and demoralized the Austro-German forces, and prevented their marching on Moscow and suppressing the Soviet regime. They defeated the Interventionist attack in the South, by resisting and routing the French and Italian divisions that were landed by the Allies in Odessa with the intention of supporting the nationalistic Directorium in Kiev. They fought Denikin and other White generals, and were largely instrumental in making the victories of the Red Army possible. But some *povstantsi* elements have now joined the Green and other bands operating against the Communists. They also comprise the greater part of the Makhno forces, possessing even machine guns and artillery. Makhno is particularly dangerous. At one time he had served in the Red Army; but he mutinied, opening the front to Denikin, for which treachery he was outlawed by Trotsky. Since then Makhno has been fighting against the Bolsheviki and helping the enemies of the Revolution.
From the adjoining office, occupied by Petrovsky’s secretary, loud talking and a woman’s hysterical voice kept disturbing our conversation. “What is going on there, I wonder,” the Chairman exclaimed at last, stepping to the door. As he opened it, a young peasant woman rushed toward him, throwing herself at his feet.
“Save us, Little Father!” she cried. “Have mercy!”
Petrovsky helped her up. “What is the matter?” he asked kindly.
Amid sobs she related that her husband, home on furlough from the Army, had gone to Kharkov to visit his sick mother. There he was arrested in a street raid as a labor deserter.

===Chinese===

反对派必须予以肃清；挑起事端并煽动农民对抗苏维埃政权的敌对分子——诸如马赫诺之流——必须予以彻底粉碎。与此同时，必须大力拓展政治教育（polit-prosvet）工作；尤其是必须教育青年将布尔什维克视为全人类革命的先锋队。正如列宁精辟指出的那样，总的来看，共产主义本质上就是一个搞好簿记核算的问题：清查盘点全俄现存与潜在的一切财富，并安排合理的平均分配。
关于农民不满的话题在我们的交谈中被反复提及。彼得罗夫斯基坦承，起义游击队（povstantsi，即武装起义农民）在革命中曾发挥过至关重要的中流砥柱作用。在好几个最危急的生死关头，他们屡次拯救了乌克兰乃至整个俄罗斯。他们通过游击战扰乱并瓦解了奥德军队的士气，阻止了德奥联军进军莫斯科绞杀苏维埃政权的企图。他们挫败了协约国在南方的武装干涉，英勇抵抗并击溃了由协约国在敖德萨登陆、企图策应基辅民族主义督政府的法意干涉军。他们同邓尼金以及其他白军将领展开浴血奋战，在很大程度上为红军赢得全面胜利立下了汗马功劳。然而，部分起义游击队成分如今却倒戈加入了对抗共产党的绿军及其他匪帮武装。他们也是马赫诺军队的主力骨干，甚至配备了机枪和大炮。马赫诺尤其危险。他曾一度在红军中服役；随后发动叛乱，向邓尼金大开阵线门户，托洛茨基因此下令将他定为叛徒通缉没收。自那以后，马赫诺便一直与布尔什维克为敌，充当着革命死敌的帮凶。
从隔壁彼得罗夫斯基秘书的办公室里，大声喧嚷和一个女人歇斯底里的哭喊声不时打断我们的交谈。“那边到底在吵吵什么，”主席终于忍不住站起身走向门口。门一推开，一名年轻农妇便猛扑上前，噗通跪倒在他的脚下。
“救救我们吧，好官老爷！”她哭喊道，“行行好吧！”
彼得罗夫斯基扶她起身。“到底怎么回事？”他温和地询问道。
在抽泣中她诉说道，她的丈夫休假探亲从部队回家，特地赶往哈尔科夫探望患病的母亲。结果在街头的一次突击检查中，他被当作劳役逃避者当场逮捕了。

===Original===

He could not prove his identity, because he had been robbed on his way to the city; all his documents and money were gone. He sent word to her about his misfortune; but by the time she reached the city she learned that her husband had been taken away with a party of other prisoners. Since then she failed to find out anything more about him. “Oh, Little Father, they’ve sure shot him,” she wailed, “and he a Red Army man who fought Denikin.”
Petrovsky sought to calm the distracted woman. “Nothing will happen to your husband,” he assured her, “if he can prove himself a soldier.”
