His men were mad with blood lust when there was nothing more to rob. You know, the soldiers and peasants think there is gold to be found in every Jewish home. This was once a prosperous city, but the rich men that did business with us lived in Kiev and Kharkov. The Jews here were just making a living, with a few of them comfortably off. Well, the many pogroms long ago robbed them of all they had, ruined their business, and despoiled their homes. Still, they lived somehow. You know how it is with the Jew﻿—he is used to mistreatment, he tries to make the best of it. But Denikin’s soldiers﻿—oh, it was Gehenna let loose.

===Chinese===

我们这儿有很多条这样的街道。”
过了一会儿，他继续说道：“在这栋绿屋顶的房子里，全家老小被斩草除根——整整9口人。凶手们还纵火焚烧了屋子——透过破碎的房门你就能看到——屋里全被烧毁焦黑。是谁干的？”他带着绝望的语气重复了我的提问。“不如问问谁没干过？先是彼得留拉（Petlura）的人来了，接着是邓尼金（Denikin），然后是波兰人，还有各路乌合之众的匪帮；愿黑色的岁月诅咒他们。匪徒一拨接一拨，带来的始终是同样的灾难。每一次小镇易手，我们都要遭受他们所有人的蹂躏。但邓尼金的人最凶残恶毒，甚至比极端仇视我们的波兰人还要残暴。邓尼金匪徒上一次在这里盘踞时，大屠杀整整持续了4天。哦，上帝啊！”
他突然停住脚步，举起双手。“哦，你们这些生活在安宁环境中的美利坚人，你们可知道整整4天意味着什么！整整4个漫长恐怖的白天，以及更加可怕的黑夜，整整4天4夜的大屠杀，杀戮没有片刻停歇。哭喊声、尖叫声，那是母亲眼睁睁看着自己的婴儿被活生生撕成碎片的凄厉惨叫。……我现在仿佛还能听见。……这让我的血液因恐惧而凝固。……这要把我逼疯了。……那些惨象。……那一团血肉模糊的残躯，曾经是我自己的骨肉，我可爱的米蕾尔（Mirele）。……她当时只有5岁啊。”他彻底崩溃了。他靠在墙上，浑身因抽泣而剧烈颤抖。
不久他平复了情绪。“这里就是大屠杀最惨烈区域的中心，”他继续说道。“请原谅我的软弱；我无法止住泪水来诉说这一切。……那就是犹太会堂。我们犹太人曾试图在里面避难。是指挥官让我们进去的。他的名字？愿灾难远离我，就像那个阴暗的名字一样。他是邓尼金手下的一名将军；指挥官，大家都这么称呼他。当再也抢不到东西时，他的手下便被嗜血的狂热冲昏了头脑。你知道，士兵和农民总以为每个犹太人家里都藏有黄金。这里曾经是一座繁华富庶的城镇，但与我们做生意的富商大多住在基辅和哈尔科夫。这里的犹太人仅仅勉强维持生计，只有少数人算得上家境殷实。唉，屡次的大屠杀早就抢光了他们所有的家当，毁掉了他们的营生，洗劫了他们的家园。尽管如此，他们总还能勉强过活。你知道犹太人是怎样熬过来的——他习惯了遭受虐待，总是尽力逆来顺受。但邓尼金的士兵——哦，那简直是地狱恶魔出笼。

===Original===

They went wild when they found nothing to take, and they destroyed what they didn’t want. That was the first two days. But with the third began the killing, mostly with swords and bayonets. On the third day the Commander ordered us to take refuge in the synagogue. He promised us safety, and we brought our wives and children there. They put a guard at the door, to protect us, the Commander said. It was a trap. At night the soldiers came; all the hooligans of the town were with them, too. They came and demanded our gold. They would not believe we had none. They searched the Holy Scrolls, they tore them and trampled upon them. Some of us could not look quietly on that awful desecration. We protested. And then began the butchery. The horror, oh, the horror of it.﻿ ﻿… The women beaten, assaulted, the men cut down with sabers.﻿ ﻿… Some of us broke through the guard at the door, and we ran into the streets. Like hounds of hell, they followed us, slashing, killing, and hunting us from house to house. For days afterwards the streets were littered with the killed and maimed. They would not let us approach our dead. They would not permit us to bury them or to help the wounded who were groaning in their misery, begging for death.﻿ ﻿… Not a glass of water could we give them.﻿ ﻿… They shot anyone coming near.﻿ ﻿… The famished dogs of the whole neighborhood came; they smelt prey. I saw them tear off limbs from the dead, from the helpless wounded.﻿ ﻿… They fed on the living﻿ ﻿… on our brothers.﻿ ﻿…”
He broke down again. “The dogs fed on them﻿ ﻿… fed on them﻿ ﻿…” he repeats amid sobs.
Someone approaches us. It is the doctor who had ministered to the sick and wounded after the last pogrom was over. He looks the typical Russian of the intelligentsia, the stamp of the idealist and student engraved upon him. He walks with a heavy limp, and his quick eye catches my unvoiced question. “A memento of those days,” he says, attempting a smile. “It troubles me a good deal and handicaps my work considerably,” he adds. “There are many sick people and I am on my feet all day. There are no conveyances﻿—they took away all the horses and cattle. I am just on my way now to poor Fanya, one of my hopeless patients. No, no, good man, it’s no use your visiting her,” he waives aside my request to accompany him. “It is like many others here; a terrible but common case.

===Chinese===

当他们发现找不到什么可抢时，就发了疯，把不想要的东西统统捣毁。那是头2天的情况。但到了第3天，杀戮开始了，主要使用的是军刀和刺刀。在第3天，指挥官命令我们去犹太会堂避难。他向我们保证安全，我们就把妻儿都带到了那里。他们派了一队卫兵守在门口，指挥官声称是为了保护我们。那其实是一个险恶的圈套。到了夜里，士兵们冲了进来；城里所有的暴徒无赖也跟他们一起蜂拥而入。他们闯进来索要黄金。他们根本不相信我们身无分文。他们搜查圣卷，将其撕毁并在脚下践踏。我们中有些人无法平静目睹这等令人发指的亵渎行径。我们提出了抗议。紧接着屠杀便爆发了。那恐怖的景象，哦，简直骇人听闻。……妇女惨遭殴打、蹂躏，男人们被军刀砍翻在地。……我们中有些人冲破了门口卫兵的阻拦，逃到了大街上。他们像地狱恶犬一般尾随其后，一路挥刀劈砍、追杀，挨家挨户搜捕我们。接下来的好几天里，街头巷尾横七竖八躺满了死伤者。他们不许我们靠近死难者。他们不许我们掩埋尸体，也不许我们救助那些在痛苦中呻吟求死的伤者。……我们连一杯水都无法递给他们。……任何人只要敢靠近，他们就开枪射杀。……附近整片地区的饥民饿犬都闻风而至，嗅到了猎物的气息。我亲眼看着它们从死者和无助的伤者身上撕扯肢体。……它们啃食活人……啃食我们的骨肉兄弟。……”
他再次失声痛哭。“野狗啃食着他们……啃食着他们……”他在啜泣声中反复念叨着。
有人朝我们走来。这是一位在大屠杀平息后一直救治伤病员的医生。他看起来是典型的俄罗斯知识分子形象，身上深刻着理想主义者和学者的印记。他走路时一瘸一拐，敏锐的目光捕捉到了我未说出口的疑问。“那是那些日子留下的纪念，”他试图挤出一丝微笑说道。“这给我带来了很大折磨，对我的救治工作造成了严重妨碍，”他补充道。“这里有太多的病人，我整天都在奔波。没有任何代步工具——他们把所有的马匹和牲口都抢光了。我现在正要赶往可怜的凡尼娅（Fanya）家，她是我那些毫无希望康复的病人之一。不，不，好心人，你去了也没用，”他摆摆手拒绝了我陪同前往的请求。“这就像这里的许多其他遭遇一样；是一个可怕却司空见惯的惨剧。

===Original===

She was a nurse, taking care of a paralytic young girl. They occupied a room on the second floor of a house nearby. On the first floor soldiers were quartered. When the pogrom began the soldiers kept the paralytic and her nurse prisoners. What happened there no one will ever know﻿ ﻿… When the soldiers had at last gone we had to use a ladder to get to the girls’ room. The brutes had covered the stairs with human excrement﻿—it was impossible to approach. When we got to the two girls, the paralytic was dead in the arms of the nurse, and the latter a raving maniac. No, no; it’s no use your seeing her.”
“Doctor,” says Reb Moishe, “why don’t you tell our American friend how you got crippled? He should hear everything.”
“Oh, that is not important, Reb Moishe. We have so many worse things.” Upon my insisting, he continues: “Well, it is not a long story. I was shot as I approached a wounded man lying in the street. It was dark, and as I was passing by I heard someone moan. I had just stepped off the sidewalk when I was shot. It was the night of the synagogue pogrom. But my mishap, man﻿—it’s nothing when you think of the nightmare in the warehouse.”
“The warehouse?” I asked. “What happened there?”
“The worst you can imagine,” the doctor replies. “Those scenes no human power can describe. It wasn’t murder there﻿—only a few were killed in the warehouse. It was the women, the girls, even children﻿ ﻿… When the soldiers pogromed the synagogue, many of the women succeeded in gaining the street. As if by some instinct they collected afterwards in the warehouse﻿—a big outhouse that had not been used for many years. Where else were the women to go? It was too dangerous at home; the mob was searching for the men who had escaped from the synagogue and was slaughtering them on the street, in their homes, wherever found. So the women and girls gathered in the warehouse. It was late at night and the place was dark and still. They feared to breathe, almost, lest their hiding-place be discovered by the hooligans. During the night more of the women folk and some of their men also found their way to the warehouse. There they all lay, huddled on the floor in dead silence. The cries and shrieks from the street reached them, but they were helpless and every moment they feared discovery. How it happened we don’t know, but some soldiers did find them.

===Chinese===

她曾是一名护士，照料着一个瘫痪的年轻女孩。她们住在附近一栋房子的二楼。一楼驻扎着士兵。大屠杀开始时，士兵们将那个瘫痪女孩和护士软禁囚禁起来。那里究竟发生了什么，永远不会有人知晓……当士兵们终于撤走后，我们不得不架起梯子才爬进姑娘们的房间。那些畜生在楼梯上涂满了人类粪便——根本无法接近。当我们来到两个女孩身边时，瘫痪女孩已经死在护士怀中，而护士则彻底成了胡言乱语的疯子。不，不；你去见她也无济于事。”
“大夫，”莫伊舍老爹说，“你为什么不告诉这位美洲朋友你自己是怎么致残的呢？他应该把一切都听一听。”
“哦，那并不重要，莫伊舍老爹。我们这里还有多得多的更悲惨的事情呢。”在我的一再坚持下，他继续说道：“好吧，这并不是一个冗长的故事。当时我正走向倒在街头的一名伤者，结果中弹了。当时天很黑，我走过时听到有人在痛苦呻吟。我刚迈下人行道，子弹就打过来了。那是犹太会堂大屠杀的那天晚上。不过比起仓库里的那场噩梦，我这点遭遇根本算不了什么。”
“仓库？”我问道，“那里发生了什么？”
“你能想象得到的最可怕的事，”医生回答道。“那些惨绝人寰的场景没有任何人类语言所能描绘。那里发生的不仅仅是屠杀——在仓库里被当场杀害的人其实只有少数几个。受难的是妇女、姑娘，甚至是幼童……当士兵们血洗犹太会堂时，许多妇女侥幸逃到了街上。出于某种本能，她们事后聚集在仓库里——那是一座荒废多年的大屋外附设库房。女人们还能逃到哪里去呢？留在家里太危险了；暴徒们正在搜捕从会堂逃跑的男子，只要在大街上或家中被搜到，就当场屠杀。因此妇女和姑娘们都躲进了仓库。当时夜色已深，那里又黑又静。她们吓得几乎不敢呼吸，生怕藏身之处被流氓匪徒发现。夜间，又有更多的女眷和一些男性也摸索着找到了仓库。他们全都挤作一团，在死寂中蜷缩在地板上。街头传来的阵阵哭喊与尖叫传入耳中，但他们无能为力，每一分每一秒都在恐惧被人发现。不知怎么搞的，还是有几名士兵发现了他们。

===Original===

There was no pogrom there, in the ordinary sense. There was worse. The Commander himself gave orders that a cordon of soldiers be stationed at the warehouse, that no pogrom was to be made, and that no one be permitted to leave without his permission. At first we did not understand the meaning of it, but the terrible truth soon dawned on us. On the second night several officers arrived, accompanied by a strong detachment, all mounted and carrying lanterns. By their light they peered into the faces of the women. They selected five of the most beautiful girls, dragged them out and rode away with them. They came again and again that night﻿ ﻿… They came every night, always with their lanterns. First the youngest were taken, girls of fifteen and twelve, even as young as eight. Then they took the older ones and the married women. Only the very old were left. There were over four hundred women and girls in the warehouse, and most of them were taken away. Some of them never returned alive; many were later found dead on the roads. Others were abandoned along the route of the withdrawing army﻿ ﻿… they returned days, weeks later﻿ ﻿… sick, tortured, everyone of them infected with terrible diseases.”
The doctor pauses, then takes me aside. “Can an outsider realize the whole depth of our misfortune?” he asks. “How many pogroms we have suffered! The last one, by Denikin, continued eight days. Think of it, eight days! Over ten thousand of our people were slaughtered; three thousand died from exposure and wounds.” Glancing toward Reb Moishe, he adds in a hoarse whisper: “There is not a woman or girl above the age of ten in our city who has not been outraged. Some of them four, five, as high as fourteen times.﻿ ﻿… You said you were about to go to Kiev. In the City Hospital there you will find seven children, girls under thirteen, that we succeeded in placing there for medical treatment, mostly surgical. Everyone of those girls has been outraged six and more times. Tell America about it﻿—will it still remain silent?”

===Chinese===

那里并没有发生通常意义上的大屠杀。发生的事情比那还要恶劣千万倍。指挥官亲自下令派一队士兵把守仓库，严禁进行劫掠屠杀，任何人未经他的许可不得擅自离开。起初我们还不明白这道命令的用意，但残酷可怕的真相很快便大白于天下。第二天夜里，几名军官在重兵护卫下赶来，全都骑着高头大马，手提马灯。借着灯光，他们仔细端详女人们的面孔。他们挑选了5名最漂亮的姑娘，强行拖走，策马而去。那晚他们一次又一次折返回来搜掠……他们每天夜里都来，每次都提着马灯。起初被掳走的是最年幼的，15岁和12岁的少女，甚至小到只有8岁的幼女。接着他们又掳走了年纪稍长者和已婚妇女。最后只剩下了老态龙钟的妇人。仓库里原本躲避着400多名妇女和女孩，其中大多数都被强行掳走了。她们中有些人再也没能活着回来；许多人后来被发现陈尸路边。还有些人被遗弃在撤退军队的沿途路旁……几天、几周后她们才辗转归来……身患重病，饱受摧残折磨，每一个人都染上了可怕的恶疾。”
医生停顿了一下，随后把我拉到一旁。“一个局外人能够体会我们遭遇的苦难有多深重吗？”他问道。“我们经历了多少次大屠杀啊！邓尼金制造的最近一次屠杀整整持续了8天。想想看吧，8天啊！我们的小镇有10000多名同胞惨遭杀戮；3000人死于受冻暴晒与伤势。”他瞥了一眼莫伊舍老爹，压低沙哑的嗓音补充道：“在我们城里，凡年满10岁以上的妇女或女孩，没有一个逃脱过被奸污蹂躏的厄运。她们中有的被蹂躏4次、5次，多达14次之多。……你说你正准备去基辅。在那里的市立医院，你会看到7名未满13周岁的女童，那是我们好不容易送进去接受医疗救治的，大多需要外科手术。那些女孩中的每一个人都被蹂躏过6次以上。把这些告诉美洲吧——难道美洲还会继续保持沉默吗？”

## XXIX Kiev / XXIX 基辅
===Original===

The Krestchatik, Kiev’s main thoroughfare, pulsates with intense life. Straight as an arrow it lies before me, a magnificent broad avenue stretching far into the distance and finally disappearing in the superb Kupetchesky Park, formerly the pride of the city. Ancient, the storms of time and human strife defying, Kiev stands picturesquely beautiful, a radiant mosaic of iridescent foliage, golden cathedrals and monasteries of exotic architecture, and green-clad mountains towering on the banks of the Dnieper flowing majestically below.
Recent days revived the bloody scenes the old city had witnessed in the centuries past, when Mongol and Tartar, Cossack, Pole, and fierce native tribes had fought for its possession. But more sanguinary and ferocious have been the struggles of yesterday. Foreign armies of occupation, German, Magyar, and Austrian, native *gaidamaki*, Poles, Russians﻿—each turned the ancient city into a shamble. Skoropadsky, Petlura, Denikin, like the savage atamans of Gogol’s tales, have vied with each other in filling the streams that crimson the Dnieper in these the darkest days of Russia.
Incredible vitality of man! Exasperating, yet blessed brevity of human memory! Today the city looks bright and peaceful﻿—forgotten is the slaughter, forgotten the sacrifices of yesterday.
The streets, full of movement and color, contrast strikingly with the sickly exhaustion of northern cities. Stores and restaurants are open, and the bakeries display appetizing *pirozhniye*, the sweets so dear to the Russian heart. Most of the business signs are still in their accustomed places, some in Russian, others in the Ukrainian language, the latter predominating since the famous decree of Skoropadsky when overnight all shingles had to be “Ukrainianized.” The boulevards are alive with people, the women larger and less beautiful than in Kharkov, the men stolid, heavy, unprepossessing.
It is a month since the Poles left the city: the Bolsheviki have not yet had time to establish completely their regime. But the reports of Polish destruction, so industriously circulated in Moscow, prove baseless. Little damage has been done by the enemy, except the burning of some railroad bridges on the outskirts of the city. The famous Sofiysky Cathedral and the Michailovsky Monastery are undisturbed in their imposing splendor.

===Chinese===

基辅（Kiev）的主要干道克列夏季克大街（Krestchatik）洋溢着勃勃生机。它笔直如箭般横亘在我眼前，一条宏伟宽阔的大道延伸向远方，最终隐入壮丽的库佩切斯基公园（Kupetchesky Park）之中——那里曾是整座城市的骄傲。古老而桀骜地傲视着岁月风霜与人间兵燹的基辅，宛如一幅风景如画的绝美图卷：斑斓绚丽的林木枝叶、金碧辉煌的大教堂、建筑风格奇特的古老修道院，以及脚下滔滔流淌的巍峨第聂伯河畔巍然耸立的苍翠青山，交相辉映成一幅璀璨的光影马赛克。
近些日子的战事，再度重现了这座古城在过去几个世纪里屡屡见证的血腥杀戮惨状：当年蒙古人与鞑靼人、哥萨克人、波兰人以及凶悍的本土部族曾为争夺它而殊死搏杀。然而，昨日的鏖战却比以往任何时候都要更为血腥残酷。外国占领军——德国人、匈牙利人和奥地利人，本土的盖达马克武装（gaidamaki）、波兰人、俄罗斯人——每一方都曾将这座千年古城沦为一座血腥的屠宰场。斯科罗帕茨基（Skoropadsky）、彼得留拉（Petlura）、邓尼金（Denikin），宛如果戈理小说中残暴凶悍的哥萨克头领（ataman），在俄罗斯这片最黑暗的岁月里争相竞逐，将滚滚第聂伯河的流水染得一片殷红。
人类的生命力何其顽强！人类健忘的记性令人扼腕，却又何其是一种莫大的恩赐！如今，这座城市显得明媚宁静——昨日的惨烈杀戮已被遗忘，昨日的惨重牺牲也已被抛之脑后。
大街小巷人流涌动、色彩斑斓，与北方城市那病态的衰竭枯槁形成了鲜明刺眼的对照。店铺与餐馆开门迎客，面包店的橱窗里摆放着诱人的皮罗什基（pirozhniye）点心——那是深受俄罗斯人钟爱的传统甜食。大多数商号招牌依旧悬挂在惯常的老位置上，有的使用俄语，有的使用乌克兰语（Ukraine），自斯科罗帕茨基发布那道著名的命令要求一夜之间所有招牌必须“乌克兰化”以来，后者已占据主导地位。林荫大道上挤满了行人，这里的妇女比起哈尔科夫（Kharkov）来身形更为丰硕，容貌却逊色三分，男人们则显得迟钝、厚重、其貌不扬。
波兰人撤离这座城市已经整整1个月了；布尔什维克尚未有充裕的时间彻底建立稳固的政权秩序。然而，在莫斯科（Moscow）广为散播的关于波兰人大肆破坏的报道，如今被证实纯属捕风捉影。除了在城郊焚毁了几座铁路桥梁之外，敌人造成的破坏微乎其微。著名的索菲亚大教堂（Sofiysky Cathedral）与米哈伊洛夫斯基修道院（Michailovsky Monastery）完好无损，依然展现着庄严雄伟的光彩。

===Original===

Without reason did Chicherin protest to the world against “the unheard-of vandalism” toward those gems of old architecture.
The Soviet institutions present the familiar picture of Moscow pattern: gatherings of worn, tired people, looking hungry and apathetic. Typical and sad. The corridors and offices are crowded with applicants seeking permission to do or to be exempt from doing this or that. The labyrinth of new decrees is so intricate, the officials prefer the easier way of solving perplexing problems by “revolutionary method,” on their “conscience,” generally to the dissatisfaction of the petitioners.
Long lines are everywhere, and much writing and handling of “papers” and documents by Sovietsky *barishni* (young ladies), in high-heeled shoes, that swarm in every office. They puff at cigarettes and animatedly discuss the advantages of certain bureaus as measured by the quantity of the *pyock* issued, the symbol of Soviet existence. Workers and peasants, their heads bared, approach the long tables. Respectfully, even servilely, they seek information, plead for an “order” for clothing, or a “ticket” for boots. “I don’t know,” “In the next office,” “Come tomorrow,” is the usual reply. There are protests and lamentations, and begging for attention and advice. Occasionally someone in the line, after days of fruitless effort, loses his temper, and a string of true Russian curses fills the room, rising above the noise and the smoke. But when the commissar hastily enters, belated from the conference of the Party Committee, the hubbub subsides, and the *barishni* appear busy at their tasks. He has a worn and worried look: his desk is piled with papers awaiting his attention, and within an hour he is expected at another session. Lucky applicant that gets a hearing; happy if action is taken on his case.
The industries are at low ebb, mainly because of the lack of raw material and coal. The decree militarizing labor is being applied with great severity; the toilers in the shops and factories are rigidly bound to their places of employment. But the machinery has been neglected, most of it is out of repair, and there is a scarcity of artisans capable of putting it in order. The men are at their posts, pretending to work, but in reality idling or engaged in the stealthy manufacture of cigarette lighters, keys, locks, and other objects for personal use or private sale.

===Chinese===

契切林（Chicherin）此前向全世界控诉波兰人对这些古代建筑瑰宝犯下“闻所未闻的野蛮暴行”，纯属毫无事实依据的虚妄之词。
各个苏维埃机关呈现出莫斯科模式的熟悉图景：聚集着大批面容憔悴、疲惫不堪的人群，看起来饥肠辘辘、麻木不仁。这是何等典型却又令人伤感的一幕。走廊与办公室里挤满了前来申办许可、或是请求免除某项义务的请愿者。新法令的迷宫错综复杂，以至于官员们更乐于采取省事的办法，凭所谓“革命手段”和个人的“良心”来裁决棘手难题，而这往往令申诉人愤懑不满。
长队随处可见，成群结队的高跟鞋苏维埃小姐（Sovietsky barishni）在每间办公室里穿梭往来，忙着起草、翻看各种“公文”与证明文件。她们一边吞云吐雾抽着香烟，一边兴高采烈地讨论着某些部门的优厚待遇——这是以发放的学术口粮（pyock）数量多寡来衡量的，而这正是苏维埃体制下生存状态的缩影。工人和农民脱下帽子，恭敬乃至奴颜婢膝地走到长桌前。他们打听情况，哀求批一张买衣服的“批条”或是领靴子的“凭券”。“我不知道”、“去隔壁办公室”、“明天再来”，通常得到的都是这副冷冰冰的回答。四周充斥着抗议、哀怨与恳求关注指点的声音。偶尔排队的人在苦候数日无果后终于忍无可忍，一连串地道的俄式国骂顿时响彻室内，盖过了喧嚣与烟雾。然而，当政委因参加党委会开会迟到而匆匆步入办公室时，喧嚣声顿时平息下去，小姐们立刻装出埋头苦干的繁忙模样。他面带倦容与焦虑：办公桌上堆积着一大摞亟待批阅的文件，而1小时内他还必须赶往另一场会议。能够得到接见的请愿者实属万幸；要是案子能得到哪怕一点处理，就更是喜出望外了。
工业生产处于极度低迷的谷底，主要是由于缺乏原材料和煤炭。将劳动力军事化的严苛法令正在雷厉风行地贯彻执行；工场和工厂里的劳动者被死死捆绑在各自的工作岗位上。然而机器设备年久失修，大部分早已破损毁坏，而且极度匮乏能够维修机器的熟练技工。工人们虽然坚守岗位，却在假装工作，实际上不是无所事事地摸鱼游荡，就是偷偷摸摸私造打火机、钥匙、挂锁以及其他供个人使用或私下倒卖的小物件。

===Original===

Many of the factories are entirely closed; others are operating with a minimum output. The sugar refineries, the most important industry of the Southeast, are working at a great deficit. Because of the total devaluation of Soviet money the State is compelled to pay its employees with products, chiefly with sugar from the old reserves. In search of documents for the Museum, I gather official statistics showing that to produce one pood (about 40 lbs.) of sugar, the government expends thirty-five, often even fifty-five pounds of the old sugar. The officials realize the extreme seriousness of the situation, but feel themselves helpless. Some skeptically, others with characteristic racial fatality, trudge on in the treadmill.
In the civil and military departments there is feverish activity, but sadly unorganized. Almost every branch is working independently, without relation to other Soviet institutions, frequently in entire ignorance and even in opposition to the policies and measures of the other executive bodies. Curious incidents result. Thus the Chairman of the All-Ukrainian Executive Committee has sent a telegraphic request to all Soviet institutions to aid the work of our Expedition, while the Secretary of the Party has at the same time issued an order against us, condemning our Mission as an attempt to deprive Ukraine of its historic documents, and threatening to confiscate the material we have collected.
The Soviet of Labor Unions occupies the huge building on the Krestchatik which was formerly the Hotel Savoy. In 1918 and 1919 that body played a most important role, its work covering the whole field of proletarian interests and its authority based on the expressed will of the industrial masses. But gradually the Soviet has been deprived of power, the government taking over its essential functions, and turning the unions into executive and administrative branches of the State machinery. The elective principle has been abolished and replaced by Communist appointment.
The labor headquarters are in great confusion. As in Kharkov, the entire Soviet and most of the managing boards of the locals have been recently “liquidated” as Menshevik or unsympathetic to the Communists, and new officials appointed by Moscow. The same atmosphere of suppressed nervousness is sensed in the unions as in the other government institutions.

===Chinese===

许多工厂已经彻底关门停产；其他工厂也仅以最低产量勉强运转。作为东南部支柱产业的制糖厂正面临严重的巨额赤字。由于苏维埃纸币彻底贬值如废纸，国家不得不以实物支付雇员薪酬，主要是从过去的旧储备中拨付食糖。在为博物馆搜集历史文献的过程中，我汇总了多份官方统计数据，上面显示：为了提炼生产1普特（约合40磅）食糖，政府往往要消耗35磅甚至55磅陈糖库存。官员们深知局势极端严峻，却感到束手无策。有些人持怀疑态度，另一些人则带着该民族特有的宿命论冷漠，在这台沉重的磨盘上麻木前行。
在民政和军务部门里，一片热火朝天的忙乱景象，组织管理却混乱不堪。几乎每一个分支机构都在各自为政，与其他苏维埃机关老死不相往来，往往对其他行政机构的政策措施一无所知，甚至公然背道而驰。这导致了匪夷所思的奇葩怪象。例如，全乌克兰执行委员会主席向所有苏维埃机关发出了电报指令，要求大力协助我们考察队的工作；与此同时，该党委员会的书记却向我们下达了一道禁令，谴责我们的考察使命是企图剥夺乌克兰的历史文献，并扬言要没收我们收集的所有资料。
工会苏维埃占据着克列夏季克大街上原萨伏伊酒店（Hotel Savoy）的那栋宏伟建筑。在1918年和1919年，该机构曾发挥过极其重要的作用，其工作涵盖了无产阶级切身利益的方方面面，其权威直接建立在产业群众明确表达的意愿之上。但慢慢地，工会苏维埃的实权被逐步剥夺殆尽，政府接管了其核心职能，将各工会变为了国家机器的执行与行政下属分支。选举制被废除，取而代之的是共产党的委派任命制。
工会总部的内部秩序一片混乱。正如在哈尔科夫的情形一样，整个工会苏维埃以及地方基层的大多数理事会，最近都被定性为孟什维克分子或对共产党怀有异心而遭到“清洗肃清”，莫斯科委派了全新的官员取而代之。与其他政府机关一样，工会内部同样笼罩着一股极力被压抑的紧张不安气息。

===Original===

The Bolsheviki do not feel secure in the city, and there are stubborn rumors of Communist reverses on the Polish front, of Wrangel advancing from the Crimea, of Odessa being taken by the Whites, and of Makhno activity in the Kiev province.
In the course of my work I come in touch with T﻿—﻿—, the Ukrainian Communist, whom I had met last winter in the Kharitonensky as a member of the delegation that had come to Moscow to plead for greater independence and self-determination for the Ukraine. He is of middle age, a university graduate and revolutionist repeatedly imprisoned under the Romanov regime. An active *borodbist* (Left Social Revolutionist of the Ukraine), he submitted to discipline when his party joined the Communists.
But he has “private opinions” which, long suppressed, seek relief. “I don’t mind discussing these questions with you,” he remarks, with an emphasis on the pronoun, “though I know that you are not a Communist﻿—”
“But I am,” I interrupt; “not indeed a Bolshevik; nor a Governmentalist, but a free, Anarchist Communist.”
“Not *our* kind of Communist. Still, you are an old revolutionist. I heard a good deal about you in Moscow, and I can call you comrade. I disagree with you, of course, but I also disagree with the policies of my Party. The Ukraine is not Russia﻿—it’s a great mistake for ‘the center’ to treat us as if we were. We could win the people to our side by having greater local autonomy and more independence. Our Ukrainian Party has used every effort to convince Moscow in this matter, but without result. We are a republic in name only; in reality we are just a Russian province.”
“Do you want entire separation?”
“No. We want to be federated with R.S.F.S.R., but not subject. We are as good Communists as they in Moscow, but our influence here would be much greater were we left free to act. We know the conditions and needs of the people better than those who sit in the Kremlin. Take, for instance, the recent wholesale suspension of the union management. It has antagonized the entire labor element against us. The same is happening in the other Soviet institutions. Only yesterday a chauffeur complained to me about our ‘Moscow methods.’ The man had been ordered to the front, but his wife died recently, leaving a paralytic boy on his hands.

===Chinese===

布尔什维克在城内深感立足未稳，坊间顽固流传着共产党在波兰前线遭遇惨败、弗兰格尔（Wrangel）正从克里米亚大举进犯、白军攻占了敖德萨（Odessa），以及马赫诺匪帮在基辅省四处活跃的种种传闻。
在工作期间，我结识了乌克兰共产党人T——，去年冬天在哈里托年斯基大宅（Kharitonensky）我曾见过他，当时他是前来莫斯科为乌克兰争取更大独立与自决权的代表团成员之一。他已步入中年，大学毕业，在罗曼诺夫王朝统治时期曾屡次身陷囹圄。作为一名积极活跃的鲍罗特巴派分子（borodbist，乌克兰左翼社会革命党人），在该党并入共产党后他服从了组织纪律。
然而，他心头长期被压抑的“个人见解”却急切寻求宣泄的出口。“我很乐意同你探讨这些问题，”他在代词上加重了语气说道，“尽管我知道你并不是一名共产党员——”
“但我确实是，”我打断了他的话；“诚然，我不是布尔什维克，也不是国家主义者，而是一名自由的无政府共产主义者。”
“你不是我们这种类型的共产党员。不过，你毕竟是一位老革命家。我在莫斯科听到过许多关于你的事迹，我可以称呼你为同志。当然，我有不同于你的观点，但我也同样不同意我们党目前的政策。乌克兰绝不是俄罗斯——‘中央’把我们当成俄罗斯本土一样对待，是大错特错的。如果我们能够享有更大的地方自治权和更多的独立性，本可以把广大民众争取到我们这边来。我们乌克兰党想尽一切办法试图在这一问题上说服莫斯科，但终究毫无结果。我们只是名义上的共和国；在现实中，我们不过是俄罗斯的一个省份罢了。”
“你们希望彻底分立脱离吗？”
“不。我们希望同俄罗斯苏维埃联邦社会主义共和国（R.S.F.S.R.）建立联邦同盟，而不是沦为属地附庸。我们同莫斯科的党员一样是优秀的共产主义者，但如果我们能放手自由行动，我们在这里的影响力会大得多。我们比坐在克里姆林宫里的那些人更懂民情疾苦。就拿最近大规模撤换工会领导层来说吧。这导致整个工人阶级对我们产生了强烈敌意。其他苏维埃机关里也正在上演同样的一幕。就在昨天，一名司机还向我抱怨我们的‘莫斯科做派’。该男子奉命开赴前线，但他的妻子最近病故，留下一名瘫痪的幼子需要照料。

===Original===

He has been trying to get his child into some hospital or home, but his petition, in the Ukrainian language, has been returned to him with the order to ‘write it in Russian.’ And that after two weeks of waiting! Now the man is to join his regiment within two days. Do you wonder that the people hate us? The ‘center’ ignores our suggestions, and we are powerless.”
Criticism of Moscow is general among the Ukrainian Communists. Often, to my surprise and consternation, I detect obvious antisemitism in their resentment of Kremlin domination. The anecdotes and puns circulating in Soviet institutions are tinged with this spirit, though some do not lack wit. Among the people at large hatred of the Jew is intense, though its active expression is held in abeyance. Yet not unfrequent are incidents such as happened this morning in the Podol, the proletarian district of the city, where a man ran amuck in the market, knife in hand, shouting: “Kill the Jews, save Russia!” He stabbed several persons before he was overpowered. It is said that the man was crazed by hunger and illness, but his sentiments are unfortunately too popular to require such an explanation.
Kiev, in the heart of the former ghetto, has lately still more increased its Hebrew population, come to the larger city in the hope of finding comparative safety from the continual wave of pogroms which swept the province since 1917. Whoever the changing political masters﻿—with the sole exception of the Bolsheviki﻿—the Jew was always the first victim, the eternal martyr. It is the concurrence of opinion that Denikin and the Poles were the most brutal and ruthless. Under the latter even Kiev was not free from antisemitic excesses, and in the Podol pogroms took place repeatedly.
In the city library, in a recent publication by a man of great literary and intellectual attainments, I read: “Pogroms are sad, but if that is the only way to get rid of the Bolsheviki, then we must have pogroms.”

===Chinese===

他一直想方设法把孩子送进某所医院或收容所，但他用乌克兰语书写的请愿书却被退了回来，上面勒令他‘用俄语重写’。而这还是在他苦苦等待了2周之后的结果！如今这名男子必须在2天内赶赴部队报到。人民痛恨我们，难道还值得大惊小怪吗？‘中央’对我们的建议置若罔闻，我们根本无能为力。”
对莫斯科的批评抱怨在乌克兰共产党人中司空见惯。经常令我既惊诧又骇然的是，在他们对克里姆林宫专横统治的怨恨不满中，我察觉到了明显的反犹太主义情绪。在苏维埃机关中流传的政治笑话和双关语段子里都夹杂着这种情绪，尽管其中有些段子倒也不乏机智风趣。在广大普通民众中间，对犹太人的仇恨情绪极其强烈，尽管公开激烈的暴行暂时尚被压制收敛。然而，诸如今晨发生在城内无产阶级聚居区波多尔（Podol）的惨剧却绝非罕见：一名男子手持利刃在集市上发狂乱窜，狂喊着：“消灭犹太人，拯救俄罗斯！”在他被制伏之前，已有数人被其刺伤。据说该男子是因饥饿和疾病而发狂丧失理智，但这种仇恨情绪不幸在社会上流毒太广，以至于根本无需借由发疯来解释。
基辅地处昔日犹太人定居区的核心地带，近来犹太人口更是急剧激增，他们涌入这座大城市，希望能在这场自1917年以来横扫全省的接连大屠杀狂潮中寻得相对的安全避难所。无论政权走马灯般如何更迭——唯独布尔什维克除外——犹太人永远是首当其冲的受害者与永恒的殉难者。众人一致认为，邓尼金匪帮和波兰人是最为凶残暴虐的罪魁祸首。在波兰人占领统治时期，甚至基辅城内也未能幸免于反犹暴行，在波多尔区大屠杀更是屡屡上演。
在市立图书馆里，我读到了某位颇具文学造诣和深厚学养的学者最近发表的一篇著作，上面赫然写道：“大屠杀固然可悲，但如果这是摆脱布尔什维克的唯一途径，那我们就必须发动大屠杀。”

## XXX In Various Walks / XXX 各行各业的众生相
===Original===

By the aid of R﻿—﻿—, the secretary of an important labor union, I have gathered much valuable material for the Expedition. R﻿—﻿— is a Menshevik who has in some unexplained manner escaped the recent “cleaning process.” His known popularity among the workers, he believes, has saved him. “The Bolsheviki are keeping an eye on me, but they have left me alone so far,” he said significantly.
Familiar with the city, its museums, libraries, and archives, R﻿—﻿— has been a great help in my quest for data and documents. Much that is valuable has been lost, and still more has been destroyed by the workers themselves, in the interests of their safety, at the time of German occupation and White Terror. But a considerable part of the labor archives has been preserved, sufficient to reconstruct the history of the heroic struggle of the unions since their inception and throughout the stormy days of revolution and civil war. All through the Mensheviki played the role of the intellectual leaders, with the Bolsheviki and Anarchists as the revolutionary inspiration of the workers.
The headquarters of the Labor Soviet have somehow become the depository of a strange documentary mixture. Police and gendarme records, the minutes of Duma sessions, and financial statistics have found their way there, only to be forgotten. By a curious chance the first Universal of Petlura, a rare document containing the original declaration of principles and aims by the Ukrainian national democracy, has been discovered by me in a neglected drawer. A Communist official claims it as his “personal possession,” with which, however, he is willing to part for a consideration. In view of the large price demanded, the matter has now become a subject of correspondence with the Museum.
In Menshevik circles feeling against the Bolsheviki is very bitter. It is the general sentiment among them that the Communists, formerly Social Democrats, have betrayed Marx and discredited Socialism. “Asiatic revolutionists,” R﻿—﻿— calls them. There is no difference between Trotsky and the hangman Stolypin, he asserts; their methods are identical. Indeed, there was more political life under Nicholas II than there is today. The Bolsheviki, alleged Marxists, think by decrees and terror to alter the immutable law of social evolution; to skip several steps at once, as it were, on the ladder of progress.

===Chinese===

在一家重要工会的书记R——的协助下，我为考察队搜集到了大批极有价值的资料。R——是一名孟什维克，以某种未明原因在近期的“清洗肃清运动”中侥幸逃脱。他坚信，正是自己在工人群体中显赫的威望保护了他。“布尔什维克一直在紧盯着我，不过到目前为止他们还没动我，”他意有所指地说道。
R——熟悉基辅（Kiev）全城及其博物馆、图书馆与档案馆，在我搜求数据和文件的过程中提供了莫大帮助。许多珍贵文献已然下落不明，还有更多文件在德军占领和白色恐怖时期，工人们出于自身安全考量已将其亲手付之一炬。然而，工运档案中相当一部分重要文献得以完好保存下来，足以重构工会从初创之时、历经革命与内战风云激荡岁月的英勇斗争史。贯穿始终，孟什维克一直扮演着知识领袖的角色，而布尔什维克和无政府主义者则是激发工人革命斗志的精神源泉。
工会苏维埃总部不知何故竟成了杂乱无章文献的集聚地。警察与宪兵的旧卷宗、杜马会议记录以及财政统计报表不知怎地流落到了这里，随后便被彻底束之高阁。在一个被遗忘的抽屉里，我机缘巧合地发现了彼得留拉（Petlura）的第一号《普令》（Universal）——这是一份罕见的珍贵文献，载有乌克兰民族民主派对自身原则与宗旨的最初宣言。一位共产党官员声称该文献为其“私人财产”，不过只要价码合适，他乐意割爱转让。鉴于对方索要价码过于高昂，此事如今已成为考察队与博物馆公函商榷的议题。
在孟什维克圈子内部，对布尔什维克的敌意极其强烈。他们普遍认为，早先曾是社会民主党人的共产党（the Communist Party）背叛了马克思，玷污了社会主义。R——斥责他们为“亚细亚式的革命家”。他断言，托洛茨基（Trotsky）与刽子手斯托雷平（Stolypin）毫无二致；两者的手段如出一辙。事实上，沙皇尼古拉二世时期的政治生活甚至比今天还要充裕。自诩为马克思主义者的布尔什维克，妄图凭借一道道法令与红色恐怖来改变社会演进的铁律，宛如企图在社会进步的阶梯上一口气跨越数个台阶。

===Original===

The February Revolution was essentially bourgeois, but Lenin attempted to turn it by the violence of an insignificant minority into a social revolution. The complete debacle of all hopes is the result. The Communists, R﻿—﻿— believes, cannot last much longer. Russia is on the verge of utter economic collapse. The old food reserves are exhausted; production has almost ceased. Militarization of toil has failed. Trotsky’s calculations of the progressive increase of the output on the “labor front” have been exploded like Bolshevik prophecies of world revolution. The factory is not a battlefield. Converting the country into a camp of forced labor is not conducive to creative effort. It has divided the people into slaves and slave drivers, and created a powerful class of Soviet bureaucrats. Most significant of all, it has turned even the more advanced workers against the Communists. Now the Bolsheviki can count neither on the peasant nor on the proletariat; the whole country is against them. But for the stupid policy of the Allies, they would have been swept away long ago. The blockade and invasions have played into their hands. The Bolsheviki need war to keep them in power; the present Polish campaign suits them splendidly. But it is the last Communist straw. It will break, and the bloody Bolshevik experiment will come to an end. “History will write them down as the arch-enemies of the Revolution,” R﻿—﻿— concluded emphatically.
---
*Friday evening.*﻿—On the dining table at the home of Reb Zakhare, the old Zionist, burn three candles orthodoxically blessed by the housewife. The whole family are gathered for the festive occasion. But the traditional soup and meat are absent: herring and kasha are being served, and small chunks of challah, the Sabbath bread, now only partly of wheat. Besides the parents, two daughters and a son of eighteen are present. The oldest boy﻿—“Yankel was his name,” Reb Zakhare says with a heavy sigh. “He’d now be twenty-three, his memory be blessed”﻿—was killed in the pogrom the Denikin men had made just before they finally evacuated the city. He sought to defend his sister﻿—the youngest, then only fifteen. Together they were visiting a friend in the Podol when the mob broke into the street, sacking every house, pillaging, and murdering.
The old lady sits in the corner crying quietly.

===Chinese===

二月革命在本质上是资产阶级性质的，但列宁企图通过微不足道的极少数人的暴力手段，将其强行扭转为一场社会革命。其最终结局，便是所有希望的彻底破灭。R——深信，共产党维持不了多久了。俄罗斯正处于经济彻底崩溃的悬崖边缘。过去的旧粮食储备早已见底；工业生产几乎全面停滞。劳动力军事化已经一败涂地。托洛茨基关于“劳动战线”产量递增的种种盘算，就像布尔什维克关于世界革命的预言一样沦为泡影。工厂绝非杀伐的战场。把整个国家变成强迫劳动的集中营，根本不可能激发创造性劳动。这反而将人民撕裂成了奴隶与监工，催生了一个专横跋扈的苏维埃官僚阶层。最具有标志性意义的是，这甚至让觉悟最高的产业工人都站到了共产党的对立面。如今布尔什维克既指望不上农民，也指望不上无产阶级；整个国家都在反对他们。若非协约国推行愚蠢的封锁政策，他们早就被历史洪流横扫一空了。武装封锁与武装干涉反倒帮了他们的大忙。布尔什维克需要靠战争来维系政权；目前的波兰战事正中他们下怀。但这不过是共产党最后的救命稻草。这根稻草终将折断，血腥的布尔什维克实验也将彻底画上句号。“历史必将把他们钉在革命死敌的耻辱柱上，”R——斩钉截铁地总结道。
---
周五夜晚。——在老锡安主义者扎哈尔老爹（Reb Zakhare）的餐桌上，点燃着3支按照正统仪式由女主人祝圣过的蜡烛。全家老小齐聚一堂共度安息日。然而餐桌上却没有传统的肉汤与荤腥：端上桌的是鲱鱼和杂粮粥（kasha），还有几小块如今仅掺杂少许小麦粉的安息日哈拉面包（challah）。除了父母二老，两个女儿和一个18岁的儿子也围坐桌旁。大儿子——“他叫扬克尔（Yankel），”扎哈尔老爹深深叹了口气说道，“如今该有23岁了，愿上帝赐福于他的在天之灵”——是在邓尼金（Denikin）匪帮最终撤离基辅前夕发动的大屠杀中遇害的。他当时挺身保护自己年仅15岁的幼妹。兄妹俩当时正去波多尔区（Podol）探望朋友，暴徒突然冲进街道，破门砸户，劫掠洗劫，肆意屠杀。
老妇人蜷坐在角落里暗自抽泣。

===Original===

The look of frozen terror, which I have seen often lately, is in the eyes of the girls. The young man steps over to his mother and gently speaks to her. True Zionists, the family converse in Old Hebrew, making an evident concession in addressing me in Yiddish.
“At least you are free from pogroms under the Bolsheviki,” I remark.
“In a certain sense,” the old man assents; “but it is the Bolsheviki who are responsible for pogroms. Yes, yes, we had them under the Tsar also,” he interrupts my protest, “but they were nothing like those we have had since. Hatred against us has increased. To the gentiles a Bolshevik now means a Jew; a commissar is a *Zhid*,39 and every Hebrew is held responsible for the murders of the Cheka. I have lived all my life in the ghetto, and I have seen pogroms in the years past, but never such terrible things as since the Bolsheviki got into Moscow.”
“But they have made no pogroms,” I insist.
“They also hate the Jew. We are always the victims. Under the Communists we have no violent mob pogroms; at least I have not heard of any. But we have the ‘quiet pogroms,’ the systematic destruction of all that is dearest to us﻿—of our traditions, customs, and culture. They are killing us as a nation. I don’t know but what that is the worst pogrom,” he adds bitterly.
After a while he takes up the subject again:
“Some foolish Jews are proud that our people are in the government, and that Trotsky is war minister. As if Trotsky and such others are Jews! What good is it all, I ask, when our nation must suffer as before, and more?”
“The Jews have been made the political and social equals of gentiles,” I suggest.
“Equals in what? In misery and corruption. But even there we are not equal. The Jew has more to bear than the others. We are not fit for the factory﻿—we were always businessmen, traders, and now we have been ruined entirely. They have sown corruption in our youth who now think only of power; or to join the Cheka for gain. That was never before. They are destroying the dream of Palestine, our true home; they are suppressing every effort to educate our children in the proper Jewish spirit.”
---
In the Kulturliga gather Hebrew writers, poets, and teachers, most of them members of the Volkspartei when that political party was represented in the Rada by its Minister of Jewish Affairs.

===Chinese===

我在近来频频见到的那种凝固的恐惧绝望神色，浮现在姑娘们的眼眸中。年轻的小伙子走近母亲身旁，柔声轻抚安慰着她。作为坚定的锡安主义者，这家人在家里通常用古希伯来语交谈，为了迁就我，才特意改用意第绪语说话。
“至少在布尔什维克治下，你们免遭了大屠杀的浩劫，”我评论道。
“在某种意义上确实如此，”老人附和道，“但挑起大屠杀祸端的罪魁祸首恰恰正是布尔什维克。是的，是的，沙皇时期我们确实也挨过屠杀，”他打断了我的辩驳，“但以往任何一场屠杀都无法同后来的浩劫相提并论。世人对我们的仇恨变本加厉了。在非犹太人眼中，如今布尔什维克就等同于犹太人；政委就是‘犹太佬39’（Zhid），每一个犹太人都被强加了替契卡（the Cheka）杀戮罪行偿命的罪责。我一辈子都住在犹太隔离区，早年见识过无数次大屠杀，但自从布尔什维克进驻莫斯科（Moscow）以来，这种恐怖残酷的灾难简直闻所未闻。”
“但布尔什维克自己并没有发动大屠杀啊，”我坚持道。
“他们同样痛恨犹太人。我们永远是无辜的代罪羔羊。在共产党治下，确实没有暴民公开施暴的大屠杀；至少我还没听说过。但我们面对的却是‘无声的屠杀’——对我们最珍视的一切、对我们的传统、习俗和文化进行系统性的摧毁磨灭。他们正在从肉体和精神上将我们作为一个民族彻底消灭。我甚至不知道这究竟算不算最为恶毒的屠杀，”他辛酸地补充道。
沉默片刻后，他又拾起了话头：
“有些愚蠢的犹太人居然引以为豪，因为政府里有我们民族的人，托洛茨基还当上了陆军人民委员。仿佛托洛茨基这类人还算是犹太人似的！如果我们的同胞注定要像从前一样受苦遭殃，甚至变本加厉，这一切又有什么益处呢？”
“但犹太人已经在政治上和社会地位上获得了与非犹太人平等的权利，”我提醒道。
“在什么方面平等？在苦难与堕落腐败中平等吗！即便是受苦，我们也并不平等。犹太人承受的苦难远比旁人沉重得多。我们不适合去工厂做工——我们世代都是生意人和商人，如今所有的营生都被彻底摧毁了。他们在我们青年一代的心中播下了道德败坏的毒种，如今年轻人满脑子想的都是争权夺利，或是为了捞取油水而加入契卡。这在以往是不可想象的。他们正在粉碎回归巴勒斯坦故土的梦想；他们扼杀以正统犹太精神教养子女的一切努力。”
---
在文化联盟（Kulturliga）的聚会场所里，汇集着希伯来作家、诗人和教师，其中大多数人曾是人民党（Volkspartei）的党员，当年该党在乌克兰中央拉达（Rada）中曾设有犹太事务人民委员。

===Original===

Formerly the League was a powerful organization, with 230 branches throughout the South, doing cultural work among its coreligionists. The institution had much to suffer through the various political changes, the Bolsheviki were tolerant at first, and even financially aided its educational efforts. But gradually the help was withdrawn and obstacles began to be placed in the way of the League. The Communists frown upon the too nationalistic character of its work. The Yovkom, Jewish branch of the Party, is particularly antagonistic. The League’s teachers and older pupils have been mobilized into State service, and the field of its efforts narrowed down. In the provinces most of its branches have been compelled to close entirely, but in Kiev the devotion and persistence of its leading spirits still enables the League to continue.
It is the sole oasis in the city of nonpartisan intellectual and social life. Though now limited in its activities, it still enjoys great popularity among the Jewish youth. Its art classes, including drawing, painting and sculpture, are eagerly visited, and the theatrical studio is developing young actors and actresses of much promise. The rehearsals I attended, especially that of *The World’s End*, the posthumous work of an unknown dramatist, were unique in artistic conception and powerful in expression.
The younger elements that frequent the Kulturliga dream of Zion, and look to the aid of England in securing to the Jewish nation its traditional home. They are out of touch with the Western world and recent events, but their reliance on the hopes raised by the Jewish Congress is unshaken. Somehow, sometime, probably even in the not distant future, is to happen the great event and Jewry will be reestablished in Palestine. In that ardent faith they drag on their existence from day to day, intellectually vegetating, physically in misery. Their former sources of support are abolished, the government having supplied them with a bread-card of the fourth category. The latter is the Bolshevik label of the *bourzhooi*, the intelligentsia now being denounced as such, though in reality the rich middle class has sought safety at the outbreak of the Revolution. Hatred of the bourgeoisie has been transferred to the intellectuals, official agitation cultivating and intensifying this spirit.

===Chinese===

在过去，该联盟曾是一个庞大强盛的组织，在整个南方拥有230个分支机构，在同胞教友中广泛开展文化启蒙工作。随着历次政权更迭，该机构饱经磨难挫折。布尔什维克起初态度尚算宽容，甚至在财政上资助过其教育事业。但慢慢地，政府抽走了援助，并开始给联盟的工作设置重重障碍。共产党对其工作过于浓厚的民族主义色彩深恶痛绝。党内的犹太工作委员会（Yovkom）对其敌意尤为深重。联盟的教师和高年级学员被强制动员征召进入国家机关服务，其活动空间被大幅压缩。在各外省，大部分分支机构已被迫彻底关闭，唯独在基辅，依靠骨干力量的赤诚与坚守，联盟才得以勉强维系。
这里是基辅城内硕果仅存的非党派知识界与社会生活的避难绿洲。尽管如今活动范围受到极大钳制，它在犹太青年中依然享有崇高威望。其艺术培训班——涵盖素描、绘画与雕塑——吸引了大批青年求学者，其戏剧工作坊更是培养出了多位极具潜质的年轻男女演员。我观摩过的彩排排演，特别是无名剧作家遗作《世界末日》（The World’s End）的排练，在艺术构思上独树一帜，表现力震撼人心。
常来文化联盟的年轻一代心系锡安山，寄望于英国的扶持以帮助犹太民族重返传统家园。他们同西方世界和近期的外部时局严重脱节，但对犹太人代表大会激起的希望依然坚信不疑。他们深信无论如何、总有一天，甚至可能就在不久的将来，伟大的历史转折必将降临，犹太民族终将在巴勒斯坦重建家园。怀着这股炽热的信仰，他们日复一日地苦苦熬着日子，在精神上近乎荒芜，在肉体上忍受贫困。他们过去的经济来源已被尽数切断，政府仅配发给他们第四类面包卡。所谓第四类卡，就是布尔什维克贴给老爷们/资产阶级（bourzhooi）的标签，如今知识分子也被打上了此类标签，尽管真正的富裕资产阶级早在革命爆发之初就已远走避难。对资产阶级的阶级仇恨如今全被转嫁到了知识分子头上，官方的宣传鼓动更是处心积虑地助长并激化了这种对立情绪。

===Original===

They are represented as the enemies of the proletariat, traitors to the Revolution﻿—at best speculators, if not active counterrevolutionists. There is no stemming the fearful tide sweeping against them. Nor is it the spontaneous unfettering of popular sentiment. The flames are fanned by Moscow. Bolshevik agents from the center, sent as chiefs and “instructors,” systematically rouse the basest instincts. Zinoviev himself severely upbraided the local Communists and his “brother proletarians” for leniency to the bourgeoisie. “They still walk your streets,” he exclaimed at a public meeting, “clad in the best finery, while you go about in rags. They live in the luxurious homes, while you grovel in cellars. You must not permit such things any longer.”
Visits of Communist leaders are always followed by renewed “requisitions from the bourgeoisie.” The method is simple. The house porter is instructed to compile a list of holders of cards of the fourth category. In most cases they are intellectual proletarians﻿—teachers, writers, scientists. But the possession of the fourth category card is their doom: they are legitimate victims of requisition. Clothing, underwear, household goods﻿—everything is confiscated as alleged *izlishki* (superfluities).
“The most tragic part of it,” said C﻿—﻿—, the well-known Yiddish writer, “is that the *izlishki* rarely reach their proletarian destination. We all know that the really valuable things confiscated get no further than the Cheka, while the old and almost useless rags are sent to the unions, for distribution among the workers.”
“Often one does not even know who is ‘requisitioning,’ ” remarked a member of the League; “sometimes it is done by Chekists on their own account.”
“Is there no redress?” I asked; “does no one protest?”
C﻿—﻿— made a deprecating motion. “We have learned better,” be replied, “from the fate of those who dared.”
“You can’t protest against Bolshevik ‘revolutionary orders,’ as they call it,” said a young woman teacher. “I have tried it. It happened like this. One day, returning to my room, I found a stranger occupying it. On my demanding what he was doing there, he informed me that he had been assigned to it, showing me his document from the Housing Bureau. ‘And what shall I do?’ I asked. ‘You can sleep on the floor,’ he replied, stretching himself on my bed.

===Chinese===

知识分子被描绘成无产阶级的敌人、革命的叛徒——充其量不过是投机倒把分子，甚至是不折不扣的现行反革命分子。针对他们的可怕排挤浪潮汹涌澎湃，根本无法遏制。这绝非大众自发情绪的单纯宣泄，火种正是由莫斯科煽动起来的。从中央派来充当首长和“特派指导员”的布尔什维克特使，极有系统地挑动着底层最卑劣的本能。季诺维也夫（Zinoviev）本人就曾在一次公开大会上厉声斥责当地共产党人和他的“无产阶级弟兄”，怪罪他们对资产阶级过于姑息心慈手软。“他们依旧锦衣玉食招摇过市，”他大声疾呼，“而你们却衣衫褴褛。他们住在宽敞奢华的公馆里，而你们却蜷缩在阴暗地窖中。你们决不能再容忍这种事情继续下去了！”
每逢共产党高层领导莅临视察，紧接着必然掀起新一轮“向资产阶级征收多余物资”的狂潮。操作方法极其简便：门卫接到指令，编造出一份第四类配给卡持有者的名单。在绝大多数情况下，这些人其实是脑力无产者——教师、作家、科学家。然而，持有第四类卡便注定了他们的厄运：他们成了合法合规的物资没收对象。衣服、内衣、日用家具——一切物品都被冠以所谓“多余物资（izlishki）”的罪名予以查抄没收。
“最为荒诞悲惨的是，”著名意第绪语作家C——说道，“这些查抄的‘多余物资’极少能真正送到无产阶级手中。我们心知肚明，查抄到的真正值钱财物顶多流转到契卡手里便被私分扣留，而那些破旧不堪、几乎毫无用处的烂布头，才会被打发给工会，转分给工人们。”
“往往人们甚至根本搞不清楚究竟是谁在执行‘查抄征收’，”文化联盟的一位成员评论道；“有时纯粹是契卡人员中饱私囊的个人行径。”
“难道就没有任何申诉维权的途径吗？”我询问道；“难道就没人站出来抗议吗？”
C——无奈地摆了摆手。“从那些胆敢反抗者的下场中，”他回答道，“我们早已吸取了深刻教训。”
“你根本无法对抗布尔什维克所谓的‘革命法令’，”一位年轻的女教师接过话茬说道。“我曾亲身抗争过。事情是这样的：有一天我回到自己房间，赫然发现一个陌生男人大摇大摆占了屋子。面对我‘你在这里干什么’的质问，他宣称该房间已分配给他，并亮出了住房局开具的证明文件。‘那我该睡哪里？’我问。‘你可以睡地板上，’他一边回答，一边直接躺倒在我的床上。

===Original===

I protested to the higher authorities, but they refused to consider the matter. ‘The room is big enough for two,’ they insisted, though that was not the case at all. ‘But you put a strange man in my room,’ I pleaded. ‘You’ll soon get acquainted,’ they sneered, ‘we make no distinction of sex.’ I remained with some friends for a while, but they were so crowded I had to look for other quarters. For days I stood in line in the Housing Bureau, but it was impossible to get an order for a room. Meanwhile my chief threatened to report me for neglecting my work, because most of my time was spent in the Soviet offices. Finally I complained to the Rabkrin, which is supposed to protect proletarian interests. Their agent invited me to share his room, and I slapped his face. He had me arrested, and I was kept in the Cheka two months for ‘sabotage.’ ”
“It might have ended worse,” someone commented.
“When you were released,” I pursued, interested in the woman’s story, “what did you do about your room?”
She smiled sadly. “I learned a lot while sitting in the Cheka,” she said. “When I was liberated, I sought out a member of the Housing Bureau. Fortunately I had saved a pair of fine French shoes, and I presented them to him. ‘A little gift for your wife,’ I told him, not much caring which one would get it, for he is known to have several. Within twenty-four hours I received a splendid large room, furnished in true bourgeois style.”
---
The sun has set, the streets are dark, the infrequent lights flicker dimly in the foggy air. Turning the corner of the Krestchatik, on my way to the Ispolkom, I find myself in the midst of an excited crowd, surrounded by soldiers and militia. It is an *oblava* looking for labor deserters. Men and women are detained within the military circle, to be taken to the station for examination. Only a Communist card secures immediate release. Arrest means detention for days, even weeks﻿—and I have an urgent appointment at Communist headquarters. But in vain I explain to the *militsioneri* that *tovarish* Vetoshkin is expecting me. Even the name of the powerful head of the Executive Committee does not impress them. The Committee of Labor and Defense is now supreme; its orders are to detain everyone for investigation regarding his employment.

===Chinese===

我向更高层机关提出申诉，但他们断然拒绝受理。‘房间足够两个人住，’他们一口咬定，尽管事实根本不是那么回事。‘可你们把一个陌生男人塞进了我的闺房！’我苦苦哀求。‘你们很快就会混熟的，’他们冷嘲热讽道，‘我们革命者不讲男女有别。’我在朋友家里借住了几宿，但朋友家同样拥挤不堪，我不得不另寻住处。整整好几天我都泡在住房局排队，却根本办不下一张房屋分配凭条。与此同时，我的顶头上司扬言要举报我旷工渎职，因为我大部分工作时间都耗在苏维埃各个衙门里奔波。最后我向号称捍卫无产阶级利益的工农检查院（Rabkrin）进行投诉。该机构的一名办事员竟然恬不知耻地邀请我去他的宿舍同住，我当场狠狠抽了他一记耳光。他随即下令将我逮捕，并以‘怠工破坏’的罪名将我关押在契卡监狱里长达2个月之久。”
“没丢了性命就算万幸了，”有人插嘴评论道。
“那你被释放之后，”我对这位女士的离奇遭遇颇感好奇，继续追问，“你的房子最后怎么解决的？”
她凄然一笑。“在契卡里蹲大牢让我学会了太多生存法则，”她说。“一重获自由，我便设法搭上住房局的一名实权委员。万幸的是我箱底还珍藏着一双做工精良的法国进口高档女鞋，我把皮鞋双手奉上。‘送给尊夫人的小礼物，’我对他说，至于这双鞋究竟会落入哪位夫人的囊中我并不在乎，因为众所周知他金屋藏娇了好几位情妇。不到24小时，我就分到了一间宽敞豪华的大开间，里面甚至摆满了地道的资产阶级考究家具。”
---
夕阳西沉，街道漆黑一片，零星的路灯在雾蒙蒙的夜空中忽明忽暗。在前往执行委员会（Ispolkom）的途中拐过克列夏季克大街拐角处时，我发现自己突然陷入了一群骚动的人群中，四周已被荷枪实弹的士兵和民警层层包围。这是一场抓捕逃避劳动人员的突击搜捕（oblava）。男女老少被扣押在军警包围圈内，准备押送回治安所接受审问。唯独持有共产党党证的人才能当场获释。遭到逮捕便意味着长达数日乃至数周的拘留审查——而我在共产党总部还有一项十万火急的公务会晤。尽管我向民警们反复解释特派委员维托什金（Vetoshkin）同志正在焦急等我，但一切解释都无济于事。甚至连这位执行委员会实权一把手的显赫大名也未能震慑住他们。国防委员会如今享有至高无上的绝对权威；军警奉命严厉拘留每一个人，以便详尽核查其劳动就业状况。

===Original===

The arrested men and women plead, argue, and produce documents, but the soldiers remain stolid, commanding everyone to line up. I demand to see the officer in charge, but the *militsioner* remains at my side, ignoring my protests. Suddenly the crowd in front begins jostling and pushing: a fight has started on the Corner. My guard rushes forward and, taking advantage of the situation, I cross the street and step into the Ispolkom building.
Vetoshkin’s secretary meets me on the stairs. Excusing my tardiness by the *oblava* incident, I suggest the desirability of better system and judgment in the organization of such matters. The Secretary expresses regret at the stupid and irresponsible manner of the raid, but “*nitchevo ne podelayesh*” (it can’t be helped), he assures me with conviction.
The Communist banquet hall is flooded with light; the walls are decorated with red banners and inscriptions, and crimson bunting frames the large portraits of Lenin and Trotsky, with a small likeness of Lunacharsky in a less conspicuous place. The long dining table is laden with a variety of fruit and wine, and a choice meal is served the guests invited to honor the French and Italian delegates visiting the city. Angelica Balabanova is presiding; at her side are Vetoshkin and other high Soviet officials of the city, with a large sprinkling of men in military uniform.
It is an official assembly of the Communist aristocracy, with Emma Goldman and myself as the only non-Bolsheviki present by special invitation of our mutual friend Angelica. Her motherly, simple personality seems out of place in this gathering. There is deep sadness in her look, a suggestion of disapproval of all the finery and “style” put on for the occasion. Her attention is engaged by the local men at her side, who exert themselves to please the important personage “from the center.” Others are entertaining the foreign delegates, the French-speaking *tovarishi* having been placed as their neighbors. The wine is good and unstinted, the food delicious. By degrees the atmosphere loses its stiff formality, and a freer spirit descends upon the banquet table.
With coffee begin the speeches. The Russian proletariat, with the Communist Party as its advance guard, is extolled as the banner bearer of social revolution, and the firm conviction of the speedy breakdown of capitalism throughout the world is expressed.

===Chinese===

被扣押的男女老少苦苦哀求、据理力争，纷纷掏出证明文件，但士兵们冷酷无情，厉声喝令所有人排好纵队。我严正要求面见现场负责人，但民警死死贴在我身旁，对我的抗议置若罔闻。突然间，前方人群开始推搡骚动：街角爆发了一场斗殴扭打。看守我的卫兵急忙冲上前弹压，我趁势脱身穿过街道，迅速步入了执行委员会大楼。
维托什金的秘书在楼梯口迎候我。在为自己因突击搜捕（oblava）意外而迟到表达歉意之余，我顺势建议在组织此类事务时应更具系统性与审慎判断。秘书对这次搜捕行动的愚蠢与不负责任表达了遗憾，但他随即深信不疑地向我保证：“毫无办法（nitchevo ne podelayesh），谁也改变不了现实。”
共产党人的宴会大厅灯火辉煌；墙壁上装饰着鲜艳的红旗与标语，大幅列宁和托洛茨基画像被深红色的彩布精心装裱，在不甚显眼的位置还悬挂着卢那察尔斯基（Lunacharsky）的一幅小肖像。长长的餐桌上摆满了各式各样的珍奇水果与名贵美酒，精心准备的佳肴正款待着为欢迎莅临基辅的法国与意大利代表团而受邀出席的贵宾。安杰丽卡·巴拉巴诺娃（Angelica Balabanova）主持宴会；维托什金以及该市其他苏维埃高级首长簇拥在她身旁，席间还点缀着大批身着戎装的军界显贵。
这是一场共产党新贵阶层的官方盛大聚会，我和爱玛·戈尔德曼（Emma Goldman）作为仅有的非布尔什维克人士，凭借我们共同的挚友安杰丽卡的破格特邀破例出席。在她慈母般质朴的个性映衬下，这处充斥奢华做派的场合显得格格不入。她的眼眸中流露着深深的忧郁，暗含着对为了撑门面而刻意摆出的浮华排场的隐隐不以为然。身旁的当地显要们极力巴结逢迎，争相讨好这位来自“中央”的重要人物。其他人则在殷勤款待外国代表，会讲法语的同志们被特意安排坐在代表身旁。美酒甘醇充裕，佳肴丰盛可口。渐渐地，宴席褪去了僵硬刻板的繁文缛节，一股更加轻松自在的氛围笼罩在餐桌之上。
随着咖啡端上桌面，祝酒致辞正式登场。以共产党（the Communist Party）为先锋队的俄罗斯无产阶级，被颂扬为世界社会革命的旗手，发言者斩钉截铁地表达了全世界资本主义即将加速崩溃的坚定信念。

===Original===

But for the cursed Allies starving the country and supporting armed counterrevolution, Russia﻿—it is claimed﻿—would be a workers’ paradise with full liberty and welfare for all. The Mensheviki and the Social Revolutionists, traitors to the Revolution, have been silenced within the country, but abroad these lackeys of capitalism, the Kautskys, Lafargues, et al., still continue their poisonous work, maligning the Commmunists and defaming the Revolution. It is, therefore, doubly to be welcomed that the foreign delegates have come to Russia to acquaint themselves with the real situation, and that they are visiting the Ukraine where they can with their own eyes witness the great work the Communists have accomplished.
I glance at the delegates. They sit unmoved during the long speeches in the strange language, but even Angelica’s masterful rendering into French, enriched by her personality and impassioned oratory, does not seem to impress them. I detect disappointment in their faces. Perhaps they had hoped for a less official, more intimate discussion of the revolutionary problems. They have undoubtedly heard of the numerous peasant uprisings and the punitive expeditions, the frequent strikes, the Makhno movement, and the general opposition to the Communists. But these matters have been carefully avoided by the speakers, who have sought to present a picture of a unified people cooperating in the “proletarian dictatorship” and enthusiastically supporting its “advance guard, the Communist Party.”
Late at night, accompanying the foreign delegates to the railroad station, I have opportunity to learn their sentiments. “The observations we have made while in Russia and the material we have collected,” one of them remarks, “entirely disprove Bolshevik claims. We feel it our duty to tell the whole truth to our people at home.”
Next morning in the Passage, where provisions are purchased to fill out the scanty *pyock*, I meet little knots of people lamenting and crying. Nothing is being sold: the little bakery and fruit stores were visited by the authorities the previous evening and all their goods requisitioned. Deep gloom hangs over the traders and their customers. With a sense of outrage they point to the large delicatessen stores on the Krestchatik which have not been molested. “They have protection,” someone says indignantly.
“My God, my God!” a woman cries.

===Chinese===

若非万恶的协约国对苏俄推行饥饿封锁并全力扶植武装反革命，俄罗斯——发言者声称——本该成为人人享有完全自由与充裕福祉的工人的地上天国。作为革命叛徒的孟什维克与社会革命党人，在国内已被彻底肃清噤声，然而在海外，这帮资产阶级的走狗爪牙，诸如考茨基、拉法格之流，依然在继续兜售其反动毒素，恶意中伤共产党人，肆意污蔑抹黑苏维埃革命。因此，外国代表团亲临俄罗斯考察实际国情，并深入走访乌克兰（Ukraine），用他们自己的眼睛亲眼见证共产党人取得的丰功伟绩，具有双倍值得欢迎的重大意义。
我望向代表团成员。在漫长冗长、语言不通的演讲过程中，他们面无表情地枯坐在座位上，即便安杰丽卡凭借其出众的领袖魅力与激情四射的雄辩口才，将讲话精妙地翻译成法语，似乎也未能真正打动他们。我从他们的脸上捕捉到了一丝失望。或许他们原本期盼的是一场少些官腔官调、更为深入探讨革命实际难题的促膝长谈。他们无疑早已听闻过频频爆发的农民暴动、严酷的惩罚性征粮队、屡见不鲜的工人罢工、马赫诺运动以及大众对共产党的广泛反对。然而，这些尖锐议题被发言者刻意回避得一干二净，发言者一味竭力粉饰出一幅万众一心拥护“无产阶级专政”、狂热支持其“先锋队共产党”的祥和画卷。
深夜，在陪同外国代表团前往火车站的途中，我得以获悉他们的真实心声。“我们在俄罗斯期间亲眼所见的一切，以及我们搜集到的详尽事实材料，”其中一位代表直言不讳地指出，“完全推翻了布尔什维克的虚假神话。我们深感有责任向我们本国的劳苦大众讲述全部真相。”
翌日清晨，在用来采买食物以弥补匮乏学术口粮（pyock）的商业街长廊（Passage）里，我碰到了一群群正在哀号痛哭的小商贩。市场上空无一物，什么都买不到：昨晚当局突然查抄了所有的小面包房和水果铺，将所有货物悉数征收没收。浓重的阴霾笼罩在摊贩与顾客的心头。他们义愤填膺地指向克列夏季克大街上毫发无损的大型熟食商号。“那些铺子背后有大人物撑腰庇护，”有人咬牙切齿地愤慨说道。

===Original===

“It’s we poor people who gave that banquet to the delegates.”
---
She was introduced to me as Gallina﻿—a young woman in peasant dress, but of graceful figure, and with thoughtful blue eyes. “Gallina?” I wondered. “Yes, Makhno’s wife.”
Surprise and fear for her safety struggled with my admiration of her courage. Her presence in Kiev, in the very lair of the Cheka, means certain death were she to be recognized. Yet she has braved imminent peril and great difficulties in crossing the front. She has business in the city for the *povstantsi*, she said; she has also brought a message from Nestor: he is very anxious to have Emma Goldman and myself visit him. He is not far from the city, and arrangements could be made to enable us to see him.
Her manner was reserved, almost shy; but she was very positive in her views, and her expression clear and definite. She looked so frail and alone, I was overwhelmingly conscious of the great danger to which she was exposing herself. She gave me the feeling of a diminutive David rising up to smite Goliath.
“I’m not afraid,” she said simply. “You know, I usually accompany Nestor, and he is always at the head of his men,” she added with quiet pride.
She spoke with much warmth of Makhno’s military ability, his great popularity among the peasantry, and the success of his campaigns against Denikin. But she is not uncritical, nor a blind hero-worshiper. On the contrary, she dwelt much more on the significance and purpose of the rebel peasant movement than on the role of its individual leaders. In the Makhnovstchina she sees the hope of Russia’s liberation from the yoke of White generals, *pomeshtchiki* (landlords), and Bolshevik commissarship. The one is as hateful to her as the other, both equally subversive of liberty and the Revolution.
“I regard the *povstantsi* movement,” she said, “as the only true proletarian revolution. Bolshevism is the mastery of the Communist Party, falsely called the dictatorship of the proletariat. It is very far from our conception of revolution. It is the rulership of a caste, of the socialist intelligentsia which has imposed its theories upon the toilers. Their aim is State Communism, with the workers and farmers of the whole country serving as employees of the one powerful government master. Its result is the most abject slavery, suppression, and revolt, as we see on every hand.

===Chinese===

“正是我们这帮穷苦百姓在出钱给代表团买单办宴席啊。”
---
她被引荐给我时，自称叫加琳娜（Gallina）——这是一位身着朴素农家衣裙的年轻女子，身形端庄优美，眼神深邃沉静。“加琳娜？”我满腹狐疑。“是的，马赫诺的妻子。”
震惊与对她安危的揪心，与我对她惊人勇气的由衷钦佩激烈交织在一起。身处基辅，置身于契卡（the Cheka）魔窟腹地，她一旦被敌人识破身份，便绝无生还的可能。然而她却视死如归，克服了重重艰险，成功穿越了硝烟弥漫的前线。她说，自己进城是为起义游击队（povstantsi）办理紧急要务；同时她也带来了涅斯托尔（Nestor）的亲笔口信：马赫诺极其渴望爱玛·戈尔德曼（Emma Goldman）与我能前去造访他。他的营地距离基辅并不遥远，完全可以妥善安排促成我们的会面。
她的举止内敛含蓄，甚至略带羞怯；然而对于时局见解却斩钉截铁，表达清晰明了。她看起来如此纤弱单薄、孤立无援，令我深切感受到她正将自己置于何等凶险的万丈深渊之中。她给我的感觉，就像一个瘦小的年轻大卫挺身迎战巨人歌利亚。
“我并不害怕，”她坦然说道。“你知道，我向来跟随涅斯托尔左右，而他永远身先士卒冲在部队最前沿，”她带着平静的自豪补充道。
她满怀崇敬地谈起马赫诺出众的军事统帅才能、他在农民阶级中至高无上的威望，以及他抗击邓尼金（Denikin）屡建奇功的辉煌胜仗。然而她绝非缺乏批判意识，更不是盲目的个人崇拜者。相反，相较于个别领袖个人的作用，她更着重阐述了起义农民运动的深远历史意义与奋斗目标。在马赫诺运动（Makhnovstchina）中，她看到了俄罗斯摆脱白卫军白军将领、地主（pomeshtchiki）压迫以及布尔什维克政委专制暴政的唯一希望。在她看来，这两者同样面目可憎，同样在彻底扼杀自由与革命成果。
“在我看来，起义游击队（povstantsi）运动，”她说，“才是唯一真正的无产阶级革命。布尔什维克主义不过是共产党（the Communist Party）一党之专权独裁，却被冠以‘无产阶级专政’的欺世盗名旗号。这与我们心目中的革命相去甚远。这是特定特权阶层的统治，是将自身教条强加于广大劳动人民头上的社会主义知识分子阶层的专权。他们的终极目标是国家共产主义，让全国的工人和农民沦为同一个全能极权政府雇主的下属雇员。其恶果便是最屈辱的奴役、残酷镇压以及无处不在的反抗起义，正如我们举目所见的一切。”

===Original===

But the people themselves﻿—the proletarians of city and country﻿—have an entirely different ideal, even though to a great extent only instinctive. They ignore all parties and are antagonistic to the political intellectuals; they distrust the non-working, privileged elements. Our aim is the class organization of the revolutionary toiling masses. That is the sense of the great Ukrainian movement, and its best expression is to be found in the Makhnovstchina. Without the help of government or political parties the peasants drove away the landlords; by their own efforts they secured the land. Their military units have successfully fought every counterrevolutionary force. The Bolsheviki with their Red Army usually came into the already freed district, there to impose their mastery upon city and country, and proclaim their dictatorship. Is it any wonder the people hate them and fight them as bitterly as the Whites?”
She is a typical specimen of the rebel Ukrainian, of the type molded in the crucible of hard revolutionary life. All night we talked of the burning problems of the South, of the needs of the peasantry, and the activities of the *povstantsi*, whose beloved and almost venerated leader is *bat’ka*40 Makhno, the Stenka Razin of the Revolution.
She related stories of the great devotion of the peasantry to Nestor and told interesting anecdotes of his campaigns. Once, when Makhno with a small company found himself surrounded by a large Bolshevik force, he caused a marriage to be performed in the village occupied by the enemy. Makhno’s men in borrowed holiday attire attended the celebration, their famous “sawed off” guns hidden upon their bodies. In the midst of the carousal, the Red soldiers the worse for the liquor freely supplied by the villagers, the pretended holiday makers opened fire, taking the Bolshevik garrison by surprise and putting them to flight.
The very mention of Makhno’s name, Gallina said, brings terror to the enemy and frequently whole companies of the Red Army join his forces. Commissars and Communists﻿—identical terms to the *povstantsi*﻿—find no mercy, but the common soldier is always given the choice of remaining with him or going free.
“That was the case also,” she continued in her melodious voice, “with Grigoriev’s army. You have heard of him, haven’t you, comrade?

===Chinese===

但人民本身——城乡真正的无产者——却怀揣着全然不同的崇高理想，哪怕在很大程度上这仅仅源自朴素的阶级本能。他们唾弃所有政党，与玩弄权术的政治知识分子势同水火；他们深切怀疑那些不劳而获的特权寄生群体。我们的终极目标是组织起革命劳动大众的阶级联合。这正是波澜壮阔的乌克兰革命运动的灵魂所在，而其最完美的体现便凝聚在马赫诺运动之中。在没有官僚政府或政治党派插手干涉的情况下，农民们凭借自身力量赶跑了地主；靠着自己的双手夺回了土地。他们的武装力量成功击溃了各路反革命势力。而布尔什维克及其红军（Red Army）往往是在某个地区已被彻底解放之后才姗姗来迟，随即骑在城乡民众头上作威作福，擅自宣布建立专政。广大人民对他们恨之入骨、像抗击白军一样与他们血战到底，难道还值得奇怪吗？”
她是乌克兰反抗叛逆精神的典型代表，是饱经残酷革命烈火淬炼洗礼铸就的坚毅典型。整整一夜，我们都在促膝长谈南方的燃眉难题、农民阶级的迫切需求，以及起义游击队（povstantsi）的种种抗争——游击战士们爱戴乃至由衷膜拜的敬爱领袖，正是马赫诺老爹40（bat’ka Makhno），当代革命的斯捷潘·拉辛（Stenka Razin）。
她生动讲述了广大农民对涅斯托尔的无限忠诚爱戴，并分享了他征战生涯中种种引人入胜的传奇轶事。有一次，当马赫诺率领一支少数分队不幸陷入布尔什维克大军的重重合围时，他灵机一动，在敌人占领的村庄里操办了一场假婚礼。马赫诺的战士们借来节庆盛装出席婚宴，将他们著名的“锯短枪管的猎枪”暗藏在长袍之下。正当宾主开怀畅饮、红军战士们喝得酩酊大醉之际，假扮宾客的游击队员突然猛烈开火，打得布尔什维克驻军措手不及、溃不成军抱头鼠窜。
加琳娜动情地说道，只要一提起马赫诺的威名，敌人便闻风丧胆，红军往往整连整营地阵前倒戈投奔其麾下。政委与共产党员——在起义游击队眼里这两个词完全是同义词——落在游击队手里绝难得到宽恕赦免，但对于被强征入伍的普通士兵，马赫诺向来让他们自由抉择：要么留下来入伙共谋大业，要么发给路费放其自由还乡。
“当年对付格里戈里耶夫（Grigoriev）的军队时同样如此，”她用悦耳动听的语调继续讲述着，“想必你一定听说过这个人吧，同志？

===Original===

He was an officer of the Tsar, but at the outbreak of the Revolution he became a free lance. At one time he was with Petlura, then he fought him, and later he joined the Red Army. He was just a military adventurer, though not without some ability. He was very vain, loving to style himself the Ataman of Khersonstchina, because his greatest successes were in that province. Later he turned against the Bolsheviki and invited Makhno to make common cause with him. But Nestor found out that Grigoriev was planning to join Denikin; besides, he was guilty of many pogroms. The slaughter of Jews he organized in Yekaterinoslav in May of last year (1919) was especially atrocious. Makhno decided to eliminate him. He called a meeting, to which the Ataman and his men were invited. It was a large gathering at which twenty thousand peasants and *povstantsi* were present.41 Nestor publicly accused Grigoriev of counterrevolutionary designs, charged him with pogroms and denounced him as an enemy of the people. The Ataman and his staff were executed on the spot. Almost his whole force joined our *povstantsi*.”
Gallina spoke of executions in an even, ordinary tone, as of matters of common occurrence. Life in the Ukraine, among the rebel peasantry, had made constant struggle and violence the habitual tenor of her existence. Occasionally she would slightly raise her voice in indignation when reports of Jew baiting by *povstantsi* were mentioned. She felt deeply outraged by such base misrepresentation. These stories were deliberately spread by the Bolsheviki, she averred. No one could be more severe in punishing such excesses than Nestor. Some of his best comrades are Jews; there are a number of them in the Revolutionary Soviet and in other branches of the army. Few men are so loved and respected by the *povstantsi* as Yossif, the Emigrant, who is a Jew, and Makhno’s best friend.
“We are not such barbarians as we are painted,” she said with a charming smile. “But you will learn more about us when you visit us, which will not be far off, I hope.”
She listened wistfully to the news from the Western world, and plied me with questions about life in America and the attitude of its workers to Russia. The role of women on the “other side” was of intense interest to her, and she was eager to procure books dealing at length with the subject.

===Chinese===

他曾是沙皇手下的一名旧军官，但在革命爆发之际沦为了见风使舵的雇佣政客与游勇雇佣军。他一度投靠彼得留拉（Petlura），随后又反目同其大打出手，后来甚至加入了红军。他本质上不过是一个投机钻营的军阀冒险家，尽管也确实颇具军事手腕。他生性极其虚荣，狂妄自封为‘赫尔松地区的阿塔曼’（Ataman of Khersonstchina），因为他的主要战绩多半发迹于该省。后来他彻底倒戈背叛布尔什维克，并盛情拉拢马赫诺同流合污共谋大业。然而涅斯托尔却秘密查实，格里戈里耶夫正暗中盘算投降邓尼金（Denikin）；不仅如此，他双手还沾满了大肆屠杀犹太人的累累血债。去年（1919年）5月他在叶卡捷琳诺斯拉夫（Yekaterinoslav）亲手策划的屠犹血案尤为骇人听闻。马赫诺毅然决然决定将其彻底铲除。他召集了一场联合群众大会，阿塔曼及其随从受邀赴会。那是一场多达20000名农民和起义游击队（povstantsi）在场的浩大盛会41。涅斯托尔当众义正词严地揭露了格里戈里耶夫阴谋反革命的罪恶图谋，当场控诉其犯下的滔天大屠杀罪行，怒斥其为人民死敌。阿塔曼及其幕僚随从被就地正法枪决。他的整支部队几乎全员倒戈，悉数加入了我们的起义游击队。”
加琳娜在谈及处决枪决时神态自若、语气平淡，仿佛只是在谈论司空见惯的日常琐事。在乌克兰反抗叛逆农民中间的长期血火生活，早已使无休止的残酷斗争与血腥暴力成为了她生存的家常便饭。唯独当偶尔提及起义游击队涉嫌诱杀迫害犹太人的流言蜚语时，她才会义愤填膺地微微拔高声调。面对这种卑鄙无耻的污蔑中伤，她感到蒙受了莫大的奇耻大辱。她斩钉截铁地断言，这些谣言纯属布尔什维克别有用心炮制散布的诛心谎言。在严惩任何排犹过火行径方面，没有人比涅斯托尔执行纪律更加铁面无私。他麾下多名最亲密的战友便是犹太人；在革命苏维埃领导层以及军队各重要部门里都有不少犹太同志挑大梁。在起义游击队中，极少有人能像移民约西夫（Yossif）那样深受广大战士的衷心拥戴与崇高敬仰，而约西夫正是一名犹太人，也是马赫诺最知心的莫逆挚友。
“我们绝非外人描绘的那般野蛮残暴，”她带着迷人的微笑说道。“不过等你日后亲自前去造访我们时，就会对我们有更为深刻客观的了解，我坚信这一天不会太遥远了。”
她若有所思地倾听着来自西方世界的讯息，连珠炮似地向我打探美洲的现实生活，以及美国工人对苏俄革命的真实态度。大洋彼岸妇女在革命生活中的地位角色激起了她极大的浓厚兴趣，她急切渴望搜集研读详尽阐述这一议题的理论专著。

===Original===

She looked dejected on learning that almost nothing was known in the States about the Ukrainian peasant movement, but she recovered quickly, remarking: “Naturally, for we are so isolated. But some day they will know.”
---
The night had turned to dawn, and all too quickly the morning was breaking. It was high time for Gallina to be on her way. Regretfully she left us, expressing her confidence in our speedy meeting in the camp of Makhno. In complete self-possession she stepped out of the house, while with bated breath we accompanied her at a distance, fearful lest a chance identification result fatally for the daring girl.

===Chinese===

在获悉美国国内对乌克兰农民起义运动几乎一无所知之后，她神色略显黯然神伤，但很快便重新振作起来，释怀地说道：“这也是理所当然的，毕竟我们处于如此重重封锁隔绝之中。但终有一日，世人必将明晓真相。”
---
长夜已尽，黎明破晓，天色亮得飞快。加琳娜该动身上路了。她依依不舍地同我们道别，深信不久的将来我们必将在马赫诺的起义军营地重逢。她神色自若、泰然自若地迈出大门，而我们则在远处屏息凝神悄然目送护送她远去，生怕一次意外的身份盘查会给这位英勇无畏的姑娘带来灭顶之灾。

## XXXI The Cheka / XXXI 契卡
===Original===

A pall hangs over the home of my friend Kolya, the tailor. His wife is ill, the children neglected, dirty, and hungry. The plumbing is out of order, and water must be carried from the next street, four flights up. Kolya always performed the heavy work; his absence falls heavily upon the little family.
From time to time the neighbors look in on the sick woman. “Your husband will soon return,” they assure her cheerfully, but I know that all their efforts to find him have proved fruitless. Kolya is in the Cheka.
The workers of the clothing factory where my friend is employed have of late been very discontented. Their main complaint concerns the arbitrary methods of the *yatcheika*, the little group of Communists within every Soviet institution. Friction between them and the shop committee resulted in the arrest of the latter. In protest the workers declared a strike. Three delegates were sent to the Cheka with the request to release the prisoners, but the men disappeared, and Kolya was among them.
“They call the strikers counterrevolutionists,” Kolya’s sister said. “They have made a list of the ‘opposition’ in the shop, and every day someone is missing.”
“It’s the old Pirro methods,” remarked a neighbor, a young woman in charge of a children’s dining room.
“Pirro methods?” I asked in surprise.
“Don’t you know the Pirro affair? It was of a piece with the usual methods of Latsis, then head of the All-Ukrainian Extraordinary Commission. It was in the summer of 1919, and the Kiev Cheka was working﻿—”
“Working﻿—that’s right, just the proper word,” interrupted her brother.
“Yes, ‘working’ under high pressure,” she continued, “under instructions of Peters, who came from Moscow now and then. His presence in the city was always the signal for renewed arrests and shooting. Well, one day the Soviet papers announced the arrival of Count Pirro, Brazilian Ambassador. I was at the time employed in the Chinese Consulate, which gave a gala dinner in honor of the Count, on which occasion I made his acquaintance. I was surprised to find the Brazilian speaking excellent Russian, but he explained that he had spent many years in our country before the Revolution. He sighed over the old days, and did not in the least disguise his unfriendliness to Bolshevism and its methods.
“Within a few days he began to organize his staff on a large scale.

===Chinese===

一股阴沉压抑的阴霾笼罩着我朋友裁缝柯利亚（Kolya）的家。他的妻子卧病在床，孩子们无人照料，满身污垢，饥肠辘辘。供水管道坏了，必须从隔壁街道把水提上4层楼来。柯利亚以前向来包揽这等重活；他的突然消失给这个脆弱的小家庭带来了沉重无比的打击。
邻居们时不时过来探望这位病榻上的妇人。“你丈夫很快就会回来的，”他们热情宽慰她道，但我心知肚明，他们四处打探柯利亚下落的一切努力都已徒劳无功。柯利亚被关进了契卡（the Cheka）。
我这位朋友受雇的那家服装厂的工人们，近来一直怨声载道。他们的主要不满集中在基层党小组（yatcheika）——即进驻每个苏维埃机关内部的共产党核心小组——的专横跋扈做派上。党小组与车间委员会之间的摩擦矛盾最终导致后者全员遭到逮捕。为示抗议，工人们举行了罢工。厂里派出了3名代表前往契卡交涉要求释放被捕人员，但这3人却一去不复返，柯利亚正是其中之一。
“他们污蔑罢工者是反革命分子，”柯利亚的妹妹愤愤不平地说道。“他们把车间里的‘反对派’统统拉了名单，每天都有人莫名其妙失踪。”
“这完全是当年皮罗（Pirro）那一套故伎重演，”一位负责管理儿童食堂的年轻女邻居评论道。
“皮罗手段？”我惊诧地问道。
“难道你没听说过皮罗事件吗？那与当时全乌克兰特肃委员会（契卡）首脑拉齐斯（Latsis）惯用的手腕如出一辙。那是在1919年夏天，当时基辅（Kiev）契卡正在加班加点地‘干活’——”
“‘干活’——没错，用这个词太贴切了，”她的哥哥插话道。
“是的，在彼得斯（Peters）的高压指令下连轴转‘干活’，”她继续说道，“彼得斯时不时从莫斯科（Moscow）赶来坐镇。只要他一现身城里，就必然是新一轮大抓捕和大清洗枪决的信号。话说有一天，苏维埃官方报纸大肆宣扬巴西公使皮罗伯爵（Count Pirro）莅临基辅的消息。我当时正好在中国领事馆工作，领事馆特意为伯爵举行了一场盛大的欢迎晚宴，我正是在那次宴会上同他结识的。我十分惊讶地发现这位巴西人竟然能讲一口流利纯正的俄语，不过他解释称自己在革命爆发前曾在我们国家侨居多年。他言语间对昔日岁月唏嘘不已，对布尔什维主义及其严酷手段毫无掩饰其敌意反感。
“不出几天，他便开始大张旗鼓地招募筹建自己的公使馆班底。

===Original===

He asked me and my friends to recommend people for work in his consulate. ‘Except Bolsheviki,’ he said. ‘I want only bourgeois and intellectuals who have no sympathy with the Communists. They’ll be safe with me,’ he told us in confidence, hinting at the wholesale destruction of the intelligentsia by the Cheka. Many hastened to offer their services to the Count, eager for the protection offered. Pirro accepted them all, placing some in his offices, while others were put on the waiting list, and their names and addresses, recorded. To be brief, within a short time they were all arrested and most of them shot, among them Mme. Popladskaya, Pirro’s personal secretary, whom he was pretending to help join her husband in Paris. Pirro disappeared, but he was seen to leave town in Peters’ automobile. It soon became known that the alleged Brazilian Count was an agent of the Cheka, a provocateur. Many people in Kiev are positive that it was Peters himself.”
I related to my friend an incident which occurred soon after our Expedition arrived in the city. Early one morning a visitor called at our car, requesting to see the *predsedatel*. Of commanding stature, well proportioned, and straight as a young pine, he was a fine specimen of physical manhood. He had just returned from the front, he said, as if in explanation of his ridiculously belligerent appearance: two heavy guns hung from his belt, a Circassian dagger between them; at his side he carried a long sword, and a huge alarm whistle, set in silver, was suspended from his neck. His features were clean cut, nose aquiline, the sensuous lips somewhat shaded by a full beard. But the most striking feature were his eyes, the color of steel, cold, sharp, and piercing.
He introduced himself as an army man who had fought on every battlefield in the Ukraine. But he was tired of war and bloodshed, he said; he wanted a rest or at least a more peaceful occupation. The work of our Expedition appealed to him. Could he not be of service to us? Surely a large city like Kiev could not be thoroughly canvassed during our short stay. He would therefore suggest that we appoint a local man as our representative to continue the work after our Expedition should leave. He would consider himself honored to aid our important mission.

===Chinese===

他请我和我的朋友们为他的领事馆引荐工作人员。‘布尔什维克除外，’他嘱咐道。‘我只要对共产党怀无好感的资产阶级和知识分子。他们跟着我绝对安全，’他推心置腹地对我们说，言下之意暗示着契卡对知识分子阶层的大肆清洗消灭。许多人争先恐后地向伯爵自荐求职，急切渴望获得这份许诺的庇护。皮罗对所有人来者不拒统统接纳，有的当场安排进办公室工作，其余的则登记造册列入候补名单，详细记录了姓名与住址。长话短说，没过多久，这些人便被一网打尽悉数逮捕，大多数人惨遭枪决，其中就包括皮罗的私人秘书波普拉茨卡娅夫人（Mme. Popladskaya）——皮罗此前还假惺惺许诺要协助她前往巴黎同丈夫团聚。皮罗随即神秘人间蒸发，但有人目睹他坐着彼得斯（Peters）的私家汽车扬长而去。真相很快大白于天下：所谓巴西伯爵纯属契卡一手安插的密探鹰犬、卧底挑衅者。基辅城里许多人甚至笃定断言，那个化名皮罗的人其实就是彼得斯本人。”
我向朋友讲述了考察队抵城不久后发生的一桩离奇事件。清晨一大早，一位不速之客造访我们的专列车厢，指名求见主席（predsedatel）。此人身形魁梧挺拔、匀称如同一株年轻的青松，堪称一副孔武有力的强健体魄。他自称刚刚从前线撤下来，仿佛是为了解释自己一身滑稽可笑的杀伐武装：腰带上挂着两支沉甸甸的手枪，中间别着一把切尔克斯匕首；身侧佩戴着一把长长的军刀，脖颈上还挂着一枚镶银的大警笛。他五官轮廓分明，鹰钩鼻，饱满性感的嘴唇隐没在浓密的络腮胡须之下。然而最令人不寒而栗的是他的双眸——钢灰色的瞳孔，冷酷、锋利，仿佛能洞穿人心。
他自我介绍是一名战功卓著的军人，在乌克兰（Ukraine）的每一处战场上都浴血奋战过。但他厌倦了战争与杀戮，他说自己渴望歇一歇，或者至少谋一份较为和平的营生。我们考察队的工作引起了他的浓厚兴趣。他能否为我们效劳出力？毕竟像基辅这样一座庞大的都会，在短暂停留期间绝难彻底普查穷尽。因此他主动建议，由我们出面委任一名当地本地人员充当代表，在我们考察队开拔离开后继续接力推进工作。能协助我们这项崇高使命，他将深感无上光荣。

===Original===

There was nothing unusual in his proposal, since it is our practice to leave an authorized person in the larger cities to supply the Museum with documents of current history. We promised to consider his application, and within a few days he called again. I thought he looked strangely animated, perhaps even under the influence of drink. He immediately launched into exaggerated assurances of his fitness as our collaborator. He knew every important Communist in the city, he asserted; he was even on terms of intimacy with most of them. The previous night, he declared, he had spent in the company of high-placed commissars, among whom was also the chief of the Cheka. With that he began a recital of its activities, relating revolting details of torture and execution. He spoke with increasing fervor, and growing excitement. At last he announced himself a commandant. He had shot many counterrevolutionists, he boasted, and he had never felt any qualms about his work. His eyes gleamed with a fierce, savage fire, and suddenly he drew the dagger from his belt. Leaning over close to me and wildly waving the weapon, he cried, “Look at it﻿—it’s bloody to the hilt.” Then he fell back into a chair, exhausted, and with a touch of sentimentality he muttered: “I’ve had enough﻿—I’m tired﻿—I want a rest.”
“To judge from your description,” remarked the young woman, “it must have been X﻿—﻿—, one of the most notorious executioners of the provincial Cheka. He is given to such escapades, especially when under the influence of drugs, for he is addicted to cocaine. One, of his hobbies is to get himself photographed﻿—like this.”
She rose, searched a moment among her effects, and handed me a small picture. It represented a man entirely nude, gun in hand, in the posture of taking deliberate aim. I recognized our visitor.
---
*August 27, 1920.*﻿—Rumors of Bolshevik reverses are delaying our departure. There are persistent reports of Red Army defeats: Odessa is said to be evacuating, an enemy fleet in the Black Sea attacking the city, and Wrangel marching against it from the Crimea.
Nothing definite is to be ascertained in the general confusion, but from authoritative circles we have learned that Red forces are being concentrated in the vicinity. The new developments, Yossif has informed me, compelled Makhno to retire from the province.

===Chinese===

他的提议并无任何不同寻常之处，因为按惯例我们确实会在各大城市指派一名全权授权代表，负责为博物馆持续搜集输送当代现行历史文献。我们答应慎重考虑他的申请，几天后他再次登门造访。我觉得他神态异样兴奋，甚至仿佛处于酒精或某种麻醉状态之下。他一进门便连篇累牍、极度夸张地吹嘘自己胜任合作者的出众才能。他宣称自己熟识城里每一位举足轻重的共产党高层，甚至同其中大多数人过从甚密、称兄道弟。他扬言昨夜自己刚刚同几位身居要职的高级政委通宵达旦痛饮，其中就有契卡（the Cheka）的一把手头目。说到这里，他开始肆无忌惮地大肆炫耀契卡的种种血腥内幕，绘声绘色地描述着令人作呕的严刑拷打与残忍处决细节。他越说越慷慨激昂，情绪陷入病态的狂热亢奋之中。最后他自曝自己其实是一名警卫处决长官（commandant）。他洋洋自得地吹嘘自己亲手枪决过无数反革命分子，在执行这些处决时从未有过丝毫的良心谴责或不安。他的眼中闪烁着凶残嗜血的野性凶光，突然猛地从腰间拔出了匕首。他猛然贴近我的脸庞，疯狂挥舞着雪亮的凶刃狂叫道：“睁大眼睛看看吧——从刀尖到刀柄全浸透了鲜血！”随即他仿佛脱力一般颓然瘫软在椅子上，带着一丝神经质的感伤喃喃自语：“我受够了……我累垮了……我想好好歇歇了。”
“根据你的外貌描述，”年轻姑娘评价道，“此人必定就是省契卡臭名昭著的刽子手X——无疑了。他向来喜欢搞这种疯狂的恶作剧，尤其是在吸食毒品之后，因为他极度迷恋可卡因成瘾。他有一项特殊癖好，就是给自己拍摄写真照片——就像这张一样。”
她站起身来，在行李细软中翻找了片刻，递给我一张小寸照片。画面上赫然是一个全裸着身子的男子，手中端着枪，摆出全神贯注举枪瞄准的姿势。我一眼便认出了那位不速之客正是此人。
---
1920年8月27日。——布尔什维克在前线节节败退的谣传耽搁阻滞了我们的行程启程。坊间接连不断地风传红军（Red Army）惨遭溃败的消息：据说敖德萨（Odessa）守军正在紧急疏散撤离，黑海之上的敌军舰队正在大举炮击该城，而弗兰格尔（Wrangel）正挥师从克里米亚大举进犯敖德萨。
在这片人心惶惶的极度混乱之中，任何确切消息都无从核实查证，但从官方权威渠道我们获悉，大批红军主力部队正在附近集结调动。约西夫（Yossif）私下告知我，前线的剧变迫使马赫诺不得不暂时撤离该省。

===Original===

To my great regret our plan of meeting the *povstantsi* leader becomes impracticable for the present. With much anxiety I think of Gallina and her safety in the new turn of affairs.
Our Expedition faces the alternative of returning to Moscow or proceeding further South. In spite of insistent advice to the contrary, we decide to continue according to our schedule, which includes Odessa and the Caucasus.

===Chinese===

令我深感万分遗憾的是，我们同这位起义游击队（povstantsi）领袖会晤的周密计划目前不得不暂时搁浅作罢。面对局势的这一骤然突变，我内心极度忧心忡忡挂念着加琳娜的安危。
我们考察队如今面临着究竟是打道回府折返莫斯科（Moscow），还是继续深入南下挺进的两难抉择。尽管外界反复力劝切莫冒险南下，但我们依然斩钉截铁决定按照原定考察路线图坚定推进，该行程涵盖了敖德萨与高加索地区。

## XXXII Odessa: Life and Vision / XXXII 敖德萨：现实与幻象
===Original===

*September 2, 1920.*﻿—Late yesterday afternoon we reached Odessa, our little commune greatly disturbed over the fate of Alsberg. Our traveling companion, whose cheerful spirit and ready helpfulness contributed so much toward making our journey more pleasant, was arrested on August 30, while we were stopping in Zhmerinka. The local Cheka agents had received orders from Moscow to “return the American correspondent,” because he had gone to the Ukraine “without the knowledge” of the authorities. In vain we argued and produced Zinoviev’s letter giving Alsberg permission to join the Expedition. He was removed from the train, to be taken under convoy to Moscow. The wires we sent to Lenin, Zinoviev, and Balabanova, protesting against the arrest and urging the release of our friend, have so far remained unanswered.
The great city, formerly the most important shipping center of the country, was shrouded in darkness, its electric supply station having been almost completely destroyed by fire several days previously. With considerable difficulty we found our way to one of the principal thoroughfares. On the corner we were detained by a *militsioner* who informed us that it is forbidden to be about after sunset, except by special permission. It required considerable persuasion before the officer was convinced of our “reliability” and permitted us to return to the car. Our first impressions seemed to justify the disconcerting reports we had heard en route.
No more cheerful is the once beautiful city in the glow of the bright morning sun. Few persons are on the streets; the houses and parks are neglected; the pavements broken and filthy. Everywhere is evidence of the poverty and suffering in the wake of foreign occupation and civil war. Food is very scarce; prices exorbitantly high on the markets which are still permitted to operate. The peasants of the district, systematically expropriated by their changing masters, now refuse to plant more than is necessary for their own needs, leaving the cities to their fate.
Externally Odessa is quiet, and there is no sign of enemy warships in the harbor. But an atmosphere of anxious suspense is felt everywhere: bands of Green and Makhno forces are said to be in the neighborhood, and Wrangel is reported to have taken some villages northeast, in the vicinity of Rostov.

===Chinese===

1920年9月2日。——昨天傍晚时分，我们抵达了敖德萨（Odessa），我们考察队这间小小公社因阿尔斯伯格（Alsberg）的命运而深感焦虑不安。这位同行的旅伴生性乐观豁达，总是热情相助，为我们的漫长旅途增添了无数欢声笑语，却不幸于8月30日在列车经停日梅林卡（Zhmerinka）期间遭到逮捕。当地契卡（the Cheka）特工接到了来自莫斯科（Moscow）的密令，要求“遣返这名美利坚记者”，理由是他“未经当局准许”擅自前往乌克兰（Ukraine）。我们据理力争，并当面出示了季诺维也夫（Zinoviev）签署的批准阿尔斯伯格随考察队同行的通行公函，但一切交涉均无济于事。他被强行押解下车，在武装押运下遣送回莫斯科。我们随即致电列宁、季诺维也夫与巴拉巴诺娃，就这起逮捕事件提出严正抗议并敦促立刻释放友人，但迄今为止所有电报均如石沉大海。
这座昔日全俄最重要的航运海运枢纽巨擘，如今完全笼罩在一片深沉的黑暗之中——几天前其中心发电厂在一场大火中几乎被焚毁殆尽。历经千辛万苦，我们才摸索着走到了城内的一条主要干道上。在街角，一名民警拦住了我们，并厉声告知日落之后严禁在街头逗留走动，持有特殊通行证者除外。在费尽唇舌反复解释之后，执勤军官才最终确信我们的“政治可靠性”，放行准许我们返回专列车厢。初来乍到的第一印象，似乎完全坐实了我们在沿途听到的种种令人沮丧的传闻。
在清晨明媚阳光的映照下，这座昔日风景如画的滨海名城同样毫无欢愉之色。大街上行人稀稀拉拉；房屋与公园破败荒芜；路面碎裂不堪，污水横流。随处可见外国军队占领与内战浩劫遗留下来的赤贫与苦难痕迹。食物极端匮乏；在仍被获准营业的几个集市上，物价飞涨到了天文数字。该地区的农民因屡遭走马灯般政权的系统性剥夺洗劫，如今只肯耕种仅够满足自身口粮所需的极少土地，任由城市自生自灭。
表面上看，敖德萨风平浪静，港湾里不见敌军战舰的踪影。然而，一股焦躁不安的紧张压抑气氛却弥漫在每一处空气中：据说绿军与马赫诺的武装匪帮在城郊四处游弋，而弗兰格尔（Wrangel）据报已攻占了东北方向靠近罗斯托夫（Rostov）的数座村落。

===Original===

A spirit of qui vive pervades the Soviet bureaus, everyone wearing a preoccupied air as if listening for the first note of alarm and ready to take flight.
Much disorganization prevails in the unions. The new Communist management has not yet gained full control over the “liquidated” Menshevik and Anarchist leadership. Many of the latter are still at the head of labor affairs, persistently elected by the workers in open defiance of Communist orders. Among the opposition Shakhvorostov, an Anarchist of militant type, has such a strong following that the Bolsheviki have not dared to remove him. Due to his friendly efforts, the Soviet of Unions has decided to call a meeting of secretaries, whom I am to address in the interest of the Museum.
The nonpartisan proletarians, who constitute the great body of labor, look with scorn at the readiness of the Communists to flee should an enemy appear. Particularly the sailors of the destroyed Black Sea fleet, many of whom are in the city, resent the situation. The masses cannot evacuate, they say; the workers are doomed to remain, whoever comes, and to fight it out as best they can. Have not the unions, aided by the peasantry, waged successful guerrilla warfare against the Greek and Italian forces and the White generals? Then there was no distinction of political party﻿—all revolutionists fought side by side. But every time the enemy is driven out, the Communists institute their dictatorship, seek to dominate the revolutionary committee in charge of the safety of the city, and eliminate the old and tried fighters. The masses know how to protect themselves against invaders, but they resent the domination of a political party that seeks to monopolize the Revolution.
---
Semyon Petrovitch, at whose home I spend considerable time, is an intelligent nonpartisan of independent views. An able statistician, he has been permitted by the Bolsheviki to remain in the Department of Economy, where he had served under previous regimes. Semyon is convinced that the Soviet Government will find itself compelled to change its methods and practices. “The ravager cannot long remain in the country he has ravaged”﻿—he likes to repeat the alleged saying of Denikin.
