But the ire of the gods, he asserts, pursues the Bolsheviki: even their best intentions serve in practice to confound them.

===Chinese===

各个苏维埃机关衙门里弥漫着如临大敌的高度戒备恐慌，人人神情紧绷焦虑，仿佛时刻竖起耳朵捕捉第一声警报随时准备撒腿逃命。
在各工会内部，秩序一片混乱涣散。新走马上任的共产党领导班子尚未对遭到“清洗整肃”的孟什维克与无政府主义领导层建立起绝对掌控。后者的许多骨干分子依然在执掌工运事务，这是工人们公然顶撞对抗共产党命令而执意推选出来的。在反对派中间，强硬派无政府主义者沙赫沃罗斯托夫（Shakhvorostov）拥有极高的群众威望，以至于布尔什维克至今不敢轻举妄动撤换他。承蒙他的友好相助，工会苏维埃已决定召集全体书记召开一次专题会议，由我就博物馆工作向与会人员发表宣讲演说。
构成产业劳工主体的广大非党派无产阶级群众，对共产党人一遇敌情便脚底抹油准备逃之夭夭的丑态嗤之以鼻。尤其是已被解散的黑海舰队的水兵们（其中许多人滞留在城内），对这种局面更是愤恨难平。他们怒斥道，底层群众根本无路可逃；无论换谁来统治，工人们都注定要留下来，靠自己的力量殊死搏斗到底。当年难道不正是各工会在广大农民的全力协助下，对希腊、意大利武装干涉军以及各路白卫军将领发动了势如破竹的游击战吗？那时候大家不分党派彼此——所有的革命者并肩携手同生共死。然而每一次只要把强敌赶跑，共产党人便立刻跳出来建立一党专政，企图全面霸占掌管整座城市安危的革命委员会，并把久经沙场的老革命战士统统排挤清洗出门。广大群众深知如何奋起保家卫国抵御外敌，但他们绝不甘心屈从于一个企图将革命成果完全据为己有的政党的霸道专横统治。
---
谢苗·彼得罗维奇（Semyon Petrovitch）是一位具有独立思想的开明非党派人士，我常在他家中盘桓良久。作为一名出类拔萃的统计学家，布尔什维克破例允许他留任在经济局工作，此前他在历任前政权下也一直在此任职。谢苗深信，苏维埃政权终将不得不彻底改弦更张改变其治国手腕与执政方针。“劫掠者在被其蹂躏摧残的国土上绝难长久立足”——他最喜欢引用相传出自邓尼金（Denikin）之口的这句名言。但他同时断言，上天的惩罚如影随形追赶着布尔什维克：即便是他们最良好的初衷愿望，在实际推行中也往往反噬自身令其陷入狼狈不堪的绝境。

===Original===

“They have closed the stores and abolished private trade,” Semyon Petrovitch repeats, “they have nationalized, registered, and taken an invoice of everything under the sun. One would think that complete order should reign. Indeed, you cannot transfer a bed mattress from one apartment into another without special permission of the proper authorities. If you want to ride to the next station, you must get an ‘order’; if you need a sheet of paper, you have to fill several sheets with applications. Every detail of our existence has become subject to Bolshevist regulation. In short, you will find the situation in Odessa about the same as in the rest of Russia,” Semyon assured me. “But life passes by the Soviet apparatus, because life is incomparably stronger than any attempts at doctrinaire regulation.”
As in other Soviet cities, the population is supplied with a bread and products card. But except Communists, very few receive enough bread to exist. On the “bourgeois” categories none has been issued for months; in fact, since the Bolsheviki took Odessa in January. Occasionally a little salt, sugar, and matches are rationed out.
“Fortunately the markets are still permitted to exist,” Semyon explained. “The government cannot press out enough bread of the farmers to feed the cities. The *pyock* is mostly a vision. That reminds me of a certain commissar in our department, a rare type of Communist, for he has a sense of humor. Once I asked him why the Bolsheviki nationalized everything except the *izvostchiki*.42 His reply was characteristic. ‘You see,’ he said, ‘we found that if you don’t feed human beings, they continue to live somehow. But if you don’t feed horses, the stupid beasts die. That’s why we don’t nationalize the cabmen.’ ”
Life is indeed stronger than decrees; it sprouts through every crevice of the socialist armor. When private trade was forbidden and only the cooperatives were permitted to continue, all business places suddenly became inspired by feelings of altruism, and every store was decorated with the sign of the Epo (cooperative). Later, when the cooperatives were also closed and only *kustarnoye* (small scale) production remained legal, all the little stores began manufacturing cigarette lighters and rubber soles from stolen automobile tires. Subsequently new decrees were issued permitting trade only in articles of food.

===Chinese===

“他们关闭了所有的商铺，取缔了私人商业买卖，”谢苗·彼得罗维奇反复数落道，“他们把天地间的一切事物都实行了国有化、实名登记并盘点造册。外人或许以为这里必定秩序井然。事实上，你若想把一张床垫从一间公寓搬到另一间，都必须先获得有关当局的特批红头批条。你若想搭乘火车前往下一个车站，就必须办一张‘出行命令’；你若需要领一张公文纸，就得先填满整整好几页的申请报告。我们日常生活中的每一个细枝末节，全都被纳入了布尔什维克严苛管制的缰绳之下。简而言之，你会发现敖德萨的处境同全俄其他地方如出一辙，”谢苗向我保证道。“然而，现实生活却绕过了苏维埃僵化的行政官僚机器，因为生活本身的磅礴伟力，远比任何教条主义的僵硬管制强大不知多少倍。”
与其他苏维埃城市一样，全城居民配发了面包卡与副食品配给证。但除了共产党员之外，极少有人能领到足够维持温饱的面包。对于所谓“资产阶级”类别的人群，几个月来根本没发过哪怕一克粮食；事实上，自今年1月布尔什维克攻占敖德萨以来就从未配给过。偶尔才会象征性配发少许食盐、食糖和火柴。
“万幸的是，集市贸易依然被默许存在，”谢苗解释道。“政府根本无法从农民手中榨出足够的粮食来填饱城市的肚子。所谓的学术口粮（pyock）绝大多数时候纯属镜花水月。这倒让我想起了我们局里的一位特派政委，他算得上是共产党员中凤毛麟角的罕见人物，因为他居然懂得幽默风趣。有一次我问他，为什么布尔什维克把世间万物都收归国有，唯独放过了马车夫42（izvostchiki）？他的回答堪称绝妙精辟。‘你瞧，’他说道，‘我们发现要是人不给饭吃，他总能想方设法凑合活着。但要是马不给草料吃，那些蠢牲口可真会当场暴毙。这就是我们为什么不把马车夫国有化的缘故！’”
生活确实比僵死的法令更具顽强生机；它在社会主义铁甲的每一道缝隙中顽强破土萌芽。当私人商业被严令禁止、唯独消费合作社获准营业时，所有商贾铺面一夜之间突然涌现出崇高的大公无私情怀，每一家店铺都赫然挂上了统一消费公社（Epo）的醒目招牌。后来，当合作社也遭到强制取缔、唯独手工业（kustarnoye）小作坊生产尚属合法时，所有街头小店便纷纷就地取材，利用偷来的报废汽车轮胎大肆加工打火机和橡胶鞋底。随后，当局又颁布了全新法令，严禁倒卖工业品，仅获准经营食品买卖。

===Original===

Then in every store window there appeared bread and tea surrogates, while other wares were sold in the rear rooms. Finally all food stores were closed; now the illicit trade is transferred to the storekeeper’s home, and business is done on the back stairs.
“The Bolsheviki want to abolish private trade and destroy speculation,” Semyon remarked; “they want everyone to live exclusively by his labor. Yet in no place in the world is there so much speculation as in Russia; the whole country is swept by its fever. ‘Nationalization of trade means that the whole nation is in trade,’ our wits say. The truth is, we have all become speculators,” he continued sadly. “Every family now depends more on the sale of its table and bed linen than on the salaries paid by the Soviet Government. The shopkeepers, having lost their shops, still remain traders; and they are now joined by those who formerly were workers, physical as well as intellectual. Necessity is stronger than laws, my dear friend. The real factory proletarians have become declassified: they have ceased to exist, as a class, because most of the factories and mills are not working. The workers flee into the country or become *meshotchniki*.43 The Communist dictatorship has destroyed, but it cannot build up.”
---
At the house of Dr. L﻿—﻿— almost nightly gather little groups of the local intelligentsia. A man of broad culture and tolerance, L﻿—﻿—’s home is neutral ground for the most diverse political tendencies. His private sanatorium, beautifully situated on an enbankment washed by the Black Sea, was formerly famed as one of the best in Odessa. It has been nationalized, but the physician and his staff are exempt from professional mobilization and remain in charge. Dr. L﻿—﻿— is still permitted to receive a certain number of private patients, which privilege enables him to support himself and family in comparative comfort. In return he is obliged to treat without remuneration the sick assigned to the sanatorium by the authorities.
L﻿—﻿— and his wife, herself a graduate physician, are hospitable in the best Russian spirit. Though their present mode of living is far below that of former days, every comer finds a warm welcome which includes an invitation to the dining room﻿—a custom now almost entirely fallen into disuse in Russia. With charming smile and graceful gesture Mme.

===Chinese===

紧接着，每一间商铺的临街橱窗里立刻摆出了形形色色的面包和茶叶代用品，而在隐秘的后屋里则照样暗度陈仓兜售其他各种违禁百货。最终，当局将所有的食品商铺也一律查封取缔；如今，这些黑市交易彻底转入地下，转移到了商贩自己的私人寓所里，全部买卖都在逼仄的后楼梯间悄悄交割。
“布尔什维克一心想取缔私人商业贸易、彻底消灭投机倒把活动，”谢苗评论道；“他们巴望着人人都纯粹依靠自身劳动勉强度日。然而放眼全世界，没有任何一个角落的投机倒把风气会像今日俄罗斯这般泛滥成灾；全国上下都被这股黑市狂热所吞噬。正如坊间名嘴所调侃的那样：‘商业的全面国有化，意味着全国全民都在投机倒把！’残酷的真相是，我们所有人都被迫沦为了投机倒把分子，”他黯然伤神地继续说道。“每个家庭如今维持生计，全指望变卖自家的桌布、床单和细软，而不是靠苏维埃政府发放的那点微薄工资。商人们虽然丢了店铺，骨子里依然是生意人；而那些曾经的体力劳工与脑力知识分子，如今也统统卷入了黑市倒卖的大军。生存的残酷逼迫远比纸面法律更为强大，我亲爱的朋友。真正的产业工人们已经被彻底打落边缘阶层：作为一个阶级，他们已经在事实上不复存在了，因为绝大多数厂矿企业早已全面停产瘫痪。工人们要么逃亡农村乡下，要么沦为了倒爷背包客43（meshotchniki）。共产党的集权专政摧毁了一切旧世界，却根本无力建立起任何新秩序。”
---
在L——大夫的寓所里，几乎夜夜都汇聚着一群当地的知识分子精英。作为一位修养渊博、心胸宽广开明的名医，L——的宅邸成了各路政治派系聚首争鸣的避风中立圣地。他的私人疗养院坐落于黑海拍岸的优美海滨长堤之上，昔日曾名扬四海被誉为敖德萨最顶尖的医疗机构之一。尽管疗养院已被收归国有，但这位名医及其核心医护团队侥幸免于被强行专业动员，依然获准继续留任打理业务。有关当局仍特许L——大夫接诊一定配额的私人自费患者，这一特权使他得以维持全家相对优渥体面的生活开销。作为交换条件，他必须义务为当局指定安排送入疗养院的公费伤病员提供免费诊疗服务。
L——大夫及其夫人（其夫人本人也是一名科班出身的资深女医师）展现出了俄罗斯传统中最纯粹真挚的热情好客美德。尽管他们如今的日常生活水平早已远逊于往昔的优渥体面，但每一位来访者都能在这里受到温暖如春的热诚款待，甚至包括共进晚餐的盛情邀请——这种温馨传统如今在苏俄大地上几乎早已彻底绝迹。伴随着迷人的微笑与优雅大方的举止，L——夫人穿梭席间。

===Original===

L﻿—﻿— passes around tea, little lumps of yellow candy, homemade from beet sugar, and sandwiches, her manner quite innocent of any suggestion of the saving quality of her service to her famished guests.
The sanatorium was requisitioned for the “benefit of the proletariat,” the doctor informs me with a humorous twinkle in his eyes, but it is occupied exclusively by high Communist officials and several members of the Cheka. Among the latter is also a Commissar, who has repeatedly taken treatment in the institution. He suffers from acute neurosis and is an habitual user of cocaine. In spite of the near presence of the dreadful autocrat of life and death, great freedom of speech prevails in L﻿—﻿—’s home. It is the tacit understanding among his guests that the place is a free forum, a “sanctuary for the crime of thought.” Yet I notice that when a Communist happens to be present, expression is less spontaneous, more controlled. I recall the arrest of the frequenters of a similar oasis in Moscow, betrayed by a member of the family, a Bolshevik. Might not such a misfortune also happen here? Yet Dr. G﻿—﻿—, the Menshevik colleague of the host, is most outspoken against the Bolsheviki whom he holds up to ridicule, as counterfeit Marxists. The Zionists and literati present, among them Byalik, the greatest living Jewish poet, are more temperate in their criticism of the dictatorship. Their attitude is prompted by their love of the Jewry and its aspirations as a nation. They tell of the many vain efforts their most venerated representatives have made in the interests of justice to their coreligionists, only to be met with scorn and insult. R﻿—﻿—, the noted Hebrew author, relates the incident of his visit to the chief of the Cheka, to seek protection for two friends unjustly accused of speculation and in danger of being shot. In the reception room, while awaiting audience with the *predsedatel*, he was abused by the Chekists, among whom he recognized several members of the old police force and two well-known criminals of former days. “You are insulting the Soviet power by interceding for the arrested,” the Cheka Chief told him. The author urged the innocence of the accused. “If you plead for speculators, you are no better than they,” the Chief retorted. Both men were executed without trial.
Through an open window I look out upon the broad expanse of the Black Sea below.

===Chinese===

L——夫人端上热茶，端出用甜菜糖自制的一颗颗黄色小水果糖以及自制三明治，举手投足间全然没有因自己对饥肠辘辘的宾客施以救命之恩而流露出丝毫居功自傲的做派。
医生眼中闪烁着诙谐的幽默光芒向我打趣道，这所疗养院当年打着“造福广大无产阶级”的崇高旗号被查抄征收，然而如今疗养院里住着的却清一色是共产党高级显贵以及几位契卡实权头目。在这批契卡人员中有一名特派政委，经常在此疗养就医。此人患有极度严重的急性神经衰弱症，并且常年吸食可卡因成瘾。尽管这位执掌生杀大权的恐怖独裁官僚近在咫尺，L——大夫的沙龙里却弥漫着非同寻常的言论自由。在来宾之间达成了一种心照不宣的默契：这里是一处自由辩论的论坛，是“思想犯罪者的庇护避难所”。然而我也敏锐注意到，每当碰巧有共产党员在座时，大家的发言便显得不再那么无拘无束，明显有所克制收敛。我不禁回想起此前在莫斯科（Moscow）类似的一处文化绿洲里，聚会常客因被其家庭内部的一名布尔什维克亲属告发检举而全员遭到逮捕的惨痛悲剧。同样的厄运难道就不会在这里重演吗？尽管如此，主人的孟什维克同仁G——大夫在炮轰布尔什维克时言辞最为辛辣犀利，他毫不留情地将布尔什维克作为伪马克思主义者嘲讽奚落。在场的锡安主义者与文人学者——其中包括当世最伟大的犹太桂冠诗人比亚利克（Byalik）——在抨击专政政权时则相对要温和审慎得多。他们的立场完全出于对犹太民族同胞的热爱以及对民族独立复兴的强烈渴求。他们痛陈本民族最德高望重的代表人士为了替教友争取公道平等待遇所付出的无数次徒劳努力，换来的却只有当局的白眼、鄙夷与人格侮辱。著名希伯来作家R——讲述了自己亲赴契卡求见一把手、试图为两位蒙受“投机倒把”不白之冤而危在旦夕面临枪决的朋友求情申冤的惊险遭遇。在接待室苦等主席（predsedatel）接见期间，他遭到了契卡人员的百般刁难辱骂，在这些打手中他一眼认出了几名沙皇旧警察的熟悉面孔以及两名早年臭名昭著的惯犯流氓。“你胆敢出面替被捕罪犯说情开脱，这本身就是对苏维埃政权的公然亵渎侮辱！”契卡头目厉声喝斥道。作家竭力辩白被捕者的无辜清白。“你替投机倒把分子求情，说明你跟他们根本就是一丘之貉！”头目反唇相讥。最终，这两名无辜者未经任何司法审判便被双双就地正法枪决。
透过敞开的窗扉，我远眺着窗下浩瀚无垠的黑海碧波。

===Original===

It is a quiet moonlit night. The low murmur of the waters pleasantly strikes upon the ear, as the white-capped waves with musical regularity reach the shore, softly splash against the rocks, and silently recede. They return to caress the sentinel wall that seems to draw closer toward them as if yearning for their embrace. A gentle breeze floats into the room.
Angry voices recall me to the company. The young Bundist44 D﻿—﻿— is involved in a virulent dispute with his former comrade turned Communist. D﻿—﻿— charges the Bolsheviki with having stooped to a partnership with the notorious Odessa criminals who were organized into regiments and possessed weapons and machine guns. They had first helped Ataman Grigoriev to take the city, and were later used by the Communists for the same purpose. Indebted to the thieves, the Bolsheviki did not molest them in the first days of their Odessa regime, and the “professional union” was permitted to “work” the city. Subsequently, the Soviet Government declared war upon them, but the leading spirits saved their lives by joining the Cheka.
The Communist vehemently denies that any agreement had existed between his Party and the criminals, though he admits that in some instances the thieves’ union aided the work of the Bolsheviki. The argument is becoming dangerously heated; Mme. I﻿—﻿—, grown apprehensive, holds up a warning hand. “Friends, *tovarishi*,” she cautions them, “please not so loud.”
“Do not worry,” the host laughs, “it’s the usual thing when these two come together. They are old friends; relatives, at that, for the ex-Bundist married the Bundist’s sister.”
“It is an open question,” observes Z﻿—﻿—, the literary investigator, “whether the Bolsheviki had a formal agreement with the thieves. But that they cooperated at one time is known to all of us. Well, they were also proletarians,” he adds sarcastically. “Later, of course, the Communists turned against them. But a similar fate has befallen most of us. The Left S.R.’s, the Maximalists, the Anarchists, did they not all fight together with the Communists against the Whites? And where are they now? Those who did not die on the fronts have been shot or imprisoned by the Red dictators, unless they have been bribed or cowed into serving the Cheka.”
“Only weaklings would save their lives thus,” the hostess protests.

===Chinese===

这是一个月光皎洁的宁静夜晚。海浪轻柔舒缓的低吟声悦耳地传入耳畔，白色的浪花以富有韵律的节奏拍打着海岸，轻柔地溅在礁石上，旋即无声地退去。它们卷土重来，温存地抚慰着那道宛如伫立守卫、仿佛正渴望投入其怀抱的防波哨墙。一阵凉爽清幽的微风徐徐吹入室内。
一阵激烈的愤怒争吵声将我的思绪拉回到了屋里的座谈之中。年轻的崩得分子44（Bundist）D——正与昔日同道、如今已转投共产党的故友展开唇枪舌剑的激烈交锋。D——义愤填膺地怒斥布尔什维克竟然自甘堕落，与敖德萨臭名昭著的黑帮犯罪团伙暗中勾结媾和——这些流氓歹徒被整编成武装团伙，私藏大批枪支和重机枪。他们起初曾协助阿塔曼格里戈里耶夫（Grigoriev）攻占了敖德萨，后来又被共产党人利用于同样夺取城市的军事图谋。布尔什维克因欠了这帮盗贼的人情债，在进驻敖德萨执政的最初阶段对他们百般放纵姑息，甚至特许这个“盗窃犯罪职业公会”在城内大肆“揽活干私活”。随后，苏维埃政府才公开放话向黑帮宣战，但其匪首骨干却摇身一变加入了契卡以保全性命。
共产党员情绪激动地断然否认其党曾与犯罪团伙达成过任何正式协议，尽管他也承认在某些特殊关头，窃贼工会确实曾为布尔什维克的事业出过力。争吵变得越来越剑拔弩张火药味十足；L——夫人深感不安，急忙举手示意警告。“朋友们，同志们，”她出言劝阻道，“请千万小声一点。”
“不必大惊小怪，”男主人爽朗地笑出声来，“只要这两个人凑在一块，向来都是这副德行。他们可是老相识了；更何况论起辈分还是亲戚呢，因为这位前崩得分子娶的正是那位崩得同志的亲姐姐。”
“布尔什维克当年究竟是否与盗贼团伙签署过白纸黑字的正式盟约，这尚待考证，”文学研究员Z——意味深长地评论道，“但他们双方一度狼狈为奸密切合作，在座的各位人尽皆知。好吧，毕竟那帮歹徒也算是‘无产阶级’嘛，”他语带讥讽地挖苦道。“当然了，兔死狗烹，共产党人随后便同他们反目成仇。然而，在座的大多数人何尝不是落得同样的凄凉下场？左翼社会革命党人、最高纲领派、无政府主义者，当年哪一个不是与共产党人抛头颅洒热血并肩抗击白卫军的？如今他们又身在何处呢？那些没在前线壮烈牺牲的英烈，如今全被红色独裁者关进了大牢或是拉去枪决，除非他们被收买威逼、屈膝充当契卡的走狗鹰犬。”
“唯有软骨头才会以这种卑鄙行径苟全性命，”女主人义正词严地抗议道。

===Original===

“Few can be brave with a loaded gun pointed at their temple,” the Doctor remarks with a sigh.
---
It is a cold, chilly day as I turn my steps toward Sadovaya Street. There, at a secret session, is to take place a Menshevik *costnaya gazetta* (oral newspaper), and I am to meet prominent members of the party.
The “oral newspaper” is the modern Russian surrogate for a free press. Deprived of opportunity to issue their publications, the suppressed Socialist and revolutionary elements resort to this method. In some private dwelling or “conspirative lodgings” they gather, as in the days of the Tsar, but with even greater danger and more dread of the all-pervading presence of the Cheka. They come to the “lodgings” singly, stealthily, like criminals conscious of guilt, fearful of observation and discovery. Frequently they fall into an ambush: the house maybe in the hands of a *zassada*, though no sign of it is perceptible on the outside. No one having entered, however innocently or accidentally, may depart, not even a neighbor’s child come to borrow a dish or some water for a sick member of the family. None is permitted to leave, that he may not give warning to possible victims. Such a *zassada* generally lasts for many hours, often even for several days and nights; when it is finally lifted, those caught in the net are turned over to the Cheka. Lucky if the charge of counterrevolution or banditism be not made, and the prisoners released after several weeks of detention. But the “leaders,” the known revolutionists, are kept for months, even for years, without a hearing or charge.
It is dusk. In the unlighted, dingy room it is difficult to recognize the half score of men occupying chairs, smoking and talking in subdued voices. The person for whom I inquire has not yet arrived, and I find myself a stranger in the place. I notice nervous glances in my direction; the men near me regard me with frank suspicion. One by one they leave their seats; I see them gathering in a corner, casting inimical looks at me. As I approach them, they cease talking and eye me defiantly. Their manner is militant, and presently I am surrounded by the hostile crowd.
“May I see Comrade P﻿—﻿—?” I inquire.
“Who may you be?” someone demands ironically.
To ally their suspicions, I ask for *tovarish* Astrov, the famous Menshevik leader, with whom I have an appointment.

===Chinese===

“当冰冷的枪口顶在你太阳穴上时，没有几个人还能逞英雄，”大夫长叹了一口气评论道。
---
这是一个阴冷刺骨的日子，我迈步走向萨多瓦亚街（Sadovaya Street）。在那里的一处秘密集会上，将举行一场孟什维克的“口头报纸”（costnaya gazetta）朗读宣讲会，我将同该党的多位核心领导人接头会晤。
“口头报纸”是当代俄国在新闻出版自由彻底被剥夺殆尽后应运而生的替代产物。由于出版印刷刊物的法定权利被剥夺，遭到残酷镇压的社会主义与各派革命力量只得求助于这种古老原始的秘密斗争手段。他们像沙皇黑暗统治时期一样，秘密潜入某处私人寓所或“秘密据点”集会，但如今面临的杀身凶险以及对无孔不入契卡密探的恐惧绝望，却比沙皇时期还要沉重千百倍。他们孤身一人、鬼鬼祟祟摸向“秘密据点”，宛如自知有罪的重刑犯一般，对任何风吹草动都提心吊胆生怕被跟踪识破。集会者往往极易一头撞进当局布下的天罗地网：整栋宅邸可能早已被契卡秘密设伏控制（zassada），而从门外外观看去却全然看不出丝毫破绽。一旦踏入大门，无论来者动机何等天真无辜、或是纯属偶然误入，一概休想离开半步，甚至连隔壁邻舍跑来借个盘子或为重病家人讨碗水喝的小孩也绝无放行的可能。严禁任何人擅自离开，以免走漏风声惊动其他可能自投罗网的猎物。这种秘密设伏搜捕往往一连持续数小时、甚至整整几天几夜；直到伏击最终收网撤除时，落入陷阱的无辜者才被悉数押解移送契卡受审。要是侥幸未被扣上反革命或土匪暴乱的莫须有罪名，被关押数周后或许还能重见天日。然而那些所谓的“政党领袖”与知名老革命家，则会被长年累月关押在阴暗地牢里，既不经任何开庭审理，也不出具任何正式指控。
暮色苍茫。在昏暗逼仄、未生火取暖的房间里，很难辨认清围坐在椅子上抽烟低语的十余名男子。我所要找的接头人尚未赴约，我发觉自己成了一个形单影只的陌生闯入者。我敏锐捕捉到四周投来的神经质警惕目光；身旁的男人们用毫不掩饰的怀疑神色上下打量着我。他们一个接一个地离开座位；我看到他们三三两两聚集在墙角，朝我投来充满敌意的戒备目光。当我主动向他们走近时，他们骤然停止了交谈，桀骜不驯地怒视着我。他们的神态极具攻击性，转瞬间我便被这帮如临大敌的人群团团包围。
“请问我可以见一见P——同志吗？”我开口打听道。
“你到底是何方神圣？”有人阴阳怪气地冷笑着质问道。
为了打消他们的满腹狐疑，我主动提出求见著名的孟什维克领袖阿斯特罗夫（Astrov）同志，声明我与他此前早已订下会面之约。

===Original===

Explanations follow, and at last the men appear satisfied regarding my identity. “You are looking for Astrov?” they ask in surprise. “Don’t you know? Haven’t you heard?”
“What?”
“He’s been arrested this morning.”
Bitter indignation and excitement prevail in labor and revolutionary circles as a result of the arrest. Astrov, a well-known Socialist, is a personality respected throughout Russia. His opposition to the Bolsheviki is purely intellectual, excluding hostile activities against the Government. It is reported, however, that the authorities hold him “morally responsible” for the wave of strikes that has recently swept the city. Astrov’s comrades are distracted at their failure to ascertain the fate of their leader. The Cheka declines to accept a *peredatcha* (packages of food or clothing), an omen which inspires the worst fears. It indicates strictest isolation﻿—it may even signify that the prisoner has been shot.45
---
More hated even than in Kiev is the Cheka in Odessa. Ghastly stories are told of its methods and the ruthlessness of the *predsedatel*, a former immigrant from Detroit. The personnel of the institution consists mostly of old gendarme officers and criminals whose lives had been spared “for services to be rendered in fighting counterrevolution and speculation.” The latter is particularly proscribed, the “highest form of punishment”﻿—shooting﻿—being meted out to offenders. Executions take place daily. The doomed are piled into automobile trucks, face downward, and driven to the outskirts of the city. The long line of the death-vehicles is escorted by mounted men riding wildly and firing into the air﻿—a warning to close the windows. At the appointed place the procession halts. The victims are made to undress and to take their places at the edge of the already prepared common grave. Shots resound﻿—the bodies, some lifeless, some merely wounded, fall into the hole and are hastily covered with sod.
But though the “speculation” is forbidden and the possession or exchange of Tsarist money is frequently punished by death, the members of the Cheka themselves receive part of their salary in *tsarskiye*, whose purchasing power is many times that of Soviet paper. There is considerable circulation of the forbidden currency on the markets, and it is rumored that Cheka agents themselves are the chief dealers.

===Chinese===

在一番细致详尽的解释说明之后，男人们终于对我的真实身份彻底放下了戒心。“你在找阿斯特罗夫？”他们满脸惊愕地反问道，“难道你还被蒙在鼓里吗？难道你没听说吗？”
“听说什么？”
“今天大清早他就被秘密逮捕了。”
这起突如其来的逮捕事件，在劳工界与整个革命圈子里激起了滔天的义愤与剧烈震动。阿斯特罗夫作为享誉全俄的知名社会主义领袖，在全俄各地享有崇高声望。他对布尔什维克的反对纯粹局限于学术思想与理论层面，从未参与过任何针对苏维埃政府的敌对破坏活动。然而据内部传闻，当局认定他必须对近来席卷全城的工人罢工狂潮承担所谓的“道德责任”。阿斯特罗夫的同志们因千方百计打探不到自己领袖的下落生死而急得发疯。契卡断然拒绝接收亲友送去的配给站/发放点（peredatcha，探监送物包），这一危险凶兆预示着最可怕的厄运。这不仅意味着囚犯已被实行了最严苛的彻底隔离断联——甚至极有可能暗示着这位领袖早已惨遭秘密枪决45。
---
在敖德萨，契卡激起的大众仇恨甚至比在基辅还要深重千百倍。坊间绘声绘色流传着其残暴手段以及其主席（predsedatel）——一名早年来自底特律的旧移民——冷血无情的骇人传说。该机构的爪牙班底绝大多数由沙皇时期的旧宪兵军官以及因许诺“在打击反革命与投机倒把斗争中立功赎罪”而侥幸免除死罪的刑满惯犯构成。对于投机倒把倒卖行为的镇压尤为残酷无情，动辄施以“最高惩治手段”——即当场执行枪决。处决杀戮天天都在上演。被判处死刑的待决死囚被像货物一样头朝下强行塞入重型卡车车厢，风驰电掣押往城郊荒野。这列长长的死亡车队由大批狂奔策马、朝天鸣枪警告沿街居民紧闭门窗的骑兵荷枪实弹沿途护送。抵达指定的处决荒滩后，车队缓缓停下。受害者被勒令剥光全身衣服，逼迫他们并排站在早已挖好的万人坑边缘。枪声凄厉骤响——一具具尸体，有的当场毙命，有的尚在痛苦抽搐挣扎，统统栽倒跌入深坑，随后被匆匆用草皮与泥土草草掩埋。
然而，尽管当局严禁民间从事“投机倒把”，持有或私下兑换沙皇旧币动辄招致杀身之祸被处极刑，契卡人员自身的薪水却有一部分是直接以沙皇卢布（tsarskiye）发放的，而这种硬通货在黑市上的实际购买力比苏维埃滥发的废纸纸币高出不知多少倍。在黑市暗流中，这种被明令查禁的沙皇货币依然在私下大肆流通，而坊间私下盛传，契卡内部的密探与头目恰恰正是这些黑市倒卖交易的最大幕后庄家。

===Original===

I refuse to believe the charge till a member of the Expedition informs me that he succeeded in advantageously converting some of the *tsarskiye*, officially given us in Moscow, into Soviet money. “You are taking a great risk in exchanging,” I warn him.
“No risk at all,” he replied gleefully. “Do you think I am tired of life that I would sell on the open market? I traded it through an old friend, and it was the good man N﻿—﻿— himself who did the little business for me.”
N﻿—﻿— is a high placed magistrate of the Cheka.
---
With Emma Goldman I attend a gathering of local Anarchists come to meet the “comrades from America.” The large room is filled with a mixed company﻿—students and workers, Soviet employees, soldiers, and some sailors. Every nongovernmental tendency is represented: there are followers of Kropotkin and of Stirner, adherents of positivism and immediate actionists, with a number of Sovietsky Anarchists, so called because of their friendly attitude to the Bolsheviki.
It is an informal assembly of the widest divergence of opinion. Some denounce the Communists as reactionary; others believe in their revolutionary motives, but entirely disapprove of their methods. Several consider the present stage as a transitory but inevitable phase of the Revolution. But the majority deny the historic existence of such a period. Progress, they claim, is a continuous process, every step foreshadowing and shaping the next. Long-continued despotism and terror destroy the possibilities of future liberty and brotherhood.
The most animated discussion revolves about the proletarian dictatorship. It is the basic problem, determined by one’s conception of revolution and in turn determining one’s attitude to the Bolsheviki. The younger element unreservedly condemn the Party dictatorship with its violence and bloodshed, its punitive measures, and general counterrevolutionary effects. The Sovietsky Anarchists, though regretting the ruthlessness of Communist practice, consider dictatorship inevitable at certain stages of the revolution. The discussion is drawn out for hours, and the vital question is obscured by theoretic assertions. I feel that the years of storm and stress have entirely uprooted the old values without having clarified new concepts of reality and vision.
“Can you suggest something definite in place of the dictatorship?” I ask at last.

===Chinese===

直到考察队的一名成员眉飞色舞地向我炫耀他成功将莫斯科（Moscow）官方拨发给我们的一部分沙皇卢布（tsarskiye）以极其划算的高额汇率兑换成了苏维埃纸币，我才不得不对这一指控深信不疑。“私下私兑货币你可是冒着杀头掉脑袋的天大风险啊，”我严厉警告他道。
“根本没有丝毫风险，”他得意洋洋地嬉笑道。“难道你以为我活得不耐烦了，会傻到跑去公开黑市露天交易吗？我是通过一位通天的老关系牵线搭桥的，亲自出面帮我操办这笔合算买卖的，正是大名鼎鼎的N——大人本人呢！”
而这位N——大人，恰恰正是当地契卡身居要职的一位首席执法审判官。
---
我与爱玛·戈尔德曼（Emma Goldman）一同出席了一场当地无政府主义者为迎接“来自美利坚的同志”而特意召开的秘密集会。宽敞的大厅里挤满了成分复杂的形形色色人群——青年大学生与产业工人、苏维埃公职雇员、红军士兵以及几名海军水兵。几乎所有非国家主义的思想流派在此均有代表出席：既有克鲁泡特金（Kropotkin）学说的忠实门徒，也有施蒂纳（Stirner）唯我主义的追随者，还有实证主义信徒与直接行动派，此外还点缀着数位所谓的“苏维埃派无政府主义者”——他们之所以获此绰号，纯粹是因为其对布尔什维克政权持温和同情的暧昧态度。
这是一场思想分歧极其悬殊的非正式思想交锋大集会。有些人义愤填膺地痛斥共产党人是不折不扣的反动独裁者；另一些人虽然肯定其革命动机的崇高性，却对其严酷手段深恶痛绝全盘否定。还有几位代表认为当前的极权阶段不过是革命不可逾越却终将逝去的过渡时期。然而，绝大多数与会者断然否认历史上存在此类专制过渡时期的必要性。他们坚称，历史进步是一个环环相扣的连续进程，当下的每一步实践都在直接塑造并决定着下一步的历史走向。长期的专制独裁与红色恐怖，只会彻底扼杀未来迈向自由与博爱的任何萌芽与可能。
最为剑拔弩张的激烈辩论始终围绕着“无产阶级专政”的核心命题展开。这是一个根本性的分水岭课题，它取决于每个人对革命本质的根本理解，并反过来直接决定了人们对布尔什维克的政治态度。年轻激进一代毫无保留地严厉声讨共产党的“一党专政”及其伴随的血腥暴力、惩罚性杀戮以及由此酿成的全面反革命恶果。而苏维埃派无政府主义者虽然对共产党的残酷行径深表遗憾，却依然认定在革命的特定关头专政不可避免。辩论足足僵持了数小时之久，最核心的现实问题被无休止的纯理论概念争吵所遮蔽。我深切感受到，漫长严酷的动荡风暴岁月早已将传统的旧价值观念连根拔起，却未能在此废墟之上勾勒出关于现实与未来的任何清晰明确的全新愿景。
“在摒弃专政之后，你们能否拿出某种具体明确的替代方案呢？”我终于忍不住开口问道。

===Original===

“The situation demands a unified purpose.”
“What we have is a dictatorship over the proletariat,” retorts an enthusiastic Kropotkinist.
“That is begging the question. Not the faults and shortcomings of the Bolsheviki are at issue, but the dictatorship itself. Does not the success of the Revolution presuppose the forcible abolition of the bourgeoisie and the imposing of the proletarian will upon society? In short, a dictatorship?”
“Assuredly,” assents the young woman at my side, a Left Social Revolutionist; “but not the dictatorship of the proletariat only. Rather the dictatorship of the toilers, including the peasantry as well as the city workers.”
“If the Communists would not persecute the Anarchists, I’d be with them,” remarks an Individualist Anarchist.
The others scorn his narrow partisanship, but the Kropotkinists refuse to accept dictatorship. There may be times during a revolutionary period where violence, even organized force, becomes necessary, they admit. But these matters must rest in the hands of the workers themselves and not be institutionalized in such bodies as the Cheka, whose work is detrimental and fosters a counterrevolutionary spirit among the violated masses.
The discussion gives no promise of reaching a basis for common work with the Bolsheviki. Most of those present have for years consecrated themselves to their ideal, have suffered persecution and imprisonment till at last the Revolution triumphed. Now they find themselves outlawed even by the Communists. They are deeply outraged by the “advance guard of the proletariat” turning executioners of the best revolutionary elements. The abyss is too wide to be bridged. With deep sorrow I think of the devotion, ability, and idealism thus lost to the Revolution, and of the fratricidal strife the situation inevitably involves.
---
*September 3, 1920.*﻿—Wrangel is reported to be advancing northwest after having defeated the Red Army in several engagements. Budenny’s cavalry is in retreat, leaving open the road to Rostov. Alyoshki, a suburb of Kherson, is invested by the Whites, and refugees are streaming into Odessa. Official silence feeds popular nervousness, and the wildest rumors are gaining circulation.
Our work in the city is completed, but the new military situation makes it impossible to continue our journey southeast, toward the Caucasus, as originally planned.

===Chinese===

“眼下的严峻局势迫切需要统一的奋斗目标。”
“我们如今面对的根本不是什么专政，而是凌驾于无产阶级头上的官僚专政！”一位满腔热血的克鲁泡特金主义者厉声反驳道。
“你这是在回避根本问题。目前争论的焦点不在于布尔什维克自身的缺点与过失，而在于‘专政’这种制度本身。革命的彻底胜利，难道不正是以强力消灭剥削阶级资产阶级、并将无产阶级的钢铁意志强加于整个社会为先决条件吗？简而言之，这难道不就是专政吗？”
“千真万确，”坐在我身旁的一位年轻女同志表示赞同，她是一名左翼社会革命党人；“但这绝不能仅仅演变为狭隘的无产阶级一己专政，而理应是包括广大农民阶级与城市工人在内的全体劳动人民的联合专政！”
“要是共产党人不再残酷迫害无政府主义者，我倒乐意同他们站在一起，”一位个人主义无政府主义者插话评论道。
其他人对这种狭隘自私的宗派主义嗤之以鼻，但坚定的克鲁泡特金派门徒则誓死拒绝接受任何形式的专政。他们坦承，在革命的特定风暴时期，暴力乃至有组织的武装强力确实不可或缺。然而，这些强力手段必须牢牢掌握在劳苦大众自己手中，而绝不能被异化制度化为诸如契卡（the Cheka）之类的极权官僚机关——契卡的暴行除了带来毁灭性灾难、并在惨遭蹂躏的受压迫民众心中疯狂播撒反革命仇恨之外，毫无建设性可言。
这场通宵争论未能为同布尔什维克达成任何共同合作的基础带来丝毫希望。在座的绝大多数同志多年来将毕生精力奉献给了崇高的革命理想，受尽了旧沙皇的政治迫害与铁窗酷刑，直到革命最终迎来胜利曙光。然而在今天，他们却发现自己甚至被共产党人打成了非法反动之徒。目睹所谓的“无产阶级先锋队”蜕变堕落为屠杀最优秀革命志士的专职刽子手，他们内心充满了无尽的义愤与悲凉。横亘在双方之间的鸿沟深不见底，根本无法弥合。我怀着无限沉痛的哀思，深感这场革命正在无可挽回地痛失这批出类拔萃的忠诚赤子、卓越才俊与崇高理想主义者，深感当前局势必将不可避免地滑向同胞骨肉相残的无底深渊。
---
1920年9月3日。——战报传来，弗兰格尔（Wrangel）在接连几场激战中重创红军（Red Army）主力之后，正挥师大举向西北方向长驱直入。布琼尼麾下的骑兵军全线溃退，通往罗斯托夫（Rostov）的战略咽喉要道门户大开。赫尔松城郊的阿廖什基（Alyoshki）已被白军重重包围合击，大批惊慌失措的难民正源源不断涌入敖德萨避难。官方对战局的绝对缄默更加剧了全城百姓的惊慌失措，形形色色荒诞离奇的恐怖谣言在民间不胫而走大肆疯传。
我们在敖德萨的文献搜集考察工作已宣告收官，然而急转直下的前线军事危局，却彻底粉碎了我们按照原定考察路线图向东南方向继续挺进高加索的既定计划。

===Original===

We have therefore decided that the Expedition remain in Odessa, while two of its members attempt to reach Nikolayev, there to determine the possibilities of proceeding further. The *predsedatel* and secretary have been chosen for the task.
My colleagues have already left the city to take up their quarters in the car. With our secretary, Alexandra Shakol, I am loading the collected material on a cart. There is a large quantity, and the underfed old mare is barely able to pull the weight. It is pouring, the pavement is broken and slippery; the poor beast seems about to collapse.
“Your horse is exhausted,” I remark to the driver, a peasant woman.
She does not reply. The reins have fallen out of her hands, her head is bent forward, and her body is shaken as if with the ague.
“What is the matter, *matushka*?” I call to her.
She looks up. Her eyes are red and tears are coursing down her cheeks, leaving streaks of yellow dirt behind. “Curses be upon you!” she mutters between sobs.
The horse has stopped. The rain is pouring with increased force, the chill pierces knife-like.
“Cursed be you all!” she cries vehemently.
We try to soothe her. The Secretary, a native Russian, herself of peasant stock, impulsively kisses the old woman on both cheeks. By snatches we learn that two days ago she had carted a load of hay to the city, part of her village’s share of the *razvyorstka*. Returning home she was halted by a requisitioning detachment. She pleaded that her cattle had long ago been confiscated and only the one horse left her; as the widow of a Red Army man she is exempt from further requisition. But she had no documents with her, and she was taken to the police station. The Commissar berated his men for impressing an unfit animal for army duty, and the woman was overjoyed. But as she was about to leave, he detained her. “Your horse will do for light jobs,” he said; “you will give us three days’ labor duty.”
She has been working two days now, receiving only half a pound of bread and no fodder for the beast except a little straw. This morning she was ordered to our quarters.
Most of the vehicles and horses have been nationalized; those still privately owned are subject to temporary requisition by the Tramot (transport bureau) for a specified number of hours weekly. In vain we hail the passing *izvostchiki*; all pretend to be “on Soviet duty.” The woman grows hysterical.

===Chinese===

因此我们全队一致商定：考察队大队人马暂且留守敖德萨待命，抽调两名成员冒险前往尼古拉耶夫（Nikolayev），以便实地核实下一步继续前行的可能性。主席（predsedatel）与秘书被委以重任担纲此行。
我的同仁们此前已先行撤离市区返回车厢驻扎。我正与我们的秘书亚历山德拉·沙科尔（Alexandra Shakol）一起，将连日来搜集到的大批文献资料奋力装上一辆破旧的运货大车。搜集到的物资堆积如山，而那匹常年饥肠辘辘、骨瘦如柴的老母马几乎连大车的自身重量都拉拽不动。天降倾盆暴雨，坑洼不平的碎石路面泥泞湿滑不堪；这匹可怜的老牲口四蹄踉跄，仿佛随时都会倒地毙命。
“你的马已经彻底累垮脱力了，”我向赶车人——一位老农妇轻声提醒道。
她毫无回应。缰绳从她那双粗糙布满老茧的手中滑落垂下，她的头深深埋在胸前，全身剧烈颤抖，仿佛疟疾发作打摆子一般。
“怎么了，大娘（matushka）？”我关切地大声呼唤她道。
她缓缓抬起头来。她的双眼布满血丝红肿不堪，两行浑浊的泪水顺着满是沟壑的脸颊无声滚落，在泥垢斑斑的脸上冲刷出两道黄色的泥痕。“愿你们全都不得好死遭天谴！”她在抽泣声中咬牙切齿地喃喃诅咒着。
老马彻底停下了脚步。暴雨倾盆越下越大，冰冷刺骨的寒风如利刃般穿透单薄的衣衫。
“诅咒你们这帮丧尽天良的畜生！”她歇斯底里地失声痛哭怒吼起来。
我们急忙上前极力安抚她。秘书是土生土长的俄罗斯农家苦出身的姑娘，情不自禁地动情亲吻着老农妇的双颊。断断续续地，我们才拼凑出了事情的残酷原委：整整2天前，她拉着满满一车干草进城，充缴其所在村庄应摊派的余粮征集制（razvyorstka）指标公粮。在交完差返乡途中，她被一队军警查抄征发队强行截留。她苦苦哀求说自家的牲畜牲口早在很久以前就被全数没收殆尽，全家仅剩下这一匹老马相依为命；而且作为阵亡红军战士的遗孀，按苏维埃律令理应完全豁免任何进一步的物资征调。然而她身上没有携带任何户籍证明文件，便被不由分说押解扭送到了治安派出所。政委在痛斥了手下军警不该把一匹完全不符合军用标准的劣马拉来充数之后，老妇人本以为逢凶化吉喜出望外。然而正当她牵马准备离开时，政委却冷酷地扣下了她。“你的劣马虽上不了前线，干点搬运杂活倒还凑合，”政委面无表情地下令道，“你必须留在城里给我们服满3天的劳役出苦力。”
如今她已经被强迫连续干了整整2天重活，每天仅仅领到半磅（半磅）发霉的面包充饥，而老马除了分到一点点发黄的碎秸秆外根本没吃到一粒饲料草料。今晨，她又奉命被派来我们的寓所听差装卸物资。
城里绝大多数马车与马匹早已被强行收归国有；那些侥幸仍归私人拥有的残存车辆，也必须每周定期接受交通局（Tramot）规定时长的强制无偿征调服役。面对这般绝境，我们在凄风苦雨中徒劳地向疾驰而过的马车夫（izvostchiki）招手呼救；然而每一个车夫都佯称自己“正在执行紧急苏维埃军政要务”扬长而去。老妇人的神经终于彻底崩溃失控了。

===Original===

The horse is apparently unable to move further. The rain is wetting our material, the wind tearing at our newspaper files and blowing precious leaves into the street. At last with voices and shoulders we induce the horse to proceed, and after a long ride we reach the railroad station. There we hastily write a “receipt” testifying that the “requisitioned peasant and horse” have fulfilled their labor duty, give the woman a chunk of bread and a few tidbits for her children, and send her home bowing and repeating, “Bless you, good *barin*,46 God bless you.”

===Chinese===

老马显然再也迈不动半步了。倾盆大雨正在无情浇透我们历经千辛万苦搜集来的珍贵档案文献，狂风疯狂撕扯着报纸合订本，将一片片价值连城的历史文献残页吹得漫天飞舞、散落在泥泞的大街上。最终，我们一边拼命吆喝，一边用肩膀使出浑身解数在泥泞中死命推车，终于迫使老马重新挪动了脚步；历经漫长艰难的泥泞跋涉，我们终于把物资运抵了火车站。在车站里，我们急忙挥笔开具了一份正式的公函“收据凭条”，郑重证明这名“被征调的农民及其马匹”已经圆满履行完毕其全部劳役工时义务，随后我们硬塞给老妇人一大块顶饱的面包以及几样送给孩子们的精美点心；老妇人感激涕零，一边深深鞠躬作揖，一边泣不成声地反复念叨着：“菩萨保佑您，仁慈的老爷46（barin），愿老天爷厚报保佑您啊！”

## XXXIII Dark People / XXXIII 蒙昧的大众
===Original===

Railroad communication between Odessa and Nikolayev is suspended, but we have been informed that a motor truck belonging to the Maritime Ossobiy Otdel (Cheka) is to leave for that city at midnight on September 6.
Accompanied by the Secretary, I proceeded early in the evening to the place of departure. For hours, we tramped unfamiliar streets and tortuous alleys without finding the appointed place. Fearfully my companion clung to me, Odessa’s reputation for lawlessness and the brutality of its bandit element filling us with alarm. In the darkness we lost our bearings and kept circling within the crooked alleys near the docks, when suddenly there came the command, “Who goes there?” and we faced guards pointing guns at us. Fortunately we had secured the military password.
“Tula-Tar﻿—”
“Tarantass,” the soldier completed, permitting us to pass and directing us on our way.
It was after 2 a.m. when we reached the Maritime Otdel. But no machine was in sight, and we were overwhelmed with disappointment at the thought that we had missed the rare opportunity of reaching Nikolayev. Inquiries at the Cheka elicited the unwilling information that the motor had not yet arrived, and no one knew when it was due.
We spent the night in the street, the Chekists refusing us permission to remain indoors. At five in the morning the car arrived, piled high with clothing and ammunition for the Nikolayev garrison. Quickly we climbed to the top, soon to be joined by a number of soldiers accompanied by women. Everything seemed ready for the start, when the chauffeur announced that the gasoline issued him was not sufficient to carry us to our destination, two hundred miles northeast. A short, heavyset sailor, addressed as commandant and apparently in charge of the journey, gruffly ordered everyone off the truck. His command ignored, he drew a revolver, and we all made haste to obey.
“Now you’ll have enough juice,” he declared.
The soldiers protested: they were the convoy sent to accompany the consignment to Nikolayev. Swearing and cursing, the drunken sailor consented to their riding, and they climbed back pulling several girls up after them.
“No heifers!” the sailor shouted, but the women, stretching themselves on top of the load, paid no attention. The commandant got into a violent altercation with the chauffeur, accusing him of delaying the departure and threatening to arrest him.

===Chinese===

敖德萨（Odessa）与尼古拉耶夫（Nikolayev）之间的铁路交通已经完全中断，但我们获悉，隶属于海运特别处（契卡）的一辆载重卡车定于9月6日午夜启程开往该市。
在秘书的陪同下，我于傍晚早早动身前往出发地点。几个小时里，我们在陌生的街巷与蜿蜒曲折的胡同里四处摸索，却怎么也找不到接头地点。同伴心惊胆战地死死抓着我的胳膊，敖德萨臭名昭著的治安混乱以及黑帮匪徒的残暴凶狠让我们心惊肉跳。在漆黑一团中，我们在靠近码头的逼仄小巷里团团打转彻底迷失了方向，突然间传来一声厉喝：“口令，谁在那里？”几支黑洞洞的枪口直直对准了我们。万幸的是我们此前早已获悉了当夜的军用口令。
“图拉——塔——”
“塔兰塔斯（Tarantass），”哨兵补全了下半截口令，放行准许我们通过，并为我们指明了前行的道路。
等我们赶到海运特别处时，已是凌晨2点过后了。然而现场根本见不到任何车辆的踪影，一想到可能痛失前往尼古拉耶夫的宝贵良机，我们内心充满了沮丧。向契卡人员打探的结果令人心寒：他们不耐烦地告知卡车尚未抵达，何时能到谁也说不准。
我们在街头露宿了一整夜，契卡特工冷酷地严禁我们进入室内避寒。直到清晨5点，卡车终于轰鸣着开进院子，车上高高堆满了运往尼古拉耶夫守备部队的军服与弹药。我们手脚并用迅速爬上了车顶，紧接着又有一批士兵和几名随行女子爬了上来。一切似乎整装待发，司机却突然宣布配发的汽油根本不够开到东北方向200英里外的目的地。一名被尊称为长官、矮壮结实的海军水兵显然是这趟押运的带队负责人，粗暴凶狠地厉声喝令所有人统统滚下卡车。见众人无动于衷，他猛地拔出左轮手枪，我们见状吓得急忙仓皇跳下车来。
“这下你们的油料总算够用了吧！”他恶狠狠地叫嚣道。
士兵们纷纷提出抗议：他们是奉命押送这批军用物资前往尼古拉耶夫的武装护卫队。骂骂咧咧、污言秽语不绝于耳，酩酊大醉的水兵最终勉强同意他们登车，士兵们爬回车顶，并顺手拉了几个姑娘上去。
“不许带母牛上去！”水兵破口大骂，但女人们横七竖八躺在货物顶上，对他的叫骂充耳不闻。这位长官随即同司机爆发了激烈的口角互骂，指责司机故意延误发车，并扬言要把司机当场逮捕法办。

===Original===

The driver pleaded that the truck had not been loaded on time; its belated arrival was not his fault. The Chekist cursed and swore in a manner that surpassed anything I had ever before heard in Russia, the variegated complexity of his oaths defying even approximate rendering into English. Meanwhile the number of passengers had increased. The sailor grew furious, and again displaying his Colt, he forced everyone to climb down. Three times the process was repeated, no one daring to resist the drunken commandant. We stood in the driving rain, the uncovered clothing on the truck getting soaked, while the chauffeur was pretending to be busy with the machine, yet stealthily watching the Chekist. Presently the latter walked out of the yard, whereupon the driver also disappeared. After an hour he returned carrying a large can and followed by half a score of men and women. He announced that all was in readiness, and there began a scramble of the new arrivals for a place. At last the huge machine got into motion, the living mass on top desperately clinging for support as we gradually gained speed. “You’ll never make half the way with that load,” the commandant shouted, rushing out into the street and threateningly waving his gun.
Over hills, down valleys, and across fields the auto sped, the chauffeur driving recklessly and every moment endangering our lives as the machine tore its way over large holes in the ground or wildly rushed down steep inclines. Our route lay along the sea and over waste land still bearing evidence of past military activities. The large estate of Sukhomlinov, the great Russian magnate, stretched for miles before us entirely deserted, the celebrated cattle appropriated by the villagers, the place now uncultivated. “No seed,” laconically commented one of the peasants. “What would be the use?” another replied.
Long lines of wagons drawn by oxen and loaded with flour and potatoes crawled in the distance﻿—the proceeds of the *razvyorstka* being delivered to Odessa.
The sailors, talkative and jolly, passed their time railing at the three peasants, typical Ukrainians. The latter took their banter in good humor, somewhat awed and not always comprehending the full drift of the slangy Russian. They were much friendlier with the soldiers, themselves Ukrainians, and presently they began exchanging experiences.

===Chinese===

司机辩解称卡车装载严重延误；车子迟到根本不是他的过错。契卡水兵随即破口大骂，其污言秽语的花样与下流程度，远远超出了我在俄罗斯以往听闻过的任何脏话，其词汇恶毒复杂之极，甚至根本无法用英文哪怕大致翻译出来。与此同时，围拢上来的乘客却越聚越多。水兵暴跳如雷，再次亮出手中的柯尔特手枪，强逼着所有人再度滚下卡车。这出闹剧足足反复上演了3次，没有一个人胆敢反抗这位酒疯大发的手枪长官。我们伫立在倾盆暴雨之中，车上裸露未盖的军服被雨水无情浇透，而司机则假装低头摆弄发动机，暗中却在偷偷窥视着水兵的一举一动。少顷，水兵晃晃悠悠走出了大院，司机见状也随即脚底抹油溜得无影无踪。约莫1小时后，司机提着一只大铁油桶折返回来，身后还簇拥着十多名男女乘客。他高声宣布一切就绪，新来的一伙人立刻争先恐后地蜂拥爬上车顶抢占位置。终于，这台庞然大物轰然发动，随着车速不断攀升，车顶上密密麻麻的一大堆活人拼死死死抓牢身边的绳索与货物以防被甩飞出去。“就凭拉着这死沉的一大堆货，你们连一半路都休想开到！”水兵猛然从街边狂奔追出，朝天疯狂挥舞着手枪恶狠狠地叫骂道。
汽车在崇山峻岭间疾驰飞奔，爬坡过坎、穿林越野，司机开得野蛮疯狂，每当汽车从深陷的大泥坑上狂颠猛碾而过、或是沿着陡峭的险坡亡命俯冲时，我们的性命每一分每一秒都悬在一线。我们的路线沿着海岸线延伸，穿越了一大片依然清晰残留着战火摧残痕迹的荒凉废墟。俄罗斯巨贾苏霍姆利诺夫（Sukhomlinov）当年的辽阔大庄园横亘在眼前，足足绵延数英里，如今早已彻底荒芜废弃，庄园里名扬四海的良种牛群早已被周边村民私分一空，田野杂草丛生无人耕作。“没有种子，”一名老农言简意赅地冷冷哼道。“就算种了又能落着什么好？”另一人沮丧地反唇相讥。
远处，一列长长的牛车队伍在泥泞中缓缓蠕动，车上拉满了面粉与土豆——那是正在押解运往敖德萨的余粮征集制（razvyorstka）指标公粮。
几个健谈欢快的水兵一路上以拿同车的3名典型乌克兰农民寻开心打趣来打发无聊时光。老农们对这种取笑抱以淳朴憨厚的微笑，虽然神情略显敬畏，而且也未必能完全听懂那些花哨复杂的俄语黑话俚语。但他们同车上的士兵们相处得融洽得多，士兵们自己也是土生土长的乌克兰人，不一会儿双方就开始熟络地唠起了各自的坎坷经历。

===Original===

They hailed from Krasnoye Selo; the *razvyorstka* tax upon their village was very heavy, and the local Soviet had sent them to Odessa to secure a reduction of the assessment. But they received no satisfaction in the big city; they spent days in line at the various bureaus without accomplishing anything. Most of the officials just laughed at them; others ignored them. One Commissar even threatened to arrest them. Life has become harder than ever before, they complained. Under the Tsar they had been serfs; the White generals robbed them of their sons and cattle. They had set great hopes upon the Bolsheviki. “But it is the same whoever comes,” the peasants sighed; “for us poor people it’s always the same.”
Two of the soldiers had participated in the campaign against Makhno, and they were exchanging experiences. They spoke freely of his exploits, of the original methods that enabled him to defeat greatly superior forces, and of the numerous times he had been surrounded by White or Red armies, yet always escaping, often in a most miraculous manner. They admired the clever ruse by which Makhno took Yekaterinoslav, then in the hands of Petlura. A handful of his men, dressed as peasants, crossed the bridge leading to the lower part of the city, with their arms hidden in carts. On reaching the other side, they unexpectedly opened fire on the Petlura men guarding the approaches. The sudden attack struck panic to the garrison, and Makhno’s army easily took the city.
“We’ve got to catch him,” one of the soldiers concluded, as if in self-justification, “but you can’t deny it, he’s a *molodets*” (daring fellow).
Both of them had once been taken prisoners by Makhno. Their last hour had come, they thought, as together with other captives they were led before the feared *bat’ka*. A slender young man with sharp, piercing eyes faced them sternly and began haranguing them. Bolshevik Commissars were no better than White generals, he said; both oppressed the people and robbed the peasants. He, Makhno, would defend the Revolution against all enemies. He promised that the prisoners would be given the choice of joining the *povstantsi* or going home, but the Red soldiers feared Makhno was deriding them. Yet he kept his word.
“*Bat’ka* kills only Jews and Commissars,” one of the peasants drawled.
In the evening we stopped at Krasnoye Selo, in the district of the German colonies.

===Chinese===

他们全来自红色村（Krasnoye Selo）；当局摊派给他们村子的余粮征集制（razvyorstka）公粮定额重如泰山，当地村苏维埃特派他们前往敖德萨交涉，希望能争取酌情减免指标。然而在这座繁华的大城市里，他们碰了一鼻子灰毫无所获；他们在各大衙门门前排了整整好几天的长队，事情却毫无进展。绝大多数官僚对他们嗤之以鼻冷嘲热讽；其他人则对他们完全视若无睹。有一名特派政委甚至恶狠狠地扬言要将他们当场逮捕拘留。生活变得比以往任何时候都要水深火热，他们痛哭流涕地诉苦道。在沙皇专制时期他们沦为农奴；白卫军将领们又强征抢光了他们的子弟与牲畜。他们曾对布尔什维克寄予厚望。“但无论换谁来坐江山，全都是一个样，”农民们长吁短叹，“倒霉遭殃的永远是我们这些苦哈哈。”
车上的两名士兵曾亲身参与过围剿马赫诺的征剿战役，两人正津津有味地交流着实战心得。他们毫不避讳地大谈马赫诺的骁勇善战、他屡破数倍强敌的奇谋妙计，以及他无数次陷入白军或红军重重天罗地网合围之中，却总能以令人匪夷所思的奇迹般手段金蝉脱壳化险为夷。他们由衷钦佩马赫诺当年巧取叶卡捷琳诺斯拉夫（Yekaterinoslav）的绝妙骗局——当时该城尚在彼得留拉（Petlura）军队的重兵把守之下。马赫诺的一小队人马乔装打扮成普通进城卖菜的农夫，将武器暗藏在运货大车底下，大摇大摆穿过了通往下城区的跨河大桥。一抵达对岸，这批奇兵突然掀开伪装猛烈开火，将把守要道入口的彼得留拉守军打了个措手不及。这场出其不意的闪电奇袭令守备部队彻底陷入极度恐慌，马赫诺的大军随即不费吹灰之力轻取全城。
“我们肩负的军令必须将他捉拿归案，”其中一名士兵仿佛为了自我辩解般总结道，“但你平心而论，不得不承认那家伙确实是个了不起的好汉（molodets）。”
他们两人都曾一度不幸沦为马赫诺的阶下囚俘虏。他们当时以为自己的死期已到，因为他们同其他战俘一起被押解到了令人闻风丧胆的马赫诺老爹（bat'ka）面前。只见一位面容清瘦、双眼目光锐利如刀的年轻人神色冷峻地打量着他们，随即发表了一通慷慨激昂的演说。布尔什维克政委同白卫军将领相比毫无二致，马赫诺厉声怒斥道：两伙人都在残酷压迫天下百姓、横征暴敛掠夺农民。他马赫诺誓死抗击一切强权暴政以捍卫革命果实。他郑重许诺俘虏可以自由选择去留：要么弃暗投明加入起义游击队（povstantsi），要么领盘缠放行解甲归田，但红军战士们起初疑神疑鬼生怕马赫诺是在故意戏弄消遣他们。然而，马赫诺最终一诺千金言出必行。
“老爹只杀犹太人和特派政委，”其中一名老农慢条斯理地拖长腔调插话道。
傍晚时分，我们的卡车停靠在红色村歇脚，这里地处德意志殖民村落聚集区。

===Original===

The little frame houses, whitewashed and clean, were a pleasant contrast to the straw-thatched, dirty *izba* of the Russian peasant. Few men were to be seen, most of them drafted in the White or Red armies. Only women, children, and very old peasants were about. With my companion I followed a party of sailors and soldiers in search of lodgings for the night. At our approach the villagers ran terrified indoors. The sailors ordered them to bring food, but the women, weeping and imploring for mercy, called God to witness that the recent *razvyorstka* had taken their last provisions. They could offer only bread and country cheese. The Chekists swore at them, fingered their guns, and demanded to be taken to the cellar. There they appropriated whatever eatables they could find.
Distressed I left with my companion to seek hospitality for ourselves. Word had passed of the arrival of “the Commissars,” and the houses were barricaded. After many vain attempts we gained admission to a *khata* at the furthest end of the village. It was occupied by a woman and her three children, the oldest a girl of fourteen, whom her mother had hidden at our approach. She accepted our offer to pay, and set black bread and sour milk before us. Soon the neighbors began to drop in. They stood timidly on the threshold, observing us with unfriendly eyes and exchanging whispered remarks, Gradually they gained confidence, advanced toward the table, and began conversing. They were totally ignorant of the events in the world at large; what was happening in Russia, even, was entirely incomprehensible to them. They knew that the Tsar was no more and that freedom had been given the peasant. But they felt some huge deceit had been played on the “dark people” by those in high places. They were constantly harassed by the military, they complained; soldiers of every kind and armed men without uniforms kept swooping down upon the village, taxing, confiscating, and pillaging. One by one their male folks had been drafted, often not even knowing into what army, and then the boys began to be taken, as young as sixteen. Generals and commissars kept coming and carrying the last away, and now all the cattle are gone, and the fields cannot be worked except by hand in little patches, and even the smallest children must help.

===Chinese===

粉刷一新、干净整洁的一栋栋小木屋，与俄罗斯农民那些杂乱肮脏、茅草覆顶的破旧泥屋（izba）形成了赏心悦目的鲜明对照。村里极少见到成年男丁的身影，大多数男劳力都已被强征入伍抽调进了白军或红军。街头巷尾游荡着的尽是孤儿寡母和风烛残年的老态龙钟之辈。我同旅伴跟随一队水兵和士兵四处挨家挨户寻觅今夜的投宿借宿之所。见我们大队人马靠近，惊恐万状的村民们吓得纷纷仓皇逃入屋内反锁大门。水兵们厉声喝令他们立刻交出饭食，而妇女们则扑通跪倒在地哭天抢地苦苦哀求宽恕，指天画地起誓称最近一轮的余粮征集制（razvyorstka）早已把全家最后一粒保命粮食搜刮得荡然无存。她们能端出来的唯有粗硬的面包和农家酸奶酪。契卡特工们破口大骂，手指紧扣枪机扳机，厉声逼迫房主带路下地窖搜查。一进地窖，他们便将搜寻到的任何能吃的东西洗劫一空据为己有。
见此凄惨惨状，我心生悲戚，便同伴侣转身离去，试图自谋一处投宿之地。关于“特派政委进村了”的恐怖消息早已如野火般传遍全村，家家户户房门紧闭垒起路障。在历经无数次吃闭门羹之后，我们终于在村子最偏僻的尽头被获准踏入一间茅舍（khata）。屋里住着一位农妇和她的3个幼子，其中老大是一个14岁的小姑娘，母亲在我们踏入房门的瞬间急忙将女儿死死藏匿在柴房身后。她战战兢兢地收下了我们主动奉上的借宿盘缠，端出了黑面包与酸奶招待我们。不久，邻舍村民们开始陆陆续续探头探脑溜进屋来。他们怯生生地伫立在门槛边，用充满戒备敌意的目光上下打量着我们，三三两两交头接耳窃窃私语。渐渐地，他们确信我们并无恶意，便壮着胆子挪到桌前围拢过来，小心翼翼地攀谈起来。他们对外部大千世界的风云变幻一无所知；甚至连俄罗斯本土究竟发生了什么天翻地覆的剧变，在他们那质朴浑浊的头脑中也是一头雾水。他们仅仅模模糊糊知晓沙皇老子已经退位驾崩了，农民们似乎被恩准获得了自由。但他们深切本能地感到，某些深居庙堂的高官显贵正在对他们这帮“愚昧蒙昧的底层百姓”玩弄着一场弥天大谎的险恶骗局。他们痛哭流涕地诉苦道，部队官兵的骚扰横征永无宁日；形形色色的各路正规军以及连军装都没穿戴整齐的持枪土匪接二连三呼啸扑向村庄，摊派税收、查抄家底、公然抢劫。他们家里的成年男丁被一个接一个强行抓丁征走，往往连究竟被充入哪一路军队都浑然不知，随后当局甚至开始强抓年仅16岁的黄口少年。走马灯般的大帅将军和特派政委接踵而至，将村里最后一丝值钱物资搜刮得干干净净，如今所有的耕牛牲口全被拉光抢尽，偌大的农田唯有靠着妇孺老幼用双手在一小块一小块贫瘠的荒地上勉强刨食，甚至连牙牙学语的小毛头也不得不赤脚下地干活。

===Original===

Frequently the older girls are dragged away by officers and soldiers, returning later hurt and sick. In a neighboring village the punitive expedition whipped old peasants on the public square. In a place thirty versts from Krasnoye eighteen peasants hanged themselves after the commissars had left.
“Is it as bad in other parts?” my hostess asked. “How is it in Germany﻿—my people come from there.”
“Germany has also had a revolution,” I informed her. “The Kaiser is gone.”
“Rev‑o‑lution?” she repeated in utter incomprehension. “Did Germany have war?”
I spent the night on a straw heap in the outhouse, joining our party early in the morning. The scenes of the previous day repeated themselves all along our route.
---
A very old city and once an important shipbuilding center, Nikolayev has played a prominent part in the labor and socialist history of Russia. It was the scene of the first great strike in the country, in the early days of the nineteenth century. In the “Nihilist” and “People’s Will” period, it was the field of much underground revolutionary activity. In later years Nikolayev was the home of the “South Russian Union,” one of the first Social-Democratic groups in lower Ukraine, with Trotsky as its intellectual leader. Among the old archives we came upon documents relating to the case of Netchayev, which in some strange manner found their way there, though that famous terrorist had never been arrested in this city. We also discovered police “search orders” issued against Lopatin, Bakunin, and other celebrated revolutionists of that period.
Nikolayev still retains some of its former beauty, though its boulevards have been entirely denuded of trees, cut down within the two days’ interregnum between the leaving of the Whites and the coming of the Bolsheviki. The streets are oppressively quiet: the city is in direct line of Wrangel’s advance. The Communists are feverishly active to rouse the population to united defense, appealing particularly to the proletarians and reminding them of the slaughter of the workers by Slastchev, Wrangel’s chief general, notorious as a labor executioner.47
The attitude of the surrounding districts is causing the Bolsheviki much anxiety. The peasantry has been in continual rebellion against the Soviet regime, and the arbitrary methods of labor mobilization have alienated the workers.

===Chinese===

稍有姿色的大姑娘动辄被过往的军官和丘八强行拖走糟蹋，几天后才遍体鳞伤、身患重病被遗弃扔回村里。在隔壁邻村，惩罚性征粮队在村中心公共广场上公然当众用皮鞭抽打年迈的老农。在距离红色村仅30俄里的一处村庄里，在特派政委们搜刮大队扬长而去之后，整整18名绝望的农民上吊自尽。
“外面的其他地方难道也像这里一样民不聊生吗？”女主人泪眼婆娑地向我打探道。“德意志那边如今怎样了——我的祖辈正是从那里迁徙过来的。”
“德意志同样爆发了革命，”我如实相告。“德皇已经逊位逃亡了。”
“革——命？”她一脸木然完全无法理解这个生僻词汇。“德意志也打大仗了吗？”
这一夜我是在外屋牲口棚的一堆乱草堆上蜷缩度过的，翌日清晨一大早便折返回大队汇合。昨日沿途目睹的种种凄惨惨象，在接下来的整个行程中如出一辙地接连重演。
---
尼古拉耶夫是一座历史悠久的千年古城，曾是全俄举足轻重的造船工业中枢重镇，在俄罗斯工人运动与社会主义革命史上写下过极其浓墨重彩的一笔。19世纪初叶，全俄第一场波澜壮阔的工人重大罢工正是爆发于此。在“虚无主义”与“民意党”活跃的风暴岁月里，这里是全俄秘密革命地下斗争的重要策源地。在后来的岁月里，尼古拉耶夫孕育了“南俄工人同盟”——这是下乌克兰地区最早创立的社会民主主义革命组织之一，托洛茨基（Trotsky）正是其当仁不让的精神领袖。在尘封的旧档案馆里，我们偶然查阅到了与著名的涅恰耶夫（Netchayev）大案相关的核心绝密档案，这些文献以某种匪夷所思的方式辗转流落于此，尽管这位名震全俄的恐怖主义领袖生前从未在此地身陷囹圄。我们还惊喜地发掘出了当年沙皇秘密警察针对洛帕京、巴枯宁以及该时期其他享誉全俄的知名老革命家签发的绝密“搜查通缉令”。
尼古拉耶夫如今依然残存着往昔的几分优雅风姿，然而其宽阔优美的林荫大道上的参天大树却已被砍伐一空荡然无存——那是在白卫军仓皇败退与布尔什维克大军开进之间短短2天的权力真空期内，被全城断粮缺柴的市民疯狂砍伐殆尽的。大街小巷一片死寂压抑：该城正好处在弗兰格尔（Wrangel）大军正面进攻的锋芒轴线上。共产党人正在争分夺秒热火朝天地广泛发动组织群众构筑联合防线，他们特别面向产业无产阶级大声疾呼，严正提醒工人们切莫忘记弗兰格尔麾下的头号头目斯拉晓夫（Slastchev）将军当年对无产阶级犯下的血腥屠杀罪行——此人素以冷血镇压工人的刽子手47恶名昭彰。
周边周边各村镇对战局的态度，令布尔什维克当局深感坐卧不安。广大农民长期以来接连不断发动武装暴动抗击苏维埃政权，而劳动力军事动员的粗暴横蛮更是让产业工人们离心离德彻底寒了心。

===Original===

The documents I have examined at the unions and the statistics concerning the distribution of labor power (*rabsil*) and desertion, show that almost every village in the provinces of Kherson and Nikolayev has offered armed resistance. Yet the peasants have no sympathy with the monarchism of Wrangel; his victory may deprive them of the land they have taken from the large estates. Several provincial Soviets have sent delegates to Nikolayev to assure the authorities of their determination to fight the Whites. Encouraged, the Communists are conducting an intensive agitation among the peasantry along the route of Wrangel.
Fear of the Whites has revived stories of their atrocities. The Jewish population lives in mortal dread, previous occupations having been accompanied by fearful pogroms. At the “underground” restaurant near the Soviet House the guests relate incidents of almost incredible barbarities. They speak indiscriminately of Whites, Greens, Mariusa, Makhno, and others who have at various times invested the city. It is asserted that Mariusa, an amazon of mysterious identity, refrains from pillage: she “kills only Communists and Commissars.” Some insist that she is a sister of Makhno (though the latter has none), while others say she is a peasant girl who had sworn vengeance against the Bolsheviki because her lover was killed by a punitive expedition.
“The black years may know who they all be,” the hostess comments. “When Makhno was here last time people said they saw Mariusa with him. They beat and robbed Jews at the docks.”
“You are wrong,” the young Soviet employee who has been assigned to aid my work protests. “I helped to examine the men caught at that time. They were Greens and Grigoriev bandits. Mariusa wasn’t then in the city at all.”
“I heard Makhno himself speak,” remarked Vera, the daughter of the hostess, a young college girl. “It was on the square, and someone held a big black flag near him. He told the people they had nothing to fear, and that he would not permit any excesses. He said he would mercilessly punish anyone attempting a pogrom. I got a very favorable impression of him.”
“Whoever it is that makes pogroms,” her mother retorted, “we Jews are always the first victims.”
“Jews and Commissars,” the youth corrects.
“You are both﻿—you’d better look out,” a guest teased him.
“Better take off that *kurtka*” (leather jacket), another warns.

===Chinese===

我在各工会查阅调阅的绝密公文卷宗，以及关于劳动力（rabsil）调配征发与逃避劳役叛逃情况的详尽统计数据铁证如山地表明：赫尔松省与尼古拉耶夫省几乎每一个村落都曾对苏维埃政权进行过武装暴力抗税抗兵。然而，广大农民阶级对弗兰格尔企图复辟旧沙皇王朝的君主制同样深恶痛绝；白卫军的全面胜利意味着他们从大庄园手里夺取的救命田地将被彻底剥夺没收。周边数个地方省苏维埃特意派出了代表团赶赴尼古拉耶夫，向当局郑重宣誓誓死阻击白卫军的钢铁决心。大受鼓舞的共产党人随即趁热打铁，在弗兰格尔进军沿线的农村腹地展开了铺天盖地的政治宣传攻势。
对白卫军倒行逆施的深深恐惧，再度唤醒了民间关于白军血腥暴行的惨痛历史记忆。全城犹太居民更是生活在朝不保夕的死亡恐惧之中，因为以往每一次白军占领都必然伴随着惨绝人寰的大屠杀浩劫。在苏维埃大厦近旁的一处“地下”私营餐馆里，食客们绘声绘色讲述着令人发指的野蛮杀戮细节。在他们的口中，白军、绿军、神秘莫测的女头领玛柳莎（Mariusa）、马赫诺以及在不同时期轮番洗劫该城的各路武装势力被混为一谈。坊间笃定相传，身世扑朔迷离的女豪杰玛柳莎从不屑于劫掠民间财物：她“专门诛杀共产党员和特派政委”。有些人言之凿凿宣称她是马赫诺的亲妹妹（尽管众所周知马赫诺根本没有胞妹），另一些人则断言她不过是一个因情郎在惩罚性征粮队枪口下惨遭杀害而指天发誓向布尔什维克血债血偿的苦难农家复仇女。
“黑色的岁月才晓得他们到底都是些什么牛鬼蛇神，”女老板娘心有余悸地叹息道。“上一次马赫诺打进城时，街坊四邻都说亲眼看见玛柳莎伴随其左右。他们在码头上肆无忌惮地殴打洗劫犹太同胞。”
“你纯属信口开河一派胡言，”奉命专门协助我开展工作的一名年轻苏维埃机关干部当场义正词严地反驳道。“当时抓获的那批凶手我亲自参与了审讯。那帮暴徒纯粹是绿军残匪和格里戈里耶夫手下的散兵游勇。当时玛柳莎本人压根就没踏进过城里半步。”
“我曾亲耳聆听过马赫诺本人的现场演讲，”老板娘的千金薇拉（Vera）——一名年轻的女大学生插话道。“就在广场中央，当时有人在他身旁高高擎着一面黑色的大纛。他语重心长地向全城父老乡亲保证大家绝无后顾之忧，他决不容许任何害民过火行径。他当众严正宣告，凡胆敢挑起大屠杀者，一律军法从事格杀勿论。他给我留下的印象极好。”
“管他娘的是哪一路人马发动的大屠杀，”她的母亲心酸地反驳道，“横竖倒霉挨刀受死的永远是我们这些犹太人。”
“还有特派政委，”年轻干部纠正道。
“你这两样全占齐了——你最好多长几个脑袋留神自己的小命吧！”一位食客打趣调侃他道。
“赶紧把你身上那件招摇的皮夹克（kurtka）脱了吧，”另一位好心食客压低嗓门警告道。

## XXXIV A Bolshevik Trial / XXXIV 一场布尔什维克审判
===Original===

Having learned that old police records are in possession of the Extraordinary Commission, I visited Burov, the *predsedatel* of the Cheka. Very tall and broad, of coarse features and curt demeanor, he gave me the impression of a gendarme of the Romanov regime. He spoke in an abrupt, commanding tone, avoided my look, and seemed more interested in the large Siberian dog at his side than in my mission. He declined to permit me to examine the archives of the Third Department, but promised to select some material the Museum might be interested in, and asked me to call the next day.
His manner was not convincing, and I felt little faith in his assurances to aid my efforts. The following morning his secretary informed me that Burov had been too busy to attend to my request, but he could be seen at the Revolutionary Tribunal, where a trial was in progress.
On the dais of the Tribunal three men sat at a desk covered with a red cloth, the wall back of them decorated with lithographs of Lenin and Trotsky. At a small table below the dais was the defendant, a slender young man with a diminutive mustache, and near him an older man, his attorney. Burov, with the huge dog at his feet, was acting as the government prosecutor. The benches were occupied by witnesses, and soldiers were stationed in the aisles to preserve order.
The prisoner was charged with “counterrevolutionary activities,” the accusation brought by a young woman on the ground that he had “denounced her as a Communist to the Whites.” The witnesses were questioned first by the defense, then by the prosecutor and the judges. It appeared from their testimony that the prisoner and his accuser had for years lived in the same house and were known to be intimate. The proceedings dragged in a sleepy, uninteresting way until the attorney for the defense attempted to establish the fact that the woman, now a member of the Cheka, had formerly led a disreputable life. Burov slowly rose in his seat and pointing his finger at the lawyer, demanded: “Do you dare to attack the reputation of the Extraordinary Commission?” The attorney timidly appealed to the court for protection.

===Chinese===

获悉前沙皇警察的大批旧档案如今掌握在特肃委员会手中后，我专程造访了契卡（the Cheka）主席（predsedatel）布罗夫（Burov）。此人体格异常高大粗壮，五官粗鄙丑陋，举止生硬傲慢，给我的第一印象活脱脱像是一个罗曼诺夫王朝时期的沙皇宪兵。他说话带着生硬发号施令的腔调，眼神闪躲避免同我对视，比起我肩负的历史文献搜集使命，他似乎对自己身旁趴着的那条巨大的西伯利亚大狗更感兴趣。他断然拒绝准许我查阅臭名昭著的第三厅（沙皇秘密警察局）档案，但勉强答应挑拣一些博物馆可能感兴趣的零星资料，并让我翌日再去。
他的做派毫无诚意，我对他答应协助考察队工作的口头许诺根本不抱多大希望。次日清晨，他的秘书告知我布尔什维克首长公务过于繁忙抽不出空过问我的请求，但我可以前往革命法庭找他，那里正在开庭审理一起案件。
在革命法庭的高高审判席上，3名男子围坐在一张铺着鲜艳红布的办公桌后，他们身后的墙壁上悬挂装裱着列宁与托洛茨基（Trotsky）的石版肖像画。审判席下的一张小桌旁坐着受审被告——一个身形消瘦、留着一撇小胡子的年轻男子，他身旁坐着一位上了年纪的男子，那是他的辩护律师。布罗夫脚下趴着那条巨犬，本人则以政府公诉人的威严身份赫然在座。长条木椅上坐满了出庭证人，荷枪实弹的士兵沿走廊过道肃立把守以弹压维持法庭秩序。
被告被正式指控犯有“反革命破坏活动罪”，这项控告是一名年轻女子提起的，理由是被告曾“向白卫军告发检举她是共产党员”。出庭证人先接受辩方律师的盘问，随后由公诉人与审判官依次审问。从证人证言中可以看出，被告与原告多年来一直同住在一栋楼里，街坊四邻人尽皆知两人曾是亲密恋人。法庭审理程序在昏昏欲睡、枯燥乏味的氛围中拖沓冗长地进行，直到辩护律师试图举证查明：这位如今身为契卡工作人员的女原告，在旧社会时期曾长期过着声名狼藉的放荡皮肉生活。布罗夫从座位上霍然缓缓站起身来，用手指直指律师的鼻尖严厉质问道：“你竟敢公然攻击诋毁特肃委员会的崇高声誉？！”辩护律师吓得战战兢兢，急忙向审判席结结巴巴请求司法庇护。

===Original===

The presiding judge, in high boots and corduroy jacket, looking very tired and sipping cold coffee, expressed his revolutionary sympathy with the social victims of the abolished capitalist order and berated the attorney for persisting in bourgeois prejudices.
Burov examined the defense witnesses regarding their past mode of life and present political adherence. He referred to the prisoner as “that scoundrelly counterrevolutionist” and succeeded in eliciting affirmative replies to questions previously negated by the same witnesses. One of the latter, a young woman, testified to the good reputation of the defendant and his political non-partisanship. She looked frightened as Burov towered above her. He plied her with questions, and she became confused. Under the spell of the Chekist’s commanding voice she finally admitted that the accused was her brother.
A burst of indignation broke from the audience. On the bench in front of me an old man excitedly shouted: “You’ve terrorized her! She is no kin of his﻿—she’s my daughter!” The presiding judge shouted, “Silence!” and ordered the arrest of the interrupter for “behavior insulting to the high tribunal.”
During the noon recess I found an opportunity to speak to Burov. I called his attention to the character of the testimony. It was valueless, I pointed out; the witnesses were intimidated. Burov was much pleased. “There is no fooling with us, and they know it,” he said, indicating that all non-Communists are to be regarded as the natural enemies of the Bolshevik regime.
“The evidence is questionable,” I pursued; “will the prisoner be convicted?”
“I’ll demand the ‘highest measure of punishment,’ ” he replied, employing the official term for the death sentence.
“But the man may be innocent,” I protested.
“How can you speak so, *tovarish*?” he upbraided me. “You talk of evidence! Why, the uncle of this fellow was a rank *bourzhooi*, a big banker. He escaped with the Whites, and his whole family are counterrevolutionists. The best thing to do with such fellows is to *razmenyat* them” (to “change”﻿—the expression used in the South for summary execution).
Leaving the courtroom, I inadvertently stepped into a small chamber where two women sat on a bench. “*Tovarish* from the center,” one of them greeted me; “I saw you at Burov’s yesterday.”

===Chinese===

脚蹬高筒皮靴、身穿灯芯绒夹克的审判长面带倦容地抿了一口冷咖啡，义正词严地表达了自己对被推翻的资本主义旧秩序中社会受害者的革命阶级同情，并当场痛斥辩护律师依然顽固抱残守缺资产阶级的陈腐偏见。
布尔什维克公诉人布罗夫随即就辩方证人过去的生活方式以及当前的政治立场展开了严酷审讯。他一口一个指斥被告为“那个下流无耻的反革命分子”，并以高压诱导手腕成功从证人口中套出了对先前已被完全否认事实的肯定答复。在辩方证人中，有一名年轻女子出庭作证，竭力证明被告向来为人忠厚本分且在政治上保持完全中立。当高大威猛的布罗夫如铁塔般居高临下笼罩在她头顶时，姑娘吓得瑟瑟发抖面无血色。布罗夫连珠炮似地厉声逼问，姑娘被彻底逼得方寸大乱手足无措。在契卡头目那如晴天霹雳般具有绝对威慑力的严厉喝问魔咒下，她最终精神防线全面崩溃，被迫招认被告是自己的亲哥哥。
旁听席上顿时爆发出一阵强烈的义愤骚动。坐在我前排长椅上的一位老者情绪激动地霍然起身大喊：“你们这是在用恐怖手段刑讯逼供！她跟被告八竿子打不着——她是我自己的亲生闺女！”审判长猛击惊堂木厉声咆哮道：“肃静！”并当庭下令将这位贸然插话的老汉当场逮捕收押，罪名是“公然藐视亵渎崇高革命法庭”。
在正午法庭休庭间隙，我抓住了同布罗夫当面攀谈的难得机会。我委婉提醒他注意刚才那些证人证言的真实性质。我直言不讳地指出，那些口供毫无事实法律价值；证人完全是被高压恐吓逼供就范的。布罗夫对此却显得沾沾自喜得意洋洋。“跟我们契卡耍花招纯属白日做梦，那帮家伙心里明镜似的，”他直截了当地宣称，言下之意是所有的非共产党员理所当然都被视为布尔什维克政权的天然阶级敌人。
“这些证据疑点重重经不起推敲，”我进一步追问道；“被告最终会被定罪量刑吗？”
“我将代表国家公诉坚决要求判处‘最高惩治手段’，”他回答道，使用的是判处死刑枪决的官方术语。
“可这个人完全可能是清白无辜的啊，”我忍不住抗议道。
“同志，你怎么能讲出这种话来？！”他劈头盖脸训斥我道。“你居然跟我讲什么真凭实据！哼，这小子的亲舅舅可是个彻头彻尾的老爷们/资产阶级（bourzhooi），是个大银行家。当初跟着白卫军仓皇出逃，他们全家上下全都是死硬反革命分子。对付这种害群之马，最干净利落的办法就是把他们统统‘兑换’（razmenyat，南方用于表示就地枪决正法的黑话隐语）掉！”
迈出审判大厅，我漫无目的地无意中踏进了一间狭小偏僻的休息室，长椅上正坐着两名妇女。“中央来的同志，”其中一位主动向我打招呼，“昨天我在布罗夫办公室见过你。”

===Original===

She evidently took me for an official of the Moscow Cheka, and she at once became confidential. It was she who brought the charge against the prisoner, she said. They had been arrested together by the Whites, and when they were brought to the police station the defendant whispered something to the officer. She could not hear what he said, but she was sure that he had nodded in her direction. Both were locked up, but after a while the man was released while she was to be shot. She was positive the man had denounced her as a Bolshevik﻿—though she was not one at the time. She has become a Communist since, and now she is helping to fight counterrevolutionists, “same as you, *tovarish*,” she added significantly.
Her face, offensively painted even to her lips, was coarse and sensuous. Her eyes glowed with a vengeful light and the consciousness of power. Her companion, younger and more comely, resembled her in a marked degree.
“Are you sisters?” I asked.
“Cousins,” the younger one replied. “Katya is lying,” she broke out vehemently, “she’s jealous﻿—the man left her﻿—he didn’t care for her﻿—she wants revenge.”
“A lot he cares for you!” the other mocked. “You are younger﻿—that’s all. And he’s a dirty counterrevolutionist.”
The door opened and a woman entered. She looked very old, but her carriage was stately and her sad face beautiful in its frame of snowy white. “Are you a witness?” the Chekist girl demanded. “Have you been called?”
“No, my dear,” the old lady replied quietly; “I came of my own accord.” She smiled benignantly and continued in a soft, melodious voice: “Listen, dear, I am an old woman and I will soon be dying. I came to tell the truth. Why should you cause the death of that boy?” She looked kindly at the Chekist. “Bethink yourself, dear one. He has done you no harm.”
“Hasn’t he, though!” the other retorted angrily.
“My dear one,” the old lady pleaded, putting her hand affectionately on the girl’s arm, “let bygones be bygones. He cared for you and he ceased to care﻿—does he deserve death, dearest? Ah, I am old and I have seen much evil in my lifetime. Must we always go on hating and killing﻿—”
“To court!” a soldier shouted into the room. Both girls hurriedly rose, smoothed their hair, and walked out.
“Must we always be hating and killing?” the old woman repeated as she slowly followed them.

===Chinese===

她显然误将我当成了莫斯科（Moscow）契卡派来视察的高级密探干部，神情立刻变得毫无防备推心置腹起来。她神气活现地向我自夸，正是自己亲手将那名受审的男被告送上法庭告发指控的。她说，当年两人曾一同被白卫军拘捕缉拿，当被双双押解推进警署派出所时，男被告曾贴近白军军官耳边悄悄耳语了几句。她当时虽然没有听清对方究竟嘀咕了些什么，但她一口咬定看见男人的脑袋明显朝她坐着的方向使劲努嘴示意。随后两人被双双投入大牢，但过了不久，男的便被大摇大摆无罪开释，而她却被判处死刑押赴刑场执行枪决。她笃定确信正是这个男人出卖告密诬陷她是布尔什维克——尽管当时她根本还不是什么共产党员。自那以后她才火线加入了共产党，如今正在专门协助组织严厉镇压反革命分子，“就像你一样，同志，”她意味深长地补充了一句。
她的脸庞涂脂抹粉得极其刺眼扎眼，甚至连两片嘴唇也涂抹得艳俗不堪，五官粗鄙而充满肉欲气息。她的双眸中闪烁着病态狠毒的复仇凶光以及对自身所握生杀大权的狂妄亢奋。她身旁坐着的那位女伴年纪明显轻得多，容貌也更具几分清秀，但两人眉宇间却有着惊人的相似神韵。
“你们是亲姐妹俩吗？”我开口打听道。
“是表姐妹，”年轻姑娘抢先回答道。“卡佳（Katya）全是在胡编乱造满口谎言，”她突然情绪失控歇斯底里地尖声叫嚷起来，“她纯粹是在吃醋嫉妒——那男人早就把她给甩了——人家根本就瞧不上她——她这就是在公报私仇打击报复！”
“他瞧得上你又值几个铜板！”年长的那位刻薄冷笑着反唇相讥。“你不过就是占了年轻几岁的便宜罢了——仅此而已。横竖他就是个肮脏该死的反革命渣滓！”
房门悄然被推开，一位老妇人步履蹒跚地走了进来。她看起来风烛残年苍老之极，但仪态却雍容华贵、端庄高雅，满头如雪白发更加烘托衬托出那张写满悲苦却依然不失秀美的慈祥面庞。“你是出庭证人吗？”身为契卡人员的年轻姑娘神气活现地质问道。“法庭传唤你了吗？”
“没有，好姑娘，”老太太语调沉静柔和地回答道；“我是自己主动过来的。”她脸上浮现出慈祥和蔼的微笑，用极其温存悦耳的嗓音继续劝导着：“听我说，好孩子，我是个快要入土的老太婆了。我来只是想把良心话、把真相说个明白。你年纪轻轻，何苦非要把那小伙子往死路上逼呢？”她用充满慈爱的目光凝视着那位年轻的女契卡人员。“好闺女，摸摸良心好好想想吧。人家又没做过伤天害理对不起你的事。”
“他怎么就没做过！”姑娘火冒三丈气急败坏地恶狠狠顶撞回去。
“我亲爱的孩子，”老妇人满怀怜爱地把手轻轻搭在姑娘的胳膊上苦苦哀求道，“冤家宜解不宜结，过去的就让它随风去吧。他曾经爱过你，后来不爱了——难道移情别恋就该被拉去枪毙送命吗，好孩子？唉，我这辈子活得够长了，见识过人间太多太多的罪恶孽债了。难道我们大家非要一辈子这么无休止地互相仇恨、互相残杀下去吗——”
“全体起立，法庭开庭！”一名士兵猛地推开大门朝屋里大声厉吼传唤。两个姑娘闻声慌忙仓皇站起身来，匆匆理了理散乱的头发，快步鱼贯步出房间。
“难道我们大家非要一辈子这么无休止地互相仇恨、互相残杀下去吗？”老妇人一边失魂落魄地喃喃自语重复着，一边迈着沉重的步伐缓缓尾随其后跟了出去。

## XXXV Returning to Petrograd / XXXV 重返彼得格勒
===Original===

After a stay of several days we left Nikolayev, returning to Odessa by the same maritime auto truck. We followed the former route and witnessed the previous scenes again. Our reception was even more unfriendly than before.
Occasionally some good-natured soldier offered to pay with Sovietsky money, but the villagers pleaded that they could do nothing with the “colored papers,” and begged for articles of “manufacture.” The chauffeur produced a can of watered gasoline, which he had persuaded an old peasant to exchange for a smoked ham by assuring him that it was the “best kerosene in Russia.” The neighbors protested, but the old man, too frightened to refuse, gave up the treasured meat, muttering: “May the Lord have mercy on us and see you depart soon.”
In Odessa we learn that the Red Army is in full retreat from Warsaw, and Wrangel steadily advancing from the southeast. The alarming situation makes the further progress of the Expedition impossible. Our anxiety is increased by the circumstance that the mandate for the use of our car expires on October 31, after which date the Railroad Commission has the right of immediate confiscation, involving the probable loss of our material. Our repeated letters and wires have remained without reply by the Narkomput (People’s Commissariat of Ways and Communication) in Moscow. No choice is left us but to hasten back to Petrograd to deliver to the Museum our collection which has grown so large it requires an entire *tepulshka* (freight car).
*September 20﻿–﻿30.*﻿—At last we have left Odessa and are now journeying by slow stages northward. The lines are clogged with military trains, “dead” engines, and wrecked cars. At Znamenka we come upon the rear of the Twelfth Army retreating in disorder. The Bolsheviki are evacuating points along the route of the expected Polish advance. Large areas have been left without any government at all, the Communists having departed, the Poles not pursuing. The Red Army is rolling back toward Kiev and Kharkov. Our train is constantly delayed or switched to a side track, to clear the way for the military. Should the enemy advance, our Expedition may be entirely cut off from the north, finding itself between Wrangel’s forces in the south and southeast, and the Poles in the north and northwest.
Progressing only a few miles a day, we pass Birsula, Vanyarki, Zhmerinka, and Kasatin.

===Chinese===

在逗留数日之后，我们告别了尼古拉耶夫，搭乘同一辆海运特别处的卡车折返回敖德萨（Odessa）。我们沿原路返回，沿途再度目睹了此前见识过的种种凄惨景象。沿线村民对我们的态度甚至比去程时还要充满敌意冷漠。
偶尔有几名心善的红军士兵主动提出用苏维埃纸币付款买东西，但村民们苦苦哀求称他们拿这些“花花绿绿的废纸”根本买不到任何生计，恳求换取工业“制成品”。卡车司机拿出一桶掺了水的大杂烩汽油，言之凿凿向一位老农吹嘘这是“全俄最好的煤油”，硬是哄骗老汉换了一整只熏火腿。四邻街坊纷纷出面抗议，但老汉吓得战战兢兢不敢推拒，只得含泪交出珍藏的熏肉，嘴里喃喃祷告着：“愿天主怜悯我们，保佑你们这帮瘟神赶紧滚蛋吧。”
在敖德萨，我们获悉红军（Red Army）正从华沙前线全线溃退，而弗兰格尔（Wrangel）正从东南方向稳步大举推进。这般令人惊骇的危局，使得考察队继续深入南下的计划彻底成为泡影。令我们更加焦虑万分的是，我们这节专列车厢的使用特许公函将于10月31日正式到期，逾期之后铁道委员会将享有立即无偿没收车厢的法定特权，这意味着我们搜集到的大批珍贵历史文献极有可能面临付诸东流的灭顶之灾。我们拍发给莫斯科（Moscow）交通人民委员部（Narkomput）的数封公函与加急电报，始终如石沉大海杳无音讯。万般无奈之下，我们别无选择，唯有争分夺秒火速折返彼得格勒（Petrograd），将这批因搜集成果过于浩大以至于需要占用整整一节闷罐货车车厢（tepulshka）的珍贵藏品安全移交博物馆。
9月20日至30日。——我们终于撤离了敖德萨，如今正沿着铁路线极其缓慢地分段向北蠕动前行。整条铁路线被大批军用列车、“趴窝死机”的蒸汽机车以及被炸毁的残破车厢堵得水泄不通。在兹纳缅卡（Znamenka），我们一头撞上了正在溃不成军狼狈后撤的第十二集团军后卫部队。在波兰军队预定长驱直入的推进沿线，布尔什维克正在仓皇疏散撤离各处据点。大片大片的辽阔腹地沦为了完全没有政权管辖的无政府真空地带——共产党人早已撤走，波兰军队尚未进占追击。红军主力正全线向基辅（Kiev）与哈尔科夫（Kharkov）方向倒卷溃败。为了给军列让路，我们的车厢被接二连三延误滞留，或是被频繁扳道推入避难侧线。倘若强敌大举进逼，我们考察队极有可能被彻底切断北撤退路，陷入南方及东南方弗兰格尔大军与北方及西北方波军重重合围关门打狗的绝境。
列车每日仅能爬行区区数英里，我们缓慢经停了比尔祖拉、瓦尼亚尔基、日梅林卡以及卡扎京。

===Original===

The Communists no longer deny the great disaster. The Polish campaign has ended in a complete rout, and Wrangel is driving the Red Army before him. It is claimed that Makhno has joined forces with the counterrevolutionary general. The Soviet papers we occasionally find in the “educational bureau” at the stations brand the *povstantsi* leader as Wrangel’s aid. Familiar with the methods of the Communist press, we do not give credence to the “news,” but our anxiety to learn the facts of the situation is increased by the persistent report that England demands the entire withdrawal of the Bolsheviki from the Ukraine.
The approaches to Kiev are blocked, and we are detained twelve versts from the city. Two days of maneuvering bring us at last in visible distance of the passenger station, where we remain for the night. Kiev is being evacuated. A visit to the union headquarters fails to discover any prominent officials: they are preparing to leave in case of “revolutionary necessity.” There is much speculation whether the Bolsheviki will release the political prisoners before surrendering the town, as the enemy will undoubtedly execute all revolutionists who fall into its hands. On the streets joy at the departure of the Communists struggles with dread of the hated Poles.
Rising next morning at nine (by the new time; seven by the sun) I am surprised not to find my clothes in their accustomed place. Believing my friends to have played a hoax, I proceed to wake them. All my apparel and personal effects are gone: we have been robbed! The thief evidently entered through the open corridor window; the imprint of his bare feet is still on the rain-softened ground. We are certain the theft was committed by the *militsioneri* guarding the woodpile about thirty feet from our car. It was a moonlit night. No one could have climbed through the window without being seen by the sentry. In any case, a desperate undertaking, for such acts are now punishable with death. Our interviews with the soldiers, inquiry and investigation fail to throw light on the robbery, and at heart we are glad. The loss, however great, is not worth a human life.
*October 2.*﻿—Journeying toward Kursk. We still hope to be able to return south by way of Yekaterinoslav, but rumors are persistent of the taking of that city by Wrangel.
Splendid autumn day, clear and sunny.

===Chinese===

共产党人再也无法掩盖掩饰这场空前的大惨败了。波兰战局最终演变成为一场全线溃败的大溃退，而弗兰格尔正驱赶着红军主力抱头鼠窜。坊间盛传马赫诺已同这位反革命白卫军将领合流结盟。我们在各车站“文化宣教局”偶尔捡拾到的苏维埃官方报纸上，赫然将这位起义游击队（povstantsi）领袖打成弗兰格尔的走狗帮凶。由于深谙共产党报刊宣传机器的一贯套路，我们对这类“新闻”自然嗤之以鼻不予采信；然而，坊间顽固风传英国正强硬勒令布尔什维克全面撤出乌克兰（Ukraine）的绝密情报，却更加剧了我们渴望探明时局真相的焦灼心理。
通往基辅的咽喉通道已被军方重重封锁阻断，我们被阻滞扣留在距离基辅城外12俄里处。历经长达2天的艰难周折调度，我们的车厢终于被缓缓牵引推进到了肉眼能够遥望客运车站的侧线，我们在车上露宿过夜。基辅全城正在紧急大疏散。我们走访了工会总部，却找不到哪怕一位高级干部的踪影：他们全都打点好行装，做好了随时因“革命需要”脚底抹油撤离的万全准备。坊间正热烈猜测布尔什维克在弃城投降之前究竟是否会开释释放政治犯，因为一旦落入敌手，白军与波兰军队势必将所有革命志士斩尽杀绝。在大街小巷，人们因共产党即将弃城而萌生的暗自窃喜，与对凶残波兰侵略者的刻骨恐惧激烈交织在一起。
翌日清晨9点（新时间；按太阳时为7点）醒来时，我惊诧地发现自己平日叠放整齐的衣物竟然不翼而飞。起初我还以为是车厢同仁在跟我开恶作剧，便急忙敲门叫醒大家。然而，我所有的换洗衣物与随身细软全被席卷一空：我们遭贼了！窃贼显然是从列车敞开的走廊车窗翻爬进来的；在雨水泡软的泥地上，至今还清晰印着他光脚作案的一串深深脚印。我们笃定确信，这起盗窃案正是守卫在距离我们车厢约30英尺外木料堆旁的民警卫兵干的。昨夜月色皎洁如昼。若无哨兵放水甚至亲自作案，任何人爬窗作案都绝无逃脱哨兵眼睛的可能。无论如何，这堪称一场亡命徒的铤而走险，因为此类偷盗行径按现行军法一律当场格杀枪决。我们同士兵们的当面交涉、排查盘问未能查明失窃真相，但我们心底却暗自感到庆幸。财物损失固然惨重，但它终究抵不上一条活生生的人命。
10月2日。——列车正向库尔斯克（Kursk）方向缓缓挺进。我们心底依然残存着一线能够取道叶卡捷琳诺斯拉夫（Yekaterinoslav）重返南方考察的渺茫希望，然而关于该城已被弗兰格尔主力攻陷占领的谣传却甚嚣尘上不绝于耳。
这是一个秋高气爽、万里无云的明媚艳阳天。

===Original===

The country is luscious﻿—fields of black soil, primitive forests of oak and fir. But the weather is growing cold, and our car is unheated for lack of dry wood. Our supplies are almost exhausted; even the resourceful culinary art of Emma Goldman is unable to produce meals from an empty larder.
In the evening a rare sunset over the Western slope, the horizon aglow with luminous red. Broad lines of purple afloat on an azure background, its base a light yellow of frayed edges. Now the dense woods hide the sky. I catch glimpses of the paling fire shimmering through the trees. Windmills of ancient Russian type and peasant *khati*, their roofs straw-covered, sides whitewashed, slowly pass by, looking melancholy. Women at work in the fields; children driving a flock of black sheep. A solitary peasant trudges behind a pair of oxen hitched to a plow of primitive design. The country is even, flat, monotonous. It is growing dusk; our candles are exhausted.
In the gathering darkness, our little commune around the table, we exchange reminiscences. Today is the first anniversary of my release from the Federal prison.48 A year rich with experience: intense days of anti-conscription agitation and opposition to world slaughter, arrest and detention at Ellis Island, stealthy deportation, and then﻿—Russia and the life of the revolutionary period.
With the touching curiosity of the Russian in all things American our colleagues are absorbed in the story. The skyscraper, that bold striving toward the heights, is the symbol of the far-off world to them. Though theoretically familiar with American industrialism, their faith in that country as “free” is traditional, persistent, and they experience a rude awakening at the recital of the actual facts of our economic and political life. Habituated to thinking the “American” a gentleman of large and noble nature, with a touch of manly irresponsibility almost akin to “interesting madness,” they are deeply shocked by the picture of prison existence with its tortures of solitary, underground dungeon, and “bull ring.” Under the most cruel Tsarist regime, they assure me, the politicals were accorded better treatment even in the worst casemates of Petropavlovsk and Schlüsselburg.

===Chinese===

这片原野丰饶美丽——肥沃黝黑的辽阔黑土地，参天蔽日的橡树与冷杉原始森林。然而天气正一天天变得严寒刺骨，由于极度匮乏干燥的劈柴，我们的车厢里没有半点暖气。我们的给养储备已几乎告罄；即便利落能干、烹饪手艺高超的爱玛·戈尔德曼（Emma Goldman），面对空空如也的储物柜也难为无米之炊。
傍晚时分，西方的斜坡上铺展出一幅人间罕见的绝美落日图卷，整个天际线被一片瑰丽夺目的火红色晚霞彻底点燃。宽广如丝带般的深紫色霞光漂浮在蔚蓝色的天鹅绒背景之上，底边镶嵌着一抹边缘破碎的淡黄色金边。少顷，浓密的森林遮蔽了苍穹。透过树梢的缝隙，我依稀窥见斑驳闪烁的落日残火。具有古老俄罗斯特色的木风车与农舍（khati）缓缓掠过车窗，茅草覆顶、白灰粉刷的墙壁，在暮色中透出淡淡的凄凉。妇人们依然在田野里辛勤劳作；几个牧童正驱赶着一小群黑羊。一位形单影只的农夫拖着沉重的步伐，跟在一对套在原始简陋木犁上的耕牛身后艰难前行。大地平缓、辽阔、单调乏味。暮色渐浓；我们储备的蜡烛也终于燃烧殆尽。
在渐次降临的黑暗中，我们这个小小的公社围坐桌旁，互诉衷肠追忆往昔。今天恰好是我从美国联邦监狱刑满获释的一周年纪念日48。这是经历波澜壮阔、沧桑巨变的一整年：那些狂风暴雨般反征兵反战示威抗议、誓死反对帝国主义世界大屠杀的激荡岁月，在埃利斯岛（Ellis Island）遭到拘押囚禁，在深更半夜被武装押解驱逐出境，再到后来——踏上俄罗斯这片土地，投身轰轰烈烈的风暴大革命现实生活之中。
怀着俄罗斯人对美洲万事万物特有的动人好奇心，同仁们全神贯注地倾听着我的叙述。摩天大楼这种人类向苍穹桀骜挺进的建筑奇迹，在他们心中正是那个遥远国度的具象化象征。尽管在理论上他们熟稔美国的垄断工业文明，但对于将那个国家视作“自由灯塔”的朴素信念却根深蒂固、执迷不悟；当听我亲口描摹出美利坚政治经济生活的残酷现实真相时，他们经受了一场当头棒喝般的幻灭震惊。他们向来习惯于认定“美洲人”个个是心胸宽广、品格高尚的绅士，带着一股豪迈不羁、近乎于“可爱疯狂”的洒脱男子气概；因此，当听到关于铁窗囹圄中单独禁闭、阴暗水牢以及“牛圈吊打”等灭绝人性的残酷折磨时，他们被深深震撼骇呆了。他们言之凿凿向我保证，即便在沙皇专制最黑暗残酷的岁月里，关押在彼得保罗要塞和施吕塞尔堡（Schlüsselburg）最阴森死牢里的政治犯，所受到的待遇也远比这要文明人道得多。

===Original===

“Is it possible,” our Secretary asks for the third time, “that in free, cultured America a prisoner may for years be kept in solitary, deprived of exercise and visitors?”49
Only one year ago, yet how far it all seems, how removed from the present! Long trains of artillery rush through the darkness: the revolutionary army is routed on all fronts. The soldiers’ plaintive song, now Russian, now Ukrainian, grips the heart with heavy sadness, as our train slowly creeps along the flatlands of Northern Ukraine.
*October 21.* A clear, cold day. The first snow of the season on the ground, Moscow presents a familiar sight, and I feel at home after our long absence.
Eagerly I absorb the news at the Commissariat of Foreign Affairs. The Twelfth Army has precipitately retreated from Warsaw, but the Poles are not pursuing. It is officially realized now what a serious and costly mistake the campaign was, and how baseless the expectations of a revolution in Poland. It is hoped that a quick peace may be patched up without too great sacrifices on the part of Russia.
Happier is the news from other fronts. Eastern Siberia has been cleared of the last remnants of Kolchak’s army under Ataman Semyonov. In the Crimea Wrangel is almost entirely crushed, not the least share of credit admittedly belonging to Makhno. Far from aiding the counterrevolutionary forces, as had been reported, the *povstantsi* joined the fight against the White general. This development was the result of a politico-military agreement between the Bolsheviki and Makhno, the main condition of the latter being the immediate liberation of the imprisoned Anarchists and Makhnovtsi, and a guarantee of free speech and press for them in the Ukraine. The telegram sent at the time by Makhno requesting the presence of Emma Goldman and myself at the conferences did not reach us. It was not forwarded by the Foreign Office.
Our anxiety about Henry Alsberg is relieved: he is now safely in Riga, having been permitted to leave Russia after his forced return from the south. Albert Boni and Pat Quinlan are in the Cheka, no definite reason for their detention being assigned. Mrs. Harrison, my erstwhile neighbor in the Kharitonensky, is held as a British spy. Nuorteva, Soviet representative in New York, was deported from the States and is now at the head of the British-American bureau in the Foreign Office.

===Chinese===

“这怎么可能呢，”我们的秘书第三次难以置信地追问道，“难道在自由文明、文化高度发达的美利坚，一名囚犯竟然会被长年累月关押在暗无天日的单独禁闭室里，彻底剥夺一切放风运动和亲友探视的权利吗49？！”
仅仅才过去短短一年时间，然而这一切回想起来却又是何等遥远，仿佛与眼前的严酷现实恍如隔世！一列列运载重炮的军用列车在漆黑的夜色中呼啸疾驰而过：革命军在前线全线溃败。战士们哀怨动人的悲歌，时而唱着俄语，时而哼着乌克兰语，在我们列车沿着北乌克兰平原缓缓蠕动爬行的漫漫长夜里，揪痛了每一个人的心房。
10月21日。——这是一个寒风凛冽、万里无云的大晴天。漫天初雪已悄然覆盖了大地，莫斯科（Moscow）呈现出那熟悉的熟悉街景；在历经漫长的颠沛流离之后，我重返此地感觉宛如回到了自己的家园。
我迫不及待地赶往外交人民委员部，急切吸收消化各路最新前线战报。第十二集团军已从华沙城下仓皇溃退撤出，但波兰军队并未全力追击。当局高层如今在官方层面上终于清醒意识到，此前发起波兰战役是一个何等严峻致命且代价高昂的战略误判，而寄望于波兰工人爆发内部革命的幻想又是何等荒诞无稽。高层期盼能够在苏俄不承担过于割地赔款惨重代价的前提下，火速媾和缔结城下之盟。
来自其他战线的战报则令人振奋得多。在东西伯利亚，阿塔曼谢苗诺夫麾下的高尔察克（Kolchak）残部已被彻底肃清剿灭。在克里米亚半岛，弗兰格尔主力已被打得落花流水几乎全军覆没，而公认在此役中立下头功的恰恰正是马赫诺。与此前官方大肆污蔑造谣的情况截然相反，起义游击队（povstantsi）非但没有勾结反动势力，反而在围歼白卫军战役中立下了汗马功劳。这一戏剧性的战局转折，源自布尔什维克同马赫诺秘密达成的军事政治同盟协议，而马赫诺提出的核心先决条件，正是要求苏维埃政权立刻无条件释放所有在押关押的无政府主义者与马赫诺运动同袍，并由国家明文担保他们在乌克兰享有绝对的言论自由与出版自由。当年马赫诺亲自发给爱玛·戈尔德曼与我的、盛情邀请我们作为特邀代表出席和平谈判的加急电报，却被外交人民委员部恶意扣押，从未送达我们手中。
我们对亨利·阿尔斯伯格安危的揪心牵挂终于得以释怀：在被强行从南方遣送回莫斯科之后，他已获准离境，目前已平安抵达里加。阿尔伯特·博尼与帕特·昆兰依然被羁押在契卡（the Cheka）大牢里，当局从未出具任何拘留羁押的法定事由。我早年在哈里托年斯基大宅的前邻居哈里森夫人，被冠以英国间谍罪名羁押收审。曾任苏维埃驻纽约全权代表的努奥特瓦，遭到美国当局强行驱逐出境之后，如今正执掌着外交人民委员部英美事务局的一把手大印。

===Original===

Rosenberg, the bad-tempered and ill-mannered confidential secretary of Chicherin, all-powerful and cordially disliked, is about to leave for the Far East, “on an important mission,” as he informs me. Incidentally, as if by afterthought, he refers to the “funeral tomorrow,” and with a shock I learn of the death of John Reed. The Expedition is to leave this evening for Petrograd, but we decide to postpone our departure in order to pay the last tribute to our dead friend.
A fresh grave along the Kremlin wall, opposite the Red Square, the honored resting place of the revolutionary martyrs. I stand at the brink, supporting Louise Bryant who has entirely abandoned herself to her grief. She had hastened from America to meet Jack after a long separation. Missing him in Petrograd, she proceeded to Moscow only to learn that Reed had been ordered to Baku to the Congress of Eastern Peoples. He had not quite recovered from the effects of his imprisonment in Finland and he was unwilling to undertake the arduous journey. But Zinoviev insisted; it was imperative, he said, to have America represented, and like a good Party soldier Jack obeyed. But his weakened constitution could not withstand the hardships of Russian travel and its fatal infections. Reed was brought back to Moscow critically ill. In spite of the efforts of the best physicians he died on October 16.
The sky is wrapped in gray. Rain and sleet are in the air. Between the speakers’ words the rain strikes Jack’s coffin, punctuating the sentences as if driving nails into the casket. Clear and rounded like the water drops are the official eulogies falling upon the hearing with dull meaninglessness. Louise cowers on the wet ground. With difficulty I persuade her to rise, almost forcing her to her feet. She seems in a daze, oblivious to the tribute of the Party mourners. Bukharin, Reinstein, and representatives of Communist sections of Europe and America praise the advance guard of world revolution, while Louise is desperately clutching at the wooden coffin. Only young Feodosov, who had known and loved Jack and shared quarters with him, sheds a ray of warmth through the icy sleet. Kollontai speaks of the fine manhood and generous soul that was Jack. With painful sincerity she questions herself﻿—did not John Reed succumb to the neglect of true comradeship.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
